### [1] [Making AI-Assisted Claims Independently Challengeable: Publication Authority and a Protocol for Falsifiable Publication Records](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17631)
*Torsten Olivi Tiltack,Yifei Dong,Kun Yu,Xu Wang,Wei Liu,Jianlong Zhou,Ren Ping Liu,Fang Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Publication Authority as an exact-state, non-transferable, single-use publication capability, instantiated as PAC-2026 (Publication-Accountability Calculus) under its fourth semantic freeze (SF-4). Six non-compensating obligations each yield a target-bound witness, localized counterexample, or localized unverifiability; only a fresh complete all-pass record derives a single-use permit for one atomic publication transition. Evaluation via identity vectors, adversarial cases, finite models, and historical implementations supports internal coherence and bounded safety, but not factual truth or field efficacy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI-assisted claims can appear authoritative when evidence, analysis, human authorization, presentation, and correction history refer to different states. Existing mechanisms like provenance, attestation, and transparency expose history but do not specify the particular publication transition at stake, leaving a gap in ensuring that authorization applies to the exact state being published.

Method: Publication Authority is defined as an exact-state, non-transferable, single-use capability, instantiated in PAC-2026, an AIJIM Protocol candidate at its fourth bounded semantic freeze (SF-4). Six obligations cover evidence, runs and artifacts, measurement disclosure, authorization, surface correspondence, and lifecycle continuity, each producing a target-bound witness, localized counterexample, or localized unverifiability. Evaluation uses identity vectors, adversarial cases, finite models, and historical implementations.

Result: Ten finite models explored 110,764 safe reachable states; 76 unsafe configurations yielded the expected violation or observer countermodel. A reader surface passing correspondence cannot authorize publication unless the accepted record admits that surface. SF-4 separates evidence horizon from verification time and rejects authentic but causally invalid authorization. A historical predecessor path reproduced 17 frozen authorization-successor outcomes; a later instance-blind test matched all 183 scored expectations, and same-host package execution reproduced 240 archived observations.

Conclusion: Results support internal coherence, bounded safety, fault sensitivity, and limited constructibility, but do not establish factual truth, general refinement, blind interoperability, field efficacy, or standards status.

Abstract: AI-assisted claims can appear authoritative when evidence, analysis, human authorization, presentation, and correction history refer to different states. Provenance, attestation, and transparency expose history but alone do not specify the publication transition examined here. We develop Publication Authority as an exact-state, non-transferable, single-use publication capability and instantiate it in PAC-2026 (Publication-Accountability Calculus), a machine-readable AIJIM Protocol candidate. We evaluate its fourth bounded semantic freeze (SF-4), a fixed-profile specification designed for replaceable bindings. Six obligations govern evidence, runs and artifacts, measurement disclosure, authorization, surface correspondence, and lifecycle continuity. Each yields a target-bound witness, localized counterexample, or localized unverifiability; none can compensate for another. Only a fresh, complete all-pass record derives the permit consumed by one atomic publication transition. We use identity vectors, adversarial cases, finite models, and historical implementations. Ten models explored 110,764 safe reachable states; 76 unsafe configurations produced the expected violation or observer countermodel. A reader surface passing its correspondence check cannot authorize publication unless the accepted record admits that surface. SF-4 separates evidence horizon from verification time and rejects an authentic but causally invalid authorization. A historical predecessor path reproduced 17 frozen authorization-successor outcomes. A later in-house, instance-blind test of known case classes matched all 183 scored expectations; same-host package execution reproduced its 240 archived observations. Results support internal coherence, bounded safety, fault sensitivity, and limited constructibility, but not factual truth, general refinement, blind interoperability, field efficacy, or standards status.

</details>


### [2] [EvolveTrade: Experience-Driven Policy Refinement for Self-Evolving LLM Trading Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17632)
*Sehee Kim,Yumin Choi,Minki Kang,Sung Ju Hwang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EvolveTrade treats a tool-using LLM trading agent's system prompt as a text-parameterized policy that a Policy Agent revises after each interval using decision traces and realized portfolio feedback, while the backbone LLM stays frozen. Across multiple market regimes and two LLM backbones, it often outperforms fixed-policy baselines in Sharpe Ratio and Cumulative Return.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM trading agents can fuse market data, news, and executable analysis, but their tool-use policies are static and hand-written, fixed before deployment. This prevents them from adapting how they gather evidence, invoke tools, verify signals, and manage risk as market regimes change.

Method: EvolveTrade frames the system prompt of a tool-using trading agent as a text-parameterized policy. After each update interval, a Policy Agent revises this policy based on accumulated decision traces and realized portfolio feedback, keeping the backbone LLM frozen. The revised policy governs the next batch of trading decisions, enabling the agent to refine its information-acquisition and portfolio-construction procedures over time.

Result: Experiments across multiple market regimes and two LLM backbones show EvolveTrade often improves Sharpe Ratio and Cumulative Return over fixed-policy LLM baselines, achieving improved SR and CR in most evaluated settings. Behavioral analyses show self-evolved policies increase code-mediated analysis and activate regime-relevant computations; case-level policy-to-return attributions trace how policy-induced allocation changes contribute to realized return differences.

Conclusion: Adapting the reusable procedure governing tool use—rather than the underlying model weights—is a key direction for building more robust LLM trading agents.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) trading agents can combine market data, news, and executable analysis, but their behavior is often controlled by static hand-written tool-use policies that are fixed before deployment. This limits their ability to adapt how they gather evidence, invoke tools, verify signals, and manage risk under changing market regimes. We introduce EvolveTrade, a self-evolving framework that treats the system prompt of a tool-using trading agent as a text-parameterized policy. After each update interval, a Policy Agent revises this policy using accumulated decision traces and realized portfolio feedback, while keeping the backbone LLM fixed. The updated policy is then used for the next batch of trading decisions, enabling the agent to refine its information-acquisition and portfolio-construction procedure over time. Experiments across multiple market regimes and two LLM backbones show that EvolveTrade often improves Sharpe Ratio and Cumulative Return over fixed-policy LLM baselines, achieving the improved SR and CR in most evaluated settings. Behavioral analyses further show that self-evolved policies increase code-mediated analysis and activate regime-relevant computations; case-level policy-to-return attributions trace how policy-induced allocation changes contribute to realized return differences. These results suggest that adapting the reusable procedure governing tool use is a key direction for building more robust LLM trading agents.

</details>


### [3] [One Color Preprocessing Improves DSATUR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17633)
*Adam Nouira,Lucas Isenmann*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SSLD improves DSATUR for graph coloring by using an SDP (Lovász-theta-like) to precompute one high-quality color class before DSATUR finishes the coloring, matching or beating DSATUR on over 1600 instances at ~195x runtime cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: DSATUR is one of the fastest heuristics for the NP-hard Graph Coloring Problem, but it typically uses more colors than state-of-the-art algorithms. The paper aims to improve coloring quality while retaining the DSATUR framework.

Method: SSLD (Semidefinite Spectral Learning with DSATUR) preprocesses a first good color class via a Semidefinite Programming formulation similar to the SDP used to compute the Lovász theta number, then lets DSATUR complete the coloring. It is claimed to be the first approach improving DSATUR through fixed color-class preprocessing. It is compared against DSATUR and a naive 1-color-class preprocessing (GISD) baseline.

Result: Evaluated on DIMACS instances, random graphs (Erdős–Rényi, Watts–Strogatz, Barabási–Albert), Frequency Assignment, and Job Shop Scheduling instances: SSLD matches or beats DSATUR in almost every case across over 1600 benchmarks and outperforms the naive GISD baseline, confirming the value of the SDP-guided first color class. Runtime is roughly 195x slower than DSATUR.

Conclusion: SDP-guided preprocessing of a first color class is a promising direction for future improvements in graph coloring heuristics, delivering better quality than DSATUR at a significant runtime cost.

Abstract: The Graph Coloring Problem (GCP) is NP-hard and DSATUR stands as one of the fastest heuristics for it despite producing colorings that typically use more colors than state-of-the-art coloring algorithms. We propose SSLD (Semidefinite Spectral Learning with DSATUR), which improves DSATUR by preprocessing a first good color class before letting DSATUR complete coloring the rest of the given graph. We obtain this color class from a Semidefinite Programming (SDP), similar to an SDP used to compute the Lovász theta number. To the best of our knowledge, SSLD is the first approach to improve DSATUR by preprocessing through fixed color classes. We evaluate SSLD against DSATUR and against a naive 1-color-class preprocessing algorithm on DIMACS instances, random graphs (Erdős--Rényi, Watts-Strogatz, Barabási--Albert), Frequency Assignment and Job Shop Scheduling instances. SSLD matches or beats DSATUR in almost every case across over 1600 benchmark instances, and out performs the naive GISD baseline, allows us to confirm the value brought by the SDP-guided choice of the first color class. This quality comes at a runtime cost of roughly 195 times slower that DSATUR, but demonstrating that SDP-guided preprocessing of a first color class is a direction for future improvements.

</details>


### [4] [Physics-Constrained Digital Twins for Sensor Integrity in Urban Pedestrian Flow: Detecting Stealthy False Data Injection with Conformal Guarantees](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17635)
*Oscar Mogollon Gutierrez,Fatemeh Ghasemi,Mohammadhossein Homaei,Andres Caro,Mar Avila*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper formalizes stealthy false data injection attacks against city-scale pedestrian counting digital twins, which are far more rank-deficient than power/water networks, and shows that a flow-conservation residual (not graph locality) provides the main detection gain.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pedestrian counting systems drive economic indicators, planning decisions and safety operations, but the digital twins built on them treat the incoming count stream as ground truth. The paper asks what happens when that stream is corrupted by stealthy false data injection, and how much protection the underlying physics (flow conservation) actually provides.

Method: They formalise stealthy false data injection for city-scale pedestrian sensing, noting the observation map is much more rank-deficient than in power/water networks. Their twin estimates directed flows on the pedestrian street graph, assimilates counts via a learned graph-localised gain, and is trained against a flow conservation residual coupling metered and unmetered segments. Detection combines the innovation with that residual, with an alarm threshold set by adaptive conformal calibration. They define the "attack margin" as the relative reduction in worst-case corruption of the estimated flow field, measured against a white-box adversary that optimizes directly through the twin, and ablate the street graph against a distance graph.

Result: On six years of Melbourne data, the attack margin reaches 0.54 against a single compromised device and falls to 0.19 when a third of the fleet is compromised, on a network where only 1.18% of walkable segments are metered. Replacing the street graph with a distance graph collapses the margin to 0.09, indicating the gain stems from the conservation law rather than from locality.

Conclusion: Physics-based flow conservation, rather than graph locality, is what buys robustness against stealthy false data injection in city-scale pedestrian sensing; detection remains effective for sparse compromises but degrades as more devices are corrupted, and adaptive conformal calibration removes the need for hand-tuned thresholds.

Abstract: City pedestrian counting systems now feed economic indicators, planning decisions and safety operations, yet the twins built on top of them treat the incoming stream as ground truth. We study what happens when it is not. We formalise stealthy false data injection for city-scale pedestrian sensing, where the map from latent flow to observation is far more rank deficient than in the power and water networks for which stealth has been characterised. Our twin estimates directed flows on the pedestrian street graph, assimilates counts through a learned graph-localised gain, and is trained against a flow conservation residual that couples metered and unmetered segments. Detection combines the innovation with that residual, and the alarm threshold is set by adaptive conformal calibration rather than by hand. To measure what the physics buys, we define the attack margin, the relative reduction in worst-case corruption of the estimated flow field, achieved against a white-box adversary that optimises directly through the twin. On six years of Melbourne data the margin reaches 0.54 against a single compromised device and falls to 0.19 when a third of the fleet is compromised, on a network where only 1.18 per cent of walkable segments are metered. Replacing the street graph by a distance graph collapses it to 0.09, which shows that the gain comes from the conservation law rather than from locality.

</details>


### [5] [What You Can't See Is Still What You Learn: A Preregistered Sixty-Society Confirmation That Evidence Masking Drives Compositional Generalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17637)
*Narcis Marincat*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A preregistered study with 64 modular systems sharing a frozen language-model backbone shows that masking what a module can read substantially improves compositional generalization, though the precise mechanism remains unresolved.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper tests the hypothesis that restricting a module's read access ("what a module can read") can improve what the system learns to compute, addressing whether evidence masking, ownership markers, and filler replacement affect compositional learning.

Method: Preregistered confirmation with sixty four-cell systems sharing a frozen language-model backbone and communicating via learned continuous packets. Five conditions varied evidence masking, ownership markers, and replacement of foreign evidence with neutral filler, across six initialization clusters, each with two data orders, on one fresh task world. Evaluation used held-out two- and three-operation compositions, a full preregistered behavioral criterion, marker-following checks, and packet interventions on audited masked systems.

Result: With markers available in both regimes, masking improved accuracy on held-out two- and three-operation compositions by median paired differences of 0.846 and 0.859; all twelve pairs cleared required margins and the full preregistered behavioral criterion passed; the unmarked replication also passed. No globally visible system passed the marker-following check, leaving the effect of usable role information unresolved. The filler condition yielded seven full generalizers but its decomposition criteria were inconclusive. Packet interventions in all eighteen audited masked systems followed predicted intermediate-value changes on eligible cases, though these success-conditioned audits do not establish mediation.

Conclusion: The results confirm a large advantage of the tested masking regime but leave its finer attribution (e.g., role-information effects) and generality open. Protocols, results, and checkpoints are public.

Abstract: Restricting what a module can read may improve what a system learns to compute. We test this in a preregistered confirmation with sixty four-cell systems sharing a frozen language-model backbone and communicating through learned continuous packets. Five conditions vary evidence masking, ownership markers, and replacement of foreign evidence with neutral filler, across six initialization clusters, each with two data orders, on one fresh task world. With markers available in both regimes, masking improved accuracy on held-out two- and three-operation compositions by median paired differences of 0.846 and 0.859; all twelve pairs cleared the required margins, and the full preregistered behavioral criterion passed. The unmarked replication also passed. No globally visible system passed the marker-following check, so the effect of usable role information remains unresolved. The filler condition yielded seven full generalizers, but its decomposition criteria were inconclusive. Packet interventions in all eighteen audited masked systems followed the predicted intermediate-value changes on eligible cases; these finite, success-conditioned audits do not establish mediation. The results confirm a large advantage of the tested masking regime, while leaving its finer attribution and generality open. Protocols, results, and checkpoints are public.

</details>


### [6] [CapMem: A Benchmark for Caption-Based Episodic Memory in Egocentric Video](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17688)
*Dingli Liang,Yiqiao Xie,Yukai Huang,Zhaokai Wang,Weitong Cai,Guangwen Feng,Jifei Song,Zhensong Zhang,Hang Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CapMem benchmark shows textual captions can serve as reusable episodic memory for long egocentric video QA, outperforming direct VideoQA under bounded frame budgets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Wearable assistants need episodic memory over egocentric video, but current vision-language models are limited by bounded frame budgets, growing visual-token costs, and long-context retrieval failures. This motivates testing whether textual captions can act as reusable episodic memory under these practical constraints.

Method: The authors define the Episodic Memory Video Caption QA task and introduce CapMem, a human-annotated benchmark of 75 videos (33.7 hours) and 1,000 multiple-choice questions across 16 scenarios. They compare full-coverage CaptionQA with 30s and 60s caption windows against direct VideoQA, add a matched-frame control across six Qwen models, and propose a caption-guided retrieve-and-verify harness.

Result: On long videos (>20 min), full-coverage CaptionQA with 30s and 60s windows outperforms direct VideoQA for 10/12 and 8/12 models, respectively. The matched-frame control retains mean accuracy gains of 3.22 and 2.55 points. The caption-guided retrieve-and-verify harness further improves accuracy by up to 5.3 points.

Conclusion: Textual caption memory is effective for episodic reasoning over long egocentric video, supporting its use as reusable memory for wearable assistants under bounded frame and token budgets.

Abstract: Wearable assistants require episodic memory over egocentric video, yet current vision-language models face bounded frame budgets, growing visual-token costs, and long-context retrieval failures. Under these practical constraints, we study whether textual captions can serve as reusable episodic memory. We define the Episodic Memory Video Caption QA task and introduce CapMem, a human-annotated benchmark with 75 videos totaling 33.7 hours, and 1,000 multiple-choice questions across 16 scenarios. On long videos (>20 min), full-coverage CaptionQA with 30s and 60s caption windows outperforms direct VideoQA for 10/12 and 8/12 models, respectively. On the same video subset, a matched-frame control across six Qwen models retains mean accuracy gains of 3.22 and 2.55 points, respectively. Our caption-guided retrieve-and-verify harness further improves accuracy by up to 5.3 points. These results support the effectiveness of caption memory for episodic reasoning over long egocentric video.

</details>


### [7] [GraphEcho: Structural Redundancy and Evidence Provenance in LLM Graph Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17695)
*Sikun Wang,Yixi Zhou,Lei Fan,Fan Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GraphEcho is a benchmark testing whether LLM graph agents mistake repeated encounters with the same evidence for independent corroboration, revealing a gap between efficient exploration and effective evidence use.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents can traverse more graph paths without gaining more independent evidence; the paper investigates whether agents misinterpret these repeated encounters as additional corroboration.

Method: GraphEcho varies path counts and evidential origins while holding evidence content fixed, then evaluates both agent judgments and active exploration. Provenance-aware post-training (PAPT) is introduced and tested on synthetic and scientific claim tasks.

Result: Controlled synthetic experiments reveal model-dependent judgment shifts, and redundant supporting paths increase the share of repeated walks across all evaluated frozen agents. PAPT reduces revisits and improves synthetic accuracy but covers fewer distinct sources; on scientific claims it reduces repetition while accuracy declines.

Conclusion: An agent can learn to stop repeating itself while overlooking needed information, exposing a gap between efficient exploration and effective evidence use. GraphEcho enables controlled evaluation of both agents' conclusions and whether exploration reaches distinct evidential sources.

Abstract: A large language model (LLM) agent can follow more graph paths without acquiring more independent evidence. GraphEcho tests whether agents mistake these repeated encounters for additional corroboration. The benchmark varies path counts and evidential origins while holding evidence content fixed, and evaluates both judgments and active exploration. Controlled synthetic experiments reveal model-dependent judgment shifts, but redundant supporting paths increase the share of repeated walks across all evaluated frozen agents. Provenance-aware post-training (PAPT) reduces revisits and improves synthetic accuracy, yet covers fewer distinct sources. On scientific claims, it continues to reduce repetition while accuracy declines. These findings expose a gap between efficient exploration and effective evidence use: an agent can learn to stop repeating itself while overlooking information it needs. GraphEcho provides a controlled way to evaluate both what graph agents conclude and whether their exploration reaches distinct evidential sources.

</details>


### [8] [GVD: Governed Versioning and Deduplication for Document Repositories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17696)
*Mohammadreza Sediqin,Shivali Dalmia,Sumukha Thoppanahalli,Abhishek Mukherji*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GVD is a fully local, LLM-free framework that unifies cross-document version linking with rule-level conflict resolution under an auditable update policy, achieving F1 of 0.97 for version-family construction and 0.94 for rule-level consistency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Document repositories evolve continuously: guidelines and policies are revised, superseded, and re-uploaded, so content recurs in different wording while newer versions refine or contradict earlier ones. These inconsistencies belong to the growing collection rather than to any single document, yet existing work treats versioning, duplicate detection, and contradiction detection as isolated pairwise tasks and stops once a pair is labeled.

Method: GVD (Governed Versioning and Deduplication) unifies cross-document version linking with rule-level conflict resolution under an auditable update policy. Incoming documents are assigned to version families via bidirectional rule alignment, and their rules are compared against family memory to identify duplicates, contradictions, asymmetric refinements, and new knowledge. Counterfactual Span Probing (CSP) resolves related pairs that inference misclassifies as neutral. Relation-specific policies suppress duplicates and escalate only consequential changes for review, retaining version lineage as an audit trail. The pipeline runs fully locally with no large language model.

Result: On 120 enterprise documents processed as 140 ingestions across 59 version families, GVD reaches an F1 of 0.97 for version-family construction and 0.94 for rule-level consistency, with CSP raising rule consistency from 0.90 to 0.94.

Conclusion: GVD addresses collection-level inconsistencies through unified versioning and deduplication with rule-level conflict resolution, delivering strong empirical performance while operating entirely locally without LLM dependence.

Abstract: Document repositories evolve continuously. Guidelines and policies are revised, superseded, and re-uploaded, so the same content recurs in different wording and newer versions refine or contradict earlier ones. These inconsistencies belong to the growing collection rather than to any single document, yet existing work treats versioning, duplicate detection, and contradiction detection as isolated pairwise tasks and stops once a pair is labeled. We present GVD (Governed Versioning and Deduplication), a framework that unifies cross-document version linking with rule-level conflict resolution under an auditable update policy. Incoming documents are assigned to version families through bidirectional rule alignment, and their rules are compared against the family memory to identify duplicates, contradictions, asymmetric refinements, and new knowledge, with Counterfactual Span Probing (CSP) resolving related pairs that inference misclassifies as neutral. Relation-specific policies suppress duplicates and escalate only consequential changes for review, retaining version lineage as an audit trail. The pipeline runs fully locally, with no large language model. On 120 enterprise documents processed as 140 ingestions across 59 version families, GVD reaches an F1 of 0.97 for version-family construction and 0.94 for rule-level consistency, with CSP raising rule consistency from 0.90 to 0.94.

</details>


### [9] [NeMo Data Designer: An Extensible Framework for Multimodal Synthetic Data Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17699)
*Johnny Greco,Nabin Mulepati,Andre Manoel,Eric Tramel,Kirit Thadaka,Mike Knepper,Dhruv Nathawani,Dane Corneil,Yev Meyer,Alex Watson,Maarten Van Segbroeck*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: NeMo Data Designer (NDD) is an open-source, general-purpose framework for multi-modal synthetic data generation, offering a declarative column-based configuration format, a plugin system, an inspectable/reproducible spec, and a built-in preview-and-revision loop.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Synthetic data generation (SDG) is inherently iterative, and practitioners need an intuitive, reproducible, and multi-modal way to specify and steer dataset generation across varied tasks.

Method: NDD uses a declarative configuration where users define each dataset column; column types span text, code, structured outputs, images, embeddings, and statistical samplers explicitly configured to steer diversity. A flexible plugin system adds column types and functionality. The configuration serves as an inspectable artifact for sharing and reproducibility. Core workflow integrates a preview-and-revision loop (generate/inspect few records, refine spec, rerun at scale). At runtime, it resolves dependencies, schedules calls to user-provided model endpoints, and retries failed requests.

Result: Case studies span structured, agentic, multimodal, and domain-specialized tasks, including datasets used in Nemotron model development and in production enterprise deployments.

Conclusion: NDD provides an open-source, general-purpose, reproducible framework for multi-modal synthetic data generation, with an architecture and programming model validated across diverse real-world and production use cases.

Abstract: We present NeMo Data Designer (NDD), an open-source, general-purpose framework for multi-modal synthetic data generation (SDG). Designed to be intuitive to use, NDD provides a declarative configuration format in which human and/or agent users define each dataset column, with column types spanning text, code, structured outputs, images, embeddings, and statistical samplers that are explicitly configured to steer dataset diversity. Additional column types and functionality can be introduced using the framework's flexible plugin system. NDD's configuration is an inspectable artifact, supporting workflow sharing and reproducibility. SDG is an inherently iterative process. NDD therefore builds a preview-and-revision loop into its core workflow, allowing users to generate and inspect a small number of records, refine the specification, and rerun generation at full scale. At runtime, NDD resolves dependencies, schedules calls to user-provided model endpoints, and retries failed requests. We describe NDD's architecture and programming model and present case studies spanning structured, agentic, multimodal, and domain-specialized tasks, including datasets used in Nemotron model development and in production enterprise deployments.

</details>


### [10] [A Systematic Evaluation of the COTQ Provincial Land Cover Product: Structural Consistency, Spectral Separability, and Relative Positioning Against ESA, ESRI, and Google Products](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17731)
*Étienne Clabaut,Samuel Foucher,Yacine Bouroubi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This study systematically evaluates Quebec's provincial 10-m land-cover product COTQ against three global 10-m LULC datasets (ESA WorldCover, ESRI LandCover, Google DynamicWorld) using structural indicators, spectral separability metrics, and targeted photo-interpretation across eight Sentinel-2 tiles representing Quebec's main bioclimatic domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-resolution LULC products from Sentinel-2 are widely used, but their performance varies in regions with complex ecological gradients and heterogeneous surfaces. In Quebec, these limitations motivated the development of the COTQ, a provincial 10-m product for annual monitoring of land occupation and soil artificialisation, whose behaviour relative to global datasets needs objective characterization.

Method: No new mapping methodology is proposed. All products are harmonized under a common legend and compared via structural indicators (object-size distributions, shape complexity, Adjusted Rand Index, Intersection over Union), spectral separability metrics from Sentinel-2 reflectance data, and photo-interpretation of disagreement areas, over eight Sentinel-2 tiles spanning Quebec's bioclimatic domains.

Result: The COTQ shows structural and spectral characteristics most similar to ESA WorldCover among the reference global products, while exhibiting systematic differences linked to class definitions and thematic priorities, especially for urban areas, wetlands, and rocky or cryptogamic surfaces.

Conclusion: This multi-criteria evaluation provides an objective characterization of the COTQ and clarifies its relative positioning with respect to existing global land-cover datasets for operational land monitoring in Quebec.

Abstract: High-resolution land use and land cover (LULC) products derived from Sentinel-2 imagery are widely used for environmental monitoring and land management, yet their performance can vary across regions with complex ecological gradients and heterogeneous surface conditions. In Quebec, these limitations motivated the development of a provincial 10-m land-cover product, the COTQ, designed to support annual monitoring of land occupation and soil artificialisation. This study presents a systematic evaluation of the COTQ product relative to three global 10-m LULC datasets: ESA WorldCover, ESRI LandCover, and Google DynamicWorld. This paper does not introduce a new mapping methodology but focuses on analysing the behaviour and consistency of the COTQ using complementary evaluation approaches. All products are harmonized under a common legend and compared using structural indicators (object-size distributions, shape complexity, Adjusted Rand Index, and Intersection over Union), spectral separability metrics derived from Sentinel-2 reflectance data, and a targeted photo-interpretation of disagreement areas. The analysis is conducted over eight Sentinel-2 tiles selected to represent the main bioclimatic domains of Québec, from temperate and boreal forests to northern tundra environments. The results show that the COTQ exhibits structural and spectral characteristics most similar to ESA WorldCover among the reference global products, while revealing systematic differences linked to class definitions and thematic priorities, particularly for urban areas, wetlands, and rocky or cryptogamic surfaces. This multi-criteria evaluation provides an objective characterization of the COTQ product and clarifies its relative positioning with respect to existing global land-cover datasets for operational land monitoring in Québec.

</details>


### [11] [Imitation Learning for Autonomous Driving in CARLA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17757)
*Jordy Kieto*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A compact 1.36M-parameter multimodal behavioral cloning policy trained on 236,882 offline windows (~3.3 h of driving) drives autonomously for hours in CARLA, collision-free on training and held-out routes, with qualitative transfer to an unseen town.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Behavioral cloning is trained offline on expert demonstrations, yet deployment is closed loop, where each action alters subsequent observations. The paper asks how much closed-loop driving competence a compact multimodal policy can actually acquire from offline demonstrations in CARLA.

Method: The policy ingests five-frame histories of RGB images, LiDAR, vehicle telemetry, and lane waypoints, and predicts throttle, brake, and steering at 20 Hz. Demonstrations were gathered in three stages, culminating in a systematic route-generation procedure that enumerates spawn points and feasible maneuvers and verifies completed autopilot routes. Training used 236,882 windows (~3.3 h of driving) from 448 captures.

Result: The released 1.36M-parameter policy drives autonomously for hours on both training and held-out routes, with no collisions in the authors' runs, and transfers qualitatively to an unseen CARLA town with different road geometry. Recovery from large trajectory deviations was observed, though not claimed as systematic without controlled evaluation. Offline metrics are reported, with measured results distinguished from qualitative closed-loop observations. Code, checkpoint, ONNX model, data sample, and an evidence audit are released.

Conclusion: A small multimodal behavioral cloning policy can attain substantial closed-loop driving competence in CARLA from purely offline demonstrations, with the authors carefully separating measured from qualitative evidence and releasing artifacts for auditing.

Abstract: Behavioral cloning trains a policy offline on expert demonstrations, but deployment is closed loop: each action affects the observations the policy receives next. We study how much closed-loop driving competence a compact multimodal policy can acquire from offline demonstrations in the CARLA simulator. The policy uses five-frame histories of RGB images, LiDAR, vehicle telemetry, and lane waypoints to predict throttle, brake, and steering at 20 Hz. Demonstrations were collected in three stages, ending with a systematic route-generation procedure that enumerates spawn points and feasible maneuvers and verifies completed autopilot routes. The released 1.36 million parameter policy was trained on 236,882 windows, representing about 3.3 hours of driving from 448 captures. The resulting policy drives autonomously for hours on training and held-out routes. In our runs, it did so without collisions and also transferred qualitatively to an unseen CARLA town with different road geometry. We also observed recovery from large trajectory deviations, although we do not claim systematic recovery without controlled evaluation. We report offline metrics and distinguish measured results from qualitative closed-loop observations. We release the code, trained checkpoint, ONNX model, data sample, and an evidence audit for the reported claims.

</details>


### [12] [SAGE: Governed Artifact Generation from Enterprise Guidelines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17775)
*Mohammadreza Sediqin,Shivali Dalmia,Sumukha Thoppanahalli,Srinivasa Karthikeya Reddy Kovvuri,Abhishek Mukherji*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SAGE is a governed multi-stage LLM pipeline that converts enterprise guideline documents into reviewable structured artifacts via a shared versioned rule store, schema-validated inter-stage contracts, and end-to-end provenance tracking, cutting turnaround from days to 20-100 minutes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enterprise guideline documents mix narrative text, complex tables, and embedded images, and converting them into structured work artifacts still requires two to three days of manual effort each. Existing language and vision-language models can extract from such documents but offer no governed workflow beyond extraction—no validation, consistency checking, or traceable artifact generation.

Method: SAGE is a governed multi-stage LLM pipeline organized around a shared versioned rule store with stable identifiers, schema-validated inter-stage contracts, and end-to-end provenance tracking. Extracted rules pass deterministic structural validation and LLM-based semantic scoring, followed by a consistency module that removes duplicates, flags contradictions, and surfaces specification gaps. Only uncertain or flagged items reach reviewers, while high-confidence outputs are auto-approved.

Result: On 120 documents, SAGE reduces turnaround from days to 20-100 minutes, achieving a 96% document-level success rate with 3.2% hallucination, extracting 3,896 rules and producing 812 artifacts ready for human review. Without governance, hallucination rises to 15.7%.

Conclusion: A governed, multi-stage LLM pipeline with shared rule storage, validated contracts, and provenance tracking substantially improves document-to-artifact conversion efficiency and reliability, while governance mechanisms are critical for suppressing hallucination.

Abstract: Enterprise guideline documents mix narrative text, complex tables, and embedded images, and converting them into structured work artifacts still takes two to three days of manual effort each. Current language and vision-language models extract from such documents but offer no governed workflow beyond extraction: no validation, no consistency checking, no traceable artifact generation. We introduce SAGE, a governed multi-stage LLM pipeline organized around a shared versioned rule store with stable identifiers, schema-validated inter-stage contracts, and end-to-end provenance tracking. Extracted rules undergo deterministic structural validation and LLM-based semantic scoring, then a consistency module that removes duplicates, flags contradictions, and surfaces specification gaps; only uncertain or flagged items reach reviewers, while high-confidence outputs are auto-approved. On 120 documents, SAGE cuts turnaround from days to 20-100 minutes, achieving a 96% document-level success rate with 3.2% hallucination, extracting 3,896 rules and producing 812 artifacts ready for human review; without governance, hallucination rises to 15.7%.

</details>


### [13] [FairCompressAgent: An Agentic Framework for Fairness-Aware Model Compression for FPGA Deployment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17786)
*Yuanbo Guo,Yiyu Shi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FairCompressAgent (FCA) is an agentic framework that unifies fairness-aware pruning, incremental quantization, and sparse low-rank factorization under a common operator interface, using a language-model planner plus measured feedback to select compression configurations that balance accuracy, fairness, and deployment cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fairness-aware model compression requires choosing methods and configurations that jointly balance accuracy, fairness, and deployment cost. These decisions are complicated further when compression methods are composed and when user requirements change, making static or one-shot selection insufficient.

Method: FCA integrates fairness-aware pruning, incremental quantization, and sparse low-rank factorization through a shared operator interface. A language-model planner uses model profiles and measured outcomes to select compression configurations, while an execution layer handles compression, fine-tuning, evaluation, and constraint-based selection. It also supports requirement updates and reports remaining constraint violation when a request is infeasible.

Result: On Fitzpatrick-17k with VGG-11, four search methods are compared over 40 measured configurations. Under an accuracy-constrained request, FCA selects a compressed model with 59.54% less inference tensor storage, validation average precision rising from 0.5141 to 0.5233, and equalized opportunity (EOpp) decreasing from 0.2251 to 0.2168. It attains the same final selection as one-shot planning using 7.33 versus 12 candidate evaluations on average under respective stopping policies. Repeated fine-tuning, held-out testing, and online requirement updates characterize stability and interactive use.

Conclusion: Measured feedback combined with explicit constraints supports the selection and interactive refinement of fairness-aware compression configurations, enabling requirement-responsive compression that balances storage, accuracy, and fairness.

Abstract: Fairness-aware model compression requires selecting methods and configurations that balance accuracy, fairness, and deployment cost. These decisions become more difficult when compression methods are composed or the user's requirements change. In this paper, we propose FairCompressAgent (FCA), an agentic framework that integrates fairness-aware pruning, incremental quantization, and sparse low-rank factorization through a common operator interface. A language-model planner uses model profiles and measured outcomes to select compression configurations, while an execution layer performs compression, fine-tuning, evaluation, and constraint-based selection. FCA also supports requirement updates and reports the remaining violation when a request cannot be satisfied. Experiments on Fitzpatrick-17k with VGG-11 compare four search methods over 40 measured configurations. Under the accuracy-constrained request, FCA selects a compressed model with 59.54% less inference tensor storage, while validation average precision increases from 0.5141 to 0.5233 and equalized opportunity (EOpp) decreases from 0.2251 to 0.2168. It reaches the same final selection as one-shot planning with 7.33 versus 12 candidate evaluations on average, under their respective stopping policies. Repeated fine-tuning, held-out testing, and online requirement updates characterize the stability and interactive use of this compression workflow. The results demonstrate how measured feedback and explicit constraints support the selection and interactive refinement of fairness-aware compression configurations.

</details>


### [14] [A Four-Stage Decomposition of Word-Problem Solving and Mechanistic Fragility in LLM Math Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17804)
*Zhongdi Qu,Carla P. Gomes*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper offers a mechanistic account of how LLMs solve grade-school math word problems via a four-stage pipeline and localizes distractor-induced failure to a single stage, Operation Planning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs solve grade-school math word problems with high accuracy, yet a single irrelevant clause can collapse performance. The paper aims to reconcile these seemingly contradictory observations through a mechanistic explanation.

Method: The authors decompose the model's internal computation into a four-stage sequential pipeline: Schema Abstraction, Operation Planning, Operand Binding, and Computation, each producing a distinct intermediate representation in an identifiable band of layers. They then use this scaffold to diagnose distractor-induced failure and validate causal roles bidirectionally.

Result: The pipeline stages are localized to identifiable layer bands, and distractor-induced corruption is localized to a single stage, Operation Planning, implemented by a set of attention heads whose causal role is validated bidirectionally.

Conclusion: The work provides a mechanistic interpretation of math word problem reasoning in LLMs and of their failure under distraction, attributing the breakdown to Operation Planning.

Abstract: Large language models solve grade-school math word problems with high accuracy, yet a single irrelevant clause inserted into the problem can collapse it. We reconcile these observations with a mechanistic account. We show that the model's internal computation decomposes into a four-stage sequential pipeline, Schema Abstraction, Operation Planning, Operand Binding, and Computation, each stage producing a distinct intermediate representation in an identifiable band of layers. Using the same scaffold to diagnose distractor-induced failure, we localize the corruption to a single stage, Operation Planning, implemented by a set of attention heads whose causal role we validate bidirectionally. In short, we provide a mechanistic interpretation of math word problem reasoning in LLMs, and their failure when distracted.

</details>


### [15] [Learning Heterogeneous Preferences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17847)
*Shiwali Mohan,Matt Hong,Dule Shu,Aniek Fransen,Shabnam Hakimi,Matt Klenk*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces individuated utility functions from rational choice theory to model subjective preferences, showing they substantially outperform universal utility models on a 575K-judgment aesthetic dataset of car wheel designs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reward modeling for AI alignment assumes a universal utility function shared across a population, treating annotator disagreement as stochastic noise. This assumption works for objective tasks but fails in subjective domains, where preferences vary systematically across individuals and thus disagreement is meaningful signal rather than noise.

Method: Grounded in rational choice theory (RCT), the authors define individuated utility functions conditioned jointly on the individual and their decision context, and propose a novel multi-stage architecture to estimate these utilities from multi-modal data.

Result: On a newly collected dataset of over 575,000 pairwise aesthetic judgments from 2,398 participants comparing automotive wheel designs, individuated utility models substantially outperform universal utility models, including foundation model baselines.

Conclusion: Disagreement reflects meaningful preference heterogeneity rather than annotation noise. The findings underscore the value of collecting annotator attributes and learning individuated utility functions, yielding reward models that explicitly represent whose preferences they encode and faithfully capture human decision diversity.

Abstract: Learning from human feedback has become a central paradigm for training modern AI systems, where models of human utility are used as reward models in policy learning. Existing methods typically assume a \emph{universal utility} function shared across a population and treat disagreement between annotators as stochastic variation. While suitable for objective tasks, this assumption breaks down in subjective domains where preferences vary systematically across individuals. We study the problem of subjective preference learning, in which observed choices arise from heterogeneous but internally consistent utility functions. Drawing upon rational choice theory, RCT \parencite{tversky1981framing}, we introduce \emph{individuated utility} functions conditioned on both the individual and their decision context, and propose a novel multi-stage architecture for estimating them from multi-modal data. We evaluate our framework on a newly collected dataset of more than $575{,}000$ pairwise aesthetic judgments from $2{,}398$ participants comparing automotive wheel designs. Our experiments show that individuated utility models substantially outperform universal utility models including foundation model baselines. Our results demonstrate that disagreement reflects meaningful preference heterogeneity rather than annotation noise. More broadly, our findings highlight the importance of collecting annotator attributes and learning individuated utility functions, enabling reward models that explicitly account for whose preferences they represent and faithfully capture human decision diversity.

</details>


### [16] [SNOMED CT Concept Recommendation from Masked Clinical Context](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17855)
*Ali Noori*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a masked-concept recommendation benchmark on SNOMED CT Entity Linking Challenge v1.2.1 data (MIMIC-IV-Note), comparing multiple retrieval methods to recommend SNOMED CT concepts from local clinical context, and finds that sparse TF-IDF outperforms retrieval augmentation, with performance heavily dependent on concept frequency and terminology coverage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standardizing clinical language to SNOMED CT is important for interoperability, analytics, and reusable phenotyping, but concept recommendation is difficult when relevant concepts are rare or absent from training data. A reproducible benchmark is needed to evaluate methods under such low-resource conditions.

Method: The authors construct a masked-concept recommendation benchmark from the SNOMED CT Entity Linking Challenge v1.2.1 data derived from MIMIC-IV-Note (75,491 annotations, 272 discharge summaries; 204 for training, 68 for testing). For each note-concept pair, the target mention is masked from local clinical context, and systems rank SNOMED CT concepts seen during training. They compare a popularity baseline, sparse TF-IDF concept prototypes, dense LSA embeddings, sparse-dense fusion, retrieved-note evidence, and a retrieval-augmented hybrid.

Result: Sparse TF-IDF achieves the best performance: Recall@1 of 14.81%, Recall@10 of 33.43%, MRR of 0.2114, and nDCG@10 of 0.2297. Retrieval augmentation does not help (Recall@10 of 31.99%, MRR of 0.1937). Performance depends strongly on concept frequency: Recall@10 is 7.74% for concepts appearing in one or two training notes versus 43.90% for concepts appearing more than ten times. Additionally, 9.66% of test note-concept pairs contain concepts unseen during training.

Conclusion: Local lexical context and terminology coverage are major determinants of recommendation quality in low-resource settings. The benchmark provides a reproducible baseline for future ontology-grounded and biomedical-encoder retrieval systems.

Abstract: Standardizing clinical language to SNOMED CT supports interoperability, analytics, and reusable phenotyping, but concept recommendation remains difficult when relevant concepts are rare or absent from training data. We present a masked-concept recommendation benchmark using the SNOMED CT Entity Linking Challenge v1.2.1 data derived from MIMIC-IV-Note. The dataset contains 75,491 annotations across 272 discharge summaries, with 204 notes used for training and 68 for historical testing. For each unique note-concept pair, the target mention is masked from a local clinical context and the system ranks SNOMED CT concepts observed during training. We compare a popularity baseline, sparse TF-IDF concept prototypes, dense latent semantic analysis embeddings, sparse-dense fusion, retrieved-note evidence, and a retrieval-augmented hybrid. Sparse TF-IDF performs best, achieving Recall@1 of 14.81%, Recall@10 of 33.43%, MRR of 0.2114, and nDCG@10 of 0.2297. Retrieval augmentation does not improve this baseline, with Recall@10 of 31.99% and MRR of 0.1937. Performance is strongly affected by concept frequency: Recall@10 is 7.74% for concepts appearing in only one or two training notes versus 43.90% for concepts appearing in more than ten. In addition, 9.66% of test note-concept pairs contain concepts unseen during training. These findings show that local lexical context and terminology coverage are major determinants of recommendation quality in low-resource settings and provide a reproducible baseline for future ontology-grounded and biomedical-encoder retrieval systems.

</details>


### [17] [The Inference Engineering Pareto Atlas: Which Optimizations Dominate the Cost, Quality, and Latency Frontier?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17863)
*Srikanta Datta Tumkur,Jay Iyer,Mehar Simhadri,Sai Pavan Kumar,Sai Kapil Kumar,Ramesh Nampelly*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper builds a cost-quality-latency Pareto atlas by measuring 54 configurations of Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct on vLLM across L4, A100, and H100 GPUs, calibrating a simulator to reduce exhaustive testing. It shows that combined optimizations reach the Pareto frontier more often than single methods, that quality evaluation reshuffles winners, and that the best configuration depends jointly on constraint, method, and GPU.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM inference optimizations are typically reported under heterogeneous conditions—different models, GPUs, prompts, and quality metrics—making their speedups hard to compare or combine, and leaving practitioners without guidance on which configuration is best under a given deployment constraint.

Method: The authors measure 54 configurations of Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct on vLLM 0.12 across L4, A100, and H100 GPUs, using these anchors to calibrate a simulator, and construct a cost-quality-latency Pareto atlas. Quality is evaluated on 200 GSM8K questions with five examples per prompt across FP16, AWQ 4-bit, FP8 weights, and FP8 KV cache; sparse attention is analyzed only in simulation.

Result: The simulator reproduces anchored measurements with cross-campaign drift below 1.5 percent. On the calibrated grid, 18 of 36 configurations reach the Pareto frontier, with combinations succeeding more often (9 of 15) than single methods (9 of 21). Quality testing changes the winners: AWQ 4-bit cuts per-token latency to 0.34x baseline on L4 but loses 5.9 percent strict GSM8K accuracy, narrowly missing the 95 percent quality floor, though flexible answer extraction recovers FP16-level accuracy, indicating a formatting rather than arithmetic loss. FP8 weights retain 99.4 percent accuracy at 0.61–0.65x baseline latency across all three GPUs and appear in three of four regime winners. A naive FP8 KV cache preserves throughput but answers none of the 200 questions correctly. Under two prompt designs, n-gram speculative decoding measures 0.90–0.98x baseline and adds no benefit. H100 wins for tight latency, while A100 wins for throughput and cost at 0.106 dollars per million tokens.

Conclusion: Speedup alone is insufficient for selecting inference optimizations; quality-aware, constraint-specific evaluation is required. The best choice depends jointly on the deployment constraint and the GPU, and combined methods are more likely to be Pareto-optimal than individual ones.

Abstract: LLM inference optimizations report speedups on different models, GPUs, prompts, and quality metrics, making them hard to compare or combine. We build a cost, quality, and latency Pareto atlas to identify the best configurations for different deployment constraints. Since exhaustive testing is impractical, we measure 54 configurations of Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct running on vLLM 0.12 across L4, A100, and H100 GPUs and use these anchors to calibrate a simulator. It reproduces measurements at anchored batch sizes, with cross campaign drift below 1.5 percent. A separate quality evaluation tests FP16, AWQ 4bit, FP8 weights, and FP8 KV cache on 200 GSM8K questions with five examples per prompt. Sparse attention is evaluated only in simulation. On the calibrated grid, 18 of 36 configurations reach the Pareto frontier. Combined methods reach it more often than individual methods, with 9 of 15 combinations versus 9 of 21 single methods. Quality testing changes the winners. AWQ 4bit reduces per token latency to 0.34 times baseline on L4 but loses 5.9 percent of strict GSM8K accuracy, narrowly missing the 95 percent quality floor within sampling uncertainty. Flexible answer extraction matches FP16 accuracy, suggesting the loss comes from formatting rather than arithmetic. FP8 weights retain 99.4 percent of baseline accuracy at 0.61 to 0.65 times baseline latency across all three GPUs and appear in three of four regime winners. A naive FP8 KV cache maintains normal throughput but answers none of the 200 questions correctly, showing why speed alone is insufficient. Under two prompt designs, n gram speculative decoding measures at 0.90 to 0.98 times baseline and adds no benefit on this stack. The best choice depends on the constraint and GPU: H100 wins for tight latency, while A100 wins for throughput and low cost at 0.106 dollars per million tokens.

</details>


### [18] [Do Frontier Models Seek Safety Evidence Before Acting?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17865)
*Omer Tafveez*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces SAFE, a benchmark probing the earlier decision point of whether models choose to acquire safety-relevant evidence before acting, revealing distinct evidence-acquisition policies across frontier models and a systematic mismatch between their stated reasoning and actual behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior evaluation of frontier models focuses on how they respond to safety information already present in context, ignoring the upstream decision of whether models actively acquire such evidence. This leaves a blind spot: deployment-time safety may depend on evidence acquisition, not just on responses to known risks.

Method: SAFE is a controlled benchmark where models make deployment decisions with optional evidence varying in retrieval cost, probability, severity, and presentation. The authors evaluate GPT-5.5, o3, Claude Opus 4.8, and Claude Sonnet 4.6, analyze Stage 1 rationales, perform a cost-obligation decomposition, and run counterfactual interventions to compare behavior with explanation.

Result: Distinct acquisition policies emerge: Opus inspects nearly by default, o3 is most skip-heavy and threshold-sensitive, and GPT-5.5 and Sonnet are intermediate. Inspection rises strongly with severity and falls with retrieval cost, while probability has weak influence (a 10% to 70% likelihood shift changes inspection by at most 21 percentage points). Rationales are dominated by expected-value reasoning. Avoidance is driven mainly by retrieval friction and explicit threats to deployment payoff rather than remediation duties from knowing. Framing strongly changes decisions near the inspection boundary yet is largely unmentioned, whereas probability is frequently cited despite little causal influence.

Conclusion: Deployment-time safety depends not only on how models respond to known risks but also on whether they acquire the evidence needed to know that acting is safe. Behavioral policies and verbal explanations can diverge substantially.

Abstract: Frontier models are often evaluated on how they respond to safety information once it is already in context. We study an earlier decision point: whether models choose to acquire safety-relevant evidence before acting. We introduce SAFE, a controlled benchmark in which models make deployment decisions with optional evidence that varies in retrieval cost, probability, severity, and presentation. Across GPT-5.5, o3, Claude Opus 4.8, and Claude Sonnet 4.6, we find distinct evidence-acquisition policies: Opus inspects nearly by default, o3 is the most skip-heavy and threshold-sensitive, and GPT-5.5 and Sonnet occupy intermediate regimes. Inspection increases strongly with severity and decreases with retrieval cost, whereas probability has much weaker behavioral influence: increasing the stated likelihood of a problem from 10% to 70% changes inspection by at most 21 percentage points. Despite these differences, Stage 1 rationales are dominated by expected-value reasoning across models. A cost-obligation decomposition further shows that avoidance is driven primarily by retrieval friction and explicit threats to the deployment payoff rather than by the remediation duties created by knowing. Counterfactual interventions reveal a further mismatch between behavior and explanation: evidence framing can strongly change decisions near the inspection boundary while going largely unmentioned, whereas probability is frequently cited despite having little causal influence. These results suggest that deployment-time safety depends not only on how models respond to known risks, but also on whether they acquire the evidence needed to know that acting is safe.

</details>


### [19] [ERPBench: A State-Grounded Evaluation Paradigm for Computer-Use Agents in Enterprise Software](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17885)
*Kratika Bhagtani,Kusha Sridhar,Maziyar Baran Pouyan,Yuying Zhao,Eugene Siow*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ERPBench is a new benchmark that evaluates screenshot-only computer-use agents on a live, reproducible ERP system, scoring tasks against database ground truth rather than on-screen outcomes. It reveals that strong general GUI performance does not transfer to enterprise reliability, with agents saving records in up to 85% of runs but writing correct values in as few as 3%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Computer-use agents are advancing rapidly but their evaluation is anchored to general desktop and web tasks, while ERP systems present distinct challenges: dense interfaces, coordinated multi-step interactions, and errors that alter persistent business records rather than surfacing on screen. Existing enterprise benchmarks rely on proprietary platforms or simulated approximations, lacking a live, reproducible, ground-truth-based evaluation.

Method: The authors introduce ERPBench, a benchmark evaluating screenshot-only agents on a live and reproducible ERP system, scoring each task against ground-truth values in its database. They also present a production-grade harness that gates agent actions behind human approval for safe deployment, which ERPBench runs autonomously. They evaluate six closed and open-source agents and characterize enterprise-specific failure modes.

Result: Strong general GUI performance does not transfer to enterprise reliability. Even when an agent reaches the right form and saves it, the stored record is often wrong: some agents save in up to 85% of runs but write the correct value in as few as 3%. The authors further characterize failure modes specific to enterprise workflows.

Conclusion: ERPBench exposes a critical gap between general GUI competence and enterprise reliability, showing that surface-level task success (reaching and saving a form) diverges sharply from database-level correctness. The benchmark and harness provide a live, reproducible, ground-truth-based foundation for safely evaluating and deploying screenshot-only agents in ERP settings.

Abstract: Computer-use agents that operate through screenshots and simulated actions are advancing rapidly, yet their evaluation remains anchored to general desktop and web tasks. Enterprise Resource Planning (ERP) systems run the finance, procurement, inventory, and customer operations of organizations worldwide, and pose distinct challenges for computer-use agents: dense interfaces, coordinated multi-step interactions, and errors that alter persistent business records rather than surfacing on screen. Existing enterprise benchmarks rely on proprietary platforms or on simulated approximations of such software. We introduce ERPBench, a benchmark that evaluates screenshot-only agents on a live and reproducible ERP system and scores each task against ground-truth values in its database. Beyond the benchmark, we present a production-grade harness that gates agent actions behind human approval for safe deployment, which ERPBench runs autonomously. Evaluating six closed and open-source agents, we demonstrate that strong general GUI performance does not transfer to enterprise reliability. Even when an agent reaches the right form and saves it, the stored record is often wrong: some agents save in up to 85% of runs but write the correct value in as few as 3%. We further characterize failure modes specific to enterprise workflows.

</details>


### [20] [OBC-Prune: Outcome-Based Calibration for Large Reasoning Model Pruning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17890)
*Ha Lan Nguyen,Huy Hoang Tran,Trac-Duy Tran,Dung D. Le*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OBC-Prune improves one-shot pruning of large reasoning models by calibrating on the causal importance of reasoning sentences (distinguishing correct from incorrect rollout tokens) rather than uniformly weighting all rollout tokens.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LRMs produce long chain-of-thought traces, creating large inference overhead. Existing pruning methods that calibrate on the model's own rollouts treat all reasoning tokens uniformly, protecting weights by statistical salience rather than by their contribution to correct reasoning. Consequently, weights behind erroneous computation survive as readily as correct ones, degrading accuracy and lengthening reasoning traces.

Method: OBC-Prune (Outcome-Based Calibration for LRM Pruning): (1) constructs difficulty-matched pairs of correct and incorrect rollouts from problems the model answers inconsistently; (2) estimates each reasoning sentence's causal importance via intervention-based analysis, measuring how removing its influence affects subsequent predictions; (3) converts these causal importance scores into per-token weights that rescale calibration activations for one-shot pruning methods (SparseGPT, Wanda, ALPS) without modifying the pruning algorithms.

Result: Evaluated on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen 1.5B, 7B, and 14B at 40% and 50% sparsity, OBC-Prune consistently outperforms state-of-the-art calibration baselines across most model sizes and sparsity levels on MATH500, LiveCodeBench, and AIME 2025.

Conclusion: Preserving causally important reasoning circuits is a substantially more effective pruning objective than uniformly preserving observed activations.

Abstract: Large reasoning models (LRMs) generate long chain-of-thought traces before answering, creating significant inference overhead. Pruning can reduce this cost, but its effectiveness depends on the calibration data used to estimate parameter importance. Recent work calibrates on the model's own rollouts instead of generic dataset, but treats all reasoning tokens uniformly, regardless of whether they contribute to successful reasoning. As a result, pruning protects weights by statistical salience rather than by their contribution to correct reasoning, so weights behind erroneous computation survive as readily as those behind correct computation. These erroneous patterns then get carried into the pruned model, degrading reasoning quality, producing both lower accuracy and longer reasoning traces. We propose Outcome-Based Calibration for Large Reasoning Model Pruning (OBC-Prune) to close this gap. OBC first constructs difficulty-matched pairs of correct and incorrect rollouts from problems the model answers inconsistently. It then estimates the causal importance of each reasoning sentence through intervention-based analysis, quantifying how removing its influence affects subsequent predictions. These causal importance scores are converted into per-token weights that rescale the calibration activations used by one-shot pruning methods (SparseGPT, Wanda, ALPS), without modifying the underlying pruning algorithms. Experiments on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen 1.5B, 7B, and 14B models at 40\% and 50\% sparsity demonstrate consistent improvements over state-of-the-art calibration baselines across most model sizes and sparsity levels on MATH500, LiveCodeBench, and AIME 2025. These results indicate that preserving causally important reasoning circuits is a substantially more effective pruning objective than uniformly preserving observed activations.

</details>


### [21] [Collaborative Memory for Multi-Agent VLM Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17921)
*Huixin Zhang,Shao-Jun Xia,Di Wang,Liangxi Liu,Hainan Xiong,Zihao Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper argues that shared visual context is the central challenge in VLM multi-agent collaboration, and proposes framing memory hierarchy, cross-agent sharing, and consistency mechanisms around the need to reconcile interpretations and update dependent reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLM agents combine perception, tools, and reasoning, and in multi-agent settings they inspect different image regions, frames, or representations. Collaboration therefore extends beyond distributed reasoning to distributed perception, making shared visual context a core problem.

Method: The authors propose a conceptual framework that organizes VLM agent collaboration around three design considerations: memory hierarchy, cross-agent sharing, and consistency mechanisms. Shared visual memory is designed to preserve not only images or textual summaries but also the dependencies among observations, agent interpretations, and subsequent reasoning.

Result: The framework characterizes how agents build on contributions from other agents, recover missing visual context, and reconcile differing interpretations as new evidence emerges, thereby shaping how information flows and evolves across VLM agents.

Conclusion: The proposed framework provides a foundation for building reliable and resource-efficient VLM agent teams.

Abstract: Vision-language model (VLM) agents combine specialized perception, tools, and reasoning to address complex visual tasks. In multi-agent settings, different agents inspect different image regions, video frames, or visual representations, so collaboration extends beyond distributed reasoning to distributed perception. This makes shared visual context a central problem in VLM agent collaboration. In this paper, we frame memory hierarchy, cross-agent sharing, and consistency mechanisms around the need to reconcile interpretations and update dependent reasoning. Effective collaboration requires agents to build on contributions from other agents, recover missing visual context, and reconcile differing interpretations as new evidence emerges. Shared visual memory preserves not only images or textual summaries but also the dependencies among observations, agent interpretations, and subsequent reasoning. Together, these design considerations shape how information flows and evolves across VLM agents. The proposed framework provides a foundation for building reliable and resource-efficient agent teams.

</details>


### [22] [Measuring AI Leadership: Development and Validation of a Multidimensional Measure for AI-Native Organizations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17965)
*Mustafa Akben,Leslie Coyne*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The authors develop and validate the AI Leadership Battery, a 36-subdimension, 11-family behavioral measure of leadership in AI-enabled work, demonstrating content, structural, reliability, and validity evidence plus incremental predictive value.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI is reshaping what leaders must judge, explain, learn, and coordinate, but existing instruments fail to measure these behaviors at the granularity required to study leadership in AI-enabled work.

Method: The AI Leadership Battery was built via established scale-development procedures: deductive item generation; content validation (definitional correspondence and distinctiveness); exploratory factor analysis and item reduction; confirmatory factor analysis in independent samples; and tests of internal consistency reliability, convergent validity, discriminant validity, and criterion-related and incremental validity.

Result: Analyses supported the Battery's content validity, multidimensional structure, and reliability, and showed distinction from selected orbiting constructs. The Battery explained additional variance beyond orbiting constructs across organizational growth, decision speed, customer/stakeholder response capability, AI-enabled team performance, AI-enabled work experience, AI security and risk management, and AI adoption and integration.

Conclusion: The AI Leadership Battery offers researchers a behavioral framework for examining how leaders in AI-enabled work regulate judgment, learning, adaptation, transparency, and accountability.

Abstract: AI is changing what leaders must judge, explain, learn, and coordinate, yet existing measures do not capture these behaviors at the level needed to study leadership in AI-enabled work. We develop the AI Leadership Battery, which organizes 36 behaviorally specific subdimensions into 11 theory-specified content families. Following established scale-development procedures, the research used deductive item generation; content validation of definitional correspondence and definitional distinctiveness; exploratory factor analysis and item reduction; confirmatory factor analysis in independent samples; and tests of internal consistency reliability, convergent validity, discriminant validity, and criterion-related and incremental validity. Across the development and validation studies, the analyses provided evidence for the Battery's content, multidimensional structure, reliability, and distinction from selected orbiting constructs. The Battery also contributed additional information beyond orbiting constructs across organizational growth, decision speed, customer/stakeholder response capability, AI-enabled team performance, AI-enabled work experience, AI security and risk management, and AI adoption and integration. The resulting measure provides researchers with a behavioral framework for examining how leaders in AI-enabled work regulate judgment, learning, adaptation, transparency, and accountability.

</details>


### [23] [Memory Has Geometry: Non-Uniform Geometric Memory for Long-Horizon Personalized AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17969)
*Jiahong Liu,Wenhao Yu,Zexuan Qiu,Menglin Yang,Irwin King*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This position paper argues that long-horizon personalization in data mining should model memory as a user-specific dynamical state space with locally heterogeneous geometry, replacing the dominant "searchable record set" abstraction with trajectory-conditioned reconstruction of user state.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most personalized AI systems represent personalization as discrete records in a largely static latent space, accessed under a single global similarity notion. This creates a mismatch for data mining: the underlying evidence is a temporal event stream, while the dominant abstraction is a searchable record set that ignores drift, heterogeneous neighborhoods, and uncertainty about the current user state.

Method: The paper proposes a conceptual reframing rather than a concrete algorithm. Memory is cast as a user-specific dynamical state space with locally heterogeneous geometry, used as a computational language (not a literal cognitive claim). This geometry captures stable versus volatile regions, variable-rate drift, heterogeneous neighborhoods, and uncertainty about the current user state. Profiles and isolated events serve as points, while interaction, feedback, and elapsed time induce trajectories. Memory access is reformulated as trajectory-conditioned reconstruction of the relevant user state rather than nearest-neighbor lookup.

Result: As a position/argument paper, no empirical results are reported; the contribution is the reframing of long-horizon personalization as trajectory-conditioned state reconstruction over a heterogeneous dynamical state space.

Conclusion: Long-horizon personalization should move beyond static record-set retrieval and be modeled as memory in a user-specific dynamical state space, where access is trajectory-conditioned reconstruction of the relevant user state rather than global nearest-neighbor search.

Abstract: Long-term memory is becoming a core substrate for personalized AI, yet most systems still represent personalization as discrete records in a largely static latent space, accessed under one global similarity notion. For data mining, this creates a mismatch: the evidence is a temporal event stream, while the dominant abstraction is a searchable record set. We argue that long-horizon personalization should instead model memory as a user-specific dynamical state space with locally heterogeneous geometry. Geometry here is a computational language, not a literal claim about cognition: it captures stable versus volatile regions, variable-rate drift, heterogeneous neighborhoods, and uncertainty about current user state. Profiles and isolated events remain useful as points, but interaction, feedback, and elapsed time induce trajectories. Memory access then becomes trajectory-conditioned reconstruction of the relevant user state, not only nearest-neighbor lookup.

</details>


### [24] [When to Call an LLM: A Confidence-Gated Hybrid for Cost-Effective Emotion Recognition in Conversational AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17977)
*Sai Babu Udayagiri,Arjun Chouhan,Ravisekhar Kanagala,Trishala Pavagada*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A systems-level comparison of three deployment options for dialogue-contextual emotion recognition in conversation (ERC): a low-cost stacked ensemble, off-the-shelf LLM prompting, and a confidence-gated hybrid that escalates only low-confidence ensemble predictions to an LLM.
