Evaluated on IEMOCAP, MELD, and CMU-MOSI, the hybrid Pareto-dominates both pure systems on all datasets while routing most traffic through the cheap ensemble.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ERC underpins agent-assist, escalation routing, and post-call analytics in CCaaS platforms, where cost and latency matter as much as accuracy. No pure system is a safe default: the ensemble wins on IEMOCAP but LLMs win on MELD and CMU-MOSI, so practitioners need a deployment recipe that balances accuracy against operational cost.

Method: Three deployment options are compared: (1) a low-cost stacked ensemble combining sentence embeddings, windowed context, and RandomForest/XGBoost/logistic-regression stacking; (2) off-the-shelf LLM prompting (GPT-4o-mini with zero-shot, few-shot, and chain-of-thought); and (3) a confidence-gated hybrid that escalates only the ensemble's least-confident predictions to the LLM, mirroring IVA-to-human-agent escalation policies in production contact centers.

Result: On IEMOCAP the ensemble significantly outperforms every LLM configuration (0.595 vs. 0.460-0.536 weighted F1, p < 0.0001) at a fraction of the cost with sub-10ms latency, but the ranking reverses on MELD and CMU-MOSI. The confidence-gated hybrid Pareto-dominates both pure systems on all three datasets (0.620, 0.643, 0.824 weighted F1) while routing the majority of traffic through the near-zero-cost ensemble, translating to roughly $10-85 per million utterances versus $99-170 for an LLM-only pipeline. Escalated turns disproportionately follow emotion or sentiment shifts, and the ensemble's confidence is well-calibrated and biased toward under- rather than over-confidence. The pattern holds across three datasets and two LLM providers.

Conclusion: Confidence-gated cascading, well established in general ML systems, transfers cleanly to dialogue-contextual ERC. The escalation policy is not an opaque cost/accuracy dial but yields an interpretable, auditable routing signal. This provides a concrete deployment recipe for CCaaS and conversational-AI platforms deciding how to allocate LLM spend.

Abstract: Emotion recognition in conversation (ERC) is a production capability behind agent-assist prompts, escalation routing, and post-call analytics in contact-center-as-a-service (CCaaS) platforms, where cost and latency constraints matter as much as accuracy. We report a systems-level comparison of three deployment options for dialogue-contextual ERC: a low-cost stacked ensemble (sentence embeddings, windowed context, RandomForest/XGBoost/logistic-regression stacking), off-the-shelf LLM prompting (GPT-4o-mini; zero-shot, few-shot, chain-of-thought), and a confidence-gated hybrid that escalates only the ensemble's least-confident predictions to the LLM - modeled on IVA-to-human-agent escalation policies used in production contact centers. On IEMOCAP, the ensemble significantly outperforms every LLM configuration (0.595 vs. 0.460-0.536 weighted F1, p < 0.0001) at a fraction of the cost and sub-10ms latency; on MELD and CMU-MOSI the ranking reverses, showing neither pure system is a safe default. The confidence-gated hybrid resolves this by Pareto-dominating both pure systems on all three datasets (0.620, 0.643, 0.824 weighted F1) while routing the majority of traffic through the near-zero-cost ensemble, translating to roughly $10-85 per million utterances versus $99-170 for an LLM-only pipeline. The escalation policy is not an opaque cost/accuracy dial: escalated turns disproportionately follow an emotion or sentiment shift, giving operators an interpretable, auditable routing signal, and the ensemble's confidence is well-calibrated and safely under- rather than over-confident. The pattern holds across three datasets and two LLM providers. Confidence-gated cascading is established in general ML systems; our contribution is showing it transfers cleanly to dialogue-contextual ERC, yielding a concrete deployment recipe for CCaaS and conversational-AI platforms deciding how to allocate LLM spend.

</details>


### [25] [Contiguity, Not Importance: Budgeted Repair of Stale KV Caches After Document Edits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17983)
*Mingyang Mao,Wyatt Mackey,Xiaomin Lin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: KV-cache reuse cuts inference cost, but edits make cached contexts stale. The paper frames in-place repair as budgeted recomputation and finds that a contiguous edit-local window is the best training-free policy, recovering ≥0.94 of the post-edit answer margin and running 13–21× faster than full re-prefill.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: KV-cache reuse reduces inference cost in retrieval-augmented generation and agentic systems, but cached contexts become stale when retrieved knowledge, working memory, or user state is edited. Under causal self-attention, even a local edit propagates to downstream KV states, so full re-prefill is reliable but costly, while refreshing only the edited span leaves downstream dependencies stale. The paper seeks a cheaper repair policy that restores consistency.

Method: The authors formulate in-place repair as budgeted recomputation and compare training-free position-selection policies on a factual RAG benchmark with matched direct and derived edits, across three model families. They evaluate candidate selectors at a primary budget, including a contiguous edit-local window versus attention-based, KV-deviation, and structural selectors, and perform mechanistic analysis of position sets under clean-state transplantation versus actual recomputation.

Result: All policies repair direct edits, but derived cases separate them. At the primary budget, the contiguous edit-local window recovers at least 0.94 of the post-edit answer margin and substantially outperforms attention-based, KV-deviation, and structural selectors. Position sets that work under clean-state transplantation can fail under actual recomputation because scattered positions inherit surrounding staleness. The edit-local advantage depends on adjacency and largely disappears when answer-bearing text moves downstream. Repair is 13–21× faster than full re-prefill.

Conclusion: Since answer-relevant edits almost always corrupt model behavior and failure severity is hard to predict, yet edit-local repair is far cheaper, the results support unconditional edit-local repair when the dependent text remains adjacent to the edit.

Abstract: KV-cache reuse can reduce inference cost in retrieval-augmented generation and agentic systems, but cached contexts may become stale when retrieved knowledge, working memory, or user state is edited. Under causal self-attention, even a local edit can affect downstream KV states. A full re-prefill reliably restores consistency but is costly, whereas refreshing only the edited span can leave downstream dependencies stale. We formulate in-place repair as budgeted recomputation and compare training-free position-selection policies on a factual RAG benchmark with matched direct and derived edits. Across three model families, all policies repair direct cases, but derived cases clearly separate them. At the primary budget, a contiguous edit-local window recovers at least 0.94 of the post-edit answer margin and substantially outperforms attention-based, KV-deviation, and structural selectors. Mechanistic analysis shows that position sets effective under clean-state transplantation can fail under actual recomputation because scattered positions inherit surrounding staleness. The edit-local advantage also depends on adjacency and largely disappears when the answer-bearing text moves downstream. Because answer-relevant edits almost always corrupt model behavior, failure severity is difficult to predict, and repair is 13-21 times faster than full re-prefill, our results support unconditional edit-local repair when the dependent text remains adjacent to the edit.

</details>


### [26] [TuiML: Machine Learning for AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17984)
*Nilesh Verma,Nick Lim,Albert Bifet,Bernhard Pfahringer*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TuiML is a self-contained machine-learning library designed for AI agents rather than human programmers, exposing all components via machine-readable metadata and schemas so agents can search, inspect, compose, and extend validated workflows, with every call seeded, traced, and exportable as runnable notebooks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing ML libraries such as Weka and scikit-learn were built for human programmers. When language-model agents use them, they rely on recalling APIs from memory and writing code, which hides what the library offers, defers errors to runtime, and loses experimental state between turns.

Method: TuiML provides native algorithms across supervised, unsupervised, time-series, data handling, tuning, and evaluation. Each component self-describes through machine-readable metadata and parameter schemas, enabling agents to search, inspect, compose validated workflows, and register new discoverable components. Calls are validated, seeded, and traced; sessions export as runnable notebooks. A single specification layer drives MCP, agent-framework adapters, a Python API, a CLI, and local model serving, with data and models kept on-machine.

Result: Benchmarks show TuiML remains predictively competitive with scikit-learn and Weka.

Conclusion: Although it appears conventional to human users, TuiML is designed agents-first, letting agents read, extend, and operate machine learning autonomously. It is open source, documented at https://tuiml.ai.

Abstract: Machine-learning libraries such as Weka and scikit-learn were designed for human programmers. Language-model agents now use these same libraries by recalling APIs from memory and writing code, an approach that hides what a library offers, delays errors until runtime, and loses experimental state between turns. We present TuiML, a self-contained machine-learning library built for AI agents, with native algorithms across supervised, unsupervised, time-series, data handling, tuning, and evaluation tasks. Every component describes itself through machine-readable metadata and parameter schemas, so an agent can search the library, inspect components, compose validated workflows, and register new ones that become discoverable in turn. Every call is validated, seeded, and traced, and sessions export as runnable notebooks, making experiments reproducible by construction. One specification layer drives the Model Context Protocol (MCP), agent-framework adapters, a Python API, a CLI, and local model serving, while data and models never leave the machine. Benchmarks show TuiML remains predictively competitive with scikit-learn and Weka. While looking like a conventional library to a human user, TuiML is designed for agents first, allowing them to read, extend, and operate machine learning autonomously. TuiML is open source, with documentation at https://tuiml.ai.

</details>


### [27] [RideWay: Benchmarking Efficient Task Completion for Tool-Using Language Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17985)
*Qingnuan Han,Boli Fang,Mingzhi Hou,Claire Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RideWay is an efficiency-centered benchmark for ride-hailing agents in a stateful tool-calling environment, paired with Efficiency Utility, a success-gated metric that penalizes excess tool calls and user-facing turns. Across 58 tasks and 24 models, the metric reaches 78.7% accuracy on held-out preferences, with strong performance on turn differences but chance-level accuracy on tool-call-only differences.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard evaluation of AI agents focuses only on task completion. In interactive service settings, however, a task-completing agent can still frustrate users via repeated questions, redundant searches, or avoidable revisions. There is a need to measure interaction efficiency alongside success.

Method: The authors introduce RideWay, a benchmark for ride-hailing agents operating in a stateful tool-calling environment, and Efficiency Utility, a success-gated metric that discounts successful trajectories based on excess tool calls and user-facing turns relative to task-specific reference effort. Penalty weights are calibrated using human paired preferences, capturing the service-workflow trade-off where extra dialogue causes visible friction while extra tool use may sometimes verify constraints.

Result: Evaluated over 58 tasks and 24 models, the fitted penalty for excess turns is roughly twice that for excess tool calls. On task-disjoint held-out preferences, Efficiency Utility achieves 78.7% accuracy overall, 90.6% when trajectories differ in turns, but only chance-level accuracy when they differ solely in tool calls.

Conclusion: RideWay makes interaction efficiency measurable alongside task success in agent evaluation, while also revealing the limits of count-based tool-use evaluation, particularly where human annotators disagree most.

Abstract: AI agents are usually evaluated by whether they complete a task. In interactive service settings, a successful agent can still frustrate users by asking repeated questions, performing redundant searches, or making avoidable revisions. We introduce RideWay, an efficiency-centered benchmark for ridehailing agents in a stateful tool-calling environment, together with Efficiency Utility, a success-gated metric that discounts successful trajectories for excess tool calls and user-facing turns relative to task-specific reference effort. Human paired preferences calibrate the relative penalties, reflecting an aggregate service-workflow trade-off: extra dialogue often creates visible friction, whereas extra tool use can sometimes verify constraints or preserve user intent. Across 58 tasks and 24 models, the fitted penalty for excess turns is about twice that for excess tool calls. On task-disjoint held-out preferences, Efficiency Utility achieves 78.7% accuracy overall: 90.6% when trajectories differ in turns, but chance-level accuracy when they differ solely in tool calls - the axis on which human annotators agree least. RideWay therefore makes interaction efficiency measurable alongside task success, while exposing the boundary of count-based tool-use evaluation.

</details>


### [28] [Multimodal Conditioning of Fine-Tuned Stable Diffusion XL for Controllable and Culturally Faithful Ulos Motif Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17987)
*Humasak Simanjuntak,Tamara Yunika Sianipar,Bronson T. M Siallagan,Difya Laurensya Ambarita,Arlinta Barus*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A multimodal generative framework combining a LoRA-fine-tuned Stable Diffusion XL with LLaMA 1.5-7B enables controllable, culturally faithful Batak Ulos motif generation, where ablations show that more conditioning mechanisms do not yield better results.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The traditional Batak Ulos weaving industry struggles to produce diverse, innovative motifs because conventional design methods are manual and limited, threatening cultural heritage preservation.

Method: Proposes a multimodal generative framework integrating a fine-tuned Latent Diffusion Model (Stable Diffusion XL v1.0 via LoRA) with a Multimodal Large Language Model (LLaMA 1.5-7B). Four conditioning mechanisms—text, image, representation, and semantic map via ControlNet—jointly guide generation, each controlling a distinct aspect from semantic intent to spatial layout. Evaluated through a five-level ablation study across three scenarios (shape transformation, colour variation, high-complexity input), plus qualitative evaluation by nine weavers and thirty public participants, and a web-based text-to-image and image-to-image prototype.

Result: Conditioning effectiveness is not proportional to the number of mechanisms combined. Text + Image + Semantic Map gave the best FID (270) and CLIP Score (0.65–0.70) but weakest SSIM (0.65). Text + Image + Representation gave the best balance (stable SSIM 0.84, competitive FID 280). All four mechanisms yielded the weakest FID (330), indicating conflicting optimization signals. Qualitative evaluation confirmed statistically significant acceptance (Wilcoxon, p=0.007 weavers; p<0.001 public).

Conclusion: Two or three well-chosen conditioning mechanisms outperform combining all four, and the framework offers a practical digital design tool for cultural heritage preservation.

Abstract: The traditional Batak Ulos weaving industry faces growing challenges in producing diverse, innovative motifs due to limitations in conventional, manually driven design methods. This study proposes a multimodal generative framework integrating a fine-tuned Latent Diffusion Model (Stable Diffusion XL v1.0 via LoRA) with a Multimodal Large Language Model (LLaMA 1.5-7B) to enable controllable, culturally faithful Ulos motif generation. Four complementary conditioning mechanisms: text, image, representation, and semantic map (via ControlNet) jointly guide the generation process, each governing a distinct aspect from semantic intent to spatial layout. A five level ablation study across three scenarios (shape transformation, colour variation, and high-complexity input) shows that conditioning effectiveness is not proportional to the number of mechanisms combined: Text + Image + Semantic Map achieved the best FID (270) and CLIP Score (0.65 - 0.70) but the weakest SSIM (0.65), while Text + Image + Representation offered the best overall balance, with stable SSIM (0.84) and competitive FID (280). Combining all four mechanisms yielded the weakest FID (330), indicating conflicting optimization signals. Qualitative evaluation by nine weavers and thirty public participants confirmed statistically significant positive acceptance (Wilcoxon, p=0.007 and p<0.001, respectively). A web-based prototype supporting text-to-image and image-to-image generation was also developed, offering a practical digital design tool for cultural heritage preservation.

</details>


### [29] [Missing Bridges: Composition-Aware Active Imitation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18004)
*Maxwell J. Jacobson,Ahmed H Qureshi,Yexiang Xue*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AALT is an active imitation learning method that requests demonstrations maximizing expected gains in start-goal connectivity, organizing demos into a latent topology of hub states and learned behaviors, achieving 72/72 tasks with only 3 extra demos in a UR5e domain.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In structured multi-task domains, the number of start-goal tasks grows combinatorially even though solutions share reusable behavior. Existing active imitation learning methods select requests by expected information gain about the expert policy, but ignore the value of composable behaviors that a single demonstration may enable across many tasks.

Method: AALT selects demonstrations that maximize expected gains in start-goal connectivity, an objective formally tied to information gain about task reachability. It organizes existing demonstrations into a topology of latent hub states connected by learned behaviors, identifies high-value bridge demonstrations likely to enable many tasks at once, and grounds each to an expert query. At inference, it plans through the topology and conditions a diffusion policy on each successive hub transition.

Result: In a simulated UR5e robot ordered-retrieval domain with 72 tasks, AALT improved from 42/72 to 72/72 (100%) success consistently using only 3 demonstrations totaling 5 transitions beyond the initial dataset. After 20 demonstrations, the strongest baseline averaged 88.6% success using 98 transitions.

Conclusion: Selecting demonstrations for gains in start-goal connectivity, rather than policy information gain alone, yields substantially more efficient active imitation learning in structured multi-task domains by exploiting reusable, composable behaviors captured in a latent topology.

Abstract: Active imitation learning reduces expert effort by allowing a learner to request the demonstrations it needs. Existing methods typically select these requests for their expected information gain about the expert policy. In structured multi-task domains, however, the number of start-goal tasks may grow combinatorially despite their solutions sharing reusable behavior. This makes composable behaviors especially valuable, since a single demonstration may help solve many tasks at once. Prior methods do not explicitly account for this value when selecting which demonstration to request. We introduce Adaptive Agents via Latent Topologies (AALT), which requests demonstrations that maximize expected gains in start-goal connectivity. We further show that this objective is formally tied to information gain about task reachability. AALT organizes existing demonstrations into a topology of latent hub states connected by learned behaviors, identifies high-value bridge demonstrations that are likely to enable many tasks at once, and grounds each to an expert query. At inference, it plans through the resulting topology and conditions a diffusion policy on each successive hub transition. In a simulated UR5e robot ordered-retrieval domain with 72 tasks, AALT improved from 42/72 to 72/72 (100%) successful tasks consistently using only 3 demonstrations totaling 5 transitions beyond the initial dataset. After 20 demonstrations, the strongest baseline averaged 88.6% success using 98 transitions.

</details>


### [30] [Anchoring What Matters: A Dual-Level Learning Framework for Visually-Grounded Multimodal Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18057)
*Xinxin Song,Siyuan Li,Tingxiong Xiao,Jinli Suo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PIVOT is a dual-level RLVR framework for LVLMs that uses self-calibrated experience replay and vision-guided advantage allocation to preserve and reinforce visually grounded reasoning behaviors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard on-policy RLVR algorithms have an optimization bottleneck in preserving visual reasoning: valuable visually grounded reasoning trajectories are discarded after a single update, and uniform token advantage allocation prevents reinforcement of critical perception or reasoning steps.

Method: PIVOT anchors policy optimization around informative visual reasoning signals via two mechanisms: (1) a self-calibrated experience replay mechanism that selectively collects and replays visually grounded historical experiences as stable reference anchors for policy optimization; and (2) a vision-guided advantage allocation mechanism that assigns additional vision-aware advantages to tokens based on their local visual support and impact on downstream reasoning.

Result: Extensive experiments across diverse benchmarks show that PIVOT achieves highly competitive performance in enhancing LVLMs' multimodal reasoning capabilities.

Conclusion: PIVOT effectively addresses the RLVR optimization bottleneck for LVLMs by combining experience replay with vision-guided advantage allocation, improving visually grounded multimodal reasoning.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) has significantly improved the reasoning capabilities of large vision-language models (LVLMs). However, standard on-policy RLVR algorithms face a critical optimization bottleneck in preserving and reinforcing visually grounded reasoning behaviors: valuable visually-grounded reasoning trajectories are discarded after a single update, while uniform token advantage allocation prevents the model from reinforcing critical perception or reasoning steps. To bridge this gap, we propose PIVOT, a dual-level learning framework that anchors policy optimization around informative visual reasoning signals. Specifically, PIVOT introduces a self-calibrated experience replay mechanism, which selectively collects and replays visually-grounded historical experiences as stable reference anchors for policy optimization. Building upon this, we further design a vision-guided advantage allocation mechanism to allocate additional vision-aware advantages to tokens based on their local visual support and impact on downstream reasoning. Extensive experiments across diverse benchmarks demonstrate that PIVOT achieves highly competitive performance in enhancing the multimodal reasoning capabilities of LVLMs.

</details>


### [31] [The Other Half of the Memory Wall: Serving 35B MoEs from SSD with Trained Routing Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18063)
*Yu Lin,Yiming Wang,Runyuan Cai,Hanze Liu,Xiaodong Zeng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Edge0 is a streaming MoE inference engine that uses a prerouter to predict the next layer's expert routing one token ahead, enabling early SSD prefetching and serving a 35B MoE at 20tok/s within 3GiB peak memory on a single 24GB machine.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MoE inference on consumer hardware is limited by weight memory (e.g., 19.5GB for a 35B model at 4-bit), and sparsity reduces per-token compute but not the bytes that must be held. Naive SSD offloading fails because layer N+1's experts must be selected before layer N's output exists, preventing reads from being hidden behind compute.

Method: Edge0 introduces a prerouter: a per-layer head that predicts the next layer's routing one token ahead. The prediction is consumed as the routing itself, so the staged expert set equals the routed set and nothing is dropped. An unmerged recovery LoRA, trained on the student path, compensates for quality lost to int4 quantization and routing replacement.

Result: On a single 24GB machine, Edge0 serves a 35B MoE at 20tok/s within 3GiB of peak active memory, staying within a few points of its fp16 teacher on average across five public benchmarks. An 8B tier runs on the same framework, and the framework, checkpoints, and adapters are open source.

Conclusion: Edge0 enables memory-bounded streaming MoE inference on consumer hardware by making routing prediction self-fulfilling, hiding SSD reads behind compute and recovering quality via a trained LoRA, with 35B and 8B tiers and open-sourced artifacts.

Abstract: Mixture-of-experts (MoE) inference on consumer hardware is bounded by weight memory: a 35B-class model is 19.5GB at 4-bit, and sparsity shrinks the compute per token, not the bytes that must be held. Naive offloading to SSD does not help on its own, because layer N+1's experts must be chosen before layer N's output exists, so the reads cannot start early enough to hide behind compute. We present Edge0, a streaming MoE inference engine that closes the gap with a prerouter: a per-layer head predicts the next layer's routing one token ahead, and the prediction is consumed as the routing itself, so the staged expert set equals the routed set and nothing is dropped. An unmerged recovery LoRA, trained on the student path, pays back the quality lost to int4 quantization and routing replacement. On a single 24GB machine, Edge0
  serves a 35B MoE at 20tok/s inside 3GiB of peak active memory, within a few points of its fp16 teacher on average across five public benchmarks. An 8B tier runs on the same framework, and the framework, checkpoints, and adapters are open source.

</details>


### [32] [Teaching AI, Robotics, & Community: A Hubs-Based K-12 Education Framework for Reaching Rural Schools](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18072)
*Maxwell J. Jacobson,Gustavo Rodriguez-Rivera,Petros Drineas,Yexiang Xue*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ARC is a hubs-based framework where colleges train undergraduate mentors and host workshops for nearby K-12 teams, with mature school programs becoming secondary hubs. A university trial produced three rural robotics teams with gains in student knowledge and mentor confidence, while a spatial Markov simulation for Indiana projects ARC reaching 74% of public K-12 schools and creating 992 robotics programs over 40 years versus 161 under natural growth.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: K-12 robotics and AI education is difficult to scale, particularly in rural regions lacking sustained technical mentorship. Existing competition programs such as FIRST offer pathways and instructional opportunities but do not remove the need for local programming and robotics expertise.

Method: The paper introduces AI, Robotics, & Community (ARC), a hubs-based framework in which colleges train undergraduate mentors and host workshops for nearby K-12 teams; mature school programs can become secondary hubs, forming a self-reinforcing education loop with geographically propagating, potentially super-linear mentorship reach. The framework is evaluated through a trial deployment at one university, using five-point Likert surveys to measure K-12 and undergraduate outcomes. A spatial Markov model of ARC's growth is then constructed and simulated using Indiana as a testbed.

Result: The trial deployment created three rural robotics teams. Mean Likert-scale increases for K-12 programming knowledge, resource access, and practice opportunities were 2.00, 2.25, and 1.25 points. Undergraduate confidence in teaching technical concepts, adapting explanations, and managing groups, along with finding mentoring enjoyable and meaningful, increased by 1.29, 1.14, 1.00, and 1.14 points. In simulation under moderate conditions, ARC reached 74% of Indiana's 1,925 public K-12 schools and produced 992 robotics programs after 40 years, compared with 161 projected under natural growth alone.

Conclusion: ARC can create and support rural robotics programs, train undergraduate AI and robotics mentors, and potentially scale mentorship across a region.

Abstract: K-12 robotics and AI education remains difficult to scale, especially in rural regions lacking sustained technical mentorship. Programs like FIRST provide competition pathways and instructional opportunities, but they do not eliminate the need for local programming and robotics expertise. We introduce AI, Robotics, & Community (ARC), a hubs-based framework where colleges train undergraduate mentors and host workshops for nearby K-12 teams. Mature school programs can become secondary hubs that support additional schools, creating a self-reinforcing education loop where mentorship reach propagates geographically and can even grow super-linearly. We first evaluate ARC through a trial deployment at one university. The trial created three rural robotics teams. On five-point Likert surveys, mean increases in K-12 programming knowledge, resource access, and practice opportunities were 2.00, 2.25, and 1.25 points. Likewise, undergraduate confidence teaching technical concepts, adapting explanations, managing groups, and finding mentoring enjoyable and meaningful increased by 1.29, 1.14, 1.00, and 1.14 points. Additionally, we create a spatial Markov model of ARC's growth and simulate it using the state of Indiana as a testbed. Under moderate conditions, we find that ARC reaches 74% of Indiana's 1,925 public K-12 schools and produces 992 robotics programs after 40 years, compared with 161 projected under natural growth alone. Together, these results show ARC can create and support rural robotics programs, train undergraduate AI and robotics mentors, and potentially scale mentorship across a region.

</details>


### [33] [Decodability is Not Causality: Dissociating Probe Readouts from Behavioral Drivers via SAE Decomposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18080)
*Devesh Tiwari,Camille Davis,Shivank Sinha,Talia Weaver,Aditya Shah,Maheep Chaudhary*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Probe-weight geometry alone does not identify the features a model causally uses; combining probe alignment with gradient sensitivity (via coherence-gated SAE ablation) separates behaviorally causal features from mere probe readouts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Linear probes decode safety-relevant concepts (e.g., truthfulness) from LM activations, but high probe accuracy only shows decodability, not that the features encoded in probe weights causally drive model behavior. This paper argues the gap cannot be closed from probe-weight geometry alone, since geometrically aligned features need not be the ones the model actually uses, so causal relevance demands intervention.

Method: A feature-level diagnostic that decomposes a deployed True/False probe into sparse-autoencoder (SAE) features, ranks them by both probe alignment and gradient sensitivity of model behavior, and ablates the resulting shared, probe-only, and random feature sets under a coherence gate. Applied to the TTPD truth probe (Buerger et al., 2024) in the instructed truth/deception setting of Long et al. (2025) on Gemma2-9B-Instruct.

Result: The two rankings overlap only weakly (about 12%, Spearman rho = 0.10). Ablation sharply dissociates them: shared features flip the output far more (up to 27%) than equally sized probe-only (6%) or random (1%) sets at full coherence, while probe-only features perturb the probe's own readout. The dissociation holds across five seeds and a held-out split, and activation-aware selection flips behavior nearly three times as often as the probe's geometric top features (17.6% vs. 6.1%).

Conclusion: In this setting, the geometric projection of a probe's weight vector alone does not identify the features the model causally uses; however, combining probe information with feature activation statistics recovers substantially more behaviorally causal features, and coherence-gated SAE intervention is needed to separate them from probe readouts.

Abstract: Linear probes can decode safety-relevant concepts such as truthfulness from language-model activations, but probe accuracy may show only decodability, not that the features the probe weights causally drive model behavior. We demonstrate that this gap cannot be closed from the geometry of probe weights alone: the features geometrically aligned with probe direction need not be the ones the model uses, so causal relevance requires intervention. We introduce a feature-level diagnostic that decomposes a deployed True/False probe into sparse-autoencoder (SAE) features, ranks those features by both probe alignment and by gradient sensitivity of the model's behavior, and ablates the resulting shared, probe-only, and random feature sets under a coherence gate. On the truth probe of Buerger et al. (2024) (TTPD), applied in the instructed truth/deception setting of Long et al. (2025) for Gemma2-9B-Instruct, the two rankings overlap only weakly (about 12%, Spearman rho = 0.10), and ablation dissociates them sharply: features the probe shares with the model flip the output far more (up to 27%) than equally sized probe-only (6%) or random (1%) features at full coherence, while probe-only features instead perturb the probe's own readout. The dissociation holds across five seeds and a held-out split, and an activation-aware selection of features flips behavior nearly three times as often as the probe's geometric top features (17.6% vs. 6.1%). In this setting, therefore, the geometric projection of a probe's weight vector alone does not identify the features the model causally uses; however, combining probe information with feature activation statistics recovers substantially more behaviorally causal features, and coherence-gated SAE intervention is needed to separate them from probe readouts.

</details>


### [34] [When Is Graph Structure Worth Its Cost? The Case for Structure Pricing in Retrieval-Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18099)
*Yuzhong Zhang,Haoyang Ma,Chao Peng,Lionel Briand,Boxi Yu,Jialun Cao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EffiRAG is a lightweight graph-based RAG system that uses the graph only to locate relevant passages and generates answers from original text, achieving higher preference and 57% lower cost than LightRAG-hybrid on UltraDomain, with even larger cost advantages as the corpus grows.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Graph-based RAG can answer questions requiring information across many documents, but graph construction typically requires numerous language-model calls during ingestion, raising the question of whether quality gains justify the added cost.

Method: EffiRAG uses the graph to locate relevant passages and generates answers from the original text, preserving source information while keeping graph construction and query processing lightweight. At larger corpus sizes (10 and 20 documents per domain), it applies a lightweight, non-LLM filter to skip low-salience chunks.

Result: On UltraDomain (120 open-ended questions across four domains), EffiRAG is preferred over LightRAG-hybrid on 93 questions versus 7, with 20 splits, and reduces total system cost by 57% (USD 0.952 to USD 0.408, including ingestion and querying LLM calls). At 10 and 20 documents per domain, it remains preferred and costs 4.2x and 4.5x less, respectively.

Conclusion: The comparisons reveal different quality-cost trade-offs, so graph-based RAG systems should be evaluated by both answer quality and cost. The results favor graph structures that locate and preserve source evidence.

Abstract: Graph-based retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) can help answer questions that require information from many documents. However, building a graph often requires many language-model calls during ingestion. It is therefore important to ask whether its quality gains justify the additional cost.
  We present EffiRAG, a graph-based RAG system designed to reduce this cost. It uses the graph to locate relevant passages and generates answers from the original text. This design preserves source information while keeping graph construction and query processing lightweight.
  We evaluate EffiRAG on UltraDomain, which contains 120 open-ended questions from four domains. Compared with LightRAG-hybrid, EffiRAG produces the preferred answer on 93 questions. LightRAG is preferred on 7, and the remaining 20 are splits. EffiRAG also reduces total system cost by 57 percent, from USD 0.952 to USD 0.408. The cost includes language-model calls during ingestion and querying.
  The advantage remains as the corpus grows. At 10 and 20 documents per domain, EffiRAG uses a lightweight, non-LLM filter to skip low-salience chunks. It remains preferred over LightRAG-hybrid. It costs 4.2 times and 4.5 times less, respectively.
  The comparisons identify different quality-cost trade-offs. Graph-based RAG systems should therefore be evaluated by both answer quality and cost. The results favor graph structure that locates and preserves source evidence.

</details>


### [35] [AutoTuneBench: Trustworthy Measurement for Agent Auto-Tuning of LLM Serving Engines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18123)
*Li Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AutoTuneBench is a benchmark and measurement protocol that makes trust architectural for LLM-agent-driven GPU kernel and serving-engine tuning, exposing four measurement failure modes in a 619-call pilot and showing how honest measurement substantially shrinks reported speedups.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents optimize GPU kernels and serving engines via a propose-measure-keep loop, but the underlying measurements are unreliable. A four-day pilot corpus of 619 model calls reveals four failure modes: strawman baselines that manufacture speedups, absolute times that do not transfer across machines, saturated tasks that nullify comparisons, and infrastructure defects that impersonate science. This undermines the credibility of claimed optimizations.

Method: The authors present AutoTuneBench, a benchmark and measurement protocol designating trust as architectural. The protocol is frozen as code with test-enforced provenance; a database-level validator rejects out-of-protocol results; anti-cheat checks execute outside the agent's modification surface; comparisons follow pre-registered readouts; and measurements anchor to externally published results, grounded in paired-seed statistics with a 5% cross-run coefficient-of-variation cap.

Result: Honest measurement rewrites headline claims: the best kernel reads 10.6x versus a naive baseline but only 2.03x versus an honest one; one configuration yields 1.174x on one machine and 1.0049x on another; a pre-registered on/off comparison nulls at a shared wall (2.4840 vs 2.4957 ms); and the KernelBench Level-1 suite admits 51% of tasks with a median speedup of 1.0001x over PyTorch eager.

Conclusion: Naive agent-driven measurement protocols systematically inflate reported gains, whereas architectural, pre-registered, provenance-enforced measurement yields far more conservative and trustworthy results. The protocol, the two-engine corpus (vLLM and SGLang), and its audit trail are released as open artifacts.

Abstract: Large language model agents tune GPU kernels and serving engines through a closed loop of propose, measure, and keep, but the measurements behind this loop are not trustworthy. We characterize four failure modes from a four-day pilot corpus of 619 model calls: strawman baselines manufacture speedups, absolute times do not transfer across machines, saturated tasks nullify comparisons, and infrastructure defects impersonate science. We present AutoTuneBench, a benchmark and measurement protocol that makes trust architectural. The protocol is frozen as code with test-enforced provenance; a database-level validator rejects out-of-protocol results; anti-cheat checks run outside the agent's modification surface; comparisons follow pre-registered readouts; and measurements anchor to externally published results, grounded in paired-seed statistics with a 5\% cross-run coefficient-of-variation cap. Honest measurement rewrites the headlines: our best kernel reads 10.6x against a naive baseline but 2.03x against the honest one; one configuration delivers 1.174x on one machine and 1.0049x on another; a pre-registered on/off comparison nulls at a shared wall (2.4840 vs 2.4957\,ms); and the KernelBench Level-1 suite admits 51\% of tasks with median speedup 1.0001x over PyTorch eager. The protocol, the two-engine corpus (vLLM and SGLang), and its audit trail are released as open artifacts.

</details>


### [36] [Designing Agentic AI Workflow Portfolios under Imperfect Selection and Compute Cost](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18126)
*Mojtaba Abdolmaleki,Stefanus Jasin,Boyu Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper formalizes the choice of running and selecting among multiple agentic AI workflows as a portfolio optimization problem, deriving bounds on the value of workflow variety and algorithms (LP relaxations, randomized rounding, ellipsoid method with a pricing oracle) for finite and implicit workflow pools, validated on ABCD, Schema-Guided Dialogue, and HotpotQA.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agentic AI systems can solve a task via multiple workflows differing in reasoning strategy, verification structure, and compute cost. Picking the single highest-average-performance workflow is suboptimal because workflows succeed on different instances; yet running more executions costs compute and produces plausible distractors that complicate final answer selection, so the joint choice of run size and workflow allocation must be optimized.

Method: The problem is cast as a workflow portfolio problem where the firm jointly chooses run size and allocation across workflow types and selects the final answer after observing outputs. Selector quality is summarized by an odds-lift index, yielding sharp bounds on the value of workflow variety. For finite workflow pools the authors develop exact formulations, LP relaxations, randomized rounding, and computable performance certificates; for large implicit workflow classes, a finite-dimensional dual and an ellipsoid method with a pricing oracle identifies workflows with high weighted accuracy net of recurring compute cost, achieving a near-optimal relaxation solution in polynomially many oracle calls under a weak condition.

Result: On ABCD, Schema-Guided Dialogue, and HotpotQA, portfolio optimization improves held-out selector accuracy over the best standalone workflow by 3.1, 7.5, and 0.9 percentage points respectively. Dual-guided workflow generation adds a further 3.5 points on ABCD and 24.1 on HotpotQA, but yields no additional gain on Schema-Guided Dialogue.

Conclusion: Complementary workflows can be exploited systematically through portfolio-and-selector optimization rather than relying on the single best average-performing workflow, and the proposed exact, relaxation-based, and oracle-based methods provide efficient, certified solutions that deliver consistent accuracy gains, with the largest benefits where workflow variety is high.

Abstract: Agentic AI systems often approach the same task through multiple workflows that differ in reasoning strategy, verification structure, and compute cost. A natural deployment policy is to use the workflow with the highest average performance, but this can be suboptimal because different workflows may succeed on different instances. We study a portfolio-and-selector paradigm in which a firm runs multiple workflow executions and selects the final answer after observing their outputs. Additional executions may uncover correct answers that the best standalone workflow misses, but they consume compute and introduce plausible distractors that complicate final selection. We formulate this as a workflow portfolio problem in which the firm jointly chooses run size and allocation across workflow types. We summarize selector quality through an odds-lift index and derive sharp bounds on the value of workflow variety. For finite workflow pools, we develop exact formulations, linear programming relaxations, randomized rounding procedures, and computable performance certificates. For large implicit workflow classes, we derive a finite-dimensional dual and an ellipsoid method using a pricing oracle to identify workflows with high weighted accuracy net of recurring compute cost. Under a weak condition, the method obtains a near-optimal solution to the relaxation with polynomially many oracle calls. We evaluate the framework on three datasets: ABCD, Schema-Guided Dialogue, and HotpotQA. Relative to the best standalone workflow, portfolio optimization improves held-out selector accuracy by 3.1, 7.5, and 0.9 percentage points, respectively. Dual-guided workflow generation adds 3.5 points on ABCD and 24.1 on HotpotQA, with no additional gain on Schema-Guided Dialogue.

</details>


### [37] [Symbolic Temporal Supervision of LLM Agents Using Contracts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18128)
*Yifeng Xiao,Pierluigi Nuzzo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ContrAgent is a contract-based framework that uses LTLf assume-guarantee contracts compiled to DFAs for deterministic online gating and offline evaluation of LLM agent tool-call traces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tool-augmented LLM agents can automate complex multi-step tasks but are prone to hallucinations, distributional instability, and adversarial manipulation, while some tool calls have irreversible consequences. Existing safeguards either grade trajectories post hoc with stochastic LLM judges or block unsafe actions one call at a time, and no single deterministic artifact supports both roles.

Method: ContrAgent captures agent behavior as a sequence of tool calls formalized as a trace over a fixed set of checkable predicates. Required behaviors are specified via assume-guarantee contracts in LTLf, each compiled to a DFA that serves dual roles: online gating of agent actions and offline evaluation of recorded traces. A contract library acts as a reusable knowledge base maintained independently of the agent model and applicable across agents in the same task domain.

Result: On four benchmarks spanning both roles, ContrAgent matches state-of-the-art LLM-judge and rule-based guardrail baselines while producing deterministic, reproducible verdicts and, in online mode, achieving orders-of-magnitude lower per-call latency.

Conclusion: A single deterministic DFA artifact derived from LTLf contracts can unify online and offline supervision of LLM agents, matching baseline effectiveness with improved reproducibility and efficiency, and enabling model-independent, reusable supervision across agents in a domain.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents augmented by tools can automate complex, multi-step tasks, such as web navigation, code generation, and workflow orchestration, by acting on external systems through tool calls. However, hallucinations, distributional instability, and adversarial manipulations in LLMs, and the irreversible consequences of certain tool calls can lead to harmful outcomes. Existing safeguards either grade recorded trajectories post hoc with stochastic LLM judges or block unsafe actions one call at a time, and no single deterministic artifact supports both roles. We present ContrAgent, a contract-based framework for symbolic temporal supervision of LLM agents. ContrAgent captures an agent's behavior as a sequence of tool calls and formalizes it as a trace over a fixed set of checkable predicates. It then specifies required behaviors using assume-guarantee contracts in linear temporal logic over finite traces (LTLf). Each contract is compiled to a deterministic finite automaton (DFA) that serves two roles: gating agent actions online and evaluating recorded traces offline. A contract library, acting as a reusable knowledge base, is maintained independently of the agent's model and can be applied across different agents within the same task domain. We show the effectiveness of our approach on four benchmarks spanning both roles, where ContrAgent matches state-of-the-art LLM-judge and rule-based guardrail baselines while producing deterministic, reproducible verdicts and, in the online mode, orders-of-magnitude lower per-call latency.

</details>


### [38] [Time-Aligned Evolving Concept Graphs for Scientific Relation Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18163)
*Fred Sun,Jingze Wang,Minkun Xu,Shangqi Guo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a time-aligned evolving concept graph framework that jointly models semantic and structural evolution by treating dated papers as shared update events, improving scientific relation forecasting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Forecasting scientific relations can guide discovery by identifying promising connections before they emerge. Existing approaches often model concept semantics and graph structure separately or summarize semantics over coarse historical snapshots, leaving semantic representations potentially misaligned with rapidly evolving graph evidence.

Method: The core idea is to treat dated papers as shared update events, reconstructing semantic and structural states from the same publication history through each prediction time. Pair-level fusion combines these states to forecast first co-occurrence, relation formation, and conditional relation type.

Result: Holding architecture and training fixed, refreshing context alongside graph updates improves mean relation AUPRC by 16.6% over frozen context. On a graph built from 187,848 papers with 270,687 concepts and 7.45 million co-occurrence links, the complete framework improves mean relation AUROC from 0.9290 for the strongest evaluated baseline to 0.9722, with mean population-weighted AUPRC 0.005778.

Conclusion: The proposed time-aligned evolving concept graph framework jointly models semantic and structural evolution, yielding substantial improvements in scientific relation forecasting over strong baselines.

Abstract: Forecasting scientific relations can guide discovery by identifying promising connections before they emerge. Existing approaches often model concept semantics and graph structure separately or summarize semantics over coarse historical snapshots, leaving semantic representations potentially misaligned with rapidly evolving graph evidence. We propose a time-aligned evolving concept graph framework that jointly models semantic and structural evolution. Its core idea is to treat dated papers as shared update events, reconstructing semantic and structural states from the same publication history through each prediction time. Pair-level fusion combines these states to forecast first co-occurrence, relation formation, and conditional relation type. Holding architecture and training fixed, refreshing context alongside graph updates improves mean relation AUPRC by 16.6% over frozen context. On a graph built from 187,848 papers with 270,687 concepts and 7.45 million co-occurrence links, the complete framework improves mean relation AUROC from 0.9290 for the strongest evaluated baseline to 0.9722, with mean population-weighted AUPRC 0.005778.

</details>


### [39] [WFM: Wiki Foundation Model for Complex Agentic Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18182)
*Junnan Dong,Linhao Luo,Senlei Zhang,Gong Chen,Taian Guo,Yifei Yu,Rong Tao,Tao Guo,Qian-Wen Zhang,Siyu An,Ruizhi Qiao,Xing Sun*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes WFM (Wiki Foundation Model), a paradigm for scalable, agent-native knowledge representation and retrieval that shifts from sparse graphs to LLM Wiki representations combining dense document contexts with markdown topological linkages. It introduces a Wiki Graph schema, query-conditioned attentive aggregation with attention variance regularization, and an NCCL-based boundary exchange protocol. Evaluations on five long-term agent memory and multi-hop reasoning benchmarks show strong performance and 10.5x distributed training acceleration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world agents need persistent non-parametric knowledge for dynamic reasoning, including long-term memory and retrieval-augmented generation. While graphs provide structured evidence, their sparse representations restrict machine readability and semantic density needed for complex agentic workflows, motivating a shift toward richer, agent-native representations.

Method: The method includes three components: (i) a Wiki Graph schema that bridges fine-grained structures with dense contexts, maintaining explicit topologies alongside continuous semantics; (ii) query-conditioned attentive aggregation for rich wiki message passing with explicit attention variance regularization; and (iii) an infrastructural NCCL boundary exchange protocol that hoists static partition indices and uses fixed-shape GPU-to-GPU collectives, bypassing CPU serialization and memory copy overheads.

Result: Extensive evaluations across five long-term agent memory and multi-hop reasoning benchmarks demonstrate remarkable performance of WFM, achieving a 10.5 times training acceleration on distributed clusters.

Conclusion: WFM offers a scalable, agent-native representation and retrieval paradigm that addresses the limitations of sparse graph embeddings for LLM Wiki, delivering both strong task performance and substantial distributed training efficiency gains, making it suitable for large-scale commercial deployment.

Abstract: Real-world agents fundamentally require persistent non-parametric knowledge for dynamic reasoning, i.e., long-term memory and retrieval-augmented generation. While graphs have shown reliable advantages in providing structured evidence, the sparse graph representations naturally restrict machine readability and semantic density required for complex agentic workflows. Driven by this limitation, the entire industry is witnessing a paradigm shift from traditional sparse graphs to LLM Wiki, an agent-native knowledge representation that couples dense document contexts with markdown files containing multi-layered topological linkages. However, parameterizing such rich semantics is challenging to encode dense textual contexts using traditional sparse graph embeddings. Moreover, learning LLM Wiki with existing graph encoders could overwhelm distributed system overheads that hinder deployment in large-scale commercial scenarios. To this end, we propose a novel paradigm Wiki Foundation Model, i.e., WFM, tailored for scalable, agent-native representation and retrieval. Specifically, (i) we formalize a Wiki Graph schema that seamlessly bridges fine-grained structures with dense contexts, maintaining explicit topologies alongside continuous semantics; (ii) A query-conditioned attentive aggregation is tailored for rich wiki message passing and explicit attention variance regularization; (iii) We engineer an infrastructural NCCL boundary exchange protocol that hoists static partition indices and leverages fixed-shape GPU-to-GPU collectives, bypassing CPU serialization and memory copy overheads. Extensive evaluations across five long-term agent memory and multi-hop reasoning benchmarks demonstrate the remarkable performance of WFM, while achieving a 10.5 times training acceleration on distributed clusters.

</details>


### [40] [Re2A: Situated Conversational Recommendation via Rubric-based Preference Reasoning and Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18249)
*Dongding Lin,Jian Wang,Xiaoyan Zhao,Wenjie Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Re2A reformulates situated conversational recommendation (SCR) as a structured reason-then-align process, using rubric-based preference reasoning and preference-conditioned optimization to jointly satisfy user preferences and scene consistency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world recommendation often occurs in shared physical environments, motivating SCR, which requires jointly reasoning over dialogue history, co-observed scenes, and in-scene item attributes. Existing methods struggle with two intertwined challenges: accurately understanding situated user preferences over the conversation, and generating responses that satisfy both user needs and grounded situations.

Method: The authors propose Re2A, a framework that casts SCR as a structured reason-then-align process. It introduces rubric-based preference reasoning, where automated rubrics guide the model to produce explicit preference states. Based on these states, a preference-conditioned optimization aligns response generation with dual objectives: user preference satisfaction and situation consistency.

Result: Extensive experiments on two SCR datasets show that Re2A consistently outperforms state-of-the-art methods, delivering more precise and context-aware conversational recommendations. Code is released at https://github.com/DongdingLin/Re2A.

Conclusion: Re2A effectively addresses the intertwined challenges of SCR by decoupling preference understanding from response generation, yielding improved and more grounded conversational recommendations.

Abstract: Real-world recommendation scenarios are commonly grounded in shared physical environments during user-recommender interactions. This motivates situated conversational recommendation (SCR), a complex task requiring recommender assistants to jointly reason over dialogue history, co-observed scenes, and in-scene item attributes. However, current approaches struggle with this setting due to two intertwined challenges: accurately understanding situated user preferences throughout the conversation and generating responses that simultaneously satisfy user needs and grounded situations. To this end, we propose Re2A, a framework that formulates SCR as a structured reason-then-align process. We introduce rubric-based preference reasoning, which uses automated rubrics to guide the model toward producing explicit preference states. Based on these states, we propose a preference-conditioned optimization to align response generation with dual objectives: user preference satisfaction and situation consistency. Extensive experiments on two SCR datasets demonstrate that Re2A consistently outperforms state-of-the-art methods, delivering more precise, context-aware conversational recommendations. Our code is available at https://github.com/DongdingLin/Re2A.

</details>


### [41] [REPAIR: Resolving Long-Tail Confusion in Scientific Retrievers via Fact-Verified Iterative Refinement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18262)
*Yerim Oh,Gunhee Kim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: REPAIR is a self-evolving data augmentation framework that iteratively synthesizes training data to improve scientific dense retrievers, outperforming 19 baselines on nine materials science and biomedical benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Precise retrieval of scientific information is constrained by long-tailed concepts and the high fact-sensitivity of scientific corpora. These issues limit the effectiveness of dense retrievers and cause hallucination-prone LLM augmentation.

Method: REPAIR iteratively synthesizes training data to address knowledge gaps through a cycle of: (1) diagnosis of long-tail concepts, (2) API-guided evidence expansion, and (3) differentiation via hard negative mining, grounding retrieval in factual reality for fine-grained distinctions.

Result: REPAIR significantly outperforms 19 strong baselines across nine materials science and biomedical benchmarks.

Conclusion: Diagnosing and factually augmenting data to address long-tail deficits is essential for robust scientific retrieval.

Abstract: Precise retrieval of scientific information is fundamentally constrained by long-tailed concepts and high fact-sensitivity of scientific corpora. These challenges often limit the effectiveness of dense retrievers and hallucination-prone LLM augmentation. To address this, we present REPAIR, a self-evolving data augmentation framework for scientific dense retrievers. REPAIR iteratively synthesizes training data to address knowledge gaps by cycling through diagnosis of long-tail concepts, API-guided evidence expansion, and differentiation via hard negative mining. This process effectively grounds retrieval in factual reality to resolve fine-grained distinctions. Extensive experiments demonstrate that REPAIR significantly outperforms 19 strong baselines on nine materials science and biomedical benchmarks. Our work highlights that diagnosing and factually augmenting data to long-tail deficits is essential for robust scientific retrieval.

</details>


### [42] [BENCHCOMPASS: From Scores to Signals for Training and Harness Decisions in Payment-Domain LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18270)
*Sijie Dong,Wei Ren,Xuanwei Hu,Jiawei Luo,Zifan Wang,Xiaoyun Feng,Hui Cai,Lyuxin Xue,Peng Lu,Jianshe Li,Xin Zhang,Wei Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BENCHCOMPASS is a payment-domain benchmark built from typed evidence packs with LLM quality checks, attack variants, and expert admission, covering knowledge, context-grounded reasoning, and robustness; it reveals distinct failure modes across 16 models and remains unsaturated.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Payment operations are critical financial infrastructure, yet the value of LLMs there is unclear: payment rules change quickly, evidence is fragmented, and decisions depend on transaction state, participant role, region, and payment rail. Existing benchmarks cannot isolate whether failures stem from missing payment-rule knowledge, poor use of supplied evidence, or brittleness under imperfect harness inputs.

Method: Construction pipeline builds scenario-grounded tasks from typed evidence packs, applies LLM-based quality checks, creates task-input attack variants, and reserves final item admission for domain experts. Release includes an expert-reviewed Pro benchmark (payment knowledge, context-grounded scenario reasoning, Attacked Open robustness) plus a lower-assurance Normal pool for inspection/future curation.

Result: Across 16 model variants, BENCHCOMPASS exposes qualitatively different failure modes: missing parametric payment knowledge, incomplete reasoning over supplied rules, and failure to reject plausible but invalid workflows. Benchmark remains unsaturated: best frontier model reaches 89.6% on Open Context-Grounded Reasoning and 81.7% under attacked inputs; a representative 32B open-weight model reaches 69.8% and 42.6%.

Conclusion: BENCHCOMPASS provides a rigorous, expert-remediated payment-domain evaluation isolating knowledge, evidence-grounded reasoning, and robustness gaps, showing frontier models remain unsaturated and open-weight models lag substantially; data and code are public.

Abstract: Payment operations are a critical financial infrastructure, but the value of large language models in this domain remains unclear because payment rules change quickly, evidence is fragmented, and decisions depend on transaction state, participant role, region, and payment rail. Existing benchmarks do not isolate whether failures come from missing payment-rule knowledge, poor use of supplied evidence, or brittleness under imperfect harness inputs. We introduce BENCHCOMPASS, a payment-domain benchmark whose construction pipeline builds scenario-grounded tasks from typed evidence packs, applies LLM-based quality checks, creates task-input attack variants, and reserves final item admission for domain experts. The release contains an expert-reviewed Pro benchmark covering payment knowledge, context-grounded scenario reasoning, and Attacked Open robustness, plus a lower-assurance Normal pool for inspection and future curation. Across 16 model variants, BENCHCOMPASS shows qualitatively different failure modes: missing parametric payment knowledge, incomplete reasoning over supplied rules, and failure to reject plausible but invalid workflows. The benchmark remains unsaturated: the best frontier model reaches 89.6% on Open Context-Grounded Reasoning and 81.7% under attacked inputs, while a representative 32B open-weight model reaches 69.8% and 42.6%. Benchmark data and code are available at https://github.com/ant-intl/BenchCompass.

</details>


### [43] [Who Audits Whom, on What Substrate, with What Evidence? An Independence-Graded Audit Protocol for Agentic AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18272)
*Mohamed Chahine Ghanem*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Auditor independence for agentic AI is redefined as a graded, three-axis property—principal, substrate, and evidence independence—aggregated by a weakest-link rule, grounded in the beta-factor common-cause-failure model and validated by structural detectability analysis and Monte Carlo simulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Independence is the foundation of assurance, yet it is still treated as binary for agentic AI systems that plan, invoke tools, and act with limited supervision—and that increasingly audit as well as being audited. A binary view cannot capture how an auditor may control, share failure modes with, or rely on self-reported evidence from the auditee.

Method: The paper defines three orthogonal axes: principal independence (who controls the auditor), substrate independence (shared foundation-model family, toolchain, or guardrails cause common-mode failure), and evidence independence (attestable versus self-reported evidence). It adapts the beta-factor common-cause-failure model from reliability engineering, specifies a seven-step protocol with third-party-verifiable outputs, performs a structural detectability analysis of a procurement-controls agent audited at three grades, and runs a Monte Carlo study of the model. Mapping is provided to the EU AI Act as amended, ISO/IEC 42006, UK public-sector risk-management guidance, and audit-regulator practice.

Result: Grading all three axes on a single audit and aggregating by the weakest link exposes assurance gaps that binary treatment hides. The Monte Carlo study shows that a conventional internal audit of an agent—using a real audit team, a second agent, and provider logs—surfaces only 5.9% of the faults it could in principle see and detects nothing at all in half the fault classes.

Conclusion: Independence for agentic AI auditors should be graded, not binary, across principal, substrate, and evidence axes, with weakest-link aggregation applied symmetrically when the auditor is itself an agent. This provides a formally grounded, externally verifiable rubric that aligns with emerging regulation and standards.

Abstract: Agentic AI systems plan, invoke tools and act with limited supervision; they are now both the subject of audits and, increasingly, the auditor. Independence, the foundation of assurance,is still applied to them as a binary. We argue that it must be graded along three orthogonal axes: principal independence (who controls the auditor), substrate independence (an auditor sharing the auditee's foundation-model family, toolchain or guardrails fails with it) and evidence independence (whether evidence is attestable rather than self-reported). Each axis has precedent; the contribution is to grade all three on a single audit, aggregate them by the weakest link, and apply the same rubric when the auditor is itself an agent. We give the model a formal basis by transplanting the beta-factor model of common-cause failure from reliability engineering, a seven-step protocol whose outputs a third party can verify, a structural detectability analysis of a procurement-controls agent audited at three grades, and a Monte Carlo study of the model in which a conventional internal audit of an agent-a real audit team, a second agent, provider logsp-surfaces 5.9% of the faults it could in principle see and none at all in half the fault classes. We map the triple to the EU AI Act as amended, ISO/IEC 42006, UK public-sector risk-management guidance and audit-regulator practice.

</details>


### [44] [Building Trust in Artificial Intelligence: A Necessity for Railway Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18278)
*Lefebvre Renard Clément,Lébé Vincent,Da Silva Ribeiro Pereira Ricardo,Sundell Johan,Jaoul Arnaud Saiah Kenza,Mijatovíc Nenad*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper argues that AI adoption in railways and other safety-critical domains requires a system-level approach to trustworthiness built on three pillars—robustness, Operational Design Domain (ODD), and explainability—integrated within a safe MLOps environment to achieve regulatory compliance and stakeholder acceptance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI is confined to non-safety-critical applications in the railway industry because of strict standards and regulations. To expand AI into mission-critical railway applications, trust in data science and AI algorithms must be increased and regulatory compliance achieved. The authors also aim to show that deep trust in AI, as demanded by emerging regulatory frameworks worldwide, can unlock AI's full potential and accelerate adoption in mission-critical domains.

Method: The paper proposes a review of three main fields essential for trustworthy and compliant AI: (1) robustness—the ability of an AI system to maintain its performance level under any circumstances (ISO 24029); (2) Operational Design Domain (ODD)—explicit definition of the operating conditions under which a system is intended to operate, per the newly published DIN DKE SPEC 99004; and (3) explainability—the ability of an AI system to express the important factors influencing its results in a human-understandable way. A system view is deemed necessary to ensure all trustworthiness requirements interact continuously in a safe MLOps environment.

Result: Algorithms and methods for robustness, ODD, and explainability are already well investigated by non-railway actors and are ready to use for railway applications. The system view ensures the continuous interaction of all trustworthiness requirements within a safe MLOps environment, thereby fostering acceptance from regulators, operators, and the public.

Conclusion: By integrating robustness, ODD, and explainability in a safe MLOps environment, AI can reach compliance and be deployed in safety-critical railway applications. Beyond safeguarding such applications, fostering deep trust in AI as now required by regulatory frameworks worldwide will unlock its full potential and transform the pace of adoption across mission-critical domains.

Abstract: Artificial Intelligence (AI) is currently only applied to non-safety critical applications due to the strict standards and regulations for railway industries. We propose to review the three main fields necessary to increase trust in data science and AI algorithms and reach compliance: robustness, Operational Design Domain (ODD), and explainability. Robustness is the ability of an AI system to maintain its level of performance under any circumstances (ISO24029). ODDs allow the explicit definition of operating conditions under which a system is intended to operate, according to the recently published DIN DKE SPEC 99004. Explainability is the property of an AI system to express important factors influencing the AI system results in a way that humans can understand. Those 3 domains of research are already well investigated by nonrailway actors, with algorithms and methods ready to use for railway applications. A system view is necessary to ensure all trustworthy requirements interact continuously in a safe MLOps environment thereby fostering acceptance from regulators, operators and the public. Beyond safeguarding safety-critical applications, we aim to show that fostering deep trust in AI, as now required by regulatory frameworks worldwide, will unlock its full potential and transform the pace of adoption across mission-critical domains.

</details>


### [45] [Where Should Agents Live? Energy-Memory Characterization of Agentic AI for the Edge-Cloud Continuum](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18283)
*Carolina Fortuna,Vid Hanžel,Tim Strnad,Blaž Bertalanič*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces agentic-eCAL, a metric generalizing Energy Cost of AI Lifecycle (eCAL) to directed multi-agent LLM workflows by combining a closed-form two-rate single-call energy model (compute-bound prefill, memory-bound decode) with 7-layer OSI data transport, validated across NVIDIA A100/H100 benchmarks, 16 open-weight models, and 8 orchestration topologies. Inter-agent text transport costs only 0.25% of workflow energy across 5G RAN, metro, and optical links; the dominant distribution cost in edge-cloud agent placement stems from induced inference and context processing rather than transmission itself.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As telecommunication networks move toward autonomous 5G-Advanced and 6G operations, agentic AI workflows (LLMs performing multi-step reasoning, invoking diagnostic tools, retrieving domain knowledge, and coordinating across agent teams) are increasingly deployed across the edge-cloud continuum. Although the biological brain performs complex cognition on roughly 20W, contemporary LLMs are highly energy- and memory-intensive, making sustainable lifecycle orchestration a critical operational priority. Existing AI lifecycle metrics, however, evaluate only isolated single-model inferences or ignore multi-agent execution graphs entirely, leaving network operators without foundational models to assess whether distributed agent communication incurs meaningful energy costs and where agent teams should physically reside across edge-cloud tiers.

Method: The authors introduce agentic-eCAL, generalizing the Energy Cost of AI Lifecycle (eCAL) metric to directed multi-agent workflows. The metric couples a closed-form two-rate single-call energy model, distinguishing compute-bound prefill from memory-bound decode, with 7-layer OSI data transport modeling. It is grounded in hundreds of GPU benchmark configurations on NVIDIA A100 and H100 GPUs, spanning 16 open-weight models and 8 orchestration topologies, used to validate metric components and study workflow placement implications.

Result: Inter-agent text transport accounts for only 0.25% of workflow energy across 5G RAN, metro, and optical links. Accordingly, in edge-cloud agent placement, the dominant energy cost of distribution is typically not the transmission of inter-agent text itself, but the additional inference and context processing induced by that communication.

Conclusion: The proposed agentic-eCAL provides a generalizable, validated framework for quantifying energy across distributed multi-agent LLM workflows on the edge-cloud continuum. Its findings reframe placement optimization: minimizing transmission is insufficient, and operators should instead target reductions in communication-induced inference and context processing to achieve sustainable agentic AI lifecycle orchestration.

Abstract: As telecommunication networks evolve toward autonomous 5G-Advanced and 6G operations, agentic artificial intelligence (AI) workflows, where large language models (LLMs) execute multi-step reasoning, invoke diagnostic tools, retrieve domain knowledge, and coordinate across agent teams, are increasingly embedded across the edge-cloud continuum. While the biological brain accomplishes complex cognition on an exceptionally modest metabolic power budget of approximately 20W contemporary LLMs are profoundly energy- and memory-intensive, making sustainable lifecycle orchestration a critical operational priority. However, existing AI lifecycle metrics evaluate only isolated, single-model inferences or overlook multi-agent execution graphs entirely. Consequently, network operators lack foundational models to determine whether distributed agent communication incurs meaningful energy costs and where across edge-cloud tiers agent teams should physically reside. To address this gap, we introduce agentic-eCAL, generalizing the Energy Cost of AI Lifecycle (eCAL) metric to directed multi-agent workflows by coupling a closed-form two-rate single-call energy model (compute-bound prefill and memory-bound decode) with 7-layer OSI data transport. Grounded in hundreds of GPU benchmark configurations on NVIDIA A100 and H100, 16 open-weight models and 8 orchestration topologies, we validate components of the metric and study workflow placement implications. Our findings demonstrate that inter-agent text transport incurs 0.25% of workflow energy across 5G RAN, metro, and optical links. Therefore in edge-cloud agent placement the dominant energy cost of distribution is often not the transmission of inter-agent text itself, but the additional inference and context processing induced by that communication.

</details>


### [46] [What Counts as Strategic Reasoning? A Systematic Mapping of Chess Research on Humans, Engines, and Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18286)
*Paolo Ciancarini,Remo Pareschi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A systematic mapping study of 84 core study families on chess as a testbed for strategic reasoning, comparing human players, classical engines, neural/RL systems, LLMs, and hybrid approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Chess has long served as a model domain for studying search, expertise, decision-making, and AI, and the rise of LLMs has renewed its relevance as a controlled environment for investigating strategic reasoning and comparing human and artificial decision-making. A structured overview of this rapidly expanding literature is needed.

Method: Systematic mapping study. The final map comprises 84 core study families, classified along three dimensions: agent type (human players, classical engines, neural and reinforcement-learning systems, LLMs, hybrid approaches), strategic-reasoning stages, and evaluation dimensions.

Result: The literature is strongly concentrated on situation assessment, evaluation, and action selection, while explicit planning, explanation, metacognition, and human–AI collaboration remain less explored.
