LLM research emphasizes state representation and generalization, whereas grounded explanation appears more often in hybrid approaches combining language models with engines, expert knowledge, or other external structures. Two issues are aggregated rather than resolved: hybrid systems differ in where and when heterogeneous capabilities combine, and improved human performance in evaluations does not establish human–AI synergy.

Conclusion: Chess offers a useful bridge between cognitive and computational perspectives on strategic reasoning. The authors identify explicit planning, grounded and faithful explanation, metacognitive calibration, and human–AI complementarity as future research directions, and propose the two aggregated distinctions as extensions of the mapping framework.

Abstract: Chess has long served as a model domain for studying search, expertise, decision-making, and artificial intelligence. The emergence of large language models (LLMs) has renewed the relevance of chess as a controlled environment for investigating strategic reasoning and comparing human and artificial decision-making. We present a systematic mapping study of recent research spanning human players, classical chess engines, neural and reinforcement-learning systems, LLMs, and hybrid approaches. The final map comprises 84 core study families, classified according to agent type, strategic-reasoning stages, and evaluation dimensions.
  The map reveals a literature strongly concentrated on situation assessment, evaluation, and action selection, while explicit planning, explanation, metacognition, and human--AI collaboration remain less explored. LLM research places particular emphasis on state representation and generalization, whereas grounded explanation appears more frequently in hybrid approaches combining language models with engines, expert knowledge, or other external structures. Two distinctions emerge that the map aggregates rather than resolves: hybrid systems differ in where and when heterogeneous capabilities combine, and evaluations that show improved human performance do not thereby establish human--AI synergy. We propose both as extensions of the mapping framework.
  We argue that chess provides a useful bridge between cognitive and computational perspectives on strategic reasoning, and identify explicit planning, grounded and faithful explanation, metacognitive calibration, and human--AI complementarity as directions for future research.

</details>


### [47] [Visual Compliance via Executable Safety Rule Entailment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18328)
*Jisoo Kim,TaeYoon Kwack,Jinwoo Jang,Honguk Woo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GuardEn is an executable safeguard framework that compiles safety policies into atomic propositions and executes them over scene-grounded visual context, improving complex visual safety assessment by an average of 9.8 F1 points over the strongest baseline on SafetyVisionBench.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As risk patterns evolve and safety rules grow more complex, training-based end-to-end safeguards struggle with adaptability and explainable reasoning over intricate safety rules.

Method: GuardEn decomposes safety policies into atomic propositions via Safety-Rule Compilation, modeling their composition as executable code. At test time, Scene-Grounded Execution instantiates these propositions using contextual visual information from scene graphs, enabling rule-grounded, interpretable safety reasoning.

Result: On SafetyVisionBench, the programmable safeguard achieves an average improvement of 9.8 F1 points over the strongest baseline for complex visual safety assessment.

Conclusion: Programmable, rule-grounded safeguard frameworks offer a more adaptable and interpretable alternative to training-based end-to-end approaches for complex visual safety assessment.

Abstract: Recent advances in LLMs and VLMs have enabled safety systems to reason beyond simple risk patterns toward more contextual and semantic safety concerns. However, as risk patterns continue to evolve and safety rules become more complex, existing training-based end-to-end safeguards face persistent challenges in adaptability and explainable reasoning over complex safety rules. To address these challenges, we propose GuardEn (Guarding by Safety Rule Entailment), an executable safeguard framework that decomposes safety policies into atomic propositions through Safety-Rule Compilation, modeling their composition as executable code. At test time, Scene-Grounded Execution instantiates these atomic propositions with contextual visual information derived from scene graphs, enabling rule-grounded and interpretable safety reasoning. Experiments on SafetyVisionBench demonstrate the effectiveness of programmable safeguard for complex visual safety assessment, achieving an average improvement of 9.8 F1 points over the strongest baseline.

</details>


### [48] [Faithful yet Collusive: Why Chain-of-Thought Monitoring Cannot Detect Collusion in LLM Pricing Agents under Oligopolistic Competition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18346)
*Dohun Lee,Hyunwoo Park*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM pricing agents can sustain supracompetitive prices via tacit coordination; a causal graph divergence framework shows collusive behavior and chain-of-thought faithfulness dissociate, so CoT monitoring alone is insufficient as a safeguard against algorithmic collusion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs deployed as autonomous pricing agents may tacitly coordinate to sustain supracompetitive prices, raising the question of whether their chain-of-thought (CoT) can be trusted as a monitoring signal for such algorithmic collusion.

Method: A causal graph divergence framework is proposed that separately measures two dimensions of faithfulness: structural faithfulness and intent faithfulness of LLM pricing agents in Bertrand competition. Nine LLMs are evaluated under duopoly and triopoly conditions.

Result: Collusive behavior and CoT faithfulness dissociate along both dimensions: the most collusive model accurately reports cooperative intent yet reasons structurally unfaithfully, while the most structurally faithful model sustains supra-Nash pricing under both market structures.

Conclusion: CoT monitoring alone cannot serve as a standalone safeguard against algorithmic collusion.

Abstract: Large language models (LLM) deployed as autonomous pricing agents may sustain supracompetitive prices through tacit coordination. We develop a causal graph divergence framework that separately measures structural faithfulness and intent faithfulness of LLM pricing agents in Bertrand competition. Across nine LLMs under duopoly and triopoly conditions, collusive behavior and chain-of-thought (CoT) faithfulness dissociate along both dimensions: the most collusive model accurately reports cooperative intent yet reasons structurally unfaithfully, while the most structurally faithful model sustains supra-Nash pricing under both market structures. These findings establish that CoT monitoring alone cannot serve as a standalone safeguard against algorithmic collusion.

</details>


### [49] [Market Signal Injection: Adversarial Context Manipulation of LLM Pricing Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18357)
*Dohun Lee,Hyunwoo Park*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces market signal injection (MSI), a prompt-level attack that manipulates numerical formatting, competitor ordering, or qualitative commentary to shift LLM pricing agents' behavior without explicit instructions, showing sentiment attacks cause the largest profit/consumer-surplus shifts and that model susceptibility and robustness vary unpredictably.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM pricing agents may change their decisions based on how market data is presented even when numerical values are unchanged, revealing an underexplored attack surface. The authors aim to formalize this surface and assess whether susceptibility is model-dependent and defensible.

Method: The authors define market signal injection (MSI), which alters numerical formatting, competitor ordering, or qualitative market commentary without explicit instructions. They test nine open-weight models in simulated Bertrand duopoly and triopoly markets, plus three proprietary models in duopoly markets. They use matched neutral-text controls, a rule-based agent, sentinel probes on episode-held-out activations (linear AUC, MLP AUC), and evaluate input canonicalization and decision boundary anchoring (prompt constraints plus output projection) as defenses under adaptive attacks.

Result: Sentiment-based attacks produce the largest behavioral shifts, propagating across firms and altering profits and consumer surplus. Susceptibility varies across model families, and larger models are not consistently more robust. Episode-held-out probes distinguish baseline from attacked activations in all eleven re-evaluated model-condition pairs (linear AUC 1.00; MLP AUC 0.93–0.99), though this separability does not itself identify harmful pricing. Input canonicalization removes the tested sentiment attacks, while decision boundary anchoring gives partial mitigation under tested adaptive attacks.

Conclusion: Data presentation is a genuine attack surface for LLM pricing agents. Robustness is not guaranteed by scale, defenses differ in effectiveness, and activation separability is insufficient as a safety criterion, motivating defenses that account for interactions among agents in market settings.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) pricing agents may respond to how market data is presented, even when its numerical values remain unchanged. We introduce market signal injection (MSI), an attack that manipulates numerical formatting, competitor ordering, or qualitative market commentary without issuing explicit instructions. We evaluate nine open-weight models in simulated Bertrand duopoly and triopoly markets and three proprietary models in duopoly markets. Sentiment-based attacks produce the largest behavioral shifts, which propagate to other firms and alter profits and consumer surplus. Susceptibility varies across model families, and larger models are not consistently more robust. Matched neutral-text controls and a rule-based agent support a framing-based account of these shifts under the fixed demand parameters of our simulation. Episode-held-out probes distinguish baseline from attacked activations in all eleven re-evaluated model--condition pairs: linear AUC is 1.00 and MLP AUC ranges from 0.93 to 0.99. This separability does not by itself identify harmful pricing decisions. Input canonicalization removes the tested sentiment attacks, while decision boundary anchoring, which combines prompt constraints with output projection, provides partial mitigation under the tested adaptive attacks. These results identify data presentation as an attack surface for LLM pricing agents and motivate defenses that account for interactions among agents.

</details>


### [50] [Bad Genius: Counterfactual-Guided Harness Evolution Beyond Task-Specific Shortcuts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18366)
*Guojun Zhu,Xunheng Huang,Peng Yin,Jiahui Xie,Sanguo Zhang,Doudou Zhou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CHASE casts harness evolution as constraint generation over validity-preserving benchmark counterfactuals, using a Challenger to find protocol transformations with high gain destruction and a validity firewall, with statistical guarantees linking a finite counterfactual archive to an exact shortcut-neutralized benchmark B0.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Automatic harness optimization can be exploited: a released benchmark B_rel guides a Proposer to edit prompts, memory, retrieval, tools, and control code, and a "bad genius" Proposer may produce a cheating harness whose gains rely on a benchmark-wide shortcut. Task holdout only varies semantics while keeping the benchmark protocol fixed, so it fails to detect such protocol-level cheating.

Method: CHASE models harness evolution as constraint generation over validity-preserving benchmark counterfactuals. After each Proposer update, a Challenger searches for an executable protocol transformation with large gain destruction. A validity firewall ensures task semantics are preserved, and a confirmation set decides whether a counterfactual enters a finite archive. The authors formalize an exact shortcut-neutralized benchmark B0 and establish statistical guarantees relating finite counterfactual archives to B0 and characterizing sequential Challenger search.

Result: CHASE is evaluated on a synthetic benchmark and on OfficeQA, where it retains strong released-benchmark gains while substantially reducing gain destruction under valid protocol changes.

Conclusion: CHASE provides a principled counterfactual-search framework to detect and neutralize benchmark-shortcut cheating in automatically optimized agent harnesses, with theoretical guarantees and empirical validation on synthetic and OfficeQA settings.

Abstract: Reliable agent evaluation is complicated by automatic harness optimization, which repeatedly uses a released benchmark $B_{\mathrm{rel}}$ to guide a Proposer that edits prompts, memory, retrieval, tools, and control code around a fixed target agent. Task holdout varies semantic tasks but leaves the benchmark protocol fixed, so a "bad genius" Proposer can produce a cheating harness whose released-benchmark gain depends on a benchmark-wide shortcut. We introduce Counterfactual Harness Search and Evolution (CHASE), which casts harness evolution as constraint generation over validity-preserving benchmark counterfactuals. After each Proposer update, a Challenger searches for an executable protocol transformation with large gain destruction. A validity firewall checks that task semantics are preserved, while a confirmation set determines whether the counterfactual enters a finite archive. We formalize an exact shortcut-neutralized benchmark $B_0$ and establish statistical guarantees linking finite counterfactual archives to $B_0$ and characterizing sequential Challenger search. We evaluate CHASE on a synthetic benchmark and on OfficeQA, where CHASE retains strong released-benchmark gains while substantially reducing gain destruction under valid protocol changes.

</details>


### [51] [Cultural Competence in Context: A Large Language Model Passes the Turing Test in Finland](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18394)
*Otto Segersven,Pentti Henttonen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A Finnish-language Turing Test reveals that ChatGPT 5.2 passed as human, contradicting expectations that non-English contexts would expose model weaknesses; participants misattributed authorship based on colloquial Finnish cues.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Languages and cultural contexts are unevenly represented in LLM training data, so the authors expected ChatGPT 5.2 to underperform in a Finnish-language Turing Test relative to previously studied English-language US contexts.

Method: A Turing Test was conducted in Finland in Finnish. The authors also introduce model-generated role prompting as a replicable technique for comparative LLM-based Turing Tests aimed at improving construct validity.

Result: Contrary to expectations, the LLM passed the Finnish Turing Test. A prominent source of error was participants' reliance on linguistic cues—particularly colloquial Finnish—as markers of human authorship.

Conclusion: The Turing Test is reframed from a test of intelligence into a comparative method for examining whether an AI system displays credible membership in a particular social world. Its outcome reflects model capabilities, prompted identity, human participants' insider competence, and their AI literacy, making it a useful probe of the human-machine boundary across domains.

Abstract: We report the results of a Turing Test conducted in Finland in the Finnish language. Because languages and cultural contexts are unevenly represented in LLM training data, we expected the model (ChatGPT 5.2) to perform worse in a Finnish-language Turing Test than in previously studied English-language US contexts. We also present model-generated role prompting as a replicable technique for conducting comparative LLM-based Turing Tests designed to improve construct validity. Contrary to our expectations, the LLM passed the Finnish Turing Test. A prominent source of error was participants' reliance on linguistic cues, particularly colloquial Finnish, as markers of human authorship. We reframe the Turing Test from a test of intelligence to a comparative method for examining whether an AI system can display credible membership in a particular social world. Because its outcome reflects model capabilities, prompted identity, insider competence among human participants, and their AI literacy, the method provides a useful probe of the human-machine boundary across domains.

</details>


### [52] [HPOQuest: A Rare-Disease Diagnostic Agent Using Active Phenotype Acquisition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18431)
*Kamilia Zaripova,Nassir Navab,Azade Farshad,Annalisa Marsico*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HPOQuest is a training-free framework for sequential phenotype acquisition in rare-disease diagnosis; starting from sparse observed phenotypes, it maintains a probabilistic disease ranking and iteratively selects informative follow-up questions, improving Recall@1 by up to 30 points and Recall@5 by 45 points across four cohorts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Over 300 million people worldwide are affected by one of more than 7,000 known rare diseases. Diagnosis is difficult because patients initially present with incomplete and heterogeneous phenotypes, so there is a need to improve diagnosis from limited initial clinical evidence.

Method: HPOQuest is a training-free framework for sequential phenotype acquisition. It begins with a small set of observed patient phenotypes, maintains a probabilistic disease ranking, and iteratively selects informative follow-up questions to support clinicians. Confirmed phenotypes update the disease ranking, while all responses update the candidate question set.

Result: Across four benchmark cohorts, HPOQuest substantially improves diagnosis from sparse initial phenotypes, with gains of up to 30 percentage points at Recall@1 and 45 percentage points at Recall@5.

Conclusion: Sequential phenotype acquisition can substantially improve rare-disease diagnosis from limited initial clinical evidence.

Abstract: More than 300 million people worldwide are affected by one of over 7,000 known rare diseases, yet diagnosis remains difficult because patients initially present with incomplete and heterogeneous phenotypes. We present HPOQuest, a training-free framework for sequential phenotype acquisition in rare-disease diagnosis. Starting from a small set of observed patient phenotypes, HPOQuest maintains a probabilistic disease ranking and iteratively selects informative follow-up questions to support clinicians during patient assessment. Confirmed phenotypes update the disease ranking, while all responses update the candidate question set. Across four benchmark cohorts, HPOQuest substantially improves diagnosis from sparse initial phenotypes, with gains of up to 30% points at Recall@1 and 45% points at Recall@5. These results demonstrate that sequential phenotype acquisition can substantially improve rare-disease diagnosis from limited initial clinical evidence.

</details>


### [53] [WetRobo: A Reproducible Robot Kit for Coding Agents in Biological Laboratories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18435)
*Yuna Oikawa,Kei Endo,Takanori Uzawa,Yunzhe Zhang,Manan Anjaria,Lerrel Pinto,Sherry Yang,Koji Tsuda*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: WetRobo is a transferable robot kit (arm, labware, code, teleop demos, AGENTS.md skill file) that lets a coding agent, not a trained policy, adapt to new labs: a biological researcher gives natural-language tasks and the coding agent observes, writes, and runs programs. With OpenAI Codex it succeeded on lid-lifting, cap-removal, and incubator-door tasks; crucially, the coding agent solved the cap task in both Lab X and Lab Y, while a VLA fine-tuned on Lab X failed to transfer to Lab Y.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Automating biological research needs general-purpose, reproducible robot systems enabling wet-lab researchers to delegate tasks without teleoperation or neural-network training. Vision-language-action (VLA) policies for general-purpose arms lose performance when their operating environment changes, so a lab-portable, self-adapting solution is needed.

Method: WetRobo kit comprising one robot arm, lab equipment (incubator, capped reagent bottle, Petri dish), existing arm-control code, recorded teleoperation demonstrations of each task, and a general AGENTS.md skill file. A biological experimentalist supplies natural-language tasks without collecting local teleoperation data or training a network; the coding agent observes the local lab and writes and executes programs, using external tools for adaptation. Evaluated with OpenAI Codex (gpt-5.6-sol) on three real-world tasks.

Result: Three successful real-laboratory tasks: lifting a Petri dish lid, removing a bottle cap, and opening the incubator door. The coding agent completed the cap task in both Lab X and Lab Y, whereas a VLA fine-tuned on Lab X demonstrations succeeded in Lab X but failed to transfer to Lab Y.

Conclusion: Rather than training a policy per laboratory, a practical route for laboratory robotics is to distribute a kit and let a coding agent adapt it in each laboratory. Code, demonstrations, and evolved programs are available at https://github.com/tsudalab/WetRobo.

Abstract: Automating biological research requires general-purpose, reproducible robot systems that allow individual wet-lab researchers to delegate robot tasks without performing teleoperation or neural-network training. Vision-language-action policies have been proposed for general-purpose arms, but can lose performance when their operating environment changes. We therefore built WetRobo, a robot kit that can readily transfer between laboratories. It consists of one robot arm, laboratory equipment (an incubator, a reagent bottle with a cap, and a Petri dish), the existing code that moves the arm, teleoperation demonstrations of each task that we recorded, and a general AGENTS.md skill file. A biological experimentalist provides natural-language tasks without collecting local teleoperation training data or training a neural network. The coding agent observes the local laboratory and writes and executes programs, using external tools as needed for adaptation. We demonstrate use of WetRobo with OpenAI Codex (gpt-5.6-sol) on three successful tasks: lifting a Petri dish lid, removing a bottle cap, and opening the incubator door, all in real-world laboratories. The coding agent achieved the cap task in both laboratories, Lab X and Lab Y, whereas a VLA fine-tuned on Lab X demonstrations succeeded there but failed to transfer to Lab Y. These results point to a practical route for laboratory robotics: instead of training a policy for each laboratory, distribute a kit and let a coding agent adapt it in each laboratory. Code, demonstrations, and the evolved programs are available at https://github.com/tsudalab/WetRobo.

</details>


### [54] [Risk-Aware World Modeling with Flow-Guided Occupancy Evolution for Selective Trajectory Planning in Automated Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18442)
*Rongxiang Zeng,Linsen Cai,Jiafu Zhang,Yijie Zhong,Yide Tao,Shuai Wang,Nan Zheng,Hai L. Vu,Alvaro Garcia Hernandez,Yongqi Dong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RiskWorld is a risk-aware world modeling framework that jointly forecasts shared occupancy and selectively replaces trajectories, achieving the lowest 3 s collision rate among baselines on nuScenes at 11.5 FPS.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Safe automated driving requires anticipating evolving traffic risks and deciding when to revise a planned trajectory, which demands risk-aware forecasting and principled intervention rather than static current-state evaluation.

Method: RiskWorld fuses spatial risk fields and temporal actor context with visual bird's-eye-view features. Flow-guided evolution transports occupancy and scene features, with signed residuals correcting occupancy post-transport. One forecast per planning step is reused across candidates, each compared to a current-state persistence reference to yield a nonnegative collision-score correction. The current-world-selected trajectory acts as a planning anchor, replaced only when predicted risk triggers intervention and an alternative meets component-wise constraints on predicted risk and trajectory error; candidate geometries stay unchanged.

Result: On nuScenes open-loop planning using camera features, annotation-derived current/historical actor states, and dataset map context, RiskWorld achieves the lowest collision rate at a 3 s horizon and second-best average L2 error among state-of-the-art baselines, running at 11.5 FPS on a single RTX 4090 with 90.81 M parameters. Ablations show lower collision rates than current-state rescoring, and forecast reuse enables additional candidate evaluation at low marginal cost.

Conclusion: Risk-aware world modeling with shared occupancy forecasting and selective, constraint-gated trajectory replacement improves long-horizon safety while remaining computationally practical for real-time planning.

Abstract: Safe motion planning in automated driving requires anticipating evolving traffic risks and deciding when to revise the current planned trajectory. We introduce RiskWorld, a risk-aware world modeling framework for shared occupancy forecasting and selective trajectory replacement. Spatial risk fields and temporal actor context are fused with visual bird's-eye-view features. Flow-guided evolution transports occupancy and scene features, while signed residuals correct occupancy after transport. One forecast is generated per planning step and reused across candidates. Each candidate is compared with a current-state persistence reference, yielding a nonnegative collision-score correction. The trajectory selected by current-world evaluation serves as the planning anchor and is replaced only when additional predicted risk triggers intervention and an alternative satisfies component-wise constraints on predicted risk and trajectory error. Candidate geometries remain unchanged. We evaluate RiskWorld for open-loop planning on nuScenes using camera features, annotation-derived current and historical actor states, and dataset-provided map context. RiskWorld achieves the lowest collision rate at a long evaluation horizon of 3 s, and the second-best average L2 error among various state-of-the-art baselines, while running at 11.5 FPS on a single NVIDIA RTX 4090 with 90.81 M parameters. Within-setting ablations show that RiskWorld achieves lower collision rates than the current-state rescoring baseline, while forecast reuse enables additional candidates to be evaluated at low marginal computational cost.

</details>


### [55] [The Mirage of Calibrated Confidence: Trajectory-Independence of Verbalized Confidence in Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18453)
*Jisoo Yang,Jaeho Han,Trung X. Pham,Junyeong Kim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Verbalized confidence in VLMs is largely trajectory-independent—models can self-correct to a wrong answer and stay confident—so the authors propose the Trajectory-Grounding Score (TGS-self, TGS-pair) and TGS-Bench to expose a blind spot in conventional calibration metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: A calibrated VLM can self-correct, arrive at a wrong answer, and still report high confidence; existing metrics (ECE, AUROC) cannot detect that confidence is disconnected from the reasoning trajectory, so calibration training may even worsen it.

Method: The phenomenon is examined through three lenses: content variation, token masking, and the model's own hesitation markers. Two complementary metrics are introduced: TGS-self compares confidence with and without access to the model's own trajectory; TGS-pair tests whether correct trajectories receive higher confidence than flawed ones across vision, reasoning, and answer axes. TGS-Bench provides a model-agnostic suite of 10 benchmarks with controlled good/bad trajectory pairs.

Result: Verbalized confidence is insufficiently sensitive to trajectory content, and calibration training can paradoxically worsen this disconnect. Conventional calibration rankings diverge from trajectory-grounding rankings, exposing a blind spot in current evaluation practice.

Conclusion: Trajectory-grounding is a distinct and necessary axis of evaluation for VLM calibration, not captured by ECE/AUROC; TGS and TGS-Bench make it measurable and reveal misalignment between conventional calibration and genuine trajectory-sensitive confidence.

Abstract: A calibrated Vision-Language Model (VLM) can repeatedly self-correct, say "Wait, I should recheck," arrive at the wrong answer, and still report high confidence. We find that this occurs because verbalized confidence is largely trajectory-independent in the VLMs and calibration methods we evaluate. We examine this through three complementary lenses: content variation, token masking, and the model's own hesitation markers. We show that confidence is insufficiently sensitive to what the reasoning trajectory actually contains, and that calibration training can paradoxically worsen this disconnect. Since existing metrics like ECE and AUROC cannot detect this problem, we propose the Trajectory-Grounding Score (TGS) in two complementary forms: TGS-self, which compares confidence with and without access to the model's own trajectory, and TGS-pair, which tests whether the model assigns higher confidence to correct trajectories than to flawed ones along the vision, reasoning, and answer axes. We propose TGS-Bench, a model-agnostic suite spanning 10 benchmarks with controlled good/bad trajectory pairs, and show that conventional calibration rankings diverge from trajectory-grounding rankings, exposing a blind spot in current evaluation practice.

</details>


### [56] [Collective Loss of Control in LLM Agent Systems: An Epidemic Account of Mutation, Contagion, and Recovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18460)
*Xiangfan Wu,Zonghao Ying,Huiyu Wu,Xing Zheng,Huangsheng Cheng,Xiaorong Shi,Jing Guo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A multi-agent system can lose collective control via an epidemic-like mechanism of accidental mutation, contagion, and recovery; rare local deviations can coexist with substantial collective risk, supported by a deployment audit of implicit communication paths and the RogueHandoff-20 injection benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses how a multi-agent system transitions from a local deviation to a collective loss of control. Motivated by reported OpenAI agent coordination incidents, it seeks an explanation for why rare individual deviations can coexist with substantial collective risk.

Method: The authors propose an epidemic framing organized around accidental mutation (spontaneous seed deviation), contagion (communication enabling other agents to adopt and retransmit an unsafe strategy), and recovery (correction and containment). They conduct a deployment audit identifying implicit communication paths between nominally independent evaluation runs and verify transport through a default Docker backend. They also introduce RogueHandoff-20, a benchmark of 20 executable scenarios that tests recipient susceptibility by injecting unsafe trajectories generated by a modified Qwen-27B route.

Result: Across four native-pending routes, executed harm is 0-5% on normal tasks and 40-95% after injection, exceeding paired direct malicious requests by 5-45 percentage points.

Conclusion: The results support low observed baseline harm alongside high conditional susceptibility, but do not establish natural rare-event rates or demonstrate an autonomous cascade. The account motivates complementary defenses: strengthen resistance and recovery alongside prevention of spontaneous deviations, and audit and restrict unintended communication paths that can turn local failures into collective loss of control.

Abstract: How does a multi-agent system evolve from a local deviation into collective loss of control? We propose an epidemic explanation organized around accidental mutation, contagion, and recovery. A spontaneous deviation creates a seed; communication enables other agents to adopt and retransmit its unsafe strategy; collective failure can emerge when propagation outpaces correction and containment. Thus, rare individual deviations can coexist with substantial collective risk. Motivated by reported OpenAI agent coordination incidents, we examine two ingredients of this mechanism. A deployment audit identifies implicit communication paths between nominally independent evaluation runs and verifies transport through a default Docker backend. RogueHandoff-20, a benchmark of 20 executable scenarios, tests recipient susceptibility by injecting unsafe trajectories generated by a modified Qwen-27B route. Across four native-pending routes, executed harm is 0-5% on normal tasks and 40-95% after injection, exceeding paired direct malicious requests by 5-45 percentage points. These results support low observed baseline harm alongside high conditional susceptibility; they do not establish natural rare-event rates or demonstrate an autonomous cascade. The account motivates complementary defenses: strengthen resistance and recovery alongside prevention of spontaneous deviations, and audit and restrict unintended communication paths that can turn local failures into collective loss of control.

</details>


### [57] [Disentangling Long-Term Memory via Latent Neuro-Symbolic Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18461)
*Cai Ke,Xinghao Chen,Xiaoyu Shen,Keyu Chen,Siyu An,Junnan Dong,Ruifeng Xu,Ruizhi Qiao,Xing Sun*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LGM is a neuro-symbolic framework that disentangles long-term memory into a continuous latent space, using a sparse autoencoder to build query-aware latent graphs and a graph encoder to condition message passing on query embeddings, outperforming state-of-the-art baselines on long-term personalization benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Personalized agents must reason over long-term history to infer explicit preferences and implicit behavioral evidence. Early flat retrieval methods score memory fragments independently and neglect distributed information, while current structured memory frameworks rely on query-agnostic static graphs that fail to capture context-dependent relations. Moreover, raw textual memories are entangled and noisy, making fine-grained personalization and cross-session reasoning computationally prohibitive.

Method: LGM shifts long-term memory disentanglement into a continuous latent space. (i) Instead of persisting fixed graphs, it designs a tailored latent graph construction with a sparse autoencoder: for each query, it maps historical interactions into latent memory nodes and disentangles memory traces into sparse concept activations, dynamically synthesizing query-aware relational edge weights. (ii) A graph encoder treats the query embedding as a conditioning preference to direct non-linear message passing across the task-specific latent subgraph, yielding an expressive memory representation for effective activations.

Result: Extensive experiments on long-term personalization benchmarks demonstrate that LGM significantly outperforms state-of-the-art baselines in capturing both explicit and implicit preferences while enabling personalized responses.

Conclusion: LGM provides an effective neuro-symbolic solution for long-term memory disentanglement and query-aware personalization, advancing cross-session reasoning and fine-grained preference modeling.

Abstract: Personalized agents are required to reason over long-term history interactions to infer both explicit preferences and implicit behavioral evidence. While early flat retrieval methods score memory fragments independently and neglect the distributed information, current structured memory frameworks rely on query-agnostic static graphs that fail to capture the context-dependent relations. Crucially, raw textual memories are inherently entangled and noisy, making fine-grained personalization and cross-session reasoning computationally prohibitive. To this end, we present LGM, a novel neuro-symbolic framework that shifts long-term memory disentanglement into a continuous latent space. Specifically, (i) instead of persisting fixed graphs, we design a tailored latent graph construction with a sparse autoencoder. Subject to each query, it maps historical interactions into latent memory nodes and disentangles the memory traces into sparse concept activations, dynamically synthesizing query-aware relational edge weights. (ii) A graph encoder then treats the query embedding as a conditioning preference to direct non-linear message passing across the task-specific latent subgraph. This yields a highly expressive memory representation for effective activations. Extensive experiments on long-term personalization benchmarks demonstrate that LGM significantly outperforms state-of-the-art baselines in capturing both explicit and implicit preferences while enabling personalized responses.

</details>


### [58] [First Token Matters: Understanding Safety Collapse in Large Reasoning Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18471)
*Yizheng Yang,Haining Yu,Yuechen Wang,Yikai Hou,Xing Fu,Jinbo Yang,Tianqing Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper identifies a localized safety failure in Large Reasoning Models called Onset Refusal Collapse (ORC), where refusal signals drop sharply at the first generated token under harmful queries. It proposes SafeToken, a lightweight inference-time intervention that injects a learned safety anchor at reasoning onset to mitigate this failure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LRMs exhibit strong problem-solving but their safety alignment degrades on harmful queries. Existing safety-improvement approaches rely on additional training or preference optimization and provide limited understanding of the internal mechanisms behind safety failures. This work aims to uncover and address the mechanistic cause of these failures.

Method: Through token-level positional analysis of refusal dynamics, the authors identify Onset Refusal Collapse (ORC)—a sharp drop in refusal-related signal at the first generated token under harmful queries, associated with unsafe responses. They then propose SafeToken, a lightweight inference-time intervention that injects a learned continuous safety anchor precisely at reasoning onset, updating only a single token embedding.

Result: SafeToken effectively mitigates ORC, improves safety on harmful-query benchmarks, and largely preserves reasoning utility, despite updating only a single token embedding.

Conclusion: Safety failures in LRMs can arise from a transient breakdown at the critical transition from understanding to generation. A minimal, precisely targeted inference-time intervention can restore safety without sacrificing reasoning capability.

Abstract: Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) exhibit strong problem-solving abilities, yet their safety alignment often degrades when handling harmful queries. Existing approaches to improving safety largely rely on additional training or preference optimization, while offering limited understanding of the internal mechanisms behind safety failures. In this work, we investigate this failure through a token-level positional analysis of refusal dynamics and identify a localized vulnerability at the onset of reasoning, which we term Onset Refusal Collapse (ORC). We find that the refusal-related signal of LRMs drops sharply at the first generated token under harmful queries, which is associated with unsafe response generation. Motivated by this finding, we propose SafeToken, a lightweight inference-time intervention that injects a learned continuous safety anchor precisely at reasoning onset. Despite updating only a single token embedding, SafeToken effectively mitigates ORC, improves safety on harmful-query benchmarks, and largely preserves reasoning utility. These results suggest that safety failures in LRMs can arise from a transient breakdown at the critical transition from understanding to generation.

</details>


### [59] [Hyperbolic Graph Representation Learning for Differential Diagnosis on Biomedical Knowledge Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18481)
*Pietro Miotto,Lucia Mellini,Tommaso Marzi,Cesare Alippi,Elena Casiraghi,Alberto Paccanaro,Giorgio Valentini,Mauricio Soto-Gomez*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This preliminary study investigates whether hyperbolic graph embeddings, typically suited for tree-like structures, remain effective on hybrid biomedical knowledge graphs integrating ontology hierarchies with heterogeneous patient-level data, and finds they perform well in low dimensions for Mendelian-disease differential diagnosis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Biomedical knowledge graphs combine ontology-derived hierarchies with transversal associations among heterogeneous entities (phenotypes, diseases, genes, proteins, patients). This hybrid structure raises the question of whether hyperbolic embeddings, which naturally capture tree-like organization, remain useful beyond purely hierarchical graphs.

Method: The authors conduct a preliminary study of hyperbolic graph representation learning for Mendelian-disease differential diagnosis on a patient-integrated biomedical graph. They first run experiments on isolated ontology subgraphs to compare hyperbolic models against Euclidean baselines, then evaluate the models on a link-prediction task that ranks candidate diseases for each patient.

Result: On isolated ontology subgraphs, hyperbolic models achieve strong performance in substantially lower dimensions than Euclidean baselines. On the patient-level link-prediction task, results suggest hyperbolic embeddings can exploit biomedical hierarchical structure while supporting diagnostic reasoning over heterogeneous graphs.

Conclusion: Hyperbolic embeddings remain useful beyond purely hierarchical graphs, effectively leveraging hierarchical structure while supporting patient-level diagnostic reasoning on heterogeneous biomedical graphs. This is presented as a preliminary, promising direction for Mendelian-disease differential diagnosis.

Abstract: Biomedical knowledge graphs combine ontology-derived hierarchies with transversal associations among heterogeneous entities such as phenotypes, diseases, genes, proteins, and patients. This hybrid structure raises the question of whether hyperbolic embeddings, which naturally capture tree-like organization, remain useful beyond purely hierarchical graphs. We present a preliminary study of hyperbolic graph representation learning for Mendelian-disease differential diagnosis on a patient-integrated biomedical graph. Experiments on isolated ontology subgraphs show that hyperbolic models achieve strong performance in substantially lower dimensions than Euclidean baselines. We then evaluate the models on a link-prediction task that ranks candidate diseases for each patient. Results suggest that hyperbolic embeddings can exploit biomedical hierarchical structure while supporting diagnostic reasoning over heterogeneous patient-level graphs.

</details>


### [60] [Beyond Routine Compliance: Cunning Data Cultivates Safety Vigilance in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18515)
*Youjia Wang,Lin Xu,Yang Sun,Yuxiao Lu,Chengfang Fang,Jie Shi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces "cunning questions"—benign prompts containing misleading premises, atypical reasoning, or subtle inconsistencies—as a training signal to cultivate model vigilance, showing it transfers to safety-critical scenarios and improves jailbreak robustness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Safety-aligned LLMs can still fail when harmful intent is concealed within seemingly benign contexts. Robust safety requires not only knowledge of safety boundaries but also vigilance: the ability to detect unusual premises, misleading reasoning, and latent risks beneath surface-level semantics, scrutinizing a request's underlying intent and assumptions before acting.

Method: The authors introduce cunning questions, which are not necessarily safety-related but embed misleading premises, atypical reasoning, or subtle inconsistencies. They train models on such reasoning traps (Cunning training) hypothesizing transfer to safety-critical scenarios, evaluate robustness to out-of-distribution jailbreak attacks, test strengthening of subsequent safety fine-tuning, augment an existing state-of-the-art safety alignment pipeline, and perform trace analysis after matched safety fine-tuning plus a conditional theoretical analysis of when invariance learned from cunning data transfers to safety inputs.

Result: Cunning training improves robustness to out-of-distribution jailbreak attacks and strengthens subsequent safety fine-tuning. Augmenting a state-of-the-art safety alignment pipeline with Cunning establishes a new state of the art across evaluated settings, reducing mean ASR across nine backbone–benchmark combinations from 17.40% to 15.05%. Trace analysis indicates safety judgments are more likely to govern responses before harmful planning begins.

Conclusion: Cunning data can strengthen model vigilance and complement conventional safety alignment, offering a transferable mechanism for improving robustness to concealed harmful intent.

Abstract: Safety alignment teaches large language models (LLMs) to recognize harmful requests and reject risky instructions. Yet aligned models can fail when harmful intent is concealed within seemingly benign contexts. Robust safety therefore requires both knowledge of safety boundaries and \textbf{vigilance}: the ability to detect unusual premises, misleading reasoning, and latent risks beneath surface-level semantics. Vigilance requires models to scrutinize a request's underlying intent and assumptions before acting. To cultivate this capability, we introduce \textbf{cunning questions}, which are not necessarily safety-related but contain misleading premises, atypical reasoning, or subtle inconsistencies. We hypothesize that learning to look beyond such reasoning traps can transfer to safety-critical scenarios. Experiments show that Cunning training improves robustness to out-of-distribution jailbreak attacks and strengthens subsequent safety fine-tuning. Furthermore, augmenting an existing state-of-the-art safety alignment pipeline with Cunning establishes a new state of the art across our evaluated settings, reducing mean ASR across nine backbone--benchmark combinations from 17.40\% to 15.05\%. Trace analysis after matched safety fine-tuning suggests that safety judgments are more likely to govern responses before harmful planning begins. A conditional theoretical analysis further characterizes when invariance learned from cunning data can transfer to safety-related inputs. These findings suggest that cunning data can strengthen model vigilance and complement conventional safety alignment.

</details>


### [61] [AeroWeaver: An Embodied-Agent Harness for Weaving Aerial Skills into Distributed, Adaptive Swarm Execution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18520)
*Jiabin Lou,Yirong Yang,Haopeng Wang,Xuxin Lv,Xinyu Liu,Diyuan Hou,Xuehong Liu,Rongye Shi,Wenjun Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AeroWeaver is an embodied-agent harness that bridges LLM-based semantic planning and executable UAV swarm behavior by linking semantic decisions to governed skills, organizing role-conditioned local agents, and refining skill selection online via role-indexed experience.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Collective intelligence for UAV swarms requires coordination through local perception and information exchange. While LLM agents improve natural-language task understanding and high-level planning, applying them to UAV swarms faces three challenges: grounding model decisions in executable capabilities, reconciling global task reasoning with distributed execution, and leveraging mission-specific experience for continual adaptation.

Method: AeroWeaver is an embodied-agent harness that: (1) connects semantic decisions to governed skills, (2) organizes role-conditioned local agents for distributed coordination, and (3) uses role-indexed state-action-reward experience to refine skill selection online.

Result: Experiments and runtime validation show that AeroWeaver maintains valid skill execution under tested conditions and supports body-local multi-UAV operation without a central agent generating joint actions from global context. Reward-guided online updates provide a training-free path for adaptive learning of swarm agents from accumulated execution experience.

Conclusion: AeroWeaver demonstrates a viable embodied-agent approach for UAV swarms, achieving grounded, distributed, and adaptively learned collective behavior without centralized joint-action generation or model retraining. Code is available at https://github.com/Admire-ljb/AeroWeaver.

Abstract: Collective intelligence is a collaborative autonomy paradigm in which multiple agents pursue shared objectives through local perception, information exchange, and coordinated action. UAV swarms embody this paradigm by coordinating multiple vehicles in tasks such as search, inspection, and tracking. Recent advances in large language model (LLM) agents have strengthened natural-language task understanding and high-level planning, providing a flexible semantic interface between mission descriptions and collective behavior. While these advances expand semantic reasoning, applying LLM agents to UAV swarms raises challenges in grounding model decisions in executable capabilities, reconciling global task reasoning with distributed execution, and using mission-specific experience for continual adaptation. To address these challenges, we introduce AeroWeaver, an embodied-agent harness that weaves individual UAV skills into coordinated mission-level behavior. AeroWeaver connects semantic decisions to governed skills, organizes role-conditioned local agents for distributed coordination, and uses role-indexed state-action-reward experience to refine skill selection online. Experiments and runtime validation show that AeroWeaver maintains valid skill execution under tested conditions and supports body-local multi-UAV operation without a central agent generating joint actions from global context, while reward-guided online updates provide a training-free path for adaptive learning swarm agents from accumulated execution experience. Code: https://github.com/Admire-ljb/AeroWeaver.

</details>


### [62] [TRIPROBE: Probing Task Separability Beyond Classification for XAI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18525)
*Amirhossein Sadough,Freek Hens,Aleksa Bokšan,Mohammad Mahdi Dehshibi,Mahyar Shahsavari*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TriProbe is a multi-level probing framework that diagnoses why multi-task learning pipelines succeed or fail by tracing task separability across inputs, learned features, and classifiers using the Maximum Fisher's Discriminant Ratio.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern evaluation of learning pipelines often reduces to downstream accuracy, which leaves unanswered the question of why tasks succeed or fail. This limited evaluation obscures the underlying causes of task failure and provides little guidance for improving data or architectures.

Method: TriProbe decomposes multi-task problems into binary subtasks and applies three complementary probes: a Foundational Probe on input spaces, a Latent Probe on feature representations, and a Final Probe on classifier outputs. It uses Maximum Fisher's Discriminant Ratio as a principled separability metric to identify bottlenecks and affected task pairs.

Result: Experiments on the Roshambo sEMG benchmark show that TriProbe reveals hidden breakdowns that downstream accuracy alone would miss.

Conclusion: TriProbe enables explainable diagnosis of task separability and provides actionable guidance for data collection, validation, and architecture design beyond simple accuracy-based evaluation.

Abstract: Modern evaluation of learning pipelines often reduces to downstream accuracy, leaving open the question of why tasks succeed or fail. TriProbe addresses this gap with a multi-level probing framework for explainable diagnosis of task separability. Rather than treating models as black boxes, TriProbe traces how separability evolves across inputs, learned features, and final classifiers. It decomposes multi-task problems into binary subtasks and applies three complementary probes: a Foundational Probe on input spaces, a Latent Probe on feature representations, and a Final Probe on classifier outputs. Using Maximum Fisher's Discriminant Ratio as a principled separability metric, TriProbe identifies bottlenecks and affected task pairs. Experiments on the Roshambo sEMG benchmark show how TriProbe reveals hidden breakdowns, guiding data collection, validation, and architecture design.

</details>


### [63] [Recursive Reasoning or Statistical Extrapolation? In-Context Learning in Multi-Agent Interdependent Decision-Making](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18591)
*Yu Liu,Wenwen Li,Yifan Dou,Guangnan Ye*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper tests whether in-context learning (ICL) in LLM agents reflects genuine strategic reasoning or mere statistical extrapolation, using multi-agent incomplete-information games with recursive belief reasoning and a rational expectations equilibrium (REE) benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ICL allows LLM agents to improve decisions from interaction history, but it is unclear whether this improvement stems from refined internal reasoning or simply from extrapolating statistical patterns in the context. Disentangling these mechanisms is important for understanding the true capabilities and limits of LLM reasoning.

Method: The authors study LLM agents in multi-agent incomplete-information games requiring recursive belief reasoning. They construct a public goods game and manipulate the statistical structure of historical feedback, then evaluate decision quality against a history-independent rational expectations equilibrium (REE) benchmark.

Result: When historical statistical patterns are disrupted, the benefits of longer context largely disappear, degrading decision quality to the no-context baseline. This degradation is sharply amplified by stronger strategic interdependence.

Conclusion: In such strategic environments, ICL behavior is more consistent with statistical extrapolation than with strategic reasoning. The work extends mechanistic study of ICL to strategic multi-agent settings, introduces REE as a diagnostic tool, and offers a reusable framework for probing the boundaries of LLM reasoning in recursive belief tasks.

Abstract: In-context learning (ICL) enables large language model (LLM) agents to improve decisions using interaction history, yet it remains unclear whether such improvement reflects refined internal reasoning or mere extrapolation of statistical patterns. To disentangle these mechanisms, we study LLM agents in multi-agent incomplete-information games that require recursive belief reasoning. By constructing a public goods game and manipulating the statistical structure of historical feedback, we evaluate decision quality against a history-independent rational expectations equilibrium (REE) benchmark. Our experiments reveal that when historical statistical patterns are disrupted, the benefits of longer context largely vanish, degrading decision quality to the no-context baseline in a way sharply amplified by stronger strategic interdependence. These results suggest that, in such strategic environments, ICL behavior is more consistent with statistical extrapolation than with strategic reasoning. Our work extends the mechanistic study of ICL to strategic multi-agent settings, introduces REE as a diagnostic tool for distinguishing reasoning from extrapolation, and provides a reusable framework for probing the boundaries of LLM reasoning in recursive belief tasks.

</details>


### [64] [Reasoning through Evolution: Automatic Meta-path Discovery for LLM-based Fake News Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18597)
*Ziyi Zhou,Xiaoming Zhang,Hui Pang,Yuting Zhang,Tiesunlong Shen,Bingyu Yan,Erik Cambria,Litian Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MAGER is a multi-agent genetic evolution framework that automatically discovers meta-paths optimized for LLM reasoning, compressing propagation graphs into informative subgraphs to enable frozen LLMs to perform structure-aware fake news detection without heavy supervision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Propagation structures are key evidence for fake news detection, but existing methods rely on supervised GNNs that need substantial labeled data and generalize poorly. LLMs have strong reasoning ability yet cannot directly consume raw propagation graphs due to modality mismatch and information overload, making structure-aware reasoning unreliable in zero-shot and few-shot settings.

Method: MAGER uses a multi-agent genetic evolution framework to automatically discover meta-paths optimized for LLM reasoning. These evolved meta-paths compress complex propagation graphs into informative subgraphs, mitigating information overload and modality mismatch. A graph in-context learning strategy retrieves semantically and structurally similar demonstrations to enhance classification and reasoning.

Result: Extensive experiments show MAGER substantially improves frozen LLMs as standalone fake news detectors in data-efficient (zero-shot and few-shot) settings.

Conclusion: Meta-path evolution combined with graph in-context learning effectively bridges propagation graph structures and LLM reasoning, offering a data-efficient, generalizable alternative to supervised GNN-based fake news detection.

Abstract: Propagation structures provide crucial evidence for fake news detection, yet existing approaches primarily rely on supervised GNN-based models, which require substantial labeled data and exhibit limited generalization. Although large language models (LLMs) exhibit strong reasoning capabilities, directly feeding them raw propagation graphs creates a significant modality mismatch and severe information overload, making structure-aware reasoning unreliable in zero-shot and few-shot settings. To bridge this gap, we propose MAGER, a multi-agent genetic evolution framework that automatically discovers meta-paths optimized for LLM reasoning. By compressing complex propagation graphs into informative subgraphs, the evolved meta-paths alleviate both information overload and modality mismatch, enabling frozen LLMs to perform structure-aware veracity reasoning. We further introduce a graph in-context learning strategy that retrieves semantically and structurally similar demonstrations to strengthen classification and reasoning. Extensive experiments show that MAGER substantially improves frozen LLMs as standalone fake news detectors in data-efficient settings. Our code is available at https://github.com/SenticNet/MAGER.

</details>


### [65] [The Uneven Impact of Generative AI on Student Learning: Examining the Roles of Reliance, Evaluation Literacy, and Course Policy in AI-related Courses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18676)
*Lydia Manikonda,Mei Si,Sirajam Munira,Oshani Seneviratne,Kristin Bennett*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Survey of 118 students across 12 AI-related courses identified four GenAI user clusters and showed that benefits and negative impacts of GenAI use are shaped by reliance patterns, evaluation literacy, and instructor policy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: GenAI is transforming student learning, but the roles of course context, cognitive reliance, evaluation literacy, and early reliance in shaping learning experiences remain underexplored.

Method: Survey responses from 118 students across 12 AI-related courses were analyzed using clustering to identify user groups, comparisons across tool versions, tool counts, and instructor policies, and multivariable regression models predicting academic benefit, positive impact, and negative impact.

Result: Four user clusters emerged: high-use/high-benefit, light-use/low-reliance/low-benefit, and two moderate-use groups with differing benefits. Significant differences appeared between free- and premium-version users, single- and multiple-tool users, and students under different instructor policies. Academic benefit was associated with early reliance and academic task support; positive impact with cognitive reliance, academic task support, confidence in GenAI reliability, and instructor policy; negative impact with early reliance and attitudinal change. The early reliance–negative impact association strengthened with higher evaluation literacy. Perceived GenAI-enhanced learning reflected cognitive, performance, and self-efficacy benefits, while stress and diminished critical thinking concerns related to lower perceived benefits.

Conclusion: Institutions need better policies to address emerging inequities so students can benefit from increasingly capable AI systems.

Abstract: Generative artificial intelligence (GenAI) is changing how students learn, yet the roles of course context, cognitive reliance, evaluation literacy, and early reliance remain underexplored. Using survey responses from 118 students across 12 AI-related courses at our institution, we examined differences in GenAI use and perceived learning experiences. We identified four user clusters: high-use students reporting many benefits, light users reporting less reliance and fewer benefits, and two moderate-use groups reporting different levels of benefit. We also found significant differences between free- and premium-version users, single- and multiple-tool users, and students experiencing different instructor policies. In multivariable regression models, academic benefit was associated with early reliance and academic task support; positive impact was associated with cognitive reliance, academic task support, confidence in GenAI reliability, and instructor policy; and negative impact was associated with early reliance and attitudinal change. The association between early reliance and negative impact became stronger as evaluation literacy increased. Finally, perceptions of GenAI-enhanced learning appear to reflect cognitive, performance, and self-efficacy benefits, while concerns about stress and diminished critical thinking are associated with lower perceived learning benefits. These findings suggest that institutions need better policies to address such inequities so that institutions can enable students to benefit from increasingly capable AI systems.

</details>


### [66] [Beyond Truncation: Rethinking LLM Decoding as Ensemble Pruning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18723)
*Dunyao Xue,Chengshuo Du,Zhengbo Wang,Wenlin Dai,Cheng Meng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ME-Decoding frames LLM token selection as ensemble pruning, using a Mahalanobis distance-driven subset optimization to boost semantic diversity while preserving high probabilities, via an adaptive-bandwidth kernel similarity matrix and a near-linear greedy algorithm with approximation guarantees.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing decoding selection strategies rely mainly on scalar probabilities, ignoring geometric semantic relationships among candidates, which causes candidate redundancy. Geometry-aware alternatives typically need complex optimization or directly reweight original token probabilities, incurring large computational overhead or inference instability.

Method: Decoding is formulated as a subset optimization problem with a Mahalanobis distance-driven objective balancing semantic diversity and high probability. Redundant generation paths are dynamically discounted using a token similarity matrix built from an adaptive-bandwidth kernel over token embeddings. An efficient greedy selection algorithm with near-linear complexity in candidate size under early stopping is devised, with theoretical approximation guarantees.

Result: Extensive experiments across diverse reasoning and generation tasks show consistently strong performance, with ME-Decoding acting as a robust, plug-and-play module with negligible inference overhead.

Conclusion: ME-Decoding addresses redundancy and overhead in LLM decoding by combining geometric diversity-aware subset optimization with an efficient, theoretically grounded greedy algorithm, offering a practical plug-and-play decoding enhancement.

Abstract: We introduce Mahalanobis-Ensemble Decoding (ME-Decoding), a novel Large Language Model (LLM) decoding framework that frames candidate token selection as ensemble pruning. Existing selection strategies rely predominantly on scalar probabilities, ignoring geometric semantic relationships and causing candidate redundancy. Meanwhile, current geometry-aware methods often require complex optimization or directly reweighting the original token probabilities, leading to significant computational overhead or inference instability. To address this, we formulate decoding as a subset optimization problem using a Mahalanobis distance-driven objective to enhance semantic diversity while preserving high probabilities. Specifically, we dynamically discount redundant generation paths using a token similarity matrix, constructed via an adaptive-bandwidth kernel over token embeddings. We further devise an efficient greedy selection algorithm with near-linear complexity in the candidate size under early stopping, while establishing its theoretical approximation guarantees. This renders ME-Decoding a robust, plug-and-play module with negligible inference overhead. Extensive experiments across diverse reasoning and generation tasks demonstrate that our method consistently achieves strong performance.

</details>


### [67] [Which LLM is Best for Translating Natural Language Goals to PDDL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18731)
*Tomas Balyo,Lukas Chrpa,G. Michael Youngblood*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper empirically evaluates whether state-of-the-art LLMs can translate informal natural language testing goals from video game testers into well-formed PDDL targets, using a carefully designed prompt template and real-world benchmarks across six models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Expressing planning goals in formal languages like PDDL restricts accessibility to non-experts, creating a gap between human intent and machine execution. The paper investigates whether LLMs can bridge this gap by reliably converting informal natural language goals into well-formed PDDL.

Method: A carefully designed prompt template, refined through iterative experimentation, was used to maximize accuracy and response coherence. Six contemporary LLMs were systematically assessed on correctness, speed, and error tendencies using real-world, domain-specific benchmarks.

Result: All models achieved correctness exceeding 92%. Gemini 2.5 Flash reached the highest accuracy at 96% with the lowest false positives, while GPT-4.1 led in response speed. Failures occasionally arose from language ambiguity and limitations in domain representation.

Conclusion: LLMs show significant progress as bridges between natural language objectives and automated planning pipelines, yet critical distinctions in performance and ongoing gaps remain, particularly around ambiguity and domain representation.

Abstract: Bridging the gap between human intent and machine execution remains a challenge in automated planning, where expressing goals in formal languages like PDDL restricts accessibility to non-experts. This paper empirically evaluates whether current Large Language Models (LLMs) can reliably translate natural language testing goals, written in informal language by video game testers, into well-formed PDDL targets suitable for classical planning. We present a carefully designed prompt template, integrating insights from iterative experimentation, aimed at maximizing both accuracy and response coherence from multiple state-of-the-art LLMs. Six contemporary models are systematically assessed on correctness, speed, and error tendencies using real-world, domain-specific benchmarks. All models demonstrate high correctness, exceeding 92\%, with Gemini 2.5 Flash achieving the highest accuracy at 96\% and the lowest incidence of false positives, while GPT-4.1 leads in response speed. Despite these advances, critical distinctions exist in model performance, and occasional failures arise from language ambiguity and limitations in domain representation. Our analysis underscores both the significant progress and ongoing gaps in enabling LLMs to act as robust bridges between natural language objectives and automated planning pipelines.

</details>


### [68] [Clueing up LLMs with Tool-Augmented Deductive Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18736)
*Rebecca Ansell,Autumn Toney-Wails*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper uses a multi-agent LLM implementation of the board game Clue as a testbed for multi-step agentic deductive reasoning, and shows that augmenting agents with a structured possibility matrix—an explicit tool-based memory encoding deductive constraints—improves reasoning quality and task success over a baseline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs still struggle with logically consistent deductive reasoning over extended interactions, especially tasks requiring evidence integration across multiple steps, consistency with prior inferences, and belief updates under new constraints. A strategic, multi-step environment is needed to expose these limitations and evaluate reasoning enhancements.

Method: The authors implement a text-based, multi-agent version of the board game Clue as an evaluation environment. Six LLM-based agents (GPT-4o-mini and Gemini-2.5-Flash, three agents per model family) play turn-based games, establishing baseline performance across repeated games. They then introduce a tool-augmented approach where a structured possibility matrix converts implicit game state from generated reasoning logs into an explicit representation of remaining possibilities, encoding extended-turn memory and deductive constraints and offloading these from the agent. The augmented approach is compared against the baseline.

Result: Baseline performance is established across repeated games for both model families; the tool-augmented approach is evaluated against this baseline to assess its impact on reasoning quality and task success.

Conclusion: Tool augmentation via an explicit possibility matrix supports reasoning quality and task success for autonomous agents in a strategic deductive reasoning environment, indicating that offloading memory and constraint tracking to structured tools can enhance LLM deductive reasoning.

Abstract: Despite recent advances in large language models (LLMs), performing logically consistent deductive reasoning over extended interactions remains challenging. Tasks that require integrating evidence across multiple reasoning steps, maintaining consistency with prior inferences, and updating beliefs under new constraints can surface limitations in current models while providing a useful testbed for evaluating reasoning enhancements. In this paper, we implement a text-based, multi-agent version of the classic board game Clue as an environment to evaluate multi-step, agentic deductive reasoning. In this setting, agents must infer hidden information from a sequence of observations, maintain consistency across turns, and reason over an evolving set of logical constraints. We instantiate six LLM-based agents (GPT-4o-mini and Gemini-2.5-Flash) as players that engage in turn-based gameplay; using three agents per model family, we establish baseline performance across repeated games. We then introduce a tool-augmented approach in which a structured possibility matrix converts implicit game state from generated reasoning logs into an explicit representation of remaining possibilities. The possibility matrix encodes extended-turn memory and deductive constraints, offloading these tasks from the agent. We compare this approach against the baseline to evaluate how tool augmentation supports reasoning quality and task success for autonomous agents in a strategic reasoning environment.

</details>


### [69] [Version- and Scope-Aware Question Answering over Normative Documents: A Deployed System and an End-to-End Evaluation at Production Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18769)
*Liuyin Wang,Shuaipeng Jin,Jiwei Shi,Jensen Hsu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A governed system that resolves document version and scope via explicit rules before generation outperforms a hosted "upload-and-ask" retrieval service on normative documents, scoring 97.7 vs 88.1 on 200 stratified benchmark questions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Answering questions grounded in normative documents requires information beyond any single passage: whether the document is the version in force, whether it applies to the relevant jurisdiction, subject, and date, and traceability of each normative claim to source text. Hosted retrieval services have lowered engineering costs, making "upload the documents and ask" a default approach; the paper questions whether this default suffices.

Method: Evaluation over approximately 73,000 candidate normative documents in a production deployment, using a stratified sample of 200 questions with a gold source document per question. The sampling rule was fixed prior to observing any system outputs or scores. The hosted service is compared against a governed system that resolves version and scope through explicit rules before generation.

Result: The governed system scored 97.7 overall versus 88.1 for the hosted service, a 9.6-point gap computed from unrounded means. The question set, evaluated answer text, scores, and reproduction scripts are public.

Conclusion: Explicit rule-based resolution of version and scope before generation yields measurable accuracy gains over relying on hosted retrieval services. The governed configuration has run as a commercial product since January 2026, serving 1,126 registered users and roughly 100,000 calls per workday by mid-April 2026.

Abstract: Correctly answering a question grounded in normative documents often depends on information outside any single passage: whether the retrieved document is the version currently in force; whether it applies to the jurisdiction, subject (such as an institution or applicant), and date at issue; and whether each normative claim can be traced to its supporting source text. Hosted retrieval services have substantially lowered the engineering cost of building an initial system over such corpora, making "upload the documents and ask" a common default. We evaluate this default on approximately 73,000 candidate normative documents supplied to a production deployment. The evaluation uses a stratified sample of 200 questions from our published benchmark, with a gold source document for every question; the released sampling rule reads no system outputs or scores. We compare the hosted service with a governed system that resolves version and scope through explicit rules before generation. The governed system scored 97.7 overall, while the hosted service scored 88.1, a gap of 9.6 points computed from unrounded means. The question set, the answer text evaluated for both systems, the scores, and the scripts used to reproduce the reported benchmark statistics are public. The governed configuration has operated as a commercial product since January 2026 and serves 1,126 registered users; named customer organizations include Zhipu AI and Lecheng Health. By mid-April 2026, it had reached roughly 100,000 calls per workday.

</details>


### [70] [CERA-MoA: Co-Evolving Routing Mechanisms with Continually Learning LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18779)
*Jiaxuan Jiang,Liyuan He,Zhixuan Fang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CERA-MoA is an iterative reinforcement learning framework that co-evolves a dynamic router and independently learning agent policies, using a predictive familiarity estimator and cumulative-threshold adaptive routing to balance performance and efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Mixture-of-Agents paradigms separate query routing from agent fine-tuning, which creates two limitations: routing strategies cannot adapt to agents' evolving capabilities during post-training, and agents cannot achieve synergistic data-driven specialization.

Method: The framework iteratively co-evolves a dynamic router with independent agent policies via reinforcement learning. A predictive familiarity estimator uses mid-layer hidden states to assess semantic competence among agents, avoiding full-rollout overhead. Based on these scores, a cumulative-threshold adaptive routing mechanism activates a minimal tailored agent subset. Targeted training samples are proactively allocated to agents according to their evolving competence to encourage capability differentiation.

Result: Extensive experiments across various domains show that CERA-MoA outperforms state-of-the-art static-agent routing and fixed-workflow fine-tuning baselines.

Conclusion: Co-evolving routing and agent policies resolves the disconnect in prior MoA systems, enabling adaptive, data-driven agent specialization while balancing task performance and efficiency.

Abstract: Current Mixture-of-Agents (MoA) paradigms generally treat query routing and agent fine-tuning as separate processes, limiting their ability to respond to evolving agent capabilities. This disconnect prevents routing strategies from adapting to evolving agent capabilities during post-training and prevents agents from achieving synergistic data-driven specialization. To resolve this, we introduce CERA-MoA (Co-Evolving Router with continually learning Agents for Mixture-of-Agents), an iterative reinforcement learning framework where the dynamic router and independent agent policies co-evolve. We design a predictive familiarity estimator that leverages mid-layer hidden states to evaluate semantic competence among agents, avoiding the overhead of full rollouts. Based on these familiarity scores, a cumulative-threshold adaptive routing mechanism dynamically activates a tailored minimal agent subset, achieving a trade-off between task performance and efficiency. By proactively allocating targeted training samples to agents based on their evolving competence, CERA-MoA promotes capability differentiation. Extensive experiments across various domains demonstrate that CERA-MoA outperforms state-of-the-art static-agent routing and fix-workflow fine-tuning baselines.

</details>


### [71] [Compositional Policy Violations: When Step-Level Compliance Fails In Agentic AI Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18820)
*Ashwini Kurady,Sri Sai Charith Grandhi,Rajesh Gupta,Sumit Mamoria*

Main category: cs.AI
