TL;DR: Step-scoped governance of agentic workflows cannot detect Compositional Policy Violations (CPVs), where each step passes its own check while the composed execution violates org-level policy; a provenance-aware trace-level runtime architecture is proposed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Organizations deploy agentic workflows in regulated settings, but their governance is almost entirely step-scoped (input-output classifiers, per-turn rails, span-level evaluators). The policies that actually matter—referral thresholds, authority limits, review requirements—are properties of the whole execution, not of any single step. This mismatch creates a structural blind spot: no increase in step-scoped monitor accuracy can close it, because a predicate over one step cannot evaluate a property that step does not determine.

Method: The paper formalizes CPVs as the failure of step-level compliance to compose, and presents a taxonomy of four types: Authority Creep, Threshold Laundering, Cumulative Sum Violation, and Context Collapse. It shows that the correct repair for each class is determined by where the guarded quantity mutates. It then introduces a provenance-aware runtime architecture that evaluates policies over complete execution traces, recomputing guarded quantities from raw provenance instead of the pipeline's derived representation.

Result: The analysis establishes that step-scoped monitoring is provably insufficient for CPV detection, and that the appropriate remediation differs by CPV class according to the mutation locus of the guarded quantity. The trace-level, provenance-based architecture is put forward as the mechanism that makes whole-execution policy evaluation feasible.

Conclusion: Effective governance of agentic workflows requires shifting from step-scoped to execution-scoped policy evaluation. Because CPVs arise from composition rather than individual steps, detection and repair must operate on complete traces and reconstruct guarded quantities from raw provenance.

Abstract: Agentic workflows now make consequential decisions in regulated settings, and the governance placed around them is almost entirely step-scoped: input-output classifiers, per turn rails, and span-level evaluators. The policies organizations actually hold, such as referral thresholds, authority limits, and review requirements, are properties of the whole execution rather than of any one step. This mismatch admits a failure mode we call a Compositional Policy Violation (CPV): every individual step passes its own check while the composed execution violates the governing policy. A predicate over a single step cannot evaluate a property that step does not determine, so no improvement in the accuracy of the step-scoped monitors detects this class. We define CPVs as the failure of step-level compliance to compose, and present a taxonomy of four types: Authority Creep, Threshold Laundering, Cumulative Sum Violation, and Context Collapse. We show that the correct repair for each class is dictated by where the guarded quantity mutates. We then introduce a provenance-aware runtime architecture that evaluates policies over complete execution traces, recomputing guarded quantities from raw provenance rather than the pipeline's derived representation.

</details>


### [72] [Infinite-Parameter LLMs: Generating and Adapting Weights from Live Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18842)
*Jinli Hu,Ross M. Clarke,Yichuan Zhang,José Miguel Hernández-Lobato*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes the Infinite-Parameter LLM, an architecture that learns from live interaction by writing run-time data into weights via a hypernetwork-based low-rank modulation with online Bayesian belief updates, rather than relying on prompt-based in-context learning or retrieval.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scaling laws and Mixture-of-Experts architectures achieve strong results but are built on static pretraining data. A deployed model encounters valuable data at run time (user-supplied facts, corrections) that it cannot learn from because its weights are frozen. Such knowledge is instead placed in the prompt via retrieval or instruction and discarded after each request, wasting compute, occupying the context window, and failing to persist across turns.

Method: The authors propose the Infinite-Parameter LLM, inspired by MoE. A compact hypernetwork converts run-time data into a low-rank modulation of a shared base network, so feed-forward weights are generated from live data rather than stored in a fixed bank. Unlike prior weight generators that read context once and freeze, they maintain a Bayesian belief over the generator's latent code and update it online, re-deriving the effective weight from this evolving belief as the session proceeds. The stored footprint stays fixed while compilable weights are effectively infinite.

Result: The architecture is claimed to amortize compute for run-time knowledge, free the context window, persist learned knowledge across turns, and generalise better than in-context use. The authors specify an evaluation protocol designed to test these claims directly against in-context learning and retrieval.

Conclusion: Carrying run-time knowledge and behaviour in weights rather than in the prompt offers advantages in compute, context efficiency, persistence, and generalisation. The paper contributes an architecture design and an evaluation protocol, framing weight-based live learning as an alternative to prompt-based mechanisms.

Abstract: The scaling laws hold that a language model grows more capable with more parameters and more training data, and Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures have ridden these laws to remarkable results, activating only a fraction of an enormous stored parameter bank for each token. That success is built on static pretraining data. A deployed model faces a different world, where much of the data that would make it more useful is not in its training set but in the live interaction it is currently handling, such as the facts a user supplies or the corrections they give. A conventional model cannot learn from this data, because its weights are frozen after training. Instead, the knowledge and behaviour supplied at run time are placed in the prompt, by retrieval or instruction, and re-read on every request only to be discarded once the request ends. We ask how an architecture could learn from live interaction by writing it into its weights. Taking inspiration from MoE, we propose the \textbf{Infinite-Parameter LLM}. A compact hypernetwork turns the data given at run time into a low-rank modulation of a shared base network, so the feed-forward weights are generated from live data rather than stored in a fixed bank. Where prior weight generators read the context once and freeze, we carry a Bayesian belief over the generator's latent code and update it online, so the effective weight is re-derived from that evolving belief as the session proceeds rather than fixed after one read. The stored footprint stays fixed, yet the weights the model can compile are effectively infinite. For the knowledge and behaviour supplied at run time, carrying them in the weights rather than the prompt is amortized in compute, frees the context window, persists across turns, and can generalise better than in-context use. We specify an evaluation protocol that tests exactly this against in-context learning and retrieval.

</details>


### [73] [Suppressed, Not Erased: A Representational Trace of Edited Facts Survives Even Weight-Free Knowledge Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18985)
*Priyansh Srivastava,Romit Chatterjee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Benchmarks for knowledge editing only certify local behavior, not how much of the original fact survives internally. Using a linear trace probe on GPT-2-XL across three editors and 50 CounterFact edits, the original object stays linearly decodable well above chance after successful edits—even for GRACE, which changes no base weights. This suggests editing suppresses rather than erases the original association in representational space.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing knowledge-editing benchmarks measure behavioral success on near-edit prompts but ignore whether the original fact remains encoded inside the model. The paper aims to characterize this residual knowledge directly, distinguishing genuine erasure from mere suppression of the original association.

Method: A linear trace probe trained on hidden states to test whether the original object is still recoverable after editing. Experiments on GPT-2-XL with 50 CounterFact edits using three mechanistically distinct editors: ROME, constrained fine-tuning, and the memory-based GRACE. GRACE is key because it alters zero base-model weights, isolating residual traces from incomplete weight updates. A relearning-savings instrument was also tested.

Result: Original-object probe accuracy well above the 0.50 chance level: 0.96 for ROME, 0.86 for constrained fine-tuning, and 0.79 for GRACE, despite all edits reaching 100% generation-based success. The GRACE finding shows residual knowledge persists without any base-weight change. The relearning-savings instrument did not behave reliably and is reported as a negative methodological result.

Conclusion: Behaviorally successful knowledge editing suppresses rather than erases the original association in representational space, since the original object remains linearly decodable from hidden states even when no base weights change. Local correctness benchmarks are therefore insufficient as evidence of knowledge removal. Code and data are released.

Abstract: Knowledge-editing benchmarks certify local correctness, whether an edited model produces the new fact on near-edit prompts but not how much of the original fact remains decodable inside the model. We study residual knowledge directly with a linear trace probe: after editing a fact, we ask whether the original object is still recoverable from the model's hidden states. On GPT-2-XL, across three mechanistically distinct editors applied to 50 CounterFact edits, the original object remains linearly decodable well above chance after a successful edit (probe accuracy 0.96 for ROME, 0.86 for constrained fine-tuning, and 0.79 for the memory-based editor GRACE, against a chance level of 0.50; all edits reach 100% generation-based success). The GRACE result is the most informative: GRACE changes zero base-model weights, overriding the fact through an external memory, yet the original object is still decodable from the underlying network, so the residual trace cannot be attributed to an incomplete weight update. We read this as evidence that editing, even when behaviorally successful, suppresses rather than erases the original association in representational space. We also report a relearning-savings instrument that did not behave reliably in our setting and discuss why; we treat it as a negative methodological result rather than evidence. Code and data are released.

</details>


### [74] [Function Lives Where Variance Doesn't: Task-Weighted Charts of a Language Model's Computation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18989)
*Alexandre Quemy*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper argues that the "intrinsic dimensionality" of a language model is only well-defined relative to a chosen functional, and introduces task-weighted charts — low-dimensional coordinate systems fit to a functional under its own metric — showing that next-token prediction requires 70–90% of residual-stream width, uncorrelated with activation variance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Questions like "how many dimensions does a language model's computation use?" are ill-posed without specifying a functional of the representation. Existing variance-based dimensionality estimates may not reflect the dimensions that actually carry the model's function.

Method: Task-weighted charts: fit low-dimensional coordinate systems against a chosen functional of the representation, under the functional's own metric, which reduces distillation to plain least squares. Applied across six models from three families (70M–7B parameters).

Result: Next-token prediction needs 70–90% of the residual stream's width to stay within 5% of intact perplexity, a width consumed by the rare tail of language; the variance profile predicts none of this (two directions carry 90% of GPT-2's activation variance but almost none of its function). Dimension is per-functional: model uncertainty reads from six coordinates while the full predictive distribution needs hundreds, and it grows with depth. Charts trained under the functional's metric outperform variance-based or optimal linear compression when few dimensions are kept.

Conclusion: Model dimensionality is functional-relative rather than absolute. Functionally weighted charts reveal a dissociation between activation variance and functional importance, and provide a practically superior low-dimensional distillation/compression scheme.

Abstract: How many dimensions does a language model's computation actually use? The question is ill-posed until one names a functional. Task-weighted charts make it well-posed: low-dimensional coordinate systems fit against a chosen functional of the representation, under the functional's own metric, turning distillation into plain least squares. Across six models from three families, spanning 70m to 7B parameters, next-token prediction needs 70--90% of the residual stream's width to stay within 5% of intact perplexity, a width consumed by the rare tail of language, and the variance profile predicts none of it: two directions carry 90% of GPT-2's activation variance and almost none of its function. Dimension is per-functional: the model's own uncertainty reads from six coordinates where the full predictive distribution needs hundreds; and it grows with depth. The dissociation is exploitable: when only a few dimensions can be kept, charts trained under the functional's metric preserve the model's predictions better than variance-based or optimal linear compression.

</details>


### [75] [Lost in Perception: Isolating Perceptual and Reasoning Failures in Multimodal Physics and Geometry Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18991)
*Raj Jaiswal,Sree Krishna Uppalapati,Dhruvkumar Patel,Ria Khatoniar,Tanuja Ganu,Rajiv Ratn Shah*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A five-task diagnostic experiment across physics and geometry benchmarks isolates multimodal LLM failures into perception, reasoning, or both, showing that diagram misperception degrades accuracy and that downstream reasoning errors are domain-specific.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most multimodal LLM evaluations on scientific reasoning treat perception and reasoning as a single measurable process, conflating where failures actually originate.

Method: A five-task diagnostic experiment across physics and geometry benchmarks that separates perception failures from reasoning failures, comparing accuracy across raw images versus human-authored captions and applying corrected captions to test recovery.

Result: Incorrect diagram interpretation harms performance even on problems models solve correctly from text alone; accuracy generally improves from raw images to human-authored captions; recovery under corrected captions is high for some models, distinguishing perception-blocked failures from genuine reasoning bottlenecks. Reasoning errors following perception failures are domain-dependent: physics failures become calculation errors, geometry failures become conceptual misapplication. As a side discussion, InternS1-mini underperforms the weakest experimental model on every task, with reasoning traces frequently truncating before completion.

Conclusion: Perception and reasoning should be evaluated separately in multimodal scientific benchmarks, since domain-specific reasoning failures are masked when the two are measured jointly.

Abstract: Multimodal LLMs report strong performance on scientific reasoning benchmarks, yet most treat perception and reasoning as a single measurable process. We introduce a five-task diagnostic experiment across physics and geometry benchmarks that isolates failures to perception, reasoning, or both. Incorrect diagram interpretation degrades performance even on problems models solve correctly from text alone, and accuracy generally rises from raw images to human-authored captions. Recovery under corrected captions is high for some models, separating perception-blocked failures from genuine reasoning bottlenecks. Which reasoning error follows a perception failure depends on domain: physics failures resolve into calculation errors, geometry into conceptual misapplication. As a discussion beyond our core experiments, InternS1-mini, despite heavy scientific pretraining and thinking capabilities, falls below the weakest model from experiments on every task, with reasoning traces frequently truncating before completion.

</details>


### [76] [Compiled Agency: Frontier General-Purpose Coding Agents Build Winning Game Players from Bare Interaction - from Flappy Bird to StarCraft II and Civilization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18996)
*Joey Xiao,Haonan Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Gauntlet is a develop-freeze-evaluate framework where a general-purpose coding agent receives only a game description, a raw observation/action interface, and an empty policy file; it autonomously engineers a standalone controller that is frozen and scored on held-out instances with zero model calls during play. On an unpublished procedural roguelike, held-out success spans 0-86% with a sharp generational threshold; at full scale, a compiled raw-API controller defeats every fair StarCraft II built-in AI plus two cheating variants, and single-session programs win complete games of Civilization (Freeciv) by total conquest on held-out seeds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents have repeatedly failed to convert game knowledge into competent play even when researchers build scaffolding around the model (perception, memory, skill libraries, planners, executable-policy scaffolds). Rapid progress in coding agents raises two sharper questions: can frontier models now win games at all, and can they win them unaided, building the entire player themselves?

Method: A develop-freeze-evaluate framework that ports games from small arcades to full commercial-scale titles under one deliberately bare contract: a general-purpose coding agent gets a game description, a raw observation/action interface, and an empty policy file—no strategy, algorithm, or architecture. In a single autonomous session the agent experiments with the live game and engineers a standalone controller; the result is frozen and scored on held-out instances with zero model calls during play.

Result: On an unpublished procedural roguelike, held-out success spans 0-86% and reveals a sharp generational threshold: every observed session of a newest-generation system outperforms the best session of its predecessor. At full-game scale, a compiled raw-API controller defeats every fair StarCraft II built-in AI and two cheating variants, and single-session programs win complete games of Civilization (Freeciv) by total conquest on held-out seeds—at modest rates against novice AI, but a first for this genre standalone, without per-turn model calls and a hand-crafted tactical layer.

Conclusion: Frontier coding agents begin to track long-horizon strategy. The frozen programs are inspectable. The authors call this capability compiled agency: development experience compiled into a persistent executable agent whose architecture is built by the model.

Abstract: LLM agents have repeatedly struggled to convert knowledge of a game into competent play, even when researchers build the agent around the model - supplying perception, memory, skill libraries, planners, or executable-policy scaffolds. Rapid progress in coding agents raises two sharper questions: can frontier models now win games at all, and can they win them unaided, building the entire player themselves? We introduce Gauntlet, a develop-freeze-evaluate framework that ports games from small arcades to full commercial-scale titles, behind one deliberately bare contract: a general-purpose coding agent receives a game description, a raw observation/action interface, and an empty policy file - no strategy, no algorithm, no architecture. In a single autonomous session the agent experiments with the live game and engineers a standalone controller; we freeze the result and score it on held-out instances with zero model calls during play. On an unpublished procedural roguelike, held-out success spans 0-86 percent and exposes a sharp generational threshold: every observed session of a newest-generation system outperforms the best session of its predecessor. At full-game scale, a compiled raw-API controller defeats every fair StarCraft II built-in AI and two cheating variants, and single-session programs win complete games of Civilization (Freeciv) by total conquest on held-out seeds. Though at modest rates against novice AI, this is a first: no prior language-agent system had won full games of this genre standalone, without per-turn model calls and a hand-crafted tactical layer. Frontier coding agents begin to track long-horizon strategy. The frozen programs are inspectable. We call this capability compiled agency: development experience compiled into a persistent executable agent whose architecture is built by the model.

</details>


### [77] [MUSE: Benchmarking Large Vision-Language Models on Multi-Modal Understanding in Situated Education](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19088)
*Luyao Zhu,Xun Wei Yee,Wei Li,Mun Thye Mak,Wee Siong Ng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MUSE is a new benchmark for evaluating large vision-language models on artistic image understanding in situated educational contexts, decoupling annotation from question generation and covering twelve tasks across perception, semantics, affect, culture, and reasoning with a focus on Southeast Asian and Western art.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LVLMs have progressed in multi-modal understanding, but their educational capabilities—especially in AI-assisted language learning requiring interpretation of artistic imagery's semantic, affective, and cultural content—remain underevaluated. Existing benchmarks focus on real-world images or domain-specific educational reasoning, with limited coverage of artistic educational content.

Method: The authors introduce MUSE, a benchmark that decouples image annotation from question generation to enable diverse tasks with controllable difficulty and reduced annotation effort. MUSE includes twelve tasks spanning visual perception, semantic and affective interpretation, culture understanding, and compositional reasoning, using curated artistic images centered on Singaporean and Southeast Asian multicultural contexts alongside Western art traditions, covering multiple themes and difficulty levels.

Result: Evaluation of open-source and proprietary models reveals substantial disparities across capability dimensions, particularly in affective interpretation and compositional reasoning. The analysis also identifies common failure modes and key challenges for developing trustworthy multi-modal models for education.

Conclusion: MUSE aims to serve as a standardized benchmark for advancing multi-modal understanding in situated educational applications, highlighting gaps in current LVLMs and guiding trustworthy educational model development.

Abstract: Large vision-language models have achieved remarkable progress in multi-modal understanding, yet their capabilities in educational settings remain insufficiently evaluated. In AI-assisted language learning, models must interpret artistic imagery, understand its semantic, affective, and cultural content, and reason about visual context to support meaningful interaction. However, existing benchmarks primarily focus on real-world images or domain-specific educational reasoning, providing limited coverage of artistic educational content. To address this gap, we introduce MUSE, a benchmark for evaluating large vision-language models on artistic image understanding in situated educational applications. MUSE decouples image annotation from question generation, enabling diverse tasks with controllable difficulty while reducing annotation effort. It comprises twelve tasks spanning visual perception, semantic and affective interpretation, culture understanding, and compositional reasoning, together with diverse artistic images deliberately curated to center Singaporean and Southeast Asian multicultural contexts alongside Western art traditions, covering multiple themes and difficulty levels. Evaluation of open-source and proprietary models reveals substantial disparities across capability dimensions, particularly in affective interpretation and compositional reasoning. Our analysis further identifies common failure modes and key challenges for developing trustworthy multi-modal models for education. We hope MUSE will serve as a standardized benchmark for advancing multi-modal understanding in situated educational applications.

</details>


### [78] [Flag Game: A Toy Model for Mechanistic Swarm Interpretability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19124)
*Elizabeth Pavlova,Hidenori Tanaka*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces the Flag Game, a toy model of collective belief formation among bounded AI agents, revealing collective belief collapse at small populations that transitions to polarization at large populations, and dissects its mechanisms via social circuit attribution and statistical mechanics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Emergent coordinated behaviors of AI agents pose critical safety risks, driven by rapid formation and spread of beliefs. Mechanistic understanding is needed for collective alignment, but a framework for studying how individual agent properties and communication produce emergent collective behavior is lacking.

Method: The authors introduce the Flag Game: a hidden country flag defines ground truth, each bounded agent observes only a private crop but can exchange beliefs and weigh social evidence from peers. They analyze it with social circuit attribution to predict which agent and view matter most to collective dynamics, verified by causal agent-patching interventions, and develop a statistical mechanical theory for larger populations.

Result: The Flag Game reproduces non-monotonic scaling of performance with population size, accuracy gains from social-awareness prompting and team diversity, and strong effects of organizational structure. Collective belief collapse at small population sizes turns into collective belief polarization as population grows, causing performance decline at large sizes while creating diversity in collective beliefs. Causal intervention efficacy decreases with population growth, but the statistical mechanical theory matches the empirical phase diagram.

Conclusion: The work takes a first step toward mechanistic swarm interpretability—a science of how individual agent properties and their communication give rise to emergent collective behavior.

Abstract: Emergent coordinated behaviors of AI agents are starting to present critical safety risks. A key phenomenon driving these behaviors is the rapid formation and spread of beliefs about the world, and mechanistic understanding is crucial for collective alignment. To this end, we introduce the Flag Game, a toy model for studying the mechanisms of collective belief formation. Concretely, a hidden country flag defines the ground truth, and each bounded agent directly observes only a private crop but can exchange beliefs and weigh social evidence from peers. Despite its simplicity, the Flag Game reproduces rich collective phenomenology: non-monotonic scaling of performance with population size, accuracy gains from social-awareness prompting and team diversity, and strong effects of organizational structure. In particular, we identify that collective belief collapse at small population sizes turns into collective belief polarization as the population grows. This polarization causes the performance decline at large population sizes, but creates diversity in collective beliefs. Finally, we dissect the mechanisms underlying collective belief collapse and polarization with two complementary approaches. We first introduce social circuit attribution, a technique to predict which agent, and what view, matters most to collective dynamics, and verify its predictions by causal interventions on agents, tracing how agent patching changes collective outcomes. However, the efficacy of causal interventions on agents decreases as the population grows. We therefore develop a statistical mechanical theory for larger populations and verify that it matches the empirical phase diagram. Together, these results take a first step toward mechanistic swarm interpretability, a science of how the properties of individual agents and their communication give rise to emergent collective behavior.

</details>


### [79] [Cognitive Extensions for Dual-Process Language Agents: Memory and Self-Reflection in Interactive Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19128)
*João Meneses dos Santos,Arlindo L. Oliveira*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper augments the dual-process SwiftSage agent with an Adaptive Memory Module and a Self-Reflection Module, improving long-horizon interactive task performance on ScienceWorld, with execution-time control identified as the dominant bottleneck.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Language agents remain brittle in interactive environments, where success demands long-horizon state tracking, valid action execution, and recovery from failed steps. The authors aim to address these weaknesses by adding modular cognitive extensions to an existing dual-process agent.

Method: The authors extend SwiftSage, a dual-process agent pairing a fast action proposer with a slower planner, via two feature-flagged modular extensions: an Adaptive Memory Module (AMM) for salience-gated episodic storage and trigger-driven retrieval, and a Self-Reflection Module (SRM) for bounded execution-time validation and corrective intervention. Both are implemented over the same execution substrate, enabling controlled ablations across four configurations: baseline, baseline+AMM, baseline+SRM, and the full system.

Result: The full system attains the best mean final score (64.62), success rate (43.17%), and successful-step efficiency (19.33 steps). SRM proves to be the strongest standalone contributor.

Conclusion: Execution-time control is the dominant bottleneck in this setting, whereas episodic memory yields the greatest benefit once the runtime loop has been stabilized.

Abstract: Language agents remain brittle in interactive environments, where success requires long-horizon state tracking, valid action execution, and recovery from failed steps. We extend SwiftSage, a dual-process agent that combines a fast action proposer with a slower planner, using two modular cognitive extensions: an Adaptive Memory Module (AMM) for salience-gated episodic storage and trigger-driven retrieval, and a Self-Reflection Module (SRM) for bounded execution-time validation and corrective intervention. Both modules are implemented as feature-flagged extensions over the same execution substrate, enabling controlled ablations on ScienceWorld. Across four configurations---baseline, baseline+AMM, baseline+SRM, and the full system---the full system achieves the best mean final score (64.62), success rate (43.17%), and successful-step efficiency (19.33 steps), while SRM is the strongest standalone contributor. The results suggest that execution-time control is the dominant bottleneck in this setting, while episodic memory becomes most useful once the runtime loop is stabilized.

</details>


<div id='cs.RO'></div>

# cs.RO [[Back]](#toc)

### [80] [Human-Centric Grasp State Assessment: Toward Transferring Subjective Evaluation to Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17540)
*Ryohei Kobayashi,Kosei Isomoto,Yuga Yano,Yuichiro Tanaka,Hakaru Tamukoh*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper proposes a framework that transfers tacit human subjective grasping criteria to robots for deformable objects, combining a VLM-based semi-automated supervisor generator with a lightweight grasp state predictor, enabling online adaptation from time-series tactile and force data using minimal human annotations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robotic grasping of deformable objects needs to match qualitative human expectations, but a semantic gap separates qualitative human judgments from quantitative robotic measurements. Conventional deep learning methods to bridge this gap require prohibitive amounts of manually annotated data for every new object.

Method: The framework integrates a Vision-Language Model (VLM)-based semi-automated supervisor generator with a lightweight grasp state predictor. A minimal set of human-annotated trials serves as contextual anchors to propagate subjective criteria to unannotated data. The prediction model then performs rapid online adaptation by sequentially estimating grasp state from time-series tactile and grasping force measurements.

Result: Experiments on three representative deformable objects and a human evaluation study with 25 participants demonstrate the feasibility of adjusting grasping force according to human-perceived grasp appropriateness in the evaluated task setting.

Conclusion: The proposed framework can transfer tacit human subjective criteria to robotic systems for appropriate grasping of deformable objects, adjusting grasping force to align with human-perceived grasp appropriateness while requiring only minimal human annotation.

Abstract: We propose a framework that transfers tacit human subjective criteria to robotic systems for the appropriate grasping of deformable objects. Achieving such behavior is challenging because a semantic gap exists between qualitative human expectations and quantitative robotic measurements. Conventional deep learning approaches for bridging this gap also require prohibitive amounts of manually annotated data for each newly encountered object. To address these challenges, our framework integrates a Vision-Language Model (VLM)-based semi-automated supervisor generator with a lightweight grasp state predictor, using a minimal set of human-annotated trials as contextual anchors to propagate subjective criteria to unannotated data. The prediction model then enables rapid online adaptation by sequentially estimating the grasp state from time-series tactile and grasping force measurements. Through experiments on three representative deformable objects and a human evaluation study with 25 participants, we demonstrate the feasibility of the proposed framework for adjusting grasping force according to human-perceived grasp appropriateness in the evaluated task setting.

</details>


### [81] [Real-Time Service Robot Replanning via Simple Button Interaction for Improved Task Success and User Experience](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17541)
*Ryo Terashima,Yuga Yano,Koshun Arimura,Hakaru Tamukoh*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper proposes a real-time, single-button human feedback mechanism that lets service robots detect failures and replan actions, and shows it improves both task success and user experience.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Service robots operating in real-world environments inevitably encounter unexpected instructions and cannot detect all failures or exceptions during a task. Without a way to correct such failures, robots may behave in ways that confuse or worry users.

Method: The authors introduce a real-time feedback function: when a robot acts incorrectly, users press a single button on a tablet to send intuitive feedback. The robot uses this signal to recognize its failure and replan appropriate actions to complete the task. Experiments compared conditions with and without the feedback function, using questionnaire responses rated on a five-point Likert scale.

Result: Adding the feedback function raised the score for "Did you feel that the robot's behavior was unexpected?" by 0.5 points and for "Did you feel anxious about the robot's behavior at times?" by 0.9 points.

Conclusion: The hypothesis that "simple interactions do not cause a negative user experience" is supported. The real-time feedback function can simultaneously improve task success and the user experience.

Abstract: Service robots must respond to unexpected instructions in real-world environments. However, robots cannot detect all failures and exceptions during a task. To address these issues, we propose a real-time feedback function that enables robots to modify their behavior based on human feedback. In this system, users can intuitively send feedback to the robot by pressing a single button on a tablet when the robot fails to act correctly. Robots use this feedback to consider their failures and replan appropriate actions to complete the task. We conducted experiments with and without the feedback function to verify the following hypothesis: "Simple interactions do not cause a negative user experience." All questionnaire responses are evaluated on a five-point Likert scale. After adding the feedback function, the response score for the question "Did you feel that the robot's behavior was unexpected?" improved by 0.5 points, and that for "Did you feel anxious about the robot's behavior at times?" improved by 0.9 points. These results support the study's hypothesis and indicate that incorporating this real-time feedback function can simultaneously improve task success and the user experience.

</details>


### [82] [Predictive Varanus: Combining CSP Conformance Monitoring with Predictive LTL Runtime Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17625)
*Angelo Ferrando,Matt Luckcuck,Pedro Ribeiro*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: PREDICTIVE VARANUS is a two-stage runtime verification pipeline that combines CSP-based conformance checking (VARANUS) with predictive LTL monitoring, using a single CSP model both as a conformance gate and, via translation to a Buchi automaton, as a constraint on explored futures—yielding earlier verdicts than standard runtime monitoring.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Runtime verification fits autonomous and robotic systems since it inspects actual execution behaviour, but it is typically reactive: violations are detected only after the bad event has occurred, which is too late in domains where failures are costly or unsafe.

Method: A two-stage verification pipeline. Stage one uses a CSP model as a conformance gate over the observed event trace; out-of-model behaviour is rejected immediately. The same CSP model is then translated into a Buchi automaton that constrains the futures explored by a predictive LTL monitor, so that model-consistent prefixes are classified as already guaranteeing satisfaction, already forcing violation, or still inconclusive for the monitored temporal property. The combined monitor is formalised and its implementation explained.

Result: The approach is illustrated on a robotic rover for nuclear-store inspection. The case study shows that combining CSP validation with predictive LTL delivers earlier verdicts than standard runtime monitoring, while reusing an existing design-time CSP model.

Conclusion: Coupling CSP-based conformance checking with predictive LTL monitoring enables anticipation of verdicts beyond the reactive limits of conventional runtime verification, and does so at low specification cost by repurposing an already available design-time CSP model.

Abstract: Runtime Verification is well suited to autonomous and robotic systems because it checks the behaviour that is actually observed during execution. Its main limitation, however, is that it is usually reactive: the monitor detects a violation only after the system has already performed a bad event. This can be too late in domains where failures are costly or unsafe. In this paper we present PREDICTIVE VARANUS, a two-stage verification pipeline that combines VARANUS, a runtime verifier that uses models written in the process algebra Communicating Sequential Processes (CSP), with predictive runtime verification for LTL. A CSP model is first used as a conformance gate over the observed event trace; the same model is then translated into a Buchi automaton that constrains the futures explored by a predictive LTL monitor. In this way, out-of-model behaviour is rejected immediately, while model-consistent prefixes can be classified as already guaranteeing satisfaction, already forcing violation, or still being inconclusive for the monitored temporal property. We formalise the combined monitor, explain its implementation, and illustrate the approach on a robotic rover for nuclear-store inspection. The case study shows how the combination of CSP validation and predictive LTL can provide earlier verdicts than standard runtime monitoring while reusing an existing design-time CSP model.

</details>


### [83] [Flexible-body Modeling, Kinematic Identification, and Assembly Accuracy of Overconstrained Spatial Linkages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17627)
*Daniel Huczala,Michael Pieber,Johannes Gerstmayr,Andreas Mair,Frederik Schulte,Silvia Glas,Tomas Postulka,Ales Vysocky,Martin Pfurner*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper presents a flexible multibody modeling framework based on the floating frame of reference formulation to analyze overconstrained rational single-loop linkages, validated against 3D-printed PLA prototypes of a Bennett four-bar mechanism. It introduces a kinematic parameter identification method for ill-conditioned Jacobians and shows that structural compliance gives these mechanisms a self-assembling tendency, enabling functional realizations from low-cost, inaccurate materials.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Overconstrained rational single-loop linkages are efficient, compact, and low-cost custom mechanisms, but their industrial deployment is limited. Rigid body formulations fail in simulations due to redundant constraints, and the assembly of theoretically non-assemblable variants motivated the need for a proper modeling approach.

Method: A flexible multibody modeling framework based on the floating frame of reference formulation, delivering an overall accuracy analysis of assembled linkage prototypes. Validation is performed against 3D-printed PLA prototypes of a Bennett four-bar mechanism, including variants with intentional joint-axis misalignment. A supplementary kinematic parameter identification methodology is proposed for mechanisms with ill-conditioned Jacobians.

Result: Experimental and simulation results reveal that overconstrained mechanisms exhibit a self-assembling tendency: structural compliance drives assembly toward the ideal geometric configuration, distributing constraint stress throughout the structure. Qualitative demonstrations using cardboard tubes and bamboo sticks confirm that functional mechanisms can be realized from low-cost, unconventional materials with limited manufacturing accuracy.

Conclusion: The proposed modeling pipeline is fully algorithmic and enables future design optimization.

Abstract: Overconstrained rational single-loop linkages are efficient, compact, and low-cost custom mechanisms, yet their deployment in industrial settings is limited. In simulations, rigid body formulations fail due to redundant constraints. This study presents a flexible multibody modeling framework based on the floating frame of reference formulation, and delivers an overall accuracy analysis of assembled linkages prototypes. The approach is validated against 3D-printed PLA prototypes of a Bennett four-bar mechanism, including variants with intentional joint-axis misalignment, which theoretically, from the rigid body point of view, cannot be assembled. A supplementary contribution is delivered in the form of a kinematic parameter identification methodology suited for this type of mechanism with ill-conditioned Jacobian. The experimental and simulation results are compared and reveal that these overconstrained mechanisms exhibit a self-assembling tendency -- structural compliance drives the assembly toward the ideal geometric configuration, distributing constraint stress throughout the structure. Additional qualitative demonstrations using cardboard tubes and bamboo sticks as link building blocks confirm that functional mechanisms can be realized from low-cost, unconventional materials with limited manufacturing accuracy. The proposed modeling pipeline is fully algorithmic and enables design optimization in the future.

</details>


### [84] [LEAP: Learning Emergent Active Perception for Quadruped Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17628)
*Ü. Bora Gökbakan,Stéphane Caron,Philippe Souères*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: LEAP learns emergent active perception for goal-oriented navigation over hazardous terrains without any hand-designed proxy objectives, using a gaze-invariant egocentric belief map and a terrain curriculum where task pressure alone drives gaze control. It reaches 92.7% success versus 74.2% (scripted) and 34.5% (passive) perception, within 4.6 points of a privileged oracle, and its navigation policies transfer unchanged to steer quadrupedal locomotion in physics simulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Active perception lets agents choose viewpoints to reduce environmental uncertainty, but learned systems typically rely on hand-designed proxy objectives (e.g., coverage or curiosity bonuses) that can conflict with the task. This work aims to achieve active perception emergently, without augmenting or distorting the task objective.

Method: The problem is formulated as goal-oriented navigation over hazardous terrains with goals discoverable only visually. An architecture for active-perception navigation policies is proposed, built on a gaze-invariant representation that integrates depth images into egocentric belief maps. Policies are trained on a terrain curriculum, where task pressure alone induces gaze control without auxiliary rewards.

Result: In held-out evaluation scenarios, LEAP achieves a 92.7% success rate, versus 74.2% for scripted perception and 34.5% for passive perception, and comes within 4.6 points of a privileged oracle. Unchanged LEAP navigation policies can also be directly applied to steering quadrupedal locomotion policies in physics simulation.

Conclusion: Active perception can emerge purely from task pressure when combined with a gaze-invariant egocentric representation and an appropriate terrain curriculum, yielding strong, transferable navigation policies without proxy objectives or task-objective augmentation.

Abstract: Active perception allows autonomous agents to select their viewpoints rather than passively process the viewpoints given to them, enabling them to target where to reduce uncertainty about their environment. Learned systems typically encourage this behavior with hand-designed proxy objectives, such as coverage or curiosity bonuses, that may conflict with the task. In this work, we propose a method to learn emergent active perception (LEAP) without augmentation of the task objective. We formulate the problem of goal-oriented navigation over hazardous terrains with goals that must be discovered visually. We then propose an architecture for navigation policies with active perception, and train them on a terrain curriculum where task pressure alone leads to the emergence of gaze control. Key to this emergence, LEAP works on a gaze-invariant representation that integrates depth images into egocentric belief maps. We validate its performance in held-out evaluation scenarios, where it achieves a 92.7% success rate, compared to 74.2% for scripted or 34.5% for passive perception, and comes within 4.6 points of a privileged oracle. We validate that LEAP navigation policies, unchanged, can be directly applied to steering quadrupedal locomotion policies in physics simulation.

</details>


### [85] [Vision-Language Grounded Task-Context-Aware Imitation Learning for Robotic Disassembly](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17714)
*Jeon Ho Kang,Igal Tamarkin,Ethan Niu,Ian Novales,Satyandra K. Gupta*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes a language-conditioned framework for long-horizon robotic disassembly. It combines hierarchical task selection with task-context-aware imitation learning to ground language instructions in spatial visual representations, improving task success by 35 percentage points over a diffusion policy baseline and 75 percentage points over a prior task-context-aware baseline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world robotic disassembly demands long-horizon execution, where robots must perform ordered manipulation tasks across multiple parts within a single scene. Multiple valid task goals and diverse assembly configurations make it hard for imitation policies to infer the intended skill from raw observations alone, especially when training data cannot cover the combinatorial diversity of real-world configurations and part geometries.

Method: The framework incorporates task context through language, providing explicit structure for skill selection and associating language-specified tasks with their corresponding manipulation targets in the visual scene. It combines hierarchical task selection with task-context-aware imitation learning to ground language instructions in spatial visual representations for robotic disassembly, without requiring explicit object annotations.

Result: The resulting framework generalizes across diverse connector geometries and assembly configurations. It improves end-to-end task success by 35 percentage points over the baseline diffusion policy and by 75 percentage points over the previous task-context-aware baseline.

Conclusion: Language-based task context is an effective means of resolving skill-selection ambiguity and target grounding in long-horizon robotic disassembly, enabling generalization across part geometries and assembly configurations without explicit object annotations.

Abstract: Real-world robotic disassembly requires long-horizon execution, where robots must perform ordered sequences of manipulation tasks across multiple parts within a single scene. Multiple valid task goals and diverse assembly configurations make it difficult for imitation policies to infer the intended skill from raw observations alone, particularly when training data cannot cover the combinatorial diversity of real-world configurations and part geometries. We show that incorporating task context through language alleviates these challenges by providing explicit structure for skill selection and associating language-specified tasks with their corresponding manipulation targets in the visual scene. The proposed framework combines hierarchical task selection with task-context-aware imitation learning to ground language instructions in spatial visual representations for robotic disassembly. The resulting framework generalizes across diverse connector geometries and assembly configurations without requiring explicit object annotations. Our method improves end-to-end task success by 35 percentage points over the baseline diffusion policy and by 75 percentage points over the previous task-context-aware baseline.

</details>


### [86] [RAF-VLA: Representation Alignment with the Future for End-to-End Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17728)
*Dogun Kim,Yongjae Lee,Joonhee Lim,Yeina Lee,Junhyeok Park,Moogeun Park,Dongsuk Kum*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: RAF-VLA (Representation Alignment with the Future) aligns a driving policy's hidden states with pretrained world-encoder future-frame representations via a simple regularization, achieving competitive NAVSIM planning performance without the training burden or inference latency of explicit future generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: World-Modeling VLAs boost planning by using future driving scenes as dense supervision, but they rely on explicit future generation. This introduces two limitations: additional training burden and inference latency. The authors aim to retain the benefit of future-based supervision while eliminating these costs.

Method: The authors propose RAF-VLA, a VLA-based autonomous driving framework that shapes planning-relevant internal representations through direct guidance from future-frame representations. It employs Future-Aligned Supervised Fine-Tuning, in which a straightforward regularization aligns the policy's hidden states with future-frame representations obtained from a pretrained world encoder while learning driving actions, avoiding explicit future generation.

Result: Extensive experiments on the NAVSIM benchmark show that RAF-VLA achieves competitive planning performance against state-of-the-art VLA planners with substantially fewer training samples seen. It incurs only 3.8% training overhead and a negligible 1 ms inference overhead.

Conclusion: By aligning internal representations with future-frame representations instead of generating future scenes, RAF-VLA retains the planning benefits of world modeling while removing its training burden and inference latency, offering an efficient alternative to World-Modeling VLAs for autonomous driving.

Abstract: Recent Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models for autonomous driving have incorporated world modeling by predicting future driving scenes alongside driving actions, demonstrating strong planning performance. Future driving scenes are utilized as dense supervision, encouraging the policy to learn rich internal representations useful for planning. However, these World-Modeling VLAs rely on explicit future generation to learn such representations, thereby introducing two key limitations: additional training burden and inference latency. To address these limitations, we propose RAF-VLA (Representation Alignment with the Future), a VLA-based autonomous driving framework that shapes planning-relevant internal representations through direct guidance from future-frame representations. RAF-VLA employs Future-Aligned Supervised Fine-Tuning, in which a straightforward regularization aligns the policy's hidden states with future-frame representations obtained from a pretrained world encoder while learning driving actions. This simple alignment allows RAF-VLA to avoid the training burden and inference latency associated with future generation. Extensive experiments on the NAVSIM benchmark show that RAF-VLA achieves competitive planning performance against state-of-the-art VLA planners with substantially fewer training samples seen. Moreover, RAF-VLA incurs only 3.8% training overhead and a negligible 1 ms inference overhead.

</details>


### [87] [CALOS: Control-Affine Lyapunov On-manifold Safety Layer for Safe Deep Reinforcement Learning for Quadrotors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17758)
*Fabrizio Cesareo,Sebastiano Mengozzi,Nicola Mimmo,Andrea Acquaviva*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: CALOS is a runtime safety layer that enforces quadrotor attitude constraints by solving a quadratic program (four tilt-angle inequalities plus a Lyapunov descent condition) as a minimum-norm torque correction, computable in real time across thousands of parallel environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep RL for quadrotor control lacks guarantees on safety constraints during training or deployment. The work aims to enforce attitude (tilt) constraints without altering the underlying learning algorithm, while remaining fast enough for massively parallel DRL training.

Method: CALOS formulates four tilt-angle inequalities and a Lyapunov descent condition as a single quadratic program whose solution is the minimum-norm correction to the policy's nominal torque. The QP is solved exactly via active-set enumeration over the three-dimensional torque space, yielding low computational cost suitable for real-time enforcement across thousands of parallel simulation environments.

Result: On trajectory-tracking tasks in NVIDIA Isaac Lab, CALOS reduces lateral tracking error by 55-60% versus an unconstrained PPO baseline and achieves zero attitude-constraint violations on the training trajectory.

Conclusion: Restricting exploration to safe regions of the state space also accelerates training convergence and improves data efficiency without producing suboptimal policies, making CALOS an effective, algorithm-agnostic runtime safety layer.

Abstract: Deep Reinforcement Learning has demonstrated remarkable capability in quadrotor control, yet learned policies offer no guarantee of respecting safety constraints during training or deployment. We present CALOS (Control-Affine Lyapunov On-manifold Safety), a runtime safety layer that enforces attitude constraints on a quadrotor without modifying the underlying learning algorithm. CALOS formulates four tilt-angle inequalities and a Lyapunov descent condition as a single quadratic program whose solution is the minimum-norm correction to the nominal torque output of the policy. The quadratic program is solved exactly via active-set enumeration over the three-dimensional torque space, with a computational cost low enough to enforce constraints in real time across thousands of parallel simulation environments, as required by modern massively parallel Deep Reinforcement Learning training. Evaluated on trajectory-tracking tasks in NVIDIA Isaac Lab, CALOS reduces lateral tracking error by 55-60% relative to an unconstrained Proximal Policy Optimization baseline while achieving zero attitude-constraint violations on the training trajectory. By restricting exploration to safe regions of the state space, the safety layer also accelerates training convergence and improves data efficiency without producing suboptimal policies.

</details>


### [88] [DRT&R: Direct Radar Teach & Repeat](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17766)
*Alexander Krawciw,Daniil Lisus,Cedric Le Gentil,Timothy D. Barfoot*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: DRT&R is a direct spinning radar navigation stack combining direct radar processing with local mapping to maximize retained information, achieving state-of-the-art localization and closed-loop off-road navigation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Radar-based navigation is robust to adverse conditions (precipitation, dust, fog, smoke) that defeat lidar. Direct methods using the full radar scan have improved on-road global localization but remain undeployed off-road and in closed-loop systems. Moreover, global direct maps smooth out viewpoint-dependent radar artifacts that could otherwise supply pose information when mapping and localization trajectories are similar.

Method: The paper introduces Direct Radar Teach & Repeat (DRT&R), a direct spinning radar-based navigation stack that combines direct radar processing with local mapping to maximize retained information.

Result: Using 344 km of on-road and 20 km of off-road data, DRT&R localizes within 4 cm in most on-road and off-road conditions and within 12 cm in geometrically degenerate and sparse environments. It is also evaluated autonomously in closed loop with an MPC controller over 10+ km on a Clearpath Warthog off-road vehicle, running in real time with state-of-the-art tracking performance for off-road radar navigation.

Conclusion: Combining direct radar processing with local mapping yields state-of-the-art localization in both on-road and off-road environments and enables real-time closed-loop off-road radar navigation.

Abstract: Radar-based navigation is appealing for its robustness to adverse conditions involving airborne particles, such as precipitation, dust, fog, and smoke, that can cause lidar-based systems to fail. Recently, direct methods that retain and use the entire radar scan rather than sparse points have improved on-road global localization performance. However, they have yet to be deployed in off-road environments or in closed-loop systems. Additionally, even direct global maps may lose information: their global nature leads to a smoothing out of viewpoint-dependent radar artifacts, which can provide pose information when mapping and localization occur along similar trajectories. This paper introduces Direct Radar Teach & Repeat (DRT&R): a direct spinning radar-based navigation stack that maximizes the amount of retained information by combining direct radar processing with local mapping. DRT&R yields state-of-the-art (SOTA) localization performance in both on-road and off-road environments. Using 344 km of on-road data and 20 km of off-road data, DRT&R is able to localize to within 4 cm in most on-road and off-road conditions, and 12 cm in geometrically degenerate and sparse environments. DRT&R is also evaluated autonomously in closed loop with an MPC controller for more than 10 km using a Clearpath Warthog off-road vehicle, demonstrating that it runs in real time and achieves SOTA tracking performance for off-road radar navigation.

</details>


### [89] [HINT-Plan: Human Intention-Aware Robot Task Planning in Context-Rich Environments using Vision Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17771)
*Yuchen Liu,Luigi Palmieri,Lujun Li,Radu State,Ilche Georgievski,Marco Aiello*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: HINT-Plan integrates human intention prediction from VLMs into formal multi-agent task planning, using hierarchical scene graphs to enable proactive human-aware robot decision-making.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing approaches to human-aware mobile robot decision-making focus mainly on collision avoidance in low-level motion planning, overlooking the challenges posed by human presence and high-level behavior. This leaves a vacancy in incorporating human intention into high-level robot task planning.

Method: HINT-Plan employs Vision Language Models (VLMs) to anticipate high-level human intentions from third-person image observations, convert them into goal states, and solve joint task-planning problems. Hierarchical Scene Graphs (SGs) serve as high-level environment representations, translating environmental topology and actionable knowledge into formal planning language to ensure executable plans.

Result: Evaluated in a photorealistic simulation, HINT-Plan achieves an overall success rate of 69.71% in joint human-robot task planning, substantially outperforming baselines by up to 35.29%, while also reducing functional conflicts.

Conclusion: The results demonstrate the effectiveness of explicitly incorporating inferred human intentions into formal multi-agent task planning for proactive human-aware robot decision-making.

Abstract: Approaches to incorporating human awareness into mobile robot decision-making mainly focus on collision avoidance in low-level motion planning, often overlooking the challenges posed by human presence and high-level behavior. To address this vacancy, we present HINT-Plan, a novel approach to integrate human intention prediction into robot task planning. HINT-Plan employs Vision Language Models (VLMs) to anticipate high-level human intentions from third-person image observations, convert them into goal states, and solve joint task-planning problems. To effectively enable scene awareness in context-rich environments, we use hierarchical Scene Graphs (SGs) as high-level representations of the environment, and translate environmental topology and actionable knowledge into formal planning language to ensure executable plans. Evaluated in a photorealistic simulation, HINT-Plan achieves an overall success rate of 69.71% in joint human-robot task planning, substantially outperforming the baselines by up to 35.29%, while also reducing functional conflicts. The results show the effectiveness of explicitly incorporating inferred human intentions into formal multi-agent task planning for proactive human-aware robot decision-making.

</details>


### [90] [SPROUT: The Open-Source Soft Growing Robot for Search and Rescue](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17781)
*Antonio Alvarez Valdivia,Ciera McFarland,Robert Reeve,Ankush Dhawan,Chad Council,Megan Richardson,Margaret McGuinness,Nathaniel Hanson*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper presents SPROUT, an open, modular soft robot system (hardware + ROS 2 software) matured with urban search and rescue professionals, offering full build documentation and demonstrating inspection/traversal in collapsed-structure training sites.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Soft robotic systems are theorized as ideal for search and rescue, but barriers prevent graduating them from the lab to the field; there is a gap in replicable, reliable deployable soft robot systems.

Method: Design and development of SPROUT (Soft Pathfinding Robotic Observation Unit) through years of interaction with professional urban search and rescue communities; modular hardware and a ROS 2-based software stack supporting task-specific payloads and control functions; dissemination of supplementary material (code, parts manifests, CAD assemblies, build instructions).

Result: SPROUT performs a variety of challenging inspection and traversal tasks in collapsed structure training sites used by first responders; the system targets operation in dusty, wet, and isolated conditions and is adaptable beyond search and rescue (infrastructure inspection, archaeology).

Conclusion: SPROUT provides a replicable, reliable, field-oriented soft robot platform, with open documentation enabling the broader robotics research community to build their own systems, helping bridge the lab-to-field gap.

Abstract: Soft robotic systems have long been theorized as ideal candidates for use in search and rescue operations; however, there have been significant barriers to entry in graduating soft robotic systems from the laboratory to the field. To address this gap in replicable, reliable soft robot systems, we present the designs for SPROUT, the Soft Pathfinding Robotic Observation Unit. The system has matured over years of interaction with professional urban search and rescue communities, with the goal of operating in dusty, wet, and isolated conditions. This manuscript contains supplementary material, including code, parts manifests, CAD assemblies, and build instructions to allow the broader robotics research community to build their own SPROUT systems. The modular hardware and ROS 2-based software stack support task-specific payloads and control functions, allowing SPROUT to be adapted for applications beyond search and rescue, including infrastructure inspection and archaeology. Here, we demonstrate SPROUT performing a variety of challenging inspection and traversal tasks in collapsed structure training sites used by first responders. The main project page is available online at https://sprout-mitll.github.io/sprout/.

</details>


### [91] [Learning Multi-Humanoid Pickup and Transport via Decentralized Object-Centric Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17824)
*Bikram Pandit,Mohitvishnu S. Gadde,Aayam Kumar Shrestha,Alan Fern*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A decentralized object-centric control framework lets humanoid teams of varying sizes cooperatively pick up and transport objects via attachment regions and gripperless bimanual pinching, with single-robot policies transferring to multi-robot settings and further gains from explicit multi-robot training, validated in simulation and on hardware.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cooperative multi-humanoid pickup and transport of objects with varying size, weight, and geometry requires teams of different sizes; existing approaches typically need per-task redesign and do not offer a unified abstraction across single-robot pickup, multi-robot transport, and robot-to-robot handover.

Method: Decentralized object-centric control: each humanoid is assigned a local attachment region on the shared object and learns pickup and transport through gripperless bimanual pinching. This attachment-based interface provides a common control abstraction spanning single-robot pickup, cooperative multi-robot transport, and robot-to-robot handover without per-task redesign. Policies are trained first on single-robot pickup and then with explicit multi-robot training.

Result: Single-robot pickup policies already transfer nontrivially to cooperative settings, indicating the abstraction captures much coordination structure. Explicit multi-robot training further improves performance, showing shared-object coupling introduces coordination dynamics worth learning directly. Validated in simulation across varying team sizes and object geometries, with sim-to-real transfer enabling real humanoids to perform cooperative manipulation.

Conclusion: An attachment-based, object-centric control abstraction supports scalable cooperative humanoid manipulation with good single-robot-to-multi-robot transfer and benefits from explicit multi-robot training, demonstrating feasibility in simulation and on real hardware.

Abstract: We study cooperative multi-humanoid pickup and transport of objects with varying size, weight, and geometry, requiring robot teams of different sizes. Our approach uses decentralized object-centric control, where each humanoid is assigned a local attachment region on the shared object and learns to realize pickup and transport through gripperless bimanual pinching. This attachment-based interface provides a common control abstraction spanning single-robot pickup, cooperative multi-robot transport, and robot-to-robot handover, without per-task redesign. We find that policies trained only on single-robot pickup already transfer nontrivially to cooperative settings, suggesting that this abstraction captures much of the structure needed for coordination. At the same time, explicit multi-robot training further improves performance, showing that shared-object coupling introduces coordination dynamics that are beneficial to learn directly. We validate the approach in simulation across varying team sizes and object geometries, and demonstrate sim-to-real transfer on hardware, where the learned controllers enable real humanoids to perform cooperative manipulation tasks.

</details>


### [92] [Adaptive-MHE : A Sampling-Based Adaptive MPC for Legged Loco-Manipulation via Moving Horizon Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17832)
*Hossein Keshavarz,Alejandro Ramirez-Serrano,Majid Khadiv*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper introduces Adaptive-MHE, an online sampling-based system identification framework using moving horizon estimation that estimates object and terrain parameters (e.g., mass, friction) and couples them with a sampling-based MPC to enable adaptive loco-manipulation, outperforming baselines and matching a ground-truth-parameter controller in simulation and hardware.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Legged robots can traverse diverse terrains, but generating effective loco-manipulation behaviors is hard because object and terrain parameters are typically unknown and differ from their simulated counterparts, creating a sim-to-real gap. Classical Sys-ID assumes differentiable dynamics, which fails for contact-rich legged systems, and existing sampling-based Sys-ID methods run offline without adapting to changing environmental conditions.

Method: Adaptive-MHE is an online sampling-based system identification framework based on moving horizon estimation. It estimates the physical parameters of objects and terrain in the environment, such as mass and friction, by matching simulated and recorded state trajectories through massively parallel rollouts without requiring differentiable dynamics. The estimate is coupled with a sampling-based model predictive controller.

Result: In both simulation and hardware experiments, the framework consistently outperforms baselines and matches the performance of a controller that has access to ground-truth parameters.

Conclusion: Online sampling-based Sys-ID via moving horizon estimation, tightly coupled with a sampling-based MPC, enables adaptive loco-manipulation in changing and uncertain environments, closing the sim-to-real gap without ground-truth knowledge or differentiable dynamics.

Abstract: Legged robots have demonstrated a remarkable ability to traverse various terrains, yet generating effective loco-manipulation behaviors remains challenging. A key difficulty is that object and terrain parameters are typically unknown to the robot, and mismatches between these parameters and their simulated counterparts introduce a sim-to-real gap that degrades control performance. Classical system identification (Sys-ID) methods often assume differentiable dynamics, an assumption that does not hold for contact-rich legged systems. Sampling-based Sys-ID avoids this restriction by directly matching simulated and recorded state trajectories through massively parallel rollouts, but existing approaches are typically applied offline and do not adapt as environmental conditions change. We present Adaptive-MHE an online sampling-based Sys-ID framework, based on moving horizon estimation (MHE), that estimates the physical parameters of objects and terrain in the environment (e.g., mass, friction) and couples this estimate with a sampling-based model predictive controller, enabling adaptive loco-manipulation in changing and uncertain environments. In simulation and hardware experiments, our framework consistently outperforms baselines and matches the performance of a controller with access to ground-truth parameters.

</details>


### [93] [VCTP: Vehicle-Conditioned Terrain Planning for Off-Road Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17834)
*Akshay Naik,Ramavarapu S. Sreenivas,Dustin Nottage,Ahmet Soylemezoglu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: VCTP plans vehicle routes by evaluating terrain elevation and surface-ID layers at eight headings, using body geometry for admissibility and loaded wheel contacts for surface cost and pitch/roll prediction, integrated with D* Lite and vehicle-state search.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: A vehicle's heading simultaneously affects the surfaces beneath its tires and its pitch and roll; conventional planning that samples terrain at the vehicle center or ignores heading decouples these relationships.

Method: VCTP retains heading-terrain coupling by querying shared elevation and surface-ID layers at eight headings. Body geometry constrains admissibility, while loaded wheel contacts determine modeled surface cost and predicted pitch and roll. D* Lite and vehicle-state search then plan routes with forward and reverse motion, and selective recomputation refreshes every affected body or contact query when observations change.

Result: In fully observed two-track simulations, wheel-contact sampling instead of vehicle-center sampling lowered modeled surface cost by 39.3% while shortening the route. In offline planning on RGator recordings, VCTP reduced modeled costs over identified surfaces and observed support relative to distance-focused planning, though incomplete coverage left full-route rankings unresolved. Selective updates matched full recomputation across all 474 comparisons using recorded map changes.

Conclusion: Wheel-contact placement and vehicle heading measurably affect route choice, and VCTP identifies the conditions under which these factors matter while maintaining equivalence between selective and full updates.

Abstract: A vehicle's heading affects both the surfaces beneath its tires and its pitch and roll. We present Vehicle-Conditioned Terrain Planning (VCTP), which retains these relationships by evaluating shared elevation and surface-ID layers at eight headings. Body geometry constrains admissibility, while loaded wheel contacts determine modeled surface cost and predicted pitch and roll. Established D* Lite and vehicle-state search use these evaluations to plan routes with forward and reverse motion. When observations change, VCTP recomputes every affected body or contact query. In fully observed two-track simulations, sampling at wheel contacts rather than at the vehicle center lowers modeled surface cost by 39.3% while shortening the route. In offline planning on RGator recordings, VCTP also reduces modeled costs over identified surfaces and observed support relative to distance-focused planning, although incomplete coverage leaves full-route rankings unresolved. Selective updates match full recomputation in all 474 comparisons using recorded map changes. These results identify when wheel-contact placement and vehicle heading affect route choice.

</details>


### [94] [Timely Activation of Safety Filters via One-Step Reachability Expansion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17904)
*Javier Borquez*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes a one-step expanded Backward Reachable Tube (BRT) to fix the mismatch between continuous-time least-restrictive safety filters and discrete-time robotic execution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Least-restrictive Hamilton-Jacobi safety filters guarantee safety only under continuous-time dynamics, but real robots apply control at discrete sampling intervals. This mismatch lets the system jump into the unsafe BRT between updates, causing failures that are theoretically avoidable.

Method: The authors introduce an expanded BRT that predicts all states capable of reaching the true BRT within a single timestep, and use this expanded boundary as the activation condition for the safety filter. They formulate this expanded set as a modified reachability problem solvable with standard continuous-time solvers.

Result: The expanded boundary acts as an earlier activation condition, ensuring safety interventions occur in time for correctness under discrete-time execution while retaining least-restrictive behavior.

Conclusion: A principled one-step expansion of the BRT restores safety guarantees for least-restrictive HJ reachability filters under discrete-time robotic execution, computable using existing continuous-time solvers.

Abstract: Least-restrictive safety filters based on Hamilton-Jacobi reachability provide strong safety guarantees by overriding a nominal controller only when the system reaches the boundary of the set of unsafe states defined as a Backward Reachable Tube (BRT). These guarantees, however, rely on the continuous-time nature of the underlying formulation. In practice, robotic systems apply control at discrete sampling intervals, which creates a mismatch where the system may jump into the unsafe BRT between updates, allowing failures that are theoretically avoidable. This work introduces a principled solution based on a one-step expanded BRT that predicts all states capable of reaching the true BRT within a single timestep. By using this expanded boundary as the activation condition for the safety filter, safety interventions occur early enough to ensure correctness under discrete-time execution. We formulate this expanded set as a modified reachability problem and compute it using standard continuous-time solvers.

</details>


### [95] [Map2Route: Benchmarking Compositional Language-Grounded Route Planning over Semantic Maps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17910)
*Muyi Bao,Hang Xu,Jingfan Tang,Zihan Liu,Yuxin Cai,Chen Lv,Wenshan Wang,Ji Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO
