TL;DR: The paper introduces Map2Route, a human-curated benchmark of 1,000 episodes across 40 scenes for compositional language-grounded route planning on pre-built semantic maps, and proposes Grounding2Route, a method combining executable code-as-grounding, verification-guided repair, and scope-aware planning that outperforms seven adapted baselines yet still trails human performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing works only partially test compositional language grounding for route planning, lacking a benchmark that covers relational/comparative/nested descriptions plus ordered constraints, must-avoid requirements, soft preferences, and spatial/route-stage scopes. This limits systematic evaluation of language-grounded route planning over semantic maps.

Method: The authors build Map2Route, a human-curated benchmark with 1,000 episodes across 40 scenes. Instructions involve relational, comparative, and nested descriptions to identify route-relevant objects/regions, and specify ordered must-pass regions, must-avoid constraints, five categories of soft preferences, and spatial and route-stage scopes. They propose Grounding2Route, which integrates executable code-as-grounding, verification-guided repair, and scope-aware planning.

Result: Grounding2Route substantially outperforms seven representative adapted baselines on all metrics. However, a considerable gap remains relative to human demonstrations, indicating the benchmark's difficulty and significant headroom for future work.

Conclusion: Map2Route provides a challenging, partially-unexplored evaluation setting for compositional language-grounded route planning, and Grounding2Route sets a strong but non-saturated baseline, leaving substantial room for progress toward human-level performance.

Abstract: We introduce Map2Route, a human-curated benchmark for compositional language-grounded route planning over pre-built semantic maps. Map2Route contains 1,000 episodes across 40 scenes, where instructions use relational, comparative, and nested descriptions to identify route-relevant objects and regions, while specifying ordered must-pass regions, must-avoid requirements, five categories of soft preferences, and spatial and route-stage scopes, which is partially tested by existing works. Alongside Map2Route, we propose Grounding2Route, which combines executable code-as-grounding with verification-guided repair and scope-aware planning.Across seven representative adapted baselines, Grounding2Route substantially outperforms existing methods in all metrics. Despite these gains, a substantial gap to human demonstrations remains, highlighting the difficulty of Map2Route and the considerable headroom for future progress. Additional qualitative results and resources are available on https://anonymous.4open.science/w/Map2Route-F05F/.

</details>


### [96] [Multi-Session Multimodal Underwater Mapping with Acoustic and Optical Imaging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17929)
*Precious Philip-Ifabiyi,Valerio Franchi,Fausto Ferreira,Nuno Gracias*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A factor graph optimization framework for multi-session, multimodal underwater seafloor mapping jointly optimizes trajectories, landmarks, sensor extrinsics, and per-session global alignments to correct inter-session offsets and intra-session drift, achieving 3.4% pixel accuracy gains over baselines on Catalan coast datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate seafloor mapping is important for marine science, archaeology, and environmental monitoring, but integrating data from heterogeneous sensors (side-scan sonar and optical cameras) acquired across separate survey sessions is difficult due to positioning drift and sensor offsets.

Method: A multi-session, multimodal underwater mapping framework based on factor graph optimization that jointly optimizes vehicle trajectories, 3D landmark positions, sensor extrinsics, and per-session global alignment transformations. It combines rigid inter-session corrections with local trajectory deformations to address both inter-session offsets and intra-session distortions from accumulated navigation error.

Result: Validated on real-world datasets along the Catalan coast. Measurable improvements in map consistency over unoptimized and rigid-alignment baselines across all metrics, including Pixel Accuracy and mean Intersection over Union, with a 3.4% pixel accuracy gain over the unoptimized baseline (~14700 m² improved semantic labelling). Qualitative results show consistent sonar-optical co-registration despite significant trajectory distortions and inter-session misalignments.

Conclusion: The framework can generate coherent multimodal seafloor maps from heterogeneous underwater surveys, showing promise for integrating separate sonar and optical survey sessions into consistent maps.

Abstract: Accurate seafloor mapping is essential for marine science, archaeology, and environmental monitoring. However, integrating data from different sensors, such as side-scan sonar and optical cameras, collected across separate survey sessions, remains challenging due to positioning drift and sensor offsets. This paper presents a multi-session, multimodal underwater mapping framework based on factor graph optimization. The method jointly optimizes vehicle trajectories, 3D landmark positions, sensor extrinsics, and per-session global alignment transformations. By combining rigid inter-session corrections with local trajectory deformations, it compensates for both inter-session offsets and intra-session distortions from accumulated navigation errors. The proposed methodology was validated on real-world datasets collected along the Catalan coast. Results show measurable improvements in map consistency over both unoptimized and rigid-alignment baselines across all metrics, including Pixel Accuracy and mean Intersection over Union. The method achieves a 3.4% improvement in pixel accuracy over the unoptimized baseline, corresponding to improved semantic labelling across approximately 14700 $\text{m}^2$ of mapped area. Qualitative results further show consistent co-registration between sonar and optical maps, even in the presence of significant trajectory distortions and inter-session misalignments. These findings demonstrate the potential of the proposed framework to generate coherent multimodal seafloor maps from heterogeneous underwater surveys.

</details>


### [97] [Feedback-Modulated Harmonic Policies for Quadruped Locomotion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17946)
*Yixuan Jia,Steven Roche,Jonathan P. How*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes representing quadruped joint trajectories as command-conditioned Fourier series with online feedback, generating coefficients and feedback weights via a context network. It compares this explicit frequency structure to an MLP baseline, finds implicit periodic modes via dynamic mode decomposition, and validates on a Unitree Go2 achieving 3.67 m/s peak speed and 5.883 kg load carrying.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Learned quadruped locomotion policies typically map observations directly to joint-level actions, leaving the periodic structure of locomotion implicit. The authors aim to investigate an alternative, explicit representation of joint trajectories to better capture and exploit the inherent periodicity of locomotion.

Method: Each joint trajectory is expressed as a command-conditioned Fourier series, modified online using robot-state feedback. A context network generates the Fourier coefficients and the weights of a per-step feedback network, whose outputs adjust joint offsets, harmonic gains, frequency, and phase during execution. In simulation, harmonic waveforms are examined alongside hidden activations of an MLP policy that directly outputs joint targets. Dynamic mode decomposition (DMD) is applied to selected MLP rollouts.

Result: Harmonic waveforms change frequency and shape with commanded speed. DMD of MLP rollouts reveals dominant activation modes near the foot-height oscillation frequency and its second harmonic, showing periodic structure even without an explicit Fourier generator. On a Unitree Go2, the simulation-trained harmonic controller achieves a provisional onboard-estimated peak speed of 3.67 m/s and carries loads up to 5.883 kg in separate trials.

Conclusion: Explicit frequency-structured representations (Fourier series with feedback) offer a viable alternative for learned quadruped locomotion, matching or complementing implicit periodic structure found in MLP policies, and transfer effectively to real hardware for high-speed and load-carrying tasks.

Abstract: Learned quadruped locomotion policies commonly map observations directly to joint-level actions, leaving the periodic structure of locomotion implicit in the policy. We investigate an alternative representation in which each joint trajectory is expressed as a command-conditioned Fourier series and modified online using feedback from the robot state. A context network generates the Fourier coefficients and the weights of a per-step feedback network, whose outputs adjust joint offsets, harmonic gains, frequency, and phase during execution. In simulation, we examine this explicit frequency structure alongside the hidden activations of an MLP policy that directly outputs joint targets. The harmonic waveforms change frequency and shape with commanded speed. Dynamic mode decomposition of selected MLP rollouts reveals dominant activation modes near the foot-height oscillation frequency and its second harmonic, showing periodic structure without an explicit Fourier generator. On a Unitree Go2, the simulation-trained harmonic controller records a provisional onboard-estimated peak speed of 3.67 meter per second and carries added loads up to 5.883 kilogram in separate trials.

</details>


### [98] [Causal-History Test-Time Scaling for Failure Recovery in Autoregressive World-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18016)
*Lin Li,Long Chen,Kwunhang,Wong,Jiaming Lei,Song Jin,Shucheng Du,Chuhan Zhang,Songchen Ma,Weihao Zhang,Jun Xiao,Kwang-Ting,Cheng*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper introduces a training-free framework that treats robot failure recovery as test-time scaling over causal histories, using three stages—progress-aware recovery trigger, history-prefix recovery, and hypothesis verification—to improve manipulation success in simulated and real-world settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: World-action models (WAMs) jointly model future visual dynamics and robot actions but are trained predominantly on successful trajectories, so they fail when real-world execution diverges from learned dynamics. This is exacerbated in autoregressive WAMs, where execution errors enter the causal history and propagate into subsequent predictions.

Method: A training-free framework reformulating failure recovery as test-time scaling over causal histories, decomposed into three coupled decisions: when to revise the causal history, where to recover a reliable history prefix, and which history configuration best supports execution. It operates via: (1) a Progress-Aware Recovery Trigger detecting persistent non-progress and intervening only when the state permits; (2) History-Prefix Recovery identifying the unreliable suffix, retrieving a historical anchor matching the current physical state, and reconstructing the causal KV state from the retained prefix conditioned on the latest real observation; and (3) Hypothesis Verification comparing future continuations from complete-history, recovered-prefix, and full-reset hypotheses to commit the best-supported one.

Result: Experiments in simulated and real-world manipulation settings show consistent improvements in task success. Ablations confirm the contribution of each recovery stage.

Conclusion: Reformulating failure recovery as test-time scaling over causal histories provides an effective, training-free way to make autoregressive world-action models more robust under execution divergence, with each of the three stages contributing to the gains.

Abstract: World-action models (WAMs) have emerged as a promising paradigm for robot manipulation by jointly modeling future visual dynamics and robot actions. However, existing WAMs are trained predominantly on successful trajectories, making them prone to failure when real-world execution diverges from the learned dynamics. This issue is amplified in autoregressive WAMs, where execution errors become part of the causal history and continue to influence subsequent predictions. To this end, we introduce \method{}, a training-free framework that reformulates failure recovery as \emph{test-time scaling over causal histories}. This formulation decomposes recovery into three coupled decisions: \emph{when} to revise the causal history, \emph{where} to recover a reliable history prefix, and \emph{which} history configuration best supports subsequent execution. Specifically, \method{} realizes these decisions through three stages: 1) \textbf{Progress-Aware Recovery Trigger} detects persistent non-progress and triggers recovery only when the current execution state permits intervention; 2) \textbf{History-Prefix Recovery} identifies the unreliable history suffix, retrieves a historical anchor matching the current physical state, and reconstructs the causal KV state from the retained prefix while conditioning on the latest real observation; and 3) \textbf{Hypothesis Verification} compares the future continuations induced by complete-history, recovered-prefix, and full-reset hypotheses, and commits the best-supported hypothesis. Experiments in both simulated and real-world manipulation settings demonstrate consistent improvements in task success, while ablations confirm the contribution of each recovery stage.

</details>


### [99] [Online Multimodal Workload Assessment in Contact-Rich Physical Human-Robot Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18031)
*Yanyi Chen,Fan Yang,Min Deng*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes an online, interpretable multimodal framework for continuously assessing interaction workload in contact-rich physical human-robot interaction, fusing interaction wrench, planar TCP kinematics, and skin conductance level into four workload-related factors whose weights adapt to path curvature and task progression.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Contact-rich physical human-robot interaction (pHRI) imposes time-varying demands on physical interaction, motor regulation, and physiological response, which creates a need for continuous assessment of interaction workload. Existing learning-based methods can perform well but tend to be opaque, so a transparent and interpretable alternative is desirable.

Method: The framework integrates interaction wrench, planar tool-center-point (TCP) kinematics, and skin conductance level (SCL) into four interpretable workload-related factors. Their relative contributions are adjusted using path curvature to reflect changes in motion demand and task progression to account for gradual physiological variation over time. Evaluation involved 24 participants across 18 controlled combinations of temperature, acoustic noise, and illuminance under two admittance-control modes. A strict leave-one-subject-out (LOSO) protocol used standardized pupil diameter (PD_z) as an independent physiological reference, with comparisons against static variants and representative state-of-the-art learning-based baselines.

Result: The framework achieves a cohort-mean 30 s block-wise Spearman correlation of ρ30 = 0.308 with the physiological reference, with positive subject-level correspondence in 23 of 24 participants. Its overall performance is comparable to the state-of-the-art learning-based baseline, while keeping the assessment process transparent via explicit workload-related factors and defined weighting rules; it also outperforms the corresponding fixed-weight formulation and maintains consistent performance across the two tested admittance-control modes.

Conclusion: The results support a transparent and interpretable approach to continuous interaction workload assessment in contact-rich pHRI.

Abstract: Contact-rich physical human--robot interaction (pHRI) imposes time-varying demands associated with physical interaction, motor regulation, and physiological response, motivating continuous assessment of interaction workload. This paper presents an online multimodal assessment framework that integrates interaction wrench, planar tool-center-point (TCP) kinematics, and skin conductance level (SCL) into four interpretable workload-related factors. Their relative contributions are adjusted using path curvature to reflect changes in motion demand and task progression to account for gradual physiological variation over time. The framework was evaluated with 24 participants across 18 controlled combinations of temperature, acoustic noise, and illuminance under two admittance-control modes. Strict leave-one-subject-out (LOSO) evaluation used standardized pupil diameter ($\mathrm{PD}_z$) as an independent physiological reference and included comparisons with static variants and representative state-of-the-art learning-based baselines. The proposed framework achieves a cohort-mean $30\,\mathrm{s}$ block-wise Spearman correlation of $ρ_{30}=0.308$ with the physiological reference, with positive subject-level correspondence in 23 of 24 participants. Its overall performance is comparable to the state-of-the-art learning-based baseline. At the same time, our framework keeps the assessment process transparent through explicit workload-related factors and defined weighting rules, while outperforming the corresponding fixed-weight formulation. The framework also maintains consistent performance across the two tested admittance-control modes. These results support a transparent and interpretable approach to continuous interaction workload assessment in contact-rich pHRI.

</details>


### [100] [Geometric Shortcuts for Complex Trunk Postures: Dual-Helicity Coupling Enables Low-Dimensional Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18050)
*Huishi Huang,Danlu Chen,Matteo Lo Preti,Jun Liu,Marcelo H. Ang,Cecilia Laschi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Elephant trunks may achieve complex postures through a low-dimensional geometric shortcut—dual-helicity coupling of oppositely handed oblique muscles—rather than fine segmental activation. A simplified soft-robotic prototype reproduces a broad library of elephant-like postures by varying only two geometric parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Elephant trunks produce highly complex postures, yet the mechanism need not rely solely on precise, fine-grained segmental muscle activation. The authors seek a simpler, low-dimensional control principle that could explain this complexity.

Method: The paper proposes dual-helicity coupling between opposite-handed oblique muscles as a geometric shortcut. This is tested in a simplified soft-robotic prototype, varying only two geometric parameters to generate postures.

Result: Varying only two geometric parameters in the prototype generates a broad library of elephant-like postures, supporting the dual-helicity coupling hypothesis.

Conclusion: The findings suggest a dual-layer control architecture for elephant trunks, with implications for continuum robot design and biological hypotheses about trunk motor control.

Abstract: How do elephant trunks generate complex postures without relying solely on fine segmental activation? We propose that part of this complexity arises from a low-dimensional geometric shortcut: dual-helicity coupling between opposite-handed oblique muscles. In a simplified soft-robotic prototype, varying only two geometric parameters generates a broad library of elephant-like postures, suggesting a dual-layer control architecture with implications for continuum robot design and biological hypotheses.

</details>


### [101] [CLASP: A Cluster-Level Autonomous Selective Picking Robot with a Soft Rolling-Band Gripper for Fresh-Market Blueberry Harvesting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18051)
*Yixuan Xia,Yilin Cai,Natalia Belen Espinoza,Changying Li,Zilfina Rubio Ames,Xin Zhang,Yue Chen*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: CLASP is a cluster-level autonomous selective picking robot with a soft active rolling-band gripper that enables selective harvesting of fresh-market blueberries by exploiting detachment-force separation between mature and immature fruit.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fresh-market blueberries require selective, gentle picking, which is labor-intensive and expensive. Over-the-row machine harvesters are fast but non-selective, bruising mixed-ripeness fruit and limiting yield to the processing market. Existing selective robotic harvesters typically target individual fruits rather than fruit clusters, limiting efficiency for small, densely clustered blueberries.

Method: The paper presents CLASP, comprising a Soft Active Rolling-Band Gripper (SARB-Gripper) with two compliant bands that envelop the cluster and roll against the fruit to draw mature berries off in sequence, using closed-loop pulling-force regulation to keep applied load below the immature detachment threshold. A global-to-local perception pipeline pairs an eye-to-hand camera for global cluster detection and target selection with an eye-in-hand camera for local localization and cluster orientation estimation.

Result: Field measurements confirm a clear detachment-force separation between mature and immature fruit. The SARB-Gripper reproduces a commanded pulling force to within 3.7%. In end-to-end field trials, CLASP autonomously grasped 23 of 25 presented clusters (92%). The component cost is approximately $3326 per unit.

Conclusion: CLASP offers a scalable, cost-effective approach to selective cluster-level harvesting for fresh-market blueberries.

Abstract: Fresh-market blueberries require selective, gentle picking, which is labor-intensive and expensive. Over-the-row machine harvesters are fast but non-selective, bruising mixed-ripeness fruit and limiting yield to the processing market. Selective robotic harvesters typically target individual fruits rather than fruit clusters, which limits harvesting efficiency for small, densely clustered blueberries. This paper presents CLASP, a Cluster-Level Autonomous Selective Picking robot with a Soft Active Rolling-Band Gripper (SARB-Gripper). Two compliant bands envelop the cluster and roll against the fruit, drawing mature berries off in sequence, while closed-loop regulation of the pulling force keeps the applied load below the immature detachment threshold. A global-to-local perception pipeline pairs an eye-to-hand camera for global cluster detection and target selection with an eye-in-hand camera for local localization and cluster orientation estimation. Field measurements confirm a clear detachment-force separation between mature and immature fruit, and the SARB-Gripper reproduces a commanded pulling force to within \SI{3.7}{\percent}, enabling selective harvesting at the cluster level. In end-to-end field trials, CLASP autonomously grasped 23 of 25 presented clusters (\SI{92}{\percent}). With the component cost of approximately \$3326 per unit, CLASP offers a scalable approach to selective cluster-level harvesting for fresh-market blueberries.

</details>


### [102] [An Efficient Algorithm for Minimum-Pressure Growth Planning of Vine Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18070)
*Andres C. Torres,Tobia Marcucci,Elliot W. Hawkes*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper addresses minimum-pressure growth planning for vine robots navigating around polytopic obstacles, deriving a growth pressure equation, proving existence of an optimal piecewise-linear path, and reducing the problem to a shortest-path problem with time-dependent weights solved via modified Dijkstra's algorithm, validated in simulation and hardware.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vine robots extend from their tip to navigate cluttered environments, but little work has addressed growth planning (finding optimal growth paths). Existing planners ignore the growth pressure required to follow a path, which can cause the robot to burst if too high. There is a need for planners that find minimum-pressure paths.

Method: The authors derive a growth pressure equation for vine robots of arbitrary shape, showing that a minimum-pressure path always exists that is piecewise-linear and bends only at specific obstacle points. They reduce the growth-planning problem to a shortest-path problem with time-dependent weights, solved efficiently with a modified Dijkstra's algorithm.

Result: The algorithm finds globally optimal solutions in 2D and approximate solutions in 3D, with error vanishing as a discretization parameter approaches zero. Speed and scalability are demonstrated through numerical simulations, and results are validated with hardware experiments. An open-source implementation, VinePlanner (Python), is provided.

Conclusion: The proposed approach efficiently computes minimum-pressure growth paths for vine robots around polytopic obstacles, ensuring global optimality in 2D and near-optimality in 3D, with practical validation and a publicly available high-performance implementation.

Abstract: Vine robots navigate cluttered environments by extending from their tip. Although their ability to operate in such environments has been extensively demonstrated, little work has addressed growth planning, i.e., finding optimal growth paths. Moreover, existing planners do not account for the growth pressure necessary to follow a given path, which can cause the robot to burst when it is too high. In this paper, we address the problem of finding minimum-pressure paths for vine robots growing around polytopic obstacles. We propose an efficient algorithm that is guaranteed to find globally optimal solutions in 2D and approximate solutions in 3D, with an error that vanishes as a discretization parameter approaches zero. First, we derive a growth pressure equation for vine robots of arbitrary shape, which we use to show that there always exists a minimum-pressure path that is piecewise-linear and can bend only at specific points on the obstacles. We then leverage this observation to reduce the growth-planning problem to a shortest-path problem with time-dependent weights, which we efficiently solve using a modified Dijkstra's algorithm. We demonstrate the speed and scalability of our approach through numerical simulations. We also validate our algorithm with hardware experiments and provide an open-source and high-performance implementation in the Python package, VinePlanner: https://github.com/Ahsoka/VinePlanner.

</details>


### [103] [Characterizing Refraction-Induced Ranging Bias in Underwater Collaborative Localization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18073)
*Timothy Kogucki,Alan Papalia*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This work quantifies how refraction-induced bias in acoustic ranging degrades multi-agent collaborative localization over kilometer scales, using HYCOM reanalysis, ray tracing, and a centralized factor graph estimator.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-agent range-aided navigation is promising for large-scale underwater localization, but its accuracy depends on range measurement quality. Sound speed variability causes acoustic ray refraction, while standard sensor fusion pipelines assume straight-line propagation for tractability. This refraction systematically biases ranges to be longer than the straight-line assumption predicts, yet the effect of this bias on multi-agent collaborative localization at kilometer scales remains unexplored.

Method: Simulated experiments with several agents operating over kilometer scales. The simulation uses HYCOM reanalysis data to recreate realistic oceanographic conditions, ray tracing to generate refraction-informed ranges, and a centralized multi-agent factor graph estimator to quantify the resulting measurement bias on estimated trajectories.

Result: Preliminary results indicate that refraction-induced bias can induce significant degradation of estimated trajectories, particularly in regions with sharp sound-speed gradients. The simulation environment is shared at https://github.com/UMich-RobotExploration/manta-ray.

Conclusion: Refraction-induced ranging bias can significantly degrade multi-agent collaborative localization on kilometer scales, especially where sound-speed gradients are sharp, motivating refraction-aware approaches. The released simulation environment supports further studies.

Abstract: This work studies how refraction-induced bias on acoustic ranging affects multi-agent collaborative localization in a range of oceanographic conditions and spatial scales. While multi-agent range-aided navigation, which uses range measurements to either fixed infrastructure or other agents, is a promising solution to the challenges of large-scale underwater localization, its accuracy depends strongly on the quality of range measurements. Sound speed variability induces refraction (bending) of acoustic rays, yet, for algorithmic tractability, standard sensor fusion pipelines assume straight-line propagation. This refraction systematically biases range measurements to be longer than the straight-line assumption predicts. However, the effects of this bias on multi-agent collaborative localization on kilometer scales remains unexplored. We present a series of simulated experiments with several agents operating over kilometer scales. The simulation uses HYCOM reanalysis data to recreate realistic oceanographic conditions, ray tracing to generate refraction-informed ranges, and a centralized multi-agent factor graph estimator to quantify the resulting measurement bias on estimated trajectories. Preliminary results indicate that refraction-induced bias can induce significant degradation of estimated trajectories, particularly in regions with sharp sound-speed gradients. We also share the simulation environment to support further studies https://github.com/UMich-RobotExploration/manta-ray.

</details>


### [104] [Not All Layers Need Tuning: Diagnosing and Directing Adaptation in Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18084)
*Shahram Najam Syed,Arthur Jakobsson,Prayuj Sachdev,Jeffrey Ichnowski*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Adaptation cost in VLAs is region-structured (vision encoder, language backbone, action head) and can be measured before fine-tuning via a label-free diagnostic that allocates variable-rank LoRA adapters, matching or beating uniform LoRA and full fine-tuning across five architectures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fine-tuning VLAs for new deployments is expensive, yet existing methods apply uniform-capacity adapters everywhere, implicitly assuming all network regions need equal adjustment. This paper challenges that assumption and asks whether adaptation cost is structured enough to be measured and exploited before fine-tuning.

Method: The authors measure per-region adaptation cost as normalized parameter displacement under region-isolated fine-tuning across five architecturally diverse VLAs (OpenVLA-OFT, pi_0, SmolVLA, DTP, Octo; 93M-7B). They propose a pipeline that observes, diagnoses, allocates, and adapts: from ten unlabeled target observations and without fine-tuning, a diagnostic estimates per-region cost by combining reference-free gradient and Monte Carlo Dropout signals with a CKA score against a cached source reference; an allocator converts estimates into variable-rank LoRA adapters under a parameter budget, freezing well-calibrated regions; standard LoRA fine-tuning then trains the adapters.

Result: The diagnostic ranks regions within each deployment at a median Spearman of 0.91. Allocation matches or exceeds uniform LoRA at every tested budget on LIBERO and CALVIN. On a physical xArm-7 under an instruction-wording shift, the pipeline matches full fine-tuning with 0.04% of its trainable parameters. On five held-out scenes evaluated without retraining, it leads every baseline, achieving 11-23 successes out of 30 rollouts versus 8-18 for the strongest parameter-efficient baseline at equal or larger budgets and 2-11 for full fine-tuning.

Conclusion: Adaptation cost in VLAs is structured enough to be measured before fine-tuning begins, enabling a diagnostic-driven, budget-aware allocation of variable-rank adapters that outperforms uniform and full fine-tuning while using a tiny fraction of trainable parameters.

Abstract: Fine-tuning a Vision-Language-Action (VLA) model for a new deployment environment is expensive, yet most methods apply uniform-capacity adapters to every network region as if every region requires equal adjustment. This paper tests that assumption on five architecturally diverse VLAs (OpenVLA-OFT, $π_0$, SmolVLA, DTP, Octo; 93M-7B parameters). Measuring per-region adaptation cost as normalized parameter displacement under region-isolated fine-tuning reveals an adaptation spectrum in which appearance shifts concentrate cost in the vision encoder, instruction shifts in the language backbone, and novel-object shifts in the vision encoder together with the action head, across all five architectures. To exploit this structure, we introduce a pipeline that observes, diagnoses, allocates, and adapts. From ten unlabeled target observations and without fine-tuning, the diagnostic estimates per-region cost by combining reference-free gradient and Monte Carlo Dropout signals with a Centered Kernel Alignment score against a cached source reference; the allocator converts the estimates into variable-rank LoRA adapters under a parameter budget and freezes well-calibrated regions; and standard LoRA fine-tuning trains the resulting adapters. The diagnostic ranks regions within each deployment at a median Spearman of 0.91, and the allocation matches or exceeds uniform LoRA at every budget we tested on LIBERO and CALVIN. On a physical xArm-7, the pipeline matches full fine-tuning under an instruction-wording shift with 0.04% of its trainable parameters, and on five held-out scenes evaluated without retraining it leads every baseline, with 11-23 successes of 30 rollouts against 8-18 for the strongest parameter-efficient baseline at equal or larger budgets and 2-11 for full fine-tuning. These results suggest that adaptation cost in VLAs is structured enough to measure before fine-tuning begins.

</details>


### [105] [ReRadar: Robust Radar Global Localization via Rotation-Equivariant Descriptor Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18092)
*Duc Manh Nguyen,Truong Giang Dao,Gia Nghiem Luong,Viet Trung Hoang,Anh Quang Nguyen*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ReRadar is a radar global localization pipeline using rotation-equivariant steerable CNNs, group pooling, and NetVLAD to build rotation-invariant descriptors, combined with landmark matching to estimate 3-DoF pose, achieving high Recall@1 across multiple datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Global localization with scanning millimeter-wave radar is challenging because existing place-recognition descriptors often discard the spatial structure required for accurate pose retrieval.

Method: Extracts rotation-equivariant intermediate features via steerable convolutional neural networks; forms rotation-invariant descriptors through group pooling and NetVLAD aggregation; combines descriptor retrieval with landmark-based matching for 3-DoF pose estimation.

Result: With target-dataset adaptation: 99.37% Recall@1 (OORD Bellmouth), 91.44% Recall@1 with 80.99% F1_max (Mulran DCC01), 99.38% Recall@1 (falling-snow Boreas). Without target data, cross-dataset model reaches 98.07% Recall@1 on OORD, comparable to evaluated SOTA.

Conclusion: ReRadar effectively addresses radar global localization by preserving spatial structure via equivariant features and rotation-invariant descriptors, delivering strong retrieval and pose estimation performance across diverse conditions and competitive cross-dataset generalization.

Abstract: Global localization with scanning millimeter-wave radar remains challenging because place-recognition descriptors often discard spatial structure needed for accurate pose retrieval. We present ReRadar, a radar global localization pipeline that extracts rotation-equivariant intermediate features using steerable convolutional neural networks, forms rotation-invariant descriptors through group pooling and NetVLAD aggregation, and combines descriptor retrieval with landmark-based matching to estimate the robot's three-degree-of-freedom (3-DoF) pose. Across fixed database-query evaluations, ReRadar with target-dataset adaptation achieves 99.37% Recall@1 on OORD Bellmouth, 91.44% Recall@1 with 80.99% F1_max on Mulran DCC01, and 99.38% Recall@1 on falling-snow Boreas sequence. Without target-dataset data, the cross-dataset model reaches 98.07% Recall@1 on OORD, performing comparably to the evaluated state-of-the-art methods.

</details>


### [106] [Beyond Pixel Similarity: Task-Aware Evaluation of GAN-Based Synthetic Sonar Data for Robotic Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18100)
*Hannan Ejaz Keen,Muhammad Moazam Fraz,Karsten Berns*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Pixel-level image-fidelity metrics (SSIM, PSNR, MSE) do not reliably predict downstream object-detection performance of GAN-generated synthetic sonar imagery, motivating task-aware evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Synthetic data can lower the cost of collecting and annotating training data for robotic perception, yet generating sensor observations that preserve task-relevant characteristics is difficult, especially for sonar imagery. It is unclear whether conventional image-fidelity metrics adequately reflect the downstream perception performance of such synthetic data.

Method: A Pix2Pix conditional GAN is trained with four discriminator configurations differing in receptive field (PixelGAN, PatchGAN-16, PatchGAN-70, ImageGAN) on sonar imagery from two datasets. Evaluation combines conventional fidelity metrics (SSIM, PSNR, MSE) with task-oriented testing: YOLOX-S, YOLOX-L, and Faster R-CNN detectors are trained only on real sonar imagery and then evaluated on the GAN-generated images under identical test samples and annotations.

Result: A discrepancy emerges between image fidelity and downstream detection: the configuration with the best SSIM, PSNR, and MSE does not consistently yield the best detection performance, while PatchGAN configurations achieve strong detection results despite not attaining the highest pixel-level similarity scores.

Conclusion: For the datasets and models considered, pixel-level image-fidelity metrics alone may not consistently capture the task-relevant realism of synthetic sonar observations, motivating task-aware evaluation for synthetic sensor data intended for robotic perception.

Abstract: Synthetic data can reduce the cost of collecting and annotating training data for robotic perception, but generating sensor observations that preserve the characteristics relevant to downstream perception remains challenging, particularly for sonar imagery. In this work, we investigate whether conventional image-fidelity metrics adequately reflect the downstream perception performance of GAN-generated synthetic sonar data. We employ a Pix2Pix conditional generative adversarial network with four discriminator configurations characterized by different receptive fields: PixelGAN, PatchGAN-16, PatchGAN-70, and ImageGAN. The models are trained using sonar imagery from two datasets and evaluated using conventional image-fidelity metrics, including Structural Similarity Index (SSIM), Peak Signal-to-Noise Ratio (PSNR), and Mean Squared Error (MSE). To complement these pixel-level measures with task-oriented evaluation, YOLOX-S, YOLOX-L, and Faster R-CNN detectors are trained exclusively on real sonar imagery and subsequently evaluated on the GAN-generated images using identical test samples and annotations across all discriminator configurations. The results reveal a discrepancy between image-fidelity and downstream object-detection performance: the configuration achieving the best SSIM, PSNR, and MSE does not consistently yield the best detection performance. In particular, PatchGAN configurations achieve strong downstream detection results despite not achieving the highest pixel-level similarity scores. These findings suggest, for the datasets and models considered, pixel-level image-fidelity metrics alone may not consistently capture the task-relevant realism of synthetic sonar observations and motivate the use of task-aware evaluation for synthetic sensor data intended for robotic perception.

</details>


### [107] [Technical Report: One-Step Drifting Action Heads for GR00T N1.7](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18108)
*Xihe Shao*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Replacing GR00T N1.7's iterative diffusion-transformer action head with a one-step drifting head cuts action-head forward time from ~45.3 ms to ~5.0 ms and total backbone-plus-head time from ~70.0 ms to ~30.6 ms, but consistently lowers LIBERO success (64.0±4.0% Spatial, 52.0±1.0% Goal, 26.0±2.6% Long), a trade-off not attributable to random seed variance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: One-step action generation promises large inference-cost savings for VLA policies, yet whether it preserves closed-loop task success is unresolved. This report asks whether swapping an iterative diffusion-transformer action head for a one-step drifting head in GR00T N1.7 yields usable speedups without systematic success loss.

Method: A GR00T N1.7 variant replaces the iterative diffusion-transformer action head with a one-step drifting action head, plus an overlap-conditioned extension for asynchronous chunk replacement. Multi-seed drifting runs were trained on two NVIDIA A800 GPUs; latency and success were measured on LIBERO with synchronous evaluation.

Result: Action-head forward time drops from ~45.3 ms to 5.0 ms and backbone-plus-head time from ~70.0 ms to 30.6 ms. Success across three seeds is 64.0±4.0% (LIBERO-Spatial), 52.0±1.0% (Goal), and 26.0±2.6% (Long); low seed variance indicates the degradation is systematic rather than random-initialization noise.

Conclusion: The one-step drifting head is a speed--success trade-off, not an overall improvement. Likely causes include deterministic one-step mode averaging, batch-dependent geometry estimation, long open-loop chunk execution, and the fact that synchronous LIBERO evaluation never exercises the asynchronous overlap path.

Abstract: One-step action generation can substantially reduce the inference cost of vision-language-action (VLA) policies, but its effect on closed-loop task success remains an open question. This technical report studies a GR00T N1.7 variant in which the iterative diffusion-transformer action head is replaced by a one-step drifting action head, together with an overlap-conditioned extension for asynchronous chunk replacement. All multi-seed drifting runs were trained on two NVIDIA A800 GPUs. On LIBERO, the action head reduces the mean model-forward time of the action head from approximately $45.3\,\mathrm{ms}$ to $5.0\,\mathrm{ms}$, while the measured backbone-plus-head time falls from approximately $70.0\,\mathrm{ms}$ to $30.6\,\mathrm{ms}$. However, this speedup is accompanied by a systematic reduction in task success. Across three drifting seeds, success is $64.0\pm4.0\%$ on LIBERO-Spatial, $52.0\pm1.0\%$ on LIBERO-Goal, and $26.0\pm2.6\%$ on LIBERO-Long. The low seed variance indicates that the degradation is not explained by random initialization alone. We report the result as a speed--success trade-off rather than an overall improvement, and discuss likely contributing factors including deterministic one-step mode averaging, batch-dependent geometry estimation, long open-loop chunk execution, and the fact that synchronous LIBERO evaluation does not exercise the asynchronous overlap path.

</details>


### [108] [A Comprehensive Review of Generative Physical Artificial Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18111)
*Satyam Gaba,Krutiksinh Rana,Siva Sai,Vinay Chamola,Dusit Niyato*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This survey analyzes Generative Physical AI (GPAI), the integration of large foundation models with physical embodiments in robotics, proposing a taxonomy of five model classes and examining their complementarity, applications, and limitations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The integration of large-scale foundation models with physical embodiments has driven major robotics advances, yielding autonomous agentic systems that perceive, reason, and act in complex real-world settings. This progress motivates a comprehensive, structured analysis of GPAI systems to clarify their architectural foundations, current applications, and key limitations.

Method: The paper is a survey that introduces a taxonomy of five distinct GPAI approaches: Robot Foundation Models (RFMs) for cross-platform skill transfer; Vision-Language Action (VLA) models for end-to-end multi-modal perception and control; Large Behavior Models (LBMs) for human-like movement generation; Diffusion Policy Models (DPMs) for diffusion-based, temporally coherent action generation; and World Foundation Models (WFMs) for physics-compliant simulation and data generation. It also analyzes how these approaches complement one another: WFMs generate training data for VLAs and DPMs, RFMs enable cross-platform deployment of learned policies, and LBMs provide motion priors for natural behavior.

Result: Through examples spanning autonomous vehicles, industrial automation, healthcare robotics, and humanoid systems, the survey identifies significant performance improvements and summarizes promising research directions in data-efficient learning, sim-to-real transfer, edge-compatible architectures, and safety frameworks.

Conclusion: The survey advances embodied AI for IoT-connected environments in which intelligent agents interact with networked sensors, actuators, and edge devices.

Abstract: The integration of large-scale foundation models with physical embodiments has led to significant advancements in robotics known as Generative Physical Artificial Intelligence (GPAI). These agentic AI systems autonomously perceive, reason, and act in complex real-world situations. This survey comprehensively analyzes GPAI systems, focusing on their architectural foundations, current applications, and key limitations. We introduce a taxonomy of five distinct approaches: Robot Foundation Models (RFMs) for cross-platform skill transfer; Vision-Language Action (VLA) models for end-to-end multi-modal perception and control; Large Behavior Models (LBMs) for human-like movement generation; Diffusion Policy Models (DPMs) for diffusion model-based temporally coherent action generation; and World Foundation Models (WFMs) for physics-compliant simulation and data generation. We examine how these approaches complement each other: WFMs generate training data for VLAs and DPMs, RFMs enable cross-platform deployment of learned policies, while LBMs provide motion priors for natural behavior. Through examples across autonomous vehicles, industrial automation, healthcare robotics, and humanoid systems, we identify significant performance improvements and summarize promising research directions in data-efficient learning, sim-to-real transfer, edge-compatible architectures, and safety frameworks. These insights advance embodied AI for IoT-connected environments where intelligent agents interact with networked sensors, actuators, and edge devices.

</details>


### [109] [OpenDexGrasp: Open-vocabulary Task-Oriented Dexterous Grasping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18117)
*Jiyao Zhang,Junhan Wang,Tianyu Wang,Zeyuan Chen,Anthony Bolten,Yitong Peng,Hao Dong*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: OpenDexGrasp is a unified data and generative modeling framework for open-vocabulary task-oriented dexterous grasp generation, using a dual-source dataset (OpenDexVerse) and a shared perception-action latent representation to directly generate task-consistent dexterous grasps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing dexterous grasp synthesis produces stable and physically plausible poses, but real-world manipulation requires grasps that preserve the function implied by the task. The paper addresses open-vocabulary task-oriented dexterous grasp generation, where a robot must infer functional intent from free-form language, ground it in multi-view visual observations and object geometry, and produce an executable high-DoF grasp.

Method: OpenDexVerse provides dual-source supervision organized by the Coverage-to-Alignment (C2A) Recipe: OpenDex-Scale delivers large-scale semantic and geometric coverage via automatic grasp synthesis and vision-language annotation, while OpenDex-Align supplies high-quality embodied alignment via human teleoperation and category-level transfer. OpenDexGrasp learns a shared perception-action latent representation coupling open-vocabulary vision-language context with dexterous action generation; affordance grounding and grasp generation provide complementary supervision over this latent space, enabling direct generation of task-consistent grasps without a separate affordance-to-pose inference stage.

Result: Extensive simulation and real-robot experiments demonstrate improved functional alignment, physical feasibility, generalization to unseen categories, and real-world execution success.

Conclusion: The unified data and generative modeling framework enables open-vocabulary task-oriented dexterous grasp generation, achieving better functional alignment, physical feasibility, generalization, and real-world execution success.

Abstract: Dexterous grasp synthesis has advanced rapidly in generating stable and physically plausible hand poses, but real-world manipulation requires grasps that preserve the function implied by the task. We study open-vocabulary task-oriented dexterous grasp generation, where a robot must infer functional intent from free-form language, ground it in multi-view visual observations and object geometry, and generate an executable high-degree-of-freedom grasp. We present OpenDexGrasp, a unified data and generative modeling framework for this setting. OpenDexVerse provides dual-source supervision organized by the Coverage-to-Alignment (C2A) Recipe: OpenDex-Scale offers large-scale semantic and geometric coverage through automatic grasp synthesis and vision-language annotation, while OpenDex-Align supplies high-quality embodied alignment through human teleoperation and category-level transfer. OpenDexGrasp learns a shared perception-action latent representation that couples open-vocabulary vision-language context with dexterous action generation. Affordance grounding and grasp generation provide complementary supervision over this latent space, enabling direct generation of task-consistent dexterous grasps without a separate affordance-to-pose inference stage. Extensive simulation and real-robot experiments demonstrate improved functional alignment, physical feasibility, generalization to unseen categories, and real-world execution success. Additional details and videos are available at https://opendexgrasp.github.io/.

</details>


### [110] [Fetch My Beer: Synthetic-to-real Hierarchical Policy for Smooth Pick-and-place](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18119)
*Yingyue Li,Chenyangguang Zhang,Ruida Zhang,Bowen Fu,Guangyao Zhai,Xiangyang Ji*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A synthetic-to-real framework combining physically validated data generation with a hierarchical diffusion-based controller enables smooth, stable liquid-filled container transport that suppresses sloshing and spillage, outperforming state-of-the-art manipulation policies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many real-world robotic applications require dynamically sensitive manipulation, where success depends not only on reaching a target state but on maintaining stable object dynamics throughout execution. Conventional pick-and-place paradigms and existing systems fail here because fluid simulation is too costly for online RL, human teleoperation introduces accelerations that induce sloshing during imitation learning, and current policy pipelines optimize task completion rather than dynamic stability.

Method: The authors propose a synthetic-to-real framework coupling physically validated data generation with a hierarchical, diffusion-based controller. The scalable data pipeline synthesizes grasps, filters unstable poses via a vision-language model, and validates transport trajectories through fluid simulation. The policy consists of a high-level module translating language and visual observations into SE(3) control targets, and a latent diffusion controller that plans in a compact latent space before decoding dense action chunks, enabling high control frequency.

Result: Extensive experiments show the system outperforms state-of-the-art manipulation policies in transport smoothness and dynamic stability.

Conclusion: The proposed synthetic-to-real pipeline with a hierarchical diffusion controller effectively addresses dynamically sensitive manipulation, achieving stable liquid transport and surpassing existing policies.

Abstract: Many real-world robotic applications require dynamically sensitive manipulation, where success depends not only on reaching a target state but on maintaining stable object dynamics throughout execution. We study the stable transport of liquid-filled containers, where a robot must move objects to target locations while suppressing sloshing and preventing spillage. Unlike conventional pick-and-place, this task imposes stringent requirements on motion smoothness and trajectory-level stability, exposing clear limitations in existing systems. Specifically, fluid simulation remains too costly for online reinforcement learning; human teleoperation introduces unintended accelerations that induce sloshing during imitation learning; and current policy pipelines optimize for task completion rather than dynamic stability. We propose a synthetic-to-real framework coupling physically validated data generation with a hierarchical, diffusion-based controller. The scalable data pipeline synthesizes grasps, filters unstable poses via a vision-language model, and validates transport trajectories through fluid simulation. The policy is organized with a high-level module that translates language and visual observations into SE(3) control targets, and a latent diffusion controller that first plans efficiently in a compact latent space and then decodes dense action chunks, enabling the high control frequency needed for smooth and stable motion. Extensive experiments show our system outperforms state-of-the-art manipulation policies in transport smoothness and dynamic stability. Our project page: https://fetch-my-beer.github.io/

</details>


### [111] [Prior Evolution and Task Alignment for Aerial Grasping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18153)
*Weiliang Deng,Zhengyang Dang,Yao Mu,Ximin Lyu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper uses learning to address two limitations of trajectory-optimization-based aerial grasping: high nonconvexity and initialization sensitivity, and the mismatch between handcrafted objectives and actual task success.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Aerial grasping, as seen in predatory birds, is typically reproduced via trajectory optimization, but practical deployment suffers from two issues: the optimization is highly nonconvex and initialization-sensitive, so good solutions are hard to find under limited compute; and the prescribed numerical objectives are human-designed abstractions that may not fully capture what determines task success.

Method: Within an analytical planner, the authors first learn a trajectory prior from optimized motions. This prior is then evolved via a CEM-based process that samples initializations, evaluates them with the deployed optimizer, and retains favorable ones as new supervision. An Execution-Aware Critic learns from contact, lift, and completion outcomes to predict whether optimized trajectories will succeed in physical execution; its frozen energy also serves as a differentiable grasping cost so execution data can directly shape trajectory generation.

Result: Simulation and real-world experiments show improved optimization reliability, trajectory consistency, and grasping performance.

Conclusion: Learning can be integrated into an analytical aerial-grasping planner to improve optimization reliability and align generated trajectories with execution-level task success.

Abstract: Aerial grasping is a remarkable capability exhibited by predatory birds, allowing them to capture prey through highly coordinated maneuvers in flight. Inspired by this capability, researchers have developed various formulations to reproduce such maneuvers through trajectory optimization. However, two limitations remain in practice. First, the resulting optimization problem is highly nonconvex and sensitive to initialization, making high-quality solutions difficult to obtain under a limited computational budget. Second, prescribed numerical objectives are human-designed abstractions that describe successful grasping through a limited set of mathematically tractable quantities and may not fully capture what determines task success. We investigate how learning can address these limitations within an analytical planner. Accordingly, a trajectory prior is first learned from optimized motions and then evolved through a CEM-based process that evaluates sampled initializations with the deployed optimizer and retains favorable ones as new supervision. An Execution-Aware Critic learns from contact, lift, and completion outcomes to assess whether the optimized trajectories are likely to succeed in physical execution. Its frozen energy can further serve as a differentiable grasping cost, allowing execution data to directly shape trajectory generation. Simulation and real-world experiments demonstrate improved optimization reliability, trajectory consistency, and grasping performance.

</details>


### [112] [Energy-Regularized Imitation Learning for Force- and Work-Aware Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18164)
*Toshiki Otani,Hiromu Taketsugu,Norimichi Ukita*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes an energy-aware policy fine-tuning method that learns a differentiable predictor of joint-space mechanical work and uses it as a regularizer, achieving a 2.1% work reduction with a slight success-rate gain on RLBench.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Manipulation policies are typically optimized for task success alone, without regard to physical efficiency. A key obstacle is that simulator-side energy/work quantities are non-differentiable, making them unusable as direct training signals. The paper is motivated by the need to convert such physical quantities into differentiable objectives so that pretrained policies can be fine-tuned for energy efficiency without an explicit differentiable dynamics model.

Method: The authors define a joint-space mechanical-work proxy computed from joint torque and angular displacement. They train a differentiable energy predictor that estimates this work from robot states and actions, thereby turning a non-differentiable simulator-side quantity into a differentiable regularizer. This regularizer is used to fine-tune a pretrained manipulation policy, instantiated with RVT-2 on RLBench.

Result: Evaluation covers 12 manipulation tasks spanning object contact, articulated motion, placement, pushing, and sweeping. Fine-tuning reduces average mechanical work from 208.8J to 204.4J (2.1% reduction), while mean task success rate increases slightly from 86.2% to 86.9%.

Conclusion: Work-aware policy optimization can suppress physically inefficient motion without requiring an explicit differentiable dynamics model, and it can do so without sacrificing (and even slightly improving) task success.

Abstract: This paper studies energy-aware manipulation as a physically grounded learning problem. We define a joint-space mechanical-work proxy from joint torque and angular displacement, and train a differentiable energy predictor that estimates this work from robot states and actions. The predictor converts a non-differentiable simulator-side physical quantity into a differentiable regularizer for fine-tuning a pretrained manipulation policy. We instantiate the framework with RVT-2 on RLBench and evaluate 12 manipulation tasks involving object contact, articulated motion, placement, pushing, and sweeping. The proposed fine-tuning reduces the average mechanical work from 208.8J to 204.4J (i.e., 2.1% reduction), while the mean task success rate also increases slightly from 86.2% to 86.9%. These results show that work-aware policy optimization can suppress physically inefficient motion without requiring an explicit differentiable dynamics model.

</details>


### [113] [LUMO: Designing Luminous Contact Morphology for Repeatable Whole-Finger Contact Observation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18165)
*Dong Ho Kang,Youngsu Ko,Luis Sentis*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: LUMO embeds LEDs in a compliant silicone finger pad so contact-induced deformation changes light emitted from the finger's side in a location-dependent pattern, enabling external observation of contact location; combining this optical estimate with joint torque yields force estimation with 1.44 N MAE over 931 samples.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: A low-impedance robot finger's joint torque reveals load magnitude but not where along the finger contact occurs, since a small fingertip force and a large proximal force can produce the same torque. Resolving contact force thus requires externally observable contact location.

Method: LUMO embeds LEDs that illuminate a compliant silicone pad; contact deforms the pad, altering light emerging from the finger's side in a pattern set by load location. The same structure carries the contact load, so its cross-section (pad profile, rigid carrier, lateral void) is optimized for two behaviors simultaneously: mechanical conformance under low preload with increasing carrier restriction as load rises, and optically separated side responses for different contact locations. Optimization uses rigid-soft contact simulation, ray tracing, and multi-objective Bayesian optimization.

Result: Across two silicones, six contact locations, and 10- and 30-mm spherical indenters, optimized morphologies improve neighboring-location separation relative to re-establishing-contact variation by 15-59%. Estimating contact location from the optical response using known LED spacing and combining it with joint torque gives 1.44 N normal-force MAE over 931 samples. In a two-finger hand, localized side responses appear on several links simultaneously during grasps.

Conclusion: LUMO demonstrates a co-designed mechanical-optical finger structure that makes contact location externally observable and, combined with joint torque, enables improved normal-force estimation, extending to multi-link responses in a two-finger hand.

Abstract: A low-impedance robot finger reports through joint torque how strongly it is loaded, but the same torque can arise from a small force near the fingertip or a large force near the joint. Resolving the force therefore requires knowing where along the finger contact occurred. LUMO makes that location externally observable. Embedded LEDs illuminate a compliant silicone pad, and contact deforms the pad so that light emerging from the finger's side changes in a pattern set by where the load acts. Because the same structure also carries the contact load, we optimize its cross-section, including the pad profile, rigid carrier, and lateral void, for two behaviors at once. Mechanically, the pad conforms under low preload while the carrier increasingly restricts further deformation as load rises. Optically, different contact locations produce separated responses on the finger's side. The search uses rigid--soft contact simulation, ray tracing, and multi-objective Bayesian optimization. Across two silicones, six contact locations, and 10- and 30-mm spherical indenters, the optimized morphologies improve neighboring-location separation relative to variation from re-establishing contact by \(15\)--\(59\%\). Estimating contact location from the optical response using the known LED spacing and combining it with joint torque gives \(1.44~\mathrm{N}\) normal-force MAE over 931 samples. In a two-finger hand, localized side responses appear on several links simultaneously during grasps.

</details>


### [114] [Characterizing Replay Retention Under Dynamics Shift in Model-Based Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18167)
*Everest Yang,Skye Thompson,George D. Konidaris*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper studies when recent transitions outperform full replay history in continual model-based RL under changing robot dynamics, characterizing the trade-off via change magnitude and an age-staleness AUC, and showing that replay retention depends on both change magnitude and how dynamics evolve.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Adapting to changes in robot dynamics requires learning from new data without discarding potentially useful experience. In continual model-based RL, replay collected before a dynamics change can slow adaptation, yet removing it unnecessarily reduces training data and is costly if earlier dynamics return. The paper aims to determine when recent transitions are preferable to the full replay history.

Method: The authors formalize the trade-off using two quantities: change magnitude and age-staleness area under the curve (AUC), which measures how well transition age separates stale from fresh data. They test these effects across two locomotion morphologies, two model-based RL algorithms, and Real-World RL benchmark perturbations. Since ground-truth staleness labels are unavailable on deployed robots, they evaluate whether an estimator built from interaction data can supply the quantities needed to select a replay strategy after permanent changes.

Result: Forgetting stale data helps after large permanent shifts but hurts when dynamics recur and older data becomes useful again. The results indicate that replay retention depends on change magnitude and on how the dynamics evolve. An estimator derived from interaction data can provide the quantities needed to choose a replay strategy after permanent changes.

Conclusion: Choosing a replay strategy requires predicting when older data will help or hurt, which depends jointly on change magnitude and the temporal structure of dynamics change. Replay retention decisions should therefore be adaptive rather than fixed, and can in principle be informed by interaction-data estimators when ground-truth staleness labels are unavailable.

Abstract: Adapting to changes in robot dynamics requires learning from new data without discarding experience that may still be useful. In continual model-based reinforcement learning (RL), replay collected before a dynamics change can slow adaptation, while removing it unnecessarily reduces available training data and can be especially costly if earlier dynamics return. We study when recent transitions are preferable to the full replay history. Two quantities characterize this trade-off: change magnitude and age-staleness area under the curve (AUC), measuring how well transition age separates stale from fresh data. Forgetting stale data helps after large permanent shifts but hurts when dynamics recur and older data becomes useful again. Choosing a replay strategy therefore depends on predicting when older data will help or hurt. We test these effects across two locomotion morphologies, two model-based RL algorithms, and Real-World RL benchmark perturbations. Because ground-truth staleness labels are unavailable on deployed robots, we evaluate whether an estimator built from interaction data can still provide the quantities needed to choose a replay strategy after permanent changes. Our results show that replay retention depends on change magnitude and on how the dynamics evolve.

</details>


### [115] [Approximating High Dimensional Self-Motion Manifolds via Deep Generative Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18169)
*Haitao Gao,Yang Song,Liao Wu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper reframes self-motion manifold (SMM) recovery as probabilistic sampling of a conditional posterior over configurations given a target pose, then clustering to separate disjoint components. This formulation is dimension-agnostic, and it is the first method to approximate highly redundant 4-D SMMs for a 7R manipulator, while matching state-of-the-art on 1-D SMMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: An SMM captures the geometric structure of the infinite inverse-kinematic solution set of a redundant manipulator at a fixed end-effector pose, and its efficient recovery is a prerequisite for feasible, globally optimal motion planning. Prior approaches—null-space continuation and learning-based methods—assume the SMM is a curve, so they fail to generalize to higher redundancy orders.

Method: The authors take a probabilistic view: an SMM is treated as the support of the conditional posterior distribution over configurations given a target pose. Recovery therefore reduces to (1) sampling from a learned distribution, and (2) separating disjoint components via clustering. The formulation is independent of manifold dimension and needs no architectural modification as redundancy order increases.

Result: The method approximates 1-D SMMs with performance comparable to the latest null-space continuation and learning-based approaches. It is the first method able to approximate highly redundant 4-D SMMs on a 7R manipulator for position tasks.

Conclusion: By casting SMM recovery as posterior sampling plus clustering, the approach removes the curve assumption and scales with redundancy order without architectural change, providing a general route to approximating high-dimensional SMMs. A project website with code is released.

Abstract: Self-motion manifold (SMM) characterizes the geometric structure of the infinite inverse kinematic solutions set of a redundant manipulator at a fixed end-effector pose, and its efficient recovery underpins feasible and global optimal motion planning. Existing methods such as null-space continuation and learning-based methods are formulated around the assumption that an SMM is a curve, and do not extend to higher redundancy orders. We instead adopt a probabilistic view: SMMs are the support of the conditional posterior over configurations given a target pose, so that recovering it reduces to sampling from a learned distribution and separating its disjoint components by clustering. The formulation is independent of the manifold dimension and requires no architectural change as the redundancy order grows. In this work, we demonstrate that our method can approximate 1-D SMMs with performance comparable to the latest null-space continuation and learning-based approach, and that it is the first method capable of approximating highly redundant 4-D SMMs in a 7R manipulator for position tasks. Project website: \href{https://github.com/accuracy-maker/high-dimenstional-self-motion-manifold-approximation}{https://github.com/accuracy-maker/high-dimenstional-self-motion-manifold-approximation}

</details>


### [116] [TacBPM: A Tactile-conditioned Behavior Prior Model for Dexterous Reorientation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18174)
*Jie Yin,Wanli Xing,Zeyuan Zhao,Xuezhou Zhu,Zhijie Deng,Kaifeng Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper proposes a tactile-conditioned behavior prior model for dexterous in-hand reorientation, distilling multi-scale sphere specialists into a latent controller that downstream policies reuse via residual latent actions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dexterous in-hand manipulation requires coordinating high-DoF hand joints under intermittent, contact-rich interaction. Policies must discover finger gaits preserving object stability while adapting to geometry, anisotropy, pose, contact, and sensing changes—beyond simple target-orientation tracking.

Method: The authors distill multi-scale sphere specialists into a latent controller and condition the prior on tactile-proprioceptive history so latent behavior reflects current hand-object interaction. Downstream policies reuse the fixed tactile prior through residual latent actions, reducing renewed exploration from raw joint commands.

Result: Evaluations cover arbitrary-pose transfer across anisotropic objects, commanded-axis rotation, and an arm-hand Grasp-to-AnyPose task requiring grasp, lift, transport, and goal-pose reaching for novel tool geometries and generalized placements. The method accelerates training and enables stable policies where matched raw-action PPO remains near failure, with successful sim-to-real transfer in in-hand and arm-hand tasks.

Conclusion: A tactile-conditioned behavior prior with residual latent action reuse provides an effective and transferable approach for dexterous reorientation, outperforming raw-action PPO baselines and achieving sim-to-real transfer.

Abstract: Dexterous in-hand manipulation requires policies that coordinate high-DoF hand joints through intermittent, contact-rich interaction. Beyond target-orientation tracking, such policies must discover finger gaits that preserve object stability while adapting to geometry, anisotropy, pose, contact, and sensing changes. We propose \method, a tactile-conditioned behavior prior model for dexterous reorientation. \method distills multi-scale sphere specialists into a latent controller and lets downstream policies reuse the fixed tactile prior through residual latent actions, reducing renewed exploration from raw joint commands. The prior conditions on tactile-proprioceptive history so latent behavior reflects the current hand-object interaction. We evaluate arbitrary-pose transfer across anisotropic objects, commanded-axis rotation, and an arm-hand Grasp-to-AnyPose task in which the robot must grasp, lift, transport, and reach goal poses for novel tool geometries and generalized placements. Extensive experiments demonstrate that the proposed method accelerates training and enables stable policies where matched raw-action PPO remains near failure, with successful sim-to-real transfer in in-hand and arm-hand tasks.

</details>


### [117] [OmniRisk: Omnidirectional Trajectory-Risk Learning for Agile Quadrotor Dynamic Avoidance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18191)
*Yifan He,Yang Liu,Wenhao Zhao,Hai Lin,Deping Zhang,Mingze Ma,Fei Gao,Huan Yu,Zipeng Dai,Ziming Ding*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: OmniRisk is an omnidirectional planning framework that learns trajectory-level risk offline via a circular convolutional network over LiDAR panoramas, dynamic masks, and surface velocities, enabling efficient onboard quadrotor evasion of fast-moving obstacles up to 15 m/s relative speed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sparse range observations do not directly reveal obstacle motion, and online trajectory optimizers either scale poorly with the number of obstacles or sacrifice reliability in dense, high-speed encounters. Reliable predictive avoidance within short reaction windows thus remains challenging.

Method: A fixed-dimensional tensor jointly encodes LiDAR range panoramas, dynamic masks, and Cartesian surface velocities. An asymmetric risk field aligned with obstacle velocity emphasizes approaching interactions and attenuates receding ones, and its accumulation along predicted relative trajectories yields dense supervision. A dual-branch circular convolutional network predicts terminal boundary states and dynamic risks for candidate primitives over an omnidirectional anchor lattice in a single forward pass, followed by selection and closed-form reconstruction of the chosen primitive.

Result: Real-world flights demonstrate consecutive evasive maneuvers at relative encounter speeds up to 15 m/s without fine-tuning. Risk-inference cost is independent of obstacle count, and online risk accumulation along trajectories is removed.

Conclusion: OmniRisk enables efficient onboard avoidance by shifting trajectory-level risk learning offline, decoupling inference cost from obstacle count while maintaining reliability in dense, high-speed encounters.

Abstract: Agile quadrotor avoidance of fast-moving obstacles requires anticipating collisions and selecting feasible maneuvers within short reaction windows. Reliable predictive avoidance remains challenging because sparse range observations do not directly reveal obstacle motion, while online trajectory optimizers either scale poorly with obstacle count or remain efficient at the expense of reliability in dense, high-speed encounters. We present OmniRisk, an omnidirectional planning framework that learns trajectory-level risk offline for efficient onboard evasion. A fixed-dimensional tensor combines LiDAR range panoramas, dynamic masks, and Cartesian surface velocities to represent geometry and motion jointly. We formulate an asymmetric risk field aligned with obstacle velocity that emphasizes approaching interactions and attenuates receding ones. Accumulating this risk along predicted relative trajectories provides dense supervision and discourages unnecessary hesitation after obstacles pass. A dual-branch circular convolutional network predicts terminal boundary states and dynamic risks for candidate primitives over an omnidirectional anchor lattice in a single forward pass, followed by selection and closed-form reconstruction of the selected candidate primitive. This formulation removes online risk accumulation along trajectories and makes risk-inference cost independent of obstacle count. OmniRisk enables efficient onboard avoidance, with real-world flights demonstrating consecutive evasive maneuvers at relative encounter speeds up to 15 m/s without fine-tuning. Code is available at https://github.com/VANdexj/OmniRisk.

</details>


### [118] [WAVE-Go: World-Model Navigation with Adaptive Execution for Wheel-Legged Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18193)
*Mingyi Li,Ji Li,Zhihao Ouyang,Yage He,Börje F. Karlsson*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: WAVE-Go is an image-goal navigation framework that separates world-action prediction from interruptible command execution, enabling adaptive action-prefix selection and cancellation of pending commands when new observations invalidate execution, improving success and reducing collisions for wheel-legged robots.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: World models predict navigation action consequences, but predicted action sequences can become invalid during execution, especially for wheel-legged robots facing dynamic obstacles or locomotion-mode changes. Fixed-length execution and non-adaptive replanning therefore create a mismatch between planned commands and evolving conditions.

Method: The framework decouples world-action prediction from an interruptible executor. The executor adaptively selects an action prefix and cancels pending commands when updated observations invalidate execution. A conditional-risk formulation specifies prefix selection under an estimated cumulative failure budget.
