Posture and locomotion-mode transitions are gated by clearance, stability, and task-evidence checks.

Result: WAVE-Go attains 74.1% in-distribution success and 63.3% dynamic out-of-distribution success, exceeding the strongest baseline by 4.7 and 7.7 percentage points, and reducing collisions from 4.4 to 2.9 per 100 m. Versus interruptible fixed four-command execution, it improves success by 4.0 percentage points while cutting replanning frequency by 51.2% and collision rate by 6.5%. Ablations show runtime interruption improves success, collision rate, and reaction latency at the cost of extra replanning.

Conclusion: Adaptive, interruptible execution balances navigation performance against planning overhead. Separating prediction from interruptible command execution is a viable design for robust image-goal navigation under dynamic and mode-changing conditions.

Abstract: World models can anticipate the consequences of navigation actions, but predicted action sequences may become invalid during execution, especially when wheel-legged robots encounter dynamic obstacles or change locomotion modes. We propose WAVE-Go, an image-goal navigation framework that separates world-action prediction from interruptible command execution. Its executor adaptively selects an action prefix and cancels pending commands when updated observations invalidate execution. A conditional-risk formulation specifies prefix selection under an estimated cumulative failure budget, while posture and locomotion-mode transitions require clearance, stability, and task-evidence checks. In the reported navigation evaluation, WAVE-Go achieves 74.1% in-distribution success and 63.3% dynamic out-of-distribution success, exceeding the strongest baseline by 4.7 and 7.7 percentage points, respectively, while reducing collisions from 4.4 to 2.9 per 100 m. Compared with interruptible fixed four-command execution, WAVE-Go raises success by 4.0 percentage points while reducing replanning frequency by 51.2% and collision rate by 6.5%. Execution ablations also show that runtime interruption improves success, collision rate, and reaction latency at the cost of additional replanning. These results support adaptive, interruptible execution as a means of balancing navigation performance and planning overhead. Code is available at https://github.com/vigorlee/wave-go.

</details>


### [119] [WholeBodyWAM: Learning Whole-Body World Action Models with Scalable Motion Priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18197)
*Bowei Zhang,Qiyao Zhang,Shuanghao Bai,Xinhua Wang,Meng Li,Yilei Wang,Leiwang Zhang,Jian Tang,Lu Zhou,Lei Sun,Zhengping Che*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: WholeBodyWAM is a humanoid world-action model that pretrains a language-conditioned Motion Expert on UniMotion-4K (>4K hours of heterogeneous human/humanoid motion) to provide a transferable predictive prior, then integrates it with Video and Action Experts via asymmetric MoT attention for embodiment-specific action generation, improving motion prediction, task performance, data efficiency, and real-world transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Humanoid whole-body manipulation needs coordinated whole-body dynamics, but collecting large-scale trajectories from the target robot is expensive and hard to scale. Human and humanoid whole-body motion data is abundant yet not directly usable as embodiment-specific robot actions. The paper asks whether such scalable motion resources can serve as a transferable predictive prior for humanoid world-action modeling.

Method: The authors curate UniMotion-4K, a motion corpus of over 4K hours from human videos, native 3D motion datasets, and heterogeneous humanoid platforms, canonicalized into a unified motion space. A language-conditioned Motion Expert is pretrained to predict future whole-body motion without target-robot action supervision. During robot post-training, this pretrained expert is integrated with Video and Action Experts through asymmetric Mixture-of-Transformers (MoT) attention, so predictive scene dynamics and whole-body motion jointly inform embodiment-specific action generation.

Result: Experiments show WholeBodyWAM consistently benefits from increased motion-pretraining scale, improves future-motion prediction and downstream task performance, and transfers effectively to real-world humanoid manipulation. The pretrained motion prior also substantially improves data efficiency under limited target-robot demonstrations.

Conclusion: Large-scale heterogeneous human/humanoid motion can serve as an effective transferable predictive prior for humanoid world-action modeling, enabling scale benefits, better prediction and control, real-world transfer, and strong data efficiency without requiring large target-robot trajectory collections.

Abstract: Humanoid whole-body manipulation requires coordinated whole-body dynamics, yet large-scale trajectories from a target robot are expensive to collect and difficult to scale. In contrast, whole-body motion from human and humanoid sources is abundantly available, although such data cannot be directly used as embodiment-specific robot actions. This work asks whether these scalable motion resources can instead provide a transferable predictive prior for humanoid world-action modeling. We introduce WholeBodyWAM, a humanoid world-action model that learns whole-body dynamics from large-scale heterogeneous motion before target-robot training. We curate UniMotion-4K, a motion corpus spanning more than 4K hours from human videos, native 3D motion datasets, and heterogeneous humanoid platforms, and canonicalize these diverse sources into a unified motion space. A language-conditioned Motion Expert is then pretrained to predict future whole-body motion without target-robot action supervision. During robot post-training, the pretrained Motion Expert is integrated with Video and Action Experts through asymmetric Mixture-of-Transformers (MoT) attention, enabling predictive scene dynamics and whole-body motion to jointly inform embodiment-specific action generation. Experiments show that WholeBodyWAM consistently benefits from increased motion-pretraining scale, improves future-motion prediction and downstream task performance, and transfers effectively to real-world humanoid manipulation. Moreover, the pretrained motion prior substantially improves data efficiency under limited target-robot demonstrations.

</details>


### [120] [Reinforcement Learning for Real-Time Vision-Language-Action Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18207)
*Perry Dong,Kuo-Han Hung,Dorsa Sadigh,Chelsea Finn*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Real-Time EXPO-FT decouples slow VLA action generation from fast reactive action edits, enabling RL fine-tuning that meets real-time control needs; it outperforms delayed and non-delayed baselines in all 10 Kinetix environments and raises real-world dynamic task performance from 42% to 97% within 10 minutes of online data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large pretrained VLA models offer promise for reliable robot deployment, but their high inference latency causes stale observations, creating a distribution shift that degrades reliability. Prior asynchronous policy execution methods are mostly imitation-learning-based and lack a mechanism to move beyond the training distribution toward higher reliability.

Method: The approach builds on EXPO-FT, a framework for sample-efficient, reliable VLA fine-tuning with RL, and decouples slow, expressive action generation from fast, reactive action edits. A large pretrained VLA proposes action chunks using its behavior prior, while a lightweight edit policy performs fast, reactive decision-making by editing actions in response to state changes, conditioned on the latest observation. This is instantiated as Real-Time EXPO-FT, an RL framework for finetuning real-time VLA policies.

Result: On the Kinetix benchmark, Real-Time EXPO-FT enables a delayed policy to achieve the best performance among delayed and non-delayed methods in 10 out of 10 environments. On four dynamic real-world tasks (robot object passing, ball balancing, table soccer kicking, and dynamic object picking) with online robot data capped at 10 minutes, it improves average policy performance from 42% to 97%, all without human intervention.

Conclusion: Real-Time EXPO-FT demonstrates rapid, sample-efficient adaptation to challenging real-world dynamics, meeting the real-time control requirements of dynamic manipulation and closing the gap between asynchronous execution and RL fine-tuning for reliable VLA deployment.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning fine-tuning on top of large, pretrained Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models offers promise for highly reliable robot deployment. However, because of their scale, modern VLA models suffer from high inference latency, so the observation used to select an action is often stale by execution time, creating a distribution shift that can substantially degrade reliability and performance. Prior work has explored asynchronous policy execution to reduce the effect of latency, but these methods are mostly built on imitation learning and offer no mechanism for moving beyond the training distribution toward higher reliability. We close this gap by enabling RL fine-tuning that meets the real-time control requirements of dynamic real-world manipulation. Our approach builds on EXPO-FT, a framework for sample-efficient, reliable VLA fine-tuning with reinforcement learning, and decouples slow, expressive action generation from fast, reactive action edits: a large pretrained VLA proposes action chunks using its strong behavior prior, while a lightweight edit policy performs fast, reactive decision-making by editing actions in response to changes in state, conditioned on the latest observation. We instantiate this as Real-Time EXPO-FT, an RL framework for finetuning real-time VLA policies. On the Kinetix benchmark, Real-Time EXPO-FT enables a delayed policy to achieve the best performance among delayed and non-delayed methods in 10 out of 10 environments. On four dynamic real-world tasks, robot object passing, ball balancing, table soccer kicking, and dynamic object picking, with online robot data capped at 10 minutes, Real-Time EXPO-FT improves average policy performance from 42% to 97%, all without human intervention, demonstrating rapid, sample-efficient adaptation to challenging real-world dynamics. Website: https://pd-perry.github.io/real-time-expo-ft

</details>


### [121] [CANTABILE: Learning Expressive Dynamics for Robotic Piano Performance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18213)
*Woosik Kim,Wonhyeok Choi,Sunghoon Im*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: CANTABILE, a dynamics-aware framework for robotic piano performance, moves beyond pitch/onset note accuracy to reward and evaluate expressive velocity dynamics, raising Velocity F1 from 0.06 to 0.34 on the EXPRESSIVE-51 benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work on robotic piano playing evaluates progress almost exclusively via note accuracy (which keys are pressed and when), leaving musical dynamics essential for expressive performance neither rewarded nor evaluated.

Method: The framework (i) closes the score-to-contact loop by conditioning the policy on upcoming velocity goals and mapping each key's angular velocity at onset back to MIDI velocity, (ii) couples a velocity-fidelity reward with an onset-coverage reward to prevent gaming dynamics by omitting difficult notes, and (iii) refines a frozen dynamics-aware base policy with an alpha-scaled, finger-only residual that localizes strike-intensity adaptation away from nominal note execution.

Result: On EXPRESSIVE-51, a dynamics-rich 51-song subset of RoboPianist, CANTABILE raises Velocity F1 (jointly measuring pitch, onset, and intensity within a +/-8 MIDI-velocity tolerance) from 0.06 to 0.34 over the RoboPianist baseline, improves all 51 songs, more than halves matched-note velocity error, and reduces log-mel distance to reference audio by 8%. Intensity-randomized training enables runtime control of performance intensity without retraining.

Conclusion: CANTABILE establishes dynamics as a first-class objective in robotic piano performance, demonstrating substantial gains in expressive velocity fidelity while preserving note execution and enabling runtime intensity control.

Abstract: Robotic piano playing has emerged as a standard benchmark for dexterous bimanual manipulation, yet progress on it has been measured almost entirely by note accuracy -- which keys are pressed (pitch) and when (onset) -- leaving the musical dynamics essential for expressive performance neither rewarded nor evaluated. We propose CANTABILE, a dynamics-aware framework for robotic piano performance that (i) closes the score-to-contact loop by conditioning the policy on upcoming velocity goals and mapping each key's angular velocity at onset back to MIDI velocity, (ii) couples a velocity-fidelity reward with an onset-coverage reward, so that dynamics cannot be improved by omitting difficult notes, and (iii) refines a frozen dynamics-aware base policy with an alpha-scaled, finger-only residual that localizes strike-intensity adaptation away from nominal note execution. On EXPRESSIVE-51, a dynamics-rich 51-song subset of RoboPianist, CANTABILE raises Velocity F1 -- jointly measuring pitch, onset, and intensity within a +/-8 MIDI-velocity tolerance -- from 0.06 to 0.34 over the RoboPianist baseline, improves all 51 songs, more than halves matched-note velocity error, and reduces log-mel distance to reference audio by 8%. Intensity-randomized training further enables runtime control of performance intensity without retraining.

</details>


### [122] [UMI-Bridge: Action-Anchored Latent Alignment across Human and Robot Manipulation Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18232)
*Haiyi Liu,Jingming Ma,Ke Rui,Yuteng Wei,Yuan Ma,Yushen Zuo,Honglong Tian,Haoran Jia,Weitao Zhou,Jiawei Wang,Minglei Li,Shiyi Chen,Haiyan Mao,Jiaqi Zhang,Chun Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: UMI-Bridge aligns human manipulation data (egocentric video and UMI demos) with robot data by anchoring latent representations to action equivalence instead of visual appearance, enabling data-efficient robot learning and UMI-to-robot transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-robot demonstrations are scarce, motivating the use of robot-free human data such as egocentric videos and handheld UMI demonstrations. However, differences in viewpoint, embodiment, and action supervision hinder aligning representations by manipulation motion rather than visual appearance.

Method: UMI-Bridge uses UMI as an intermediate domain for action-equivalence alignment. UMI action supervision anchors the latent space to end-effector motion and gripper behavior; synchronized head-wrist observations and paired ego-UMI clips enable cross-view/domain alignment. A dual-view latent action model (LAM) is trained on robot-free human data, then its wrist teacher and dynamics model are frozen to regularize VLA post-training on UMI and robot data. The shared wrist interface provides training-time supervision across domains while keeping the standard inference architecture.

Result: Across three real-robot tasks, UMI-Bridge reaches 91.7% mean success vs. 73.3% for Naive Co-training with matched UMI and robot data. On two data-efficiency tasks, it outperforms a full-data Robot-only baseline using only 25% of robot demos plus UMI data. It also attains 85% and 90% success on two tasks learned from UMI demos without task-specific robot demos.

Conclusion: Action-anchored latent alignment supports data-efficient robot learning and UMI-to-robot task transfer.

Abstract: Real-robot demonstrations are limited, motivating the use of human manipulation data collected without robots, including egocentric videos and handheld Universal Manipulation Interface (UMI) demonstrations. However, differences in viewpoint, embodiment, and available action supervision make it difficult to align representations across these sources according to manipulation motion rather than visual appearance. We introduce UMI-Bridge, which uses UMI as an intermediate domain to align representations according to action equivalence rather than pixel similarity. UMI action supervision anchors the latent representation to end-effector motion and gripper behavior, while synchronized head-wrist observations and paired ego-UMI clips support alignment across views and domains. We train a dual-view latent action model (LAM) on human manipulation data without robot demonstrations, then freeze its wrist teacher and dynamics model to regularize vision-language-action (VLA) post-training on UMI and robot data. The shared wrist interface enables this training-time supervision across both domains while preserving the policy's standard inference architecture. Across three real-robot tasks, UMI-Bridge achieves 91.7% mean success versus 73.3% for Naive Co-training with matched UMI and robot data. On two data-efficiency tasks, it surpasses a full-data Robot-only baseline using 25% of the robot demonstrations together with UMI data. It also achieves 85% and 90% success on two additional tasks learned from UMI demonstrations without task-specific robot demonstrations. These results support action-anchored latent alignment for data-efficient robot learning and UMI-to-robot task transfer.

</details>


### [123] [ForceDelta-VLA: Distilling Force-Conditioned ActionCorrections for Contact-Rich Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18242)
*Ju Dong,Yu Fu,Jian Chen,Yimeng Liu,Haocheng Zhao,Lei Zhang,Kaixin Bai,Liding Zhang,Diwen Zheng,Alois Christian Knoll,Angela P. Schoellig,Jianwei Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ForceDelta-VLA is a correction-distillation framework that decomposes force-aware VLA action prediction into a reusable reference action plus an explicit force/delay correction, yielding a lightweight policy that adapts to contact changes between reference-action updates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Contact-rich manipulation benefits from force-aware VLA policies, but existing methods entangle task-level motion and contact-dependent adjustment within a single action prediction. Demonstrations lack explicit labels to decompose this prediction into a reusable reference action and a correction, limiting reusability and contact responsiveness.

Method: The framework constructs an explicit force-correction target by pairing predictions from a frozen teacher's force-conditioned and learned force-agnostic modes, plus a delay-correction target that accounts for reference-action mismatch and reference-state change. Training employs asynchronous schedule replay with cached task context available at execution. The resulting lightweight policy adjusts reference actions using recent force history and robot state, reacting to contact changes between reference-action updates without regenerating full action chunks.

Result: Across nine single-arm and bimanual contact-rich tasks, ForceDelta-VLA reaches an 82.2% mean success rate versus 54.4% for the ForceVLA baseline; direct execution of the Stage-1 Temporal Teacher achieves 70.6%. Relative to ForceVLA, the complete system cuts mean peak contact force over successful trials by roughly 26% on both platforms.

Conclusion: Explicitly distilling force and delay corrections from paired teacher predictions enables a lightweight, reusable correction policy that substantially improves success and reduces contact force in contact-rich manipulation, outperforming both the base ForceVLA and the temporal teacher alone.

Abstract: Force-aware Vision-Language-Action (VLA) policies improve contact-rich manipulation, but typically combine task-level motion and contact-dependent adjustment in a single action prediction. Demonstrations provide no explicit labels for decomposing that prediction into a reusable reference action and a correction. We present ForceDelta-VLA, a correction-distillation framework that constructs an explicit force-correction target using paired predictions from a frozen teacher's force-conditioned and learned force-agnostic modes. A separate delay-correction target accounts for reference-action mismatch and the change in reference state. Training uses asynchronous schedule replay with the cached task context available during execution. The resulting lightweight policy adjusts the reference actions using recent force history and robot state, responding to contact changes between reference-action updates without regenerating complete action chunks. Across nine single-arm and bimanual contact-rich tasks, ForceDelta-VLA achieves an 82.2% mean success rate, compared with 54.4% for the original ForceVLA baseline. Direct execution of our Stage-1 Temporal Teacher achieves 70.6%. Relative to ForceVLA, the complete system reduces mean peak contact force over successful trials by approximately 26% on both platforms.

</details>


### [124] [Acting in Meters: Learning Metric Interactions for Precise Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18243)
*Lijie Wang,Zheng Lu,Yiming Wang,Heyang Yu,Kenghou Hoi,Bowen Hu,Di Cui,Tianyu Xin,Haoran Liao,Wanqi Zhong,Xingjie Fan,Yizhao Xu,Ziliang Wang,Fei Gao,Yiming Li*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper introduces a metric interaction framework that makes object-level and scene-level spatial relations explicit in Cartesian space for language-conditioned robotic manipulation, improving VLA and WAM baselines with minimal added parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Vision-Language-Action (VLA) and World-Action Models (WAM) advance language-conditioned manipulation but leave metric relations among actions, objects, and scene geometry implicit. Human manipulation couples semantic understanding of task-relevant objects with spatial feedback guiding hand motion relative to objects and surroundings, motivating explicit metric interaction modeling.

Method: A metric interaction framework modeling interactions at a shared metric scale. At the object level, Interaction-Centric Tokens (ICTs) explicitly represent end-effector pose trajectories relative to manipulated objects and are jointly denoised with actions, providing physically grounded interaction supervision. At the scene level, the Metric Action Interaction Field (MAIF) uses action and ICT queries to attend to metric scene point-cloud features and learns geometry-conditioned action corrections. A two-stage adaptation procedure integrates this into VLA and WAM baselines.

Result: Average success-rate gains of 0.80 percentage points on LIBERO and 3.59 percentage points on RoboTwin 2.0, plus 6.80 percentage points on real-world tasks and 7.45 percentage points on their out-of-distribution variants, achieved with few additional parameters and training steps.

Conclusion: Explicitly modeling object-level and scene-level metric interactions in shared Cartesian space yields consistent improvements across diverse VLA and WAM baselines, including real-world and out-of-distribution settings, at low parameter and training cost.

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action models and World-Action Models have advanced language-conditioned robotic manipulation, yet often leave metric relations among actions, objects, and scene geometry implicit. Human manipulation combines semantic understanding of task-relevant objects with spatial feedback that guides hand motion relative to objects and their surroundings. Inspired by this, we introduce a metric interaction framework that models object-level and scene-level interactions in physical Cartesian space at a shared metric scale. At the object level, Interaction-Centric Tokens (ICTs) explicitly represent end-effector pose trajectories relative to manipulated objects and are jointly denoised with actions, providing physically grounded interaction supervision. At the scene level, the Metric Action Interaction Field (MAIF) uses action and ICT queries to attend to metric scene point-cloud features and learns geometry-conditioned action corrections. Through two-stage adaptation, our framework improves diverse VLA and WAM baselines with a small number of additional parameters and training steps. Experiments demonstrate average success-rate gains of 0.80 and 3.59 percentage points on LIBERO and RoboTwin~2.0, respectively, alongside gains of 6.80 percentage points on real-world tasks and 7.45 percentage points on their out-of-distribution variants.

</details>


### [125] [A3P5 NEMESIS Integrated Rover Design for Environmental Reconnaissance and Robotic Sampling with Reproducible Mobility Analysis and an External Data Machine Learning Calibration Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18245)
*Shafi Bin Sultan,Sabik Bin Sultan,Safwan Sadad*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A design study for the A3P5 NEMESIS four-wheel rover combining photo-constrained geometric reconstruction, subsystem architecture, and a reproducible analytical assessment, with an exploratory literature search and a public-data CO calibration benchmark; physical results remain to be validated.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To combine remote inspection, environmental observation, and lightweight manipulation in a single serviceable four-wheel rover platform, while clearly separating physical prototype evidence from proposed functions and establishing priority measurements and validation requirements.

Method: Exploratory search of 5,000 records across ten queries (4,897 distinct DOIs, 1,212 metadata candidates) informed design; photo-constrained geometric reconstruction of the rover configuration (carbon-pattern enclosure, independently steered wheel assemblies, folded manipulator, inclined camera mast, side sampling equipment); analytical torque and static tipping models; and a public-data calibration benchmark using 7,344 eligible hourly observations, eight sensor/environmental predictors, chronological train/validation/test partitions, and validation-selected ridge regression with daily-block bootstrap intervals.

Result: Declared 24 kg scenario predicts 3.28 N·m gearbox-output torque per wheel on a 20-degree grade under equal load sharing; a 2 kg forward payload reduces the geometric front-tipping bound from 38.1 to 32.7 degrees. Ridge regression achieves held-out CO RMSE of 0.502 mg/m³ (95% daily-block bootstrap interval 0.435-0.569 mg/m³), though this concerns an external sensor array only.

Conclusion: The combined analysis yields a traceable engineering design study and evaluation framework—identifying priority measurements, proposed control interfaces, and mission-specific validation requirements—for a prototype whose integrated field performance remains to be established; the reported figures are design screens and benchmark results, not measured operating limits or NEMESIS accuracy.

Abstract: A3P5 NEMESIS is a four-wheel rover intended to combine remote inspection, environmental observation and lightweight manipulation within one serviceable platform. This study develops a photo-constrained geometric reconstruction, a subsystem architecture and a reproducible analytical assessment while distinguishing physical prototype evidence from proposed functions. An exploratory search retrieved 5,000 bibliographic records across ten queries, yielding 4,897 distinct DOI records and 1,212 metadata candidates; selected primary studies and technical documents informed the design. The reconstructed configuration retains the carbon-pattern enclosure, independently steered wheel assemblies, folded manipulator, inclined camera mast and side sampling equipment. A declared 24 kg scenario predicts 3.28 newton-metres of gearbox-output torque per wheel on a 20-degree grade under equal load sharing; a separate static model shows how a 2 kg forward payload reduces the geometric front-tipping bound from 38.1 degrees to 32.7 degrees. These are design screens, not measured operating limits. A public-data calibration benchmark uses 7,344 eligible hourly observations, eight sensor/environmental predictors and chronological training, validation and test partitions. Validation-selected ridge regression achieves a held-out CO root-mean-square error of 0.502 milligrams per cubic metre, with a 95% daily-block bootstrap interval of 0.435-0.569 milligrams per cubic metre. This result concerns an external sensor array and cannot establish NEMESIS accuracy. The combined analysis identifies priority measurements, proposed control interfaces and mission-specific validation requirements. The contribution is a traceable engineering design study and evaluation framework for a prototype whose integrated field performance remains to be established.

</details>


### [126] [${M}^2$Tok: Multi-head Multi-codebook Discrete Action Tokenization for Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18259)
*Chunpu Xu,Zhixuan Liang,Yuhao Zhang,Chi-Min Chan,Jessie Wang,Yang Xiao,Mengkang Hu,Xiaokang Yang,Yao Mu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper proposes M²Tok, a Multi-head Multi-codebook Action Tokenizer that reduces the discretization bottleneck in Vision-Language-Action models by improving action token reconstruction fidelity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autoregressive language models have been adapted to multimodal signals like images and actions, but raw action signals are continuous and require effective tokenization into compact discrete tokens. Existing discrete action tokenizers suffer from high reconstruction loss, failing to preserve the fine-grained dynamics needed for precise control, which creates a "discretization bottleneck" limiting downstream VLA performance.

Method: M²Tok introduces two structural innovations: (1) decomposing latent action features into multiple heads so the model can implicitly align specific heads with distinct action dimensions, and (2) assigning independent codebooks to each head for quantization, leveraging the combinatorial nature of multiple codebooks to expand representational expressivity.

Result: Evaluated on RoboTwin, Simpler-Env, and three zero-shot real-world tasks, M²Tok-based VLA achieves superior reconstruction fidelity and significantly boosts success rates of VLA models. Ablation studies confirm the effectiveness of the multi-head and multi-codebook mechanisms.

Conclusion: M²Tok substantially lowers reconstruction loss versus prior methods, mitigating the discretization bottleneck and enhancing downstream VLA policy performance.

Abstract: Recent advancements have successfully adapted autoregressive language models to process multimodal signals, such as images and actions. Since raw action signals are continuous, effective tokenization is essential to map high-dimensional inputs into compact discrete tokens for autoregressive processing. However, existing discrete action tokenizers often suffer from high reconstruction loss, failing to preserve the fine-grained dynamics required for precise control. This ``discretization bottleneck'' significantly limits the performance ceiling of downstream Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models. To address this, we propose $\mathcal{M}^2$Tok, a Multi-head Multi-codebook Action Tokenizer designed to minimize reconstruction error and enhance policy performance. Our approach introduces two key structural innovations: (1) we decompose the latent action features into multiple heads, enabling the model to implicitly align specific heads with distinct action dimensions; (2) we assign independent codebooks to each head for quantization. By leveraging the combinatorial nature of multiple codebooks, we significantly expand the representational expressivity of the tokenizer, leading to substantially lower reconstruction loss compared to previous methods. We evaluate the $\mathcal{M}^2$Tok-based VLA on the RoboTwin, Simpler-Env, and 3 zero-shot real-world tasks. Experimental results demonstrate our method not only achieves superior reconstruction fidelity but also significantly boosts the success rate of VLA models. Comprehensive ablation studies further confirm the effectiveness of the multi-head and multi-codebook mechanisms. Code is available at \href{https://github.com/cpaaax/M2Tok}{https://github.com/cpaaax/M2Tok}.

</details>


### [127] [Function-Preserving Data Generation for Zero-Shot Real-to-Sim-to-Real Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18293)
*Tianyi Xiang,Xupeng Xie,Jiahang Cao,Andrew F. Luo,Haoang Li,Jun Ma*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper proposes a function-preserving Real-to-Sim-to-Real framework that generates physically valid, geometrically diverse synthetic demonstrations by constraint-guided mesh deformation and consistent transfer of task poses and collision proxies, enabling zero-shot policy deployment for contact-rich tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scaling robot learning without large-scale real-world data is desirable, but generating geometrically diverse yet physically valid data for contact-rich tasks remains difficult. Success often hinges on precise geometric interfaces, and standard shape augmentation distorts task-critical interfaces, producing invalid contacts such as fit mismatches or interpenetration that make downstream interactions infeasible.

Method: A function-preserving Real-to-Sim-to-Real framework generates synthetic demonstrations from reconstructed assets without requiring teleoperated source trajectories. It augments task-relevant object geometries via constraint-guided mesh deformation, alongside physically consistent transfer of task poses and collision proxies. Visual domain randomization is applied during simulation rollouts to support robust zero-shot policy deployment without real-world fine-tuning.

Result: Extensive experiments in both real-world and simulation settings show robust generalization across unseen object geometries and diverse visual conditions in contact-rich and long-horizon tasks.

Conclusion: The method offers a practical path toward scalable robot learning for contact-rich tasks via shape deformation, enabling robust zero-shot deployment without real-world fine-tuning.

Abstract: Robotic data generation is a promising paradigm for scaling robot learning without collecting large-scale real-world data. However, generating geometrically diverse yet physically valid data for contact-rich tasks remains challenging, especially when success depends on precise geometric interfaces. Standard shape augmentation methods often distort task-critical interfaces, resulting in invalid contact relationships, e.g., fit mismatches or interpenetration, rendering downstream interactions infeasible. To address these limitations, we propose a function-preserving Real-to-Sim-to-Real framework that generates synthetic demonstrations from reconstructed assets without teleoperated source trajectories. Our method augments task-relevant object geometries through constraint-guided mesh deformation, together with physically consistent transfer of task poses and collision proxies. Visual domain randomization is further applied during simulation rollouts, enabling robust zero-shot policy deployment without real-world fine-tuning. Extensive experiments in both real-world and simulation settings demonstrate that our method enables robust generalization across unseen object geometries and diverse visual conditions in contact-rich and long-horizon tasks. Our method provides a practical path toward scalable robot learning for contact-rich tasks via shape deformation.

</details>


### [128] [RAFAIL: Relationship-Aware Failure Detection for Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18324)
*Loris Schneider,Edgar Welte,Rania Rayyes*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: RAFAIL detects robotic manipulation failures by applying OOD detection to task-relevant entity relationships (e.g., gripper-object), using a VLM offline to annotate progress and relationship importance, avoiding runtime VLM inference and failure data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Failure detection is critical for reliable robotic manipulation. VLMs can assess outcomes semantically but incur runtime computation costs, while generic OOD detectors often flag harmless scene variation instead of genuine failure-relevant deviations.

Method: RAFAIL focuses OOD detection on task-relevant relationships between entities. Offline, a VLM annotates successful demonstrations with task progress and relationship importance; these supervise learning of point-cloud-based relationship representations independent of policy-internal features. At runtime, relationship-specific OOD detectors score these representations while importance and progress are predicted without VLM inference. No failure data is required.

Result: RAFAIL achieves 73.4% balanced accuracy across three real-world robotic manipulation tasks, outperforming the strongest OOD- and uncertainty-based baselines.

Conclusion: Centering OOD detection on task-relevant relationships yields a failure detector that is more robust to irrelevant scene variation, requires no failure demonstrations, and avoids runtime VLM overhead.

Abstract: Detecting failures during execution is essential for reliable robotic manipulation. Vision-language models (VLMs) can assess task outcomes semantically but add runtime computation, whereas out-of-distribution (OOD) detectors may respond to harmless scene variations rather than failure-relevant deviations. We introduce RAFAIL, a framework for detecting execution failures during robotic manipulation. RAFAIL identifies failures by detecting anomalies in task-relevant relationships between entities, such as a gripper and an object or an object and its target. By focusing OOD detection on relevant parts of the observation, RAFAIL reduces sensitivity to task-irrelevant scene variation. Offline, a VLM annotates successful demonstrations with task progress and relationship importance, which are used to learn point-cloud-based relationship representations without relying on policy-internal features. At runtime, relationship-specific OOD detectors evaluate these representations while relationship importance and task progress are predicted without VLM inference. RAFAIL requires no failure data and achieves 73.4% balanced accuracy across three real-world robotic manipulation tasks, outperforming the strongest evaluated OOD- and uncertainty-based baselines.

</details>


### [129] [UAVs Meet Embodied Intelligence: Bridging Human Intents and Flying Dynamics Via Harnessing Physical-Digital AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18326)
*Yonglin Tian,Weiyi Wang,Houhua Lu,Xinyi Li,Yihao Wu,Jingyang Chen,Jianli Sun,Chengxiang Li,Yinuo Chen,Fei Lin,Tengchao Zhang,Jing Yang,Deyi Ji,Jian Di,Naiqi Wu,Yisheng Lv*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This survey characterizes "UAV embodied intelligence" (UAV EI) via a 5+5 framework (five capability dimensions, five architectural layers), reviewing progress in embodied morphology, perception, world models, planning, VLN, manipulation, and collaboration, and arguing that physical-digital AI agents offer a pathway to continuously evolving aerial autonomy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: UAVs extend embodied intelligence into continuous 3D space where perception, reasoning, embodiment, and action are tightly coupled through flight. Recent foundation models, world models, and AI agents are shifting UAV autonomy from task-specific perception/control toward systems that interpret human intent, understand open environments, reason about physical consequences, and organize complex behaviors under embodiment and flight-dynamic constraints, requiring a unified view of this emerging field.

Method: The paper introduces a 5+5 framework: UAV EI described through five capability dimensions, and EI UAVs described through five architectural layers (physical embodiment, general cognition, embodied skills, external interaction, system harnessing). Using this framework, the authors systematically review recent progress across embodied morphology, embodied perception, world models, embodied planning, vision-language navigation, embodied manipulation, and embodied collaboration.

Result: The review synthesizes recent advances across the seven surveyed areas, distinguishes UAV EI (the paradigm) from EI UAV (its system realization), and identifies the key challenges for general aerial embodied intelligence.

Conclusion: Key challenges are long-horizon autonomy, predictive physical reasoning, test-time skill acquisition, and autonomous capability evolution. The authors argue that harnessing physical-digital AI agents—persistent coupling of digital intelligence with physical sensing, dynamics, action, and feedback—provides a system-level pathway toward adaptive, continuously evolving UAV autonomy. Project resources are available via the project website and GitHub repository.

Abstract: Unmanned aerial vehicles (UAVs) extend embodied intelligence into continuous three-dimensional space, where perception, reasoning, physical embodiment, and action are tightly coupled through flight and environmental interaction. Recent advances in foundation models, world models, and AI agents are shifting UAV autonomy from task-specific perception and control toward systems that can interpret human intent, understand open environments, reason about physical consequences, and organize complex behaviors under embodiment and flight-dynamic constraints. We characterize this emerging paradigm as UAV embodied intelligence (UAV EI) and distinguish it from its system realization, the embodied-intelligent UAV (EI UAV). To provide a unified view of the field, we introduce a 5+5 framework that describes UAV EI through five capability dimensions and EI UAVs through five architectural layers spanning physical embodiment, general cognition, embodied skills, external interaction, and system harnessing. Based on this framework, we systematically review recent progress in embodied morphology, embodied perception, world models, embodied planning, vision-language navigation, embodied manipulation, and embodied collaboration. We further identify long-horizon autonomy, predictive physical reasoning, test-time skill acquisition, and autonomous capability evolution as key challenges toward more general aerial embodied intelligence. Finally, we argue that harnessing physical-digital AI agents, through persistent coupling of digital intelligence with physical sensing, dynamics, action, and feedback, provides a system-level pathway toward adaptive and continuously evolving UAV autonomy. Project resources are available at our project website and GitHub repository.

</details>


### [130] [GraphPoint: Semantic Entity Graphs and Point Trajectories for Compositional Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18358)
*Kang Luo,Hesheng Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper introduces CoMani, a benchmark for evaluating compositional reuse in robot manipulation at both subtask and cross-subtask levels, and proposes GraphPoint, a method that links semantic entity graphs to geometric control via future gripper point trajectory prediction to achieve instruction-dependent generalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robot manipulation policies often fail to generalize beyond their demonstrations, even for new instructions involving familiar objects and behaviors. When language and scenes are strongly correlated during training, policies tend to learn a fixed visual-action mapping instead of responding to the requested behavior, indicating a reliance on visual shortcuts rather than language.

Method: The authors investigate compositional reuse at two levels: within a subtask (combining familiar entities, action types, and action modifiers) and across subtasks (reusing learned subtasks in unseen long-horizon tasks). They build CoMani, a benchmark with controlled splits, matched initial scenes, and controlled changes to a single semantic factor to discourage visual shortcuts. They propose GraphPoint, which connects semantic entity graphs to geometric control by predicting future gripper point trajectories and converting them into actions using robot geometry. GraphPoint organizes the gripper and objects by semantic roles, conditions their interactions on action types and modifiers, and uses predicted progress to guide transitions during execution.

Result: Experiments and ablations on CoMani validate the effectiveness of the proposed method for instruction-dependent generalization at both the within-subtask and cross-subtask levels.

Conclusion: GraphPoint, evaluated on the CoMani benchmark, demonstrates effective instruction-dependent compositional generalization for robot manipulation at both subtask and cross-subtask levels, with code to be released.

Abstract: Robot manipulation policies often struggle to generalize beyond their demonstrations, even when new instructions involve familiar objects and behaviors. When language and scenes are strongly correlated during training, a policy can learn a fixed visual-action mapping rather than respond to the requested behavior. We investigate compositional reuse at two levels: within a subtask, combining familiar entities, action types, and action modifiers; and across subtasks, reusing learned subtasks in unseen long-horizon tasks. We introduce CoMani, a benchmark with controlled splits for evaluating both capabilities. Matched initial scenes and controlled changes to a single semantic factor encourage reliance on language rather than visual shortcuts. We further propose GraphPoint, which connects semantic entity graphs to geometric control by predicting future gripper point trajectories and converting them into actions using robot geometry. The framework organizes the gripper and objects by semantic roles and conditions their interactions on action types and modifiers, while predicted progress guides transitions during execution. Experiments and ablations on CoMani validate the effectiveness of our method for instruction-dependent generalization at both levels. Code will be released at GraphPoint.

</details>


### [131] [RecMorph: Topology-Guided Spatial Recurrence for Generalized Morphology Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18359)
*Quanrui Rao,Yong Liu,Xueming Xiao,Yingbo Luo,Kun Wu,Zhenyu Xu,Meibao Yao*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: RecMorph is a topology-guided spatial recurrent architecture that jointly performs cross-limb communication and representation transformation via a depth-first traversal of the kinematic tree, achieving state-of-the-art generalized morphology control with linear token complexity and successful transfer to physical quadruped robots.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Generalized morphology control demands a single policy that can transform information across limbs with different physical roles, coordinate whole-body motion, and scale efficiently with body size. Existing communication mechanisms address these requirements only partially.

Method: RecMorph converts the kinematic tree into a morphology-derived sequence through depth-first traversal, along which shared bidirectional recurrent transitions progressively transform limb information before action decoding. Residual preservation, RMS normalization, and input-dependent channel modulation stabilize the repeated spatial transformation, yielding linear token complexity at fixed model width and depth.

Result: Across five UNIMAL tasks, RecMorph achieves the strongest mean final training performance among evaluated generalized morphology controllers and the highest measured inference throughput on FT, while generalizing to unseen variations and bodies with up to 30 limbs. In a four-platform quadruped setting under nominal and high friction, it achieves the best macro-averaged performance, reduces nominal velocity RMSE by 43.5% relative to specialist MLPs, and one shared policy completes 40 physical Go1/Go2 trials without falls.

Conclusion: Topology-guided recurrent transformation provides an effective and efficient communication mechanism for Generalized Morphology Control and remains effective when transferred from procedural bodies to physical robot platforms. Code and experimental resources are publicly available.

Abstract: Generalized morphology control requires a single policy to transform information across limbs with different physical roles, coordinate whole-body motion, and remain efficient as body size grows. Existing communication mechanisms address these requirements only partially. We introduce RecMorph, a topology-guided spatial recurrent architecture that uses recurrent sequence computation to jointly perform cross-limb communication and representation transformation. A depth-first traversal converts the kinematic tree into a morphology-derived sequence, along which shared bidirectional transitions progressively transform limb information before action decoding. Residual preservation, RMS normalization, and input-dependent channel modulation stabilize this repeated spatial transformation, yielding linear token complexity at fixed model width and depth. Across five UNIMAL tasks, RecMorph achieves the strongest mean final training performance among the evaluated generalized morphology controllers and the highest measured inference throughput on FT, while generalizing to unseen variations and bodies with up to 30 limbs. We further migrate representative generalized controllers from UNIMAL benchmarks to a four-platform quadruped setting. RecMorph achieves the best macro-averaged performance under nominal and high friction, reduces nominal velocity RMSE by 43.5% relative to specialist MLPs, and one shared policy completes 40 physical Go1/Go2 trials without falls. These results show that topology-guided recurrent transformation provides an effective and efficient communication mechanism for Generalized Morphology Control and remains effective when transferred from procedural bodies to physical robot platforms. Code and experimental resources are publicly available at https://github.com/quanruirao/RecMorph.

</details>


### [132] [Decoupling Vision, Language, and Action for Efficient Multi-Task Robot Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18374)
*Xiatao Sun,Chen Liang,Ziyao Zeng,Qian Wang,Haoyang Zhang,Yue Sun,Qiucheng Li,Daniel Rakita*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper proposes the Decoupled Embodiment Model (DEM), which pairs a fine-tuned DINOv3 vision encoder, a frozen NeoBERT language encoder, and a single-pass MeanFlow action head instead of a billion-parameter VLM backbone. Under a controlled comparison against seven VLA baselines, DEM matches state-of-the-art success on 18 simulated and 3 real-robot manipulation tasks while being 8–17× faster and using 6–15× less energy per inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLA models attach action modules to billion-parameter VLMs and pay that backbone cost at every control step, which may be wasteful for low-level manipulation: the VLM mainly supplies vision and language embeddings, and standalone vision encoders and encoder-only language models now match or exceed large VLMs on embedding and language benchmarks. The authors ask whether such a heavy backbone is actually necessary for low-level manipulation policies.

Method: A controlled experiment in which demonstrations, training budget, tasks, and measurement platform are held fixed while the vision encoder, language encoder, and action head of a decoupled policy are varied, compared against seven VLA baselines. This yields DEM, which combines a fine-tuned DINOv3 encoder with a frozen NeoBERT encoder and a MeanFlow head that generates each action chunk in a single forward pass.

Result: On 18 simulated manipulation tasks with held-out language paraphrases and randomized scenes, plus three real-robot tasks, DEM achieves observed success comparable to state-of-the-art VLM-backbone policies under the evaluation protocol, while running at 8–17× their inference frequency and drawing 6–15× less energy per inference.

Conclusion: Within this task scope, modern decoupled components—standalone vision encoders, encoder-only language models, and a lightweight single-pass action head—offer a better success–latency–energy trade-off than VLM-backbone VLA policies.

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models attach an action module to a Vision-Language Model (VLM) with billions of parameters and pay for that backbone at every control step. For a low-level manipulation policy, this cost may be unnecessary: the VLM supplies vision and language embeddings, and recent standalone vision encoders and encoder-only language models now match or exceed large VLMs on visual embedding and language understanding benchmarks. We study this question with a controlled experiment. Holding the demonstrations, the training budget, the tasks, and the measurement platform fixed, we vary the vision encoder, the language encoder, and the action head of a decoupled policy and compare against seven VLA baselines. The study yields the Decoupled Embodiment Model (DEM), which pairs a fine-tuned DINOv3 encoder and a frozen NeoBERT encoder with a MeanFlow head that generates each action chunk in a single forward pass. On 18 simulated manipulation tasks with held-out language paraphrases and randomized scenes, and on three real-robot tasks, DEM achieves observed success comparable to state-of-the-art VLM-backbone policies under our evaluation protocol, while running at eight to seventeen times their inference frequency and drawing six to fifteen times less energy per inference. Within this task scope, modern decoupled components offer a better success--latency--energy trade-off.

</details>


### [133] [DistAL: Distance-based Advantage Learning for VLA Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18392)
*Reece O'Mahoney,Ioannis Havoutis*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: DistAL replaces low-information success/failure rewards in advantage-conditioned VLA training with an embedding-space distance reward, yielding a more informative value function and higher downstream task success rates in both simulation and real dexterous bi-manual manipulation tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision-language-action models (VLAs) have transformed robotic manipulation, and advantage conditioning iteratively improves them via a value function trained on deployment data. However, prior work uses only simple success/failure rewards, which are low-information and prevent the value function from distinguishing states of differing quality beyond apparent task progress.

Method: Inspired by an exploration of out-of-distribution (OOD) detection methods, the authors propose Distance-based Advantage Learning (DistAL), which uses a distance in an embedding space as the reward signal to train a more informative value function, which in turn trains an advantage-conditioned policy.

Result: DistAL produces a more informative value function and subsequently achieves a higher downstream task success rate, validated on a series of simulation benchmarks and dexterous bi-manual manipulation tasks on real hardware.

Conclusion: Embedding-space distance rewards offer a superior alternative to binary success/failure rewards for advantage-conditioned VLA training, improving policy performance in both simulated and real-world settings.

Abstract: Vision-language-action models (VLAs) have trans- formed the field of robotic manipulation in recent years by combining the semantic understanding of LLMs with the precise control of flow-matching policies. Advantage conditioning is a recent technique that iteratively improves VLAs by training a value function on deployment data and using this to train an advantage-conditioned policy. Previous works have only applied simple, low-information success/failure rewards, which leave the value function unable to distinguish states of differing quality beyond how far along the task they appear. Motivated by an exploration of out-of-distribution (OOD) detection methods, we introduce Distance-based Advantage Learning (DistAL), which, by using an embedding space distance as a reward, produces a more informative value function and subsequently a higher downstream task success rate. We validate our method on a series of simulation benchmarks and dexterous bi-manual manipulation tasks on real hardware.

</details>


### [134] [DetAug: Obstacle-Blind Trajectory Augmentation for Zero-shot Obstacle Avoidance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18395)
*Reece O'Mahoney,Moritz Zoellner,Ioannis Havoutis*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: DetAug augments only the transit phases of free-space teleoperation datasets with obstacle-blind perturbations, records augmentation parameters as conditioning labels, and at inference samples labels to pick the lowest-collision-cost trajectory, achieving >20pp collision-free success on SafeLIBERO and matching an obstacle-conditioned baseline on real hardware without training on obstacles.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robotic manipulation policies trained on large teleoperated datasets, which mostly consist of free-space trajectories, transfer poorly to test-time environments containing obstacles. Existing fixes split into two groups: dataset augmentation, which requires obstacle geometry in advance, and inference-time checkpoint steering, which is flexible but limited.

Method: DetAug applies an obstacle-blind augmentation scheme to the transit phases of a free-space dataset while leaving object interactions untouched, and records the augmentation parameters as an explicit conditioning label. At inference, it samples a batch of labels and executes the trajectory with the lowest collision cost.

Result: On SafeLIBERO, DetAug achieves a collision-free success rate more than 20pp above the next best method, and label-space selection outperforms guidance on the same policy by 26pp. On real hardware, inference-time steering methods collapse on tasks requiring large detours, whereas DetAug matches or exceeds an obstacle-conditioned baseline despite never seeing obstacles during training.

Conclusion: DetAug combines the strengths of dataset augmentation and inference-time steering without inheriting either drawback, achieving strong obstacle avoidance at test time from purely free-space training data.

Abstract: Policies for robotic manipulation are produced by training on large teleoperated datasets. These datasets typically consist of free-space trajectories, making them difficult to transfer to test-time environments with obstacles. Previous methods for closing this gap have largely fallen into two groups. Dataset augmentation addresses it at training time but needs obstacle geometry in advance, whereas steering an existing checkpoint at inference time avoids that requirement but is limited in flexibility. Our method draws from both areas without inheriting either drawback. DetAug applies an obstacle-blind augmentation scheme to the transit phases of a free-space dataset, leaving object interactions untouched, and records the augmentation parameters as an explicit conditioning label. At inference it samples a batch of labels and executes the trajectory with the lowest collision cost. On the SafeLIBERO benchmark DetAug achieves a collision-free success rate more than 20pp above the next best method, and selecting over the label space outperforms guidance on the same policy by 26pp. On real hardware, inference-time steering methods collapse on tasks requiring large detours, while DetAug matches or exceeds an obstacle-conditioned baseline without ever seeing obstacles in training.

</details>


### [135] [Hardware-Free Robotics Laboratories in Mixed Reality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18434)
*Santiago Berrezueta-Guzman,Habiba-Loai Khalil,Andrei Koshelev,Vanesa Metaj,Stefan Wagner*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: MR-Robotics LAB is a mixed-reality platform that replays MATLAB-generated robot trajectories at real scale in the learner's physical space via a Unity app on Meta Quest 3, with a formative study showing low setup effort, perceived support for workspace understanding, and high willingness to adopt.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robotics teaching typically relies on screen-based simulation that displays robot motion in an abstract coordinate frame rather than at real scale in the learner's own space, while hands-on hardware access is constrained by cost, safety, and scheduling.

Method: A browser-based service validates a MATLAB workspace file (.mat), normalizes units, and publishes a versioned JSON trajectory; a Unity application on Meta Quest 3 reproduces the authored joint configurations under position control and replays them at the declared frame rate in a physics-enabled scene supporting collision detection and end-effector grasping. Evaluation was a formative single-group study with engineering students using an instrument capturing perceived outcomes only, without counterbalancing or a learning measure.

Result: Participants reported low setup effort (M = 4.67 on a 5-point scale) and perceived support for workspace understanding from multi-viewpoint inspection (M = 4.56); 83% affirmed willingness to use the platform in an introductory robotics course.

Conclusion: The work contributes a reusable simulation-to-MR trajectory pathway and design guidance for hardware-free robot visualization in engineering education. Because the evaluation records only perceived outcomes without counterbalancing or a learning measure, no comparative advantage over desktop simulation is claimed.

Abstract: Teaching robotics relies on screen-based simulation, showing robot motion in an abstract coordinate frame rather than at real scale in the learner's own space, while access to physical hardware is limited by cost, safety, and scheduling constraints. We present MR-Robotics LAB, a mixed-reality (MR) platform that replays MATLAB-generated robot trajectories at real scale within the learner's physical environment. A browser-based service validates a MATLAB workspace file (.mat), normalizes units, and publishes a versioned JSON trajectory; a Unity application on a Meta Quest 3 then reproduces the authored joint configurations under position control and replays them at the declared frame rate within a physics-enabled scene that supports collision detection and end-effector grasping. A formative single-group evaluation with engineering students found that participants reported low setup effort (M = 4.67 on a 5-point scale) and perceived support for workspace understanding from multi-viewpoint inspection (M = 4.56), and 83% of participants affirmed their willingness to use the platform in an introductory robotics course. The evaluation instrument records only perceived outcomes, without counterbalancing or a learning measure, so no comparative advantage over desktop simulation is claimed. The contribution is a reusable simulation-to-MR trajectory pathway and design guidance for hardware-free robot visualization in engineering education.

</details>


### [136] [VLM-MPPI: Grounding Natural Language in Behaviorally Diverse Trajectories for Aerial Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18451)
*Hanbing Zhang,Fangguo Zhao,Zerui Li,Xin Guan,Peng Cheng,Shuo Li*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A hierarchical UAV navigation framework bridges natural-language intent and dynamically feasible flight by running six behavior-conditioned MPPI planners to generate diverse trajectory modes, projecting 3D candidates onto the onboard FPV RGB stream, and using a pretrained VLM to asynchronously select the candidate matching a language prompt, validated in simulation and real flights.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a gap between abstract natural-language semantics and low-level flight control in cluttered indoor environments, making it difficult to ground language intent into dynamically feasible quadrotor behaviors.

Method: A hierarchical framework uses a parallelized ensemble of six behavior-conditioned MPPI planners with mode-specific guiding costs and sampling biases to induce distinct, intentionally diverse trajectory modes. 3D candidates are projected onto the onboard first-person-view RGB stream, reframing language grounding as visual action selection; a pretrained VLM asynchronously selects the candidate index from the overlaid FPV image and language prompt, while MPPI replans at 20Hz and a PID low-level controller tracks the selected trajectory. The pipeline is implemented in NVIDIA Isaac Sim and on a real quadrotor with LiDAR and RGB sensing.

Result: Experiments in simulation and real-world flights demonstrate semantically meaningful behavior diversity, robust language alignment despite VLM latency, and safe, repeatable flight across all modes, achieving 100% task success in the evaluated scenarios.

Conclusion: The proposed behavior-conditioned MPPI ensemble combined with VLM-based visual candidate selection provides a practical, real-time-capable approach for language-aligned UAV navigation with diverse and safe flight behaviors in cluttered indoor settings.

Abstract: We present a hierarchical UAV navigation framework that aligns natural-language intent with dynamically feasible flight behaviors in cluttered indoor environments. To bridge the gap between abstract semantics and low-level control, we employ a parallelized ensemble of six behavior-conditioned Model Predictive Path Integral (MPPI) planners. Crucially, by designing mode-specific guiding costs and sampling biases, we induce distinct trajectory modes that converge to unique behavioral means, yielding a compact set of intentionally diverse candidates rather than mere stochastic variations. We project these 3D candidates onto the onboard first-person-view RGB stream, turning language grounding into a visual action selection problem. A pretrained vision--language model (VLM) asynchronously selects the candidate index given the overlaid FPV image and a natural-language prompt, while MPPI replans at 20Hz and a PID-based low-level controller tracks the selected trajectory. We implement the full pipeline in NVIDIA Isaac Sim and on a real-world quadrotor platform equipped with LiDAR and RGB sensing. Experiments in both simulation and real-world flights show semantically meaningful behavior diversity, robust language alignment despite VLM latency, and safe, repeatable flight across all modes, achieving 100% task success in our evaluated scenarios.

</details>


### [137] [ForwardDLO: Model-Based Bimanual Shape Matching of Unconstrained Deformable Linear Objects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18455)
*Tim Missal,Berk Guler,Lucas Domingues,Simon Manschitz,Jan Peters,Paula Dornhofer Paro Costa*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ForwardDLO is a recurrent latent dynamics model for bimanual shape control of unfixated deformable linear objects (ropes/cables), enabling cheap batched action evaluation and high-throughput planning with strong real-world and simulated performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deformable linear objects like ropes and cables are common in tasks such as untangling, cable routing, and suturing, but controlling their shape remains difficult in robot manipulation. In the general unfixated, bimanual setting—where the object rests on a surface and two arms can grasp it anywhere along its length—the joint action space is combinatorially large, and the per-prediction cost of the dynamics model limits how many candidate actions a planner can search.

Method: ForwardDLO is a recurrent latent dynamics model designed for the unfixated bimanual setting. It predicts per-segment displacements grounded in the observed rope state at each step, and contains no explicit segment-to-segment operations, which enables cheap batched evaluation of candidate actions.

Result: ForwardDLO reaches accuracy comparable to more expensive baselines. On open-loop prediction of real rope motion it achieves the lowest error among evaluated learned models, 13% below the strongest baseline. Within a fixed time budget it scores 8–22x more candidate actions than comparable-accuracy models while matching them in real-world shape matching. On a simulated routing task at 30Hz control, this throughput yields 98% task success versus at most 30% for baselines at their own budgets.

Conclusion: A recurrent latent dynamics model without explicit segment-to-segment operations can deliver accurate, high-throughput planning for bimanual shape control of unfixated deformable linear objects, converting evaluation efficiency into substantially higher task success. The model, code, and a dataset of 2.42M simulated and 14,107 real rope transitions are released.

Abstract: Ropes, cables, and other deformable linear objects appear in tasks from untangling to cable routing and suturing, yet controlling their shape remains a challenge in robot manipulation. We study model-based shape control in a general setting: the object lies unfixated on a support surface and two arms may grasp and move it anywhere along its length. Because each arm chooses a grasp point, direction, and magnitude, the joint action space is combinatorially large, and the dynamics model's per-prediction cost bounds how much of it a planner can search. We present ForwardDLO, a recurrent latent dynamics model for this unfixated bimanual setting that predicts per-segment displacements grounded in the observed rope state at every step. Our model reaches accuracy comparable to more expensive baselines while containing no explicit segment-to-segment operations, which makes batched evaluation of candidate actions cheap. On open-loop prediction of real rope motion it reaches the lowest error of the learned models we evaluate, 13% below the strongest baseline. Within a fixed time budget it scores 8 to 22 times more candidate actions than models of comparable accuracy while matching them in real-world shape matching; and on a simulated routing task at a 30Hz control rate, this throughput converts into 98% task success versus at most 30% for the baselines at their own budgets. We release the model, code, and a dataset of 2.42 million simulated and 14,107 real rope transitions at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/ForwardDLO/

</details>


### [138] [Real-Time Bounded Catenary Solver for UAV Tether Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18482)
*Max Beffert,Andreas Zell*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A catenary tether-drag solver for tethered multirotor UAVs is reformulated into a single well-conditioned transcendental equation with proven existence, uniqueness, and convergence, yielding a bounded ~6.9–7.7 µs solve time (40× faster) and validated on a flight controller.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world tethered multirotor UAVs are non-stationary, so online simulation of tether aerodynamic drag forces is essential, and online use imposes a hard bound on maximum solve time. The previous quasi-analytical catenary model solved in 0.51 ms mean but gave no worst-case guarantees or proven convergence.

Method: The catenary boundary-value problem is reduced to a single transcendental equation in one well-conditioned unknown. A closed-form bracket is derived, and monotonicity, convexity, existence, and uniqueness of the root are proven. A two-regime initial guess approximating the true root within 3.4% is proposed. A specialized variant of the hybrid rtsafe method (Newton-Raphson with bisection fallback, bounded iterations) exploits the problem structure to omit unnecessary checks while retaining correctness.

Result: The two-regime initial guess reduces mean iteration count by 68.0% to 2.36 versus textbook initialization; the specialized rtsafe variant gives up to 1.3× speedup. The full tether model achieves a nearly constant solve time of 6.9 µs mean and 7.7 µs worst case, 40× faster than an optimized re-implementation of the previous method, agreeing to a relative deviation of 8.7e-9. A Lua implementation in ArduPilot on a drone flight controller achieves 0.74 ms mean, within the scheduling budget.

Conclusion: The reformulation preserves the underlying physical model, so prior experimental validation carries over unchanged, while providing guaranteed convergence and bounded solve time suitable for embedded, resource-constrained platforms.

Abstract: For non-stationary tethered multirotor UAVs in real-world conditions, simulating the forces imposed on the drone by the aerodynamic drag of the tether becomes crucial, with online use cases placing a hard bound on the maximum solve time. In previous work, a quasi-analytical catenary tether model reached a mean solve time of 0.51 ms using a general-purpose root finder, but without any worst-case guarantees or proven convergence. In this work, we reformulate the inner solver by reducing the catenary boundary-value problem to a single transcendental equation in one well-conditioned unknown. We derive a closed-form bracket and prove monotonicity and convexity as well as existence and uniqueness of the root, which together guarantee convergence of the solver. We further propose a two-regime initial guess which approximates the true root within 3.4% and reduces the mean iteration count by 68.0% to 2.36 compared to the textbook initialization. Building on the hybrid root-finding method rtsafe (Newton-Raphson with bisection fallback giving bounded iteration counts), we implement a specialized variant that exploits the problem structure to omit unnecessary checks while retaining correctness, which gives up to 1.3 times speedup. With the proposed solver the full tether model achieves a nearly constant solve time of 6.9 us on average and 7.7 us at worst, a 40 times speedup over an optimized re-implementation of the previous method, while agreeing with it to a relative deviation of 8.7e-9. Because the reformulation leaves the underlying physical model untouched, the experimental validation of the previous work carries over unchanged. We further demonstrate its suitability for embedded, resource-constrained platforms with a Lua implementation running directly in ArduPilot on a drone's flight controller, where it stays well inside the scheduling budget with a mean solve time of 0.74 ms.

</details>


### [139] [ActionPiece: Rethinking Action Tokenization for Autoregressive Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18487)
*Shijie Lian,Bin Yu,Zhaolong Shen,Xiaopeng Lin,Yichao Du,Zhirui Zhang,Laurence T. Yang,Kai Chen*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper introduces physical rank consistency (PRC) to measure how well action tokenizers preserve local physical distance rankings after reconstruction, and proposes ActionPiece, a tokenizer that preserves physical action relationships via joint supervision of representation learning and quantization, achieving strong policy success across LIBERO, LIBERO-Plus, SimplerEnv, and VLA-Arena.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Action tokenizers in autoregressive VLA models are commonly evaluated with pointwise reconstruction metrics like MSE, but small individual errors do not capture whether context-dependent action adjustments across demonstrations are preserved. Compression may cause adjustments to be diminished, distorted, or reversed, so a relational fidelity measure is needed to complement pointwise accuracy.

Method: The authors propose physical rank consistency (PRC), which measures preservation of local physical distance rankings after reconstruction and is evaluated on decoded actions for cross-vocabulary and cross-architecture comparability. They present ActionPiece, which jointly supervises representation learning and quantization: physical rank preservation supervises near-far ordering in encoder and quantized feature distances, while quantization regularization applies the same ordering to codeword assignment distributions. These objectives augment reconstruction, producing discrete action tokens for standard autoregressive policy learning and execution through a frozen decoder.

Result: Under the same Qwen3-VL-4B policy training setup, ActionPiece achieves 94.8% on LIBERO and 68.8% on unseen LIBERO-Plus, plus 71.9% on SimplerEnv and 51.5% across VLA-Arena L0-L2. Component ablations show the two objectives jointly improve both PRC and policy success.

Conclusion: Physical relationship supervision is valuable for action tokenization, and PRC provides a relational fidelity measure that complements pointwise reconstruction metrics, with ActionPiece demonstrating improved rank consistency and policy success.

Abstract: Action tokenizers play a central role in autoregressive vision-language-action (VLA) models, determining both the targets for policy training and the executable commands recovered from predicted tokens. Their fidelity is commonly evaluated using pointwise reconstruction metrics such as mean squared error (MSE), yet small individual errors do not fully characterize how faithfully action adjustments across demonstrations are preserved. After compression, similar actions may still cluster around a representative motion, while the adjustments needed for different contexts are diminished, distorted, or even reversed. We introduce physical rank consistency (PRC) to measure how well tokenization preserves local physical distance rankings after reconstruction. Evaluating decoded actions provides a common reference across token vocabularies and decoder architectures, complementing pointwise accuracy with a measure of relational fidelity. We further present ActionPiece, which preserves physical action relationships through joint supervision of representation learning and quantization. Physical rank preservation supervises near-far ordering in encoder and quantized feature distances, while quantization regularization applies the same ordering to codeword assignment distributions. Both objectives augment reconstruction, producing discrete action tokens for standard autoregressive policy learning and execution through a frozen decoder. Under the same Qwen3-VL-4B policy training setup, ActionPiece achieves 94.8% on LIBERO and 68.8% on unseen LIBERO-Plus, with additional evaluations reaching 71.9% on SimplerEnv and 51.5% across VLA-Arena L0-L2. Component ablations show that the two objectives jointly improve PRC and policy success, demonstrating the value of physical relationship supervision for action tokenization.

</details>


### [140] [TAO-Force: Unifying Force-Aware Perception and Fast-Slow Control for Contact-Rich Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18497)
*Bohan Gan,Xuanzhang Wen,Yongsheng Zhao,Baoping Cheng,Wenhe Jia,Ye Wang,Gongxin Yao,Han Gao,Jingyao Tang,Lei Zhao,Ji Ge*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: TAO-Force is a force-conditioned VLA framework that injects force feedback into a frozen vision-language backbone via F-FiLM and uses a contact-gated fast-slow controller (slow position branch + fast admittance branch) to handle contact-rich manipulation, validated on force perception and four real-world tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models are predominantly vision-centric and use position-controlled execution, which is insufficient for contact-rich manipulation. Visual observations provide limited evidence of contact onset and interaction magnitude, and position-control policies cannot respond compliantly to rapidly changing contact dynamics. This creates both a perception gap and a control gap.

Method: TAO-Force combines force-aware policy learning with contact-regulated execution.
