For perception, it uses Force-conditioned Feature-wise Linear Modulation (F-FiLM) to inject encoded force feedback into the representations of a frozen pretrained visual-language backbone while preserving semantic priors. For control, it employs a contact-gated fast-slow architecture: a slow position-control branch tracks nominal trajectories during non-contact phases, and a fast admittance-control branch regulates physical interaction during contact phases.

Result: Detailed analyses on a force-perception task, along with real-world evaluations across four contact-rich manipulation tasks, validate the effectiveness and robustness of TAO-Force.

Conclusion: TAO-Force bridges both perception and control gaps in VLA models for contact-rich manipulation by conditioning a frozen vision-language backbone on force feedback and pairing a contact-gated, dual-branch controller, demonstrating effectiveness and robustness in real-world settings.

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models have demonstrated strong performance across diverse robotic manipulation tasks, yet their predominantly vision-centric perception and position-controlled execution remain insufficient for contact-rich manipulation. Visual observations alone often provide limited evidence of contact onset and interaction magnitude, while position-control policies cannot respond compliantly to rapidly changing contact dynamics. To bridge both the perception and control gaps, we propose TAO-Force, a force-conditioned VLA framework that combines force-aware policy learning with contact-regulated execution. For force-aware perception, TAO-Force introduces Force-conditioned Feature-wise Linear Modulation (F-FiLM) to inject encoded force feedback into the representations of a frozen pretrained visual-language backbone while preserving its semantic priors. For responsive control, it employs a contact-gated fast-slow architecture, with a slow position-control branch tracking nominal trajectories during non-contact phases and a fast admittance-control branch regulating physical interaction during contact phases. Detailed analyses on a force-perception task and real-world evaluations across four contact-rich manipulation tasks validate the effectiveness and robustness of TAO-Force.

</details>


### [141] [InterMASH: A Unified Geometric Representation for Grasp Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18504)
*Xuanze Yang,Yumeng Liu,Haiyang Xin,Changhao Li,Haowei Shen,Kai Xu,Ligang Liu,Ruizhen Hu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper proposes InterMASH, a unified geometric representation using sphere-fixed anchors with low-degree spherical harmonics to encode hand geometry, object geometry, and contact as explicit token sequences, enabling a Diffusion Transformer to jointly generate grasps across human and robotic hands.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Grasp synthesis is fundamental to human hand modeling and robotic manipulation, but a unified representation across human and robotic hands is lacking due to differences in hand morphology and surface modeling. Existing contact-map or dense implicit descriptor approaches are incomplete, computationally expensive, or redundant.

Method: InterMASH establishes cross-embodiment correspondence via sphere-fixed anchors. At each anchor, low-degree spherical harmonics compactly encode local hand geometry, object geometry, and contact, forming an explicit, interpretable token sequence. A conditional Diffusion Transformer operates directly in this representation space, jointly generating hand geometry and contact.

Result: The method achieves competitive performance with state-of-the-art methods on key physical feasibility metrics in a large-scale ShadowHand benchmark, supports joint training across multiple hands, and shows that cross-embodiment fine-tuning with human grasp data improves robotic grasp success and diversity.

Conclusion: InterMASH provides a unified, compact, and interpretable cross-embodiment grasp representation, enabling joint training and effective human-to-robot transfer for grasp synthesis.

Abstract: Grasp synthesis aims to generate stable and physically plausible hand--object interactions, and has become a fundamental problem in both human hand modeling and robotic manipulation. However, a unified representation across human and robotic hands is still lacking, mainly due to differences in hand morphology and surface modeling. Prior methods typically rely on either contact maps or dense implicit descriptors to represent interaction, but these representations are often incomplete or computationally expensive and redundant. We propose InterMASH, a unified geometric representation that establishes cross-embodiment correspondence using sphere-fixed anchors. At each anchor, low-degree spherical harmonics compactly encode local hand geometry, object geometry, and contact, forming an explicit and interpretable token sequence. Building on this natively tokenized structure, we introduce a conditional Diffusion Transformer that operates directly in the proposed InterMASH representation space and jointly generates hand geometry and contact, improving consistency and physical plausibility. Our method achieves competitive performance with state-of-the-art methods on key physical feasibility metrics in a large-scale ShadowHand benchmark, supports joint training across multiple hands, and shows that cross-embodiment fine-tuning with human grasp data can improve robotic grasp success and diversity. Project page is available at https://inter-mash.github.io/.

</details>


### [142] [ActiveScale: Scaling Active Perception for Robots across Model, Data, and Hardware](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18514)
*Shuai Zhou,Kaisheng Pang,Wenxuan Song,Wenjie Zhang,Xinhu Zheng,Haoang Li*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ActiveScale is a unified framework (model + data + hardware) that enables VLA models to perform active perception in robotic manipulation by incorporating historical video and camera-pose supervision, a scalable human-robot mid-training recipe, and a teleoperated mobile-manipulation platform.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fixed viewpoints in robotic manipulation often leave task-relevant information occluded or unobserved, making active perception essential. Yet VLA models struggle to reason across changing viewpoints and to actively acquire informative observations.

Method: The model augments a VLA with historical video observations and explicit camera-pose supervision via per-frame pose tokens and a lightweight prediction head, associating observations across viewpoints. A human-robot mid-training recipe uses 1000 hours of egocentric and robotic data to adapt the model to temporal inputs and pose supervision. The Active-perception Mobile-manipulation Platform (AMP) enables active perception and mobile manipulation via single-operator teleoperation for scalable demonstration collection.

Result: Experiments show improved success rates on active-perception tasks; ablation studies validate the contributions of camera-pose-aware modeling and egocentric mid-training.

Conclusion: The coordinated model, data, and hardware components provide an integrated foundation for studying and developing active perception in robotic manipulation.

Abstract: Active perception is essential for robotic manipulation when fixed viewpoints leave task-relevant information occluded or unobserved. However, enabling vision-language-action (VLA) models to reason across changing viewpoints and actively acquire informative observations remains challenging. We present ActiveScale, a framework that advances active perception through coordinated model, data, and hardware designs. Our model augments a VLA with historical video observations and explicit camera-pose supervision, using per-frame pose tokens and a lightweight prediction head to associate observations across viewpoints and support a coherent understanding of the scene. To learn from the camera motion naturally present in human activity, we introduce a scalable human--robot mid-training recipe using 1000 hours of egocentric and robotic data, adapting the model to temporal inputs and pose supervision. We further introduce Active-perception Mobile-manipulation Platform (AMP), a robotic platform that supports active perception and mobile manipulation through single-operator teleoperation, enabling scalable collection of demonstrations that coordinate viewpoint changes and manipulation. Experiments demonstrate improved success rates on active-perception tasks, while ablation studies validate the contributions of camera-pose-aware modeling and egocentric mid-training. Together, these components provide an integrated foundation for studying and developing active perception in robotic manipulation.

</details>


### [143] [DynoFluxBench: Benchmarking Kinodynamic Space-Time Planners in Dynamic Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18549)
*Franz Queißner,Andreas Orthey,Wolfgang Hönig*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper introduces DynoFluxBench, a benchmark framework for comparing kinodynamic planners in known dynamic environments with unbounded arrival time, and develops three baseline planners (ST-Db-RRT, ST-GBRRT, KIST) that fuse kinodynamic and space-time methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robots leaving structured, static environments must plan kinodynamically feasible and safe motions among moving obstacles, yet no dedicated benchmark framework combines both kinodynamic feasibility and dynamic-obstacle safety for comparison.

Method: The authors present DynoFluxBench for comparing kinodynamic planners in known, dynamic environments with unbounded arrival time. They develop three planners spanning different kinodynamic search paradigms: ST-Db-RRT expands with randomly selected discontinuity-bounded motion primitives via trajectory optimization; ST-GBRRT and KIST maintain a kinodynamically feasible tree with different heuristic guidance. They also analyze probabilistic completeness guarantees of the new planners in dynamic environments.

Result: Evaluated with DynoFluxBench, ST-Db-RRT reaches a first solution up to 32 times faster, while KIST and ST-GBRRT remain valuable where trajectory optimization is fragile.

Conclusion: DynoFluxBench provides a dedicated framework for benchmarking kinodynamic planners in dynamic environments, and the three planners serve as strong baselines, with ST-Db-RRT offering speed and KIST/ST-GBRRT offering robustness where trajectory optimization fails.

Abstract: Robots that leave structured, static environments must plan motions that are kinodynamically feasible and safe among moving obstacles. However, there are no dedicated benchmark frameworks that combine both aspects. To overcome this, we present DynoFluxBench, a framework to compare kinodynamic planners in known, dynamic environments with unbounded arrival time. To demonstrate its utility and establish strong baselines, we develop three dedicated planners, named ST-Db-RRT, ST-GBRRT, and KIST, that fuse kinodynamic and space-time methods, covering different kinodynamic search paradigms: ST-Db-RRT expands with randomly selected discontinuity-bounded motion primitives using trajectory optimization, whereas KIST and ST-GBRRT maintain a kinodynamically feasible tree with different heuristic guidance. We analyze the probabilistic completeness guarantees of those new planners in dynamic environments. Finally, we evaluate ST-Db-RRT, ST-GBRRT, and KIST using DynoFluxBench, showing that ST-Db-RRT reaches a first solution up to 32 times faster, while KIST and ST-GBRRT remain valuable where trajectory optimization is fragile. Videos and further analysis can be found at https://dynofluxbench.github.io/dynofluxbench/.

</details>


### [144] [GroundingVLN: Reasoning and Acting with Grounding for Vision-Language Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18581)
*Kailing Li,Yu Han,Tianwen Qian,Yuqian Fu,Jingyu Gong,Jiangming Shi,Xiaoling Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: GroundingVLN uses visual grounding as a shared interface bridging reasoning and action in vision-and-language navigation: it anchors reasoning to image locations, predicts pixel goals for a geometric planner, and is trained via a new dataset plus GEAR reinforcement learning, achieving SOTA (69.9% SR on R2R-CE, 75.1% on RxR-CE) with 0.9% of baseline training data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing VLN agents struggle to connect semantic reasoning with spatial execution due to two coupled gaps: intermediate reasoning is not explicitly anchored to visual evidence, and high-level decisions lack precise spatial goals to guide low-level motion. Cognitive science indicates human navigation hierarchically bridges these levels by anchoring cognition to landmarks and guiding locomotion toward spatial goals.

Method: The paper proposes GroundingVLN, which uses visual grounding as a shared interface. It reasons with grounding by anchoring task-relevant visual evidence to precise image locations throughout structured reasoning, then acts through grounding by predicting a progress-aligned pixel goal that a geometric planner translates into primitive actions. Training uses GroundingCOTVLN-188K, a dataset of temporally aligned grounded reasoning traces, and Grounded and Execution-Aware Reinforcement Learning (GEAR), which aligns grounded reasoning and spatial decisions with downstream execution.

Result: GroundingVLN achieves state-of-the-art performance with high sample efficiency: 69.9% SR on R2R-CE and 75.1% SR on RxR-CE, using only 0.9% as much training data as the strongest baseline. It also generalizes strongly, attaining 59.9% SR on RxR-CE when trained solely on R2R, a 20.1% gain over the strongest baseline.

Conclusion: Grounding visual reasoning and action through grounding as a shared interface, combined with grounded reasoning traces and execution-aware RL, effectively bridges semantic reasoning and spatial execution in VLN, yielding SOTA results, strong sample efficiency, and robust cross-dataset generalization.

Abstract: Although vision-language models (VLMs) possess strong visual understanding and reasoning capabilities, existing vision-and-language navigation (VLN) agents struggle to connect semantic reasoning with spatial execution. Two coupled gaps remain in this connection, as intermediate reasoning is not explicitly anchored to visual evidence and high-level decisions lack precise spatial goals to guide low-level motion. Cognitive science suggests that human navigation bridges these levels hierarchically by anchoring cognition to relevant landmarks and guiding locomotion toward spatial goals. Motivated by this principle, we propose GroundingVLN, which uses visual grounding as a shared interface between reasoning and action. GroundingVLN first reasons with grounding by anchoring task-relevant visual evidence to precise image locations throughout structured reasoning. It then acts through grounding by predicting a progress-aligned pixel goal that a geometric planner translates into primitive actions. To learn these capabilities, we construct GroundingCOTVLN-188K, a dataset of temporally aligned grounded reasoning traces, and introduce Grounded and Execution-Aware Reinforcement Learning (GEAR), which aligns grounded reasoning and spatial decisions with downstream execution. Experiments demonstrate that GroundingVLN achieves state-of-the-art performance (69.9% SR on R2R-CE and 75.1% SR on RxR-CE) with high sample efficiency, using just 0.9% as much training data as the strongest baseline. It also generalizes strongly across datasets, attaining 59.9% SR on RxR-CE when trained solely on R2R, a gain of 20.1% over the strongest baseline.

</details>


### [145] [DeformSmith: Physics Harness-Guided Hierarchical Generation of Deformable Assets for Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18620)
*Can Li,Jie Gu,Zishun Deng,Jingmin Chen,Lei Sun*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: DeformSmith is an agentic framework that automatically generates interactive, physically credible deformable assets from text or a single image, using hierarchical construction and robot interaction feedback to produce higher-quality assets than state-of-the-art baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Creating deformable assets for robot manipulation requires jointly specifying geometry, appearance, and physical properties. This is especially hard because text and images give limited evidence about deformation and contact responses, which directly determine manipulability. Automated generation must therefore resolve coupled physical constraints and use interaction evidence to guide construction and refinement.

Method: DeformSmith combines hierarchical agentic construction with a shared physics-grounded harness. It progressively builds, tests, and refines geometry, physical models, material behavior, and robot interaction until the asset is simulation- and manipulation-ready. Robot interaction closes the generation loop via manipulation feedback and replayable interaction data.

Result: DeformSmith generates assets with better visual quality and physical plausibility than state-of-the-art baselines, including PhysGen3D, PhysGM, and PhysX-Omni, while also supporting synthesis of data for robotic manipulation of deformable objects.

Conclusion: Coupled physical specification and interaction-driven refinement enable automated generation of deformable assets that are both visually and physically credible, and suited for robot manipulation data synthesis.

Abstract: Creating deformable assets for robot manipulation requires jointly specifying their geometry, appearance, and physical properties. This is especially challenging for deformable objects, since text and images provide limited evidence about how they deform and respond to contact, yet these responses directly affect their suitability for interaction. Automated generation therefore needs to resolve coupled physical requirements and use interaction evidence to guide construction and refinement. We present DeformSmith, a framework that enables automated generation of interactive, physically credible deformable assets from text or a single image. Through hierarchical agentic construction and a shared physics-grounded harness, it progressively builds, tests, and refines geometry, physical models, material behavior, and robot interaction until the resulting asset is ready for simulation and manipulation. Robot interaction closes the generation loop through manipulation feedback and replayable interaction data. Results show that DeformSmith generates assets with better visual quality and physical plausibility than state-of-the-art baselines, including PhysGen3D, PhysGM, and PhysX-Omni, while supporting the synthesis of data for robotic manipulation of deformable objects. Project page: https://can-lee.github.io/deformsmith-web/

</details>


### [146] [Benchmarking Visual-Inertial Odometry in Subterranean Environments Under Sensor Degradation, Miscalibration, and Dynamic Occlusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18628)
*Yueying Zhu,Xiang Li,Thien-Minh Nguyen,Xuehe Wang,Shenghai Yuan*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper introduces a failure-centric stress-test benchmark for Visual-Inertial Odometry (VIO) in subterranean environments using the CERBERUS dataset, evaluating four representative systems across nine perturbation settings to expose breakdown behaviors and paradigm-specific vulnerabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VIO is essential for autonomous operation in GPS-denied subterranean environments, but its reliability degrades under sensor drift, calibration errors, and dynamic occlusion. Existing evaluations focus on nominal-condition accuracy, providing limited insight into when practical deployment failures occur.

Method: A failure-centric stress-test benchmark for VIO in underground environments built on the CERBERUS dataset. Four representative VIO systems spanning filtering-, optimization-, and learning-based paradigms are systematically evaluated under nine practical perturbation settings, including IMU bias and noise variation, camera intrinsic and extrinsic drift, and dynamic scene occlusion. Analysis extends beyond conventional trajectory error to robustness limits via coverage ratio and failure thresholds.

Result: Distinct vulnerability patterns emerge across VIO paradigms: some methods are more sensitive to inertial degradation, while others are more affected by geometric miscalibration or dynamic interference. Breakdown behaviors are revealed that nominal-condition performance alone does not capture.

Conclusion: The benchmark provides deployment-oriented guidance for VIO selection, calibration prioritization, and reliable operation in challenging underground scenarios. Full benchmark scripts and evaluation pipeline will be released to support reproducible evaluation and future extensions.

Abstract: Visual-inertial odometry (VIO) is a core capability for autonomous operation in GPS-denied subterranean environments, yet its reliability can degrade sharply under sensor drift, calibration errors, and dynamic occlusion. Existing evaluations mainly emphasize nominal-condition accuracy, offering limited insight into when practical deployment failures occur. In this work, we present a failure-centric stress-test benchmark for VIO in underground environments using the CERBERUS dataset. We systematically evaluate four representative VIO systems spanning filtering-, optimization-, and learning-based paradigms under nine practical perturbation settings, including IMU bias and noise variation, camera intrinsic and extrinsic drift, and dynamic scene occlusion. Beyond conventional trajectory error, we analyze robustness limits through coverage ratio and failure thresholds, revealing breakdown behaviors that are not captured by nominal-condition performance alone. Our study shows distinct vulnerability patterns across VIO paradigms: some methods are more sensitive to inertial degradation, while others are more affected by geometric miscalibration or dynamic interference. These results provide deployment-oriented guidance for VIO selection, calibration prioritization, and reliable operation in challenging underground scenarios. To support reproducible evaluation and future extensions, we will release the full benchmark scripts and evaluation pipeline.

</details>


### [147] [From Gameplay to Policy: Towards Scalable Robot Data Collection via Gamified Robot-Free Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18650)
*Zheng Li,Liang Zhu,Junzhe Wang,Huayuan Chen,Ziyun Liu,Jiahang Cao,Xinyu Sheng,Pei Qu,Yufei Jia,Ximeng Zhang,Jiarui Xie,Zizhao Yuan,Haoang Li,Yi Cai,Jinni Zhou,Jun Ma*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper proposes Project Kitchen, a VR-based gamified egocentric data collection platform that is robot-agnostic, paired with Game2Policy which extracts embodiment-invariant affordance cues from gameplay to transfer manipulation knowledge to real robots via few-shot fine-tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Learning generalizable robot manipulation policies demands large-scale, diverse interaction data, but real-world demonstration collection is costly and hard to scale. Existing methods either depend on specific robot hardware that restricts crowdsourcing and transferability, or yield incomplete annotation and limited behavioral diversity. Inspired by how games sustain long-term human engagement, the authors seek a paradigm that converts data collection into engaging gameplay and transfers the resulting human manipulation experience to real robots.

Method: Project Kitchen: a VR-based gamified egocentric data collection platform that elicits diverse, goal-directed manipulation while remaining independent of specific robot embodiments and hardware. Game2Policy: extracts embodiment-invariant affordance cues (contact points and sub-goal states) from gameplay trajectories; an affordance model is pre-trained on game-collected data and jointly fine-tuned with downstream policies using only a handful of real-robot demonstrations.

Result: Game2Policy improves average success rates by 10.0 points in simulation and 18.3 points on real robots in the few-shot setting. User studies and quantitative analyses show that Project Kitchen promotes diverse manipulation behaviors and provides an engaging data collection experience.

Conclusion: Gamified virtual environments show potential as a scalable source of manipulation knowledge. The platform and code will be released upon acceptance.

Abstract: Learning generalizable robot manipulation policies requires large-scale and diverse interaction data, yet collecting real-world demonstrations remains costly and difficult to scale. Existing approaches to data collection are either dependent on specific robot hardware that limits crowdsourcing and transferability, or suffer from incomplete annotation and limited behavioral diversity. Inspired by how games sustain long-term human engagement, we explore an alternative paradigm that turns data collection into an engaging gameplay experience and transfers the resulting human manipulation experience to real robots. We present Project Kitchen, a VR-based gamified egocentric data collection platform that elicits diverse, goal-directed manipulation while remaining independent of specific robot embodiments and hardware, making it applicable to broader and potentially large-scale deployment. To bridge the game-to-real gap, we further introduce Game2Policy, which extracts embodiment-invariant affordance cues, including contact points and sub-goal states, from gameplay trajectories. An affordance model is pre-trained on game-collected data and then jointly fine-tuned with downstream policies using only a handful of real-robot demonstrations. Experiments show that Game2Policy improves average success rates by 10.0 points in simulation and 18.3 points on real robots in the few-shot setting. User studies and quantitative analyses further show that Project Kitchen promotes diverse manipulation behaviors and provides an engaging data collection experience. These results demonstrate the potential of gamified virtual environments as a scalable source of manipulation knowledge. The platform and code will be released upon acceptance.

</details>


### [148] [FIERCE: From Generalist Robot Policies to Fast Specialists via Progress-Failure Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18651)
*Runjia Tan,Yuang Tu,Yujie Yan,Lan Yu,Xuesong Tian,Chen Lv*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: FIERCE is a generalist-initialized RL framework that refines compact specialists using a unified task-adaptive progress-failure evaluator, eliminating the need for continued generalist queries, target-task simulators, or manual dense rewards.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Generalist robot policies provide useful initialization, but refining compact specialists through limited physical interaction requires informative learning feedback. Existing refinement approaches typically depend on continued generalist action queries, dedicated target-task simulators, or manually annotated dense rewards, which are costly and impractical in real contact-rich settings.

Method: FIERCE centers on a unified, task-adaptive progress-failure evaluator whose architecture shares an observation-language representation between an observed-progress head and an action-conditioned latent predictor; past and current predictions feed a causal sequence head for task-failure estimation. The evaluator is trained with joint supervision from progress and preference labels, synchronized commands and observations, and terminal outcomes, then adapted and calibrated on target-task rollouts. Fixed evaluator snapshots supply progress shaping and failure-risk penalties alongside independently verified terminal rewards, while evaluator and policy updates alternate as new experience is collected. Only the compact specialist is retained at deployment.

Result: The evaluation separately measures feedback quality, policy-learning efficiency, and deployment cost across simulation and two contact-rich real-world tasks.

Conclusion: FIERCE enables generalist-initialized refinement of compact specialists without continued generalist action queries, a dedicated target-task simulator, or manually annotated dense rewards. Code, model weights, and data-restoration tools are released at https://github.com/ar-mine/FIERCE.

Abstract: Generalist robot policies offer useful initialization, but refining compact specialists through limited physical interaction requires informative learning feedback. We present FIERCE, a generalist-initialized reinforcement learning framework centered on a unified, task-adaptive progress-failure evaluator. Its architecture shares an observation-language representation between an observed-progress head and an action-conditioned latent predictor whose past and current predictions feed a causal sequence head for task-failure estimation. Joint supervision from progress and preference labels, synchronized commands and observations, and terminal outcomes trains the evaluator; target-task rollouts support adaptation and calibration. Fixed evaluator snapshots provide progress shaping and failure-risk penalties alongside independently verified terminal rewards, while evaluator and policy updates alternate as new experience is collected. Refinement requires neither continued generalist action queries nor a dedicated target-task simulator or manually annotated dense rewards. Only the compact specialist is retained at deployment. The evaluation separates feedback quality, policy-learning efficiency, and deployment cost across simulation and two contact-rich real tasks. Code, model weights, and data-restoration tools are released at https://github.com/ar-mine/FIERCE.

</details>


### [149] [VLA-ULAP: Interleaving Cloud VLA Calls with Ultra-Lightweight Local Action Prediction at the Edge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18663)
*Deyu Cao,Ryuji Oi,Kosuke Matsushima,Yuxuan Pan,Ziheng Wang,Daichi Fujiki,Atsutake Kosuge*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: VLA-ULAP interleaves remote billion-parameter VLA calls with a 7.4M-parameter local action predictor, cutting VLA calls by ~49-77% while retaining ~95-100% of baseline success and reducing inference time and energy by roughly half.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Billion-parameter vision-language-action (VLA) policies require substantial onboard power, while communication delays in remote inference hinder timely responses. There is a need for a method that reduces reliance on remote/large VLA calls without sacrificing task success or responsiveness.

Method: VLA-ULAP interleaves remote VLA calls with an Ultra-Lightweight Local Action Predictor (ULAP). ULAP has ~7.4M parameters (including a frozen vision encoder) and combines current views, proprioception, and executed action history to predict action chunks in a single pass. It is trained independently, requiring no VLA hidden states, online verification, or server round trips.

Result: On Jetson Orin Nano, ULAP takes 19.9 ms and 0.183 J per inference vs 284.3 ms and 50.55 J for GR00T on RTX A6000. Across three simulated base-policy/benchmark pairs, selected operating points remove 48.8-76.7% of VLA calls while retaining 95.0-97.5% of baseline success. Against local VLA-acceleration alternatives on VLA-JEPA, ULAP uses ~49.2% less inference time and 51.0% less GPU energy per successful episode than ACT at comparable success, and 77.1% less time and 79.9% less energy than SP-VLA at equal success. Physical SO-101 experiments retain 95.2-100% of baseline success across seen and held-out placements while reducing inference time by ~47.9-58.0% and inference-device energy by 52.1-62.5%. In latency-aware LIBERO-Safety simulation, VLA-ULAP exceeds π0.5 by 11.0 and 15.5 percentage points on two tasks while roughly halving VLA calls.

Conclusion: A lightweight local action predictor interleaved with remote VLA calls can substantially reduce VLA invocations, inference time, and energy while largely preserving success rates, and can even improve success on dynamic latency-sensitive tasks by enabling faster responses.

Abstract: Billion-parameter vision--language--action (VLA) policies demand substantial onboard power, while communication delays in remote inference hinder timely responses. We propose VLA-ULAP, which interleaves remote VLA calls with an Ultra-Lightweight Local Action Predictor (ULAP). With approximately 7.4M parameters including the frozen vision encoder, ULAP combines current views, proprioception, and executed action history to predict chunks in one pass. Trained independently, it requires no VLA hidden states, online verification, or server round trips. On Jetson Orin Nano, ULAP takes 19.9 ms and 0.183 J per inference, compared with 284.3 ms and 50.55 J for GR00T on RTX A6000. Across three simulated base-policy/benchmark pairs, selected operating points remove 48.8--76.7\% of VLA calls while retaining 95.0--97.5\% of the baseline success rate. Against local VLA-acceleration alternatives on VLA-JEPA, ULAP uses an estimated 49.2\% less inference time and 51.0\% less GPU energy per successful episode than ACT at comparable success rates, and 77.1\% less time and 79.9\% less energy than SP-VLA at equal success rates. Physical SO-101 experiments retain 95.2--100\% of the baseline success rate across seen and held-out placements while reducing inference time by an estimated 47.9--58.0\% and inference-device energy by 52.1--62.5\%, based on successful-episode call counts and measured device costs. Faster responses also improve dynamic-task success rates: in latency-aware LIBERO-Safety simulation, VLA-ULAP exceeds $π_{0.5}$ by 11.0 and 15.5 percentage points on two tasks while approximately halving VLA calls.

</details>


### [150] [M$^3$P-R1: Reinforcement Learning for Large Language Model Guided Multi-Modal Motion Planning via MIP Code Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18669)
*Xingpeng Sun,Zherong Pan,Kai Cheng,Xindi Tang,Syed Talha Bukhari,Aniket Bera*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: M$^3$P-R1 fine-tunes LLMs via RL to generate executable MIP code that decomposes multi-modal motion planning tasks into variables, constraints, and objectives, achieving verifiable solver-backed solutions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-Modal Motion Planning requires joint reasoning over continuous motions and discrete mode transitions (e.g., a bipedal robot walking then grasping). Pure discrete or continuous planners cannot handle these mixed problems. Mixed-Integer Programming (MIP) is a principled framework, but constructing tractable formulations for non-convex problems in the discretization-based MIP regime is manual and domain-specific.

Method: M$^3$P-R1 fine-tunes large language models using reinforcement learning to decompose M$^3$P tasks into MIP variables, constraints, and objectives. Rather than emitting answers directly (prone to hallucination), the model generates executable Python code via MIP optimization libraries and constraint interfaces, enabling solver-backed execution. Training uses an outcome-driven reward against the solver.

Result: The model learns to compose modality-level discretization primitives and synthesize cross-modal coupling constraints, producing executable MIP programs for complex M$^3$P tasks with robust, verifiable solutions.

Conclusion: M$^3$P-R1 offers an automated, learning-based route to constructing MIP formulations for multi-modal motion planning, replacing manual, domain-specific modeling with LLM-generated, solver-verifiable code.

Abstract: Multi-Modal Motion Planning (M$^3$P) requires joint reasoning over continuous motions and discrete mode transitions, making it difficult to solve efficiently. For instance, a bipedal robot may walk to a target location and then use its arms to grasp an object. This scenario captures both mode transitions and continuous dynamics, yielding feasible paths that neither purely discrete nor continuous planners can handle. While Mixed-Integer Programming (MIP) offers a principled framework, constructing tractable formulations for non-convex problems is typically manual and domain-specific, especially in the approximate, discretization-based MIP regime needed for non-convex robotic tasks. We propose M$^3$P-R1, a reinforcement learning method that fine-tunes large language models (LLMs) to decompose M$^3$P tasks into MIP variables, constraints, and objectives. Instead of directly outputting answers, which are often prone to hallucination, the model generates executable Python code using MIP optimization libraries and constraint interfaces. This enables solver-backed execution for robust and verifiable solutions. Trained with an outcome-driven reward against the solver, M$^3$P-R1 learns to compose modality-level discretization primitives and synthesize cross-modal coupling constraints, producing executable MIP programs for complex M$^3$P tasks.

</details>


### [151] [WeaveRL: Weaving Reconstruction into Scene-Aware Fabrics for Perceptive Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18685)
*Remo Steiner,Vikram Ramasamy,David Tingdahl,Sam Mady,Karl Van Wyk,Nathan Ratliff,David Recasens Lafuente,Soha Pouya,Tuur Stuyck,Alex Millane*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper introduces a GPU-accelerated online 3D surfel reconstruction integrated into massively parallel RL training, enabling manipulation policies to learn on top of collision-avoidant geometric fabrics over sensor-derived geometry rather than hand-specified scenes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Learning policies for geometrically complex manipulation is hard. Prior work builds policies on collision-avoidant controllers like geometric fabrics, but relies on static, hand-specified scene representations. Online active 3D perception had not been integrated into massively parallel RL training, which limits scalability and generalization to real sensor data.

Method: A GPU-accelerated reconstruction method represents the scene as a collection of surfels across thousands of parallel simulation instances during active rollouts. Policies operate over these sensor-derived surfel representations, combined with geometric fabric controllers (surfel fabrics), instead of hand-specified geometry.

Result: On a suite of collision-dense manipulation tasks, surfel fabrics solve geometrically complex scenes where primitive-based baselines fail, while retaining sim-to-real transfer. Scene-aware fabric policies are more robust to novel geometry, raising collision-free task completion under unseen obstacles from 35% to 61%.

Conclusion: Integrating online surfel-based reconstruction into parallel RL training enables effective, transferable, and robust collision-avoidant manipulation policies. The authors release the reconstruction system, training code, and test dataset to support further research.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning allows robots to acquire complex skills, but producing policies for geometrically complex manipulation remains difficult. A promising approach is to learn on top of collision-avoidant controllers, such as geometric fabrics. However, these approaches have relied on static, hand-specified representations of the scene. Integrating active, online 3D perception into massively parallel RL training has so far been inaccessible. We introduce a GPU-accelerated method that reconstructs the scene as a collection of surfels across thousands of parallel simulation instances during active rollouts. This lets policies operate over sensor-derived, rather than hand-specified, geometry. On a suite of collision-dense manipulation tasks, our surfel fabrics enable policies to tackle geometrically complex scenes where primitive-based baselines fail, while maintaining sim-to-real transfer. Furthermore, policies learned with a scene-aware fabric are more robust to the introduction of novel geometry at test time, improving collision-free task completion under unseen obstacles from 35% to 61%. We release our reconstruction system, training code and test dataset to spur research in this direction.

</details>


### [152] [Toward 3D Printable Non-Planar Electroadhesive Structures for Active Anchoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18700)
*Mostafa A. Atalla,Carol R. Derla,Pep Canyelles Pericàs,Gijs Krijnen*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper uses multi-material 3D printing to fabricate non-planar electroadhesion pads with 3D-printed interdigitated electrodes, demonstrating voltage-controlled adhesion in both flat and cylindrical geometries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional electroadhesion pads are typically planar, limiting their integration into complex, built-in electrically controlled adhesion and friction on arbitrary 3D-printed parts. Multi-material 3D printing offers a route to fabricate non-planar structures with embedded electrode patterns, motivating an investigation of printed electroadhesion beyond flat geometries.

Method: Multi-material 3D printing was employed to fabricate photopolymer structures with conductive interdigitated electrodes printed as an integral part of the structure. Two geometries were produced: flat electroadhesion pads as a planar benchmark and cylindrical pads as a non-planar demonstration. Normal-force measurements were conducted to characterize voltage-controlled adhesion, including tests at 5 N preload for the cylindrical pads.

Result: Both flat and cylindrical pads exhibited voltage-controlled modulation of normal force. At 3 kV, flat pads generated approximately 0.11-0.13 N, while cylindrical pads reached approximately 0.16 N at 5 N preload.

Conclusion: The results demonstrate a step toward printed parts with built-in, electrically controlled adhesion and friction, extending electroadhesion beyond conventional planar pads.

Abstract: This paper investigates multi-material 3D printing as a method to fabricate non-planar structures with 3D-printed electrode patterns for electroadhesion. We printed flat electroadhesion pads as a planar benchmark and cylindrical pads as a non-planar demonstration, with conductive interdigitated electrodes 3D printed as part of the structure. Normal-force measurements showed voltage-controlled modulation in both geometries. At 3 kV, the flat pads generated approximately 0.11-0.13 N, while the cylindrical pads reached approximately 0.16 N at 5 N preload. These results demonstrate a step toward printed parts with built-in, electrically controlled adhesion and friction, beyond conventional planar electroadhesion pads.

</details>


### [153] [Calibrated Probabilistic Obstruction Reasoning with Vision-Language Models for Grasping in Clutter](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18718)
*Thanh-Tuan Tran,Ngoc-Chien Chu,Thanh Nguyen Canh,Nak Young Chong,Nguyen-Viet Ha,Xiem HoangVan*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: CPOR-Grasp is a calibrated probabilistic obstruction-reasoning framework that propagates uncertainty from pairwise evidence to action decisions, achieving state-of-the-art results on UNOBench and real-world grasping.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Retrieving a target from clutter requires deciding whether to grasp, remove a blocker, or defer. Existing methods commit to a single obstruction graph or removal strategy, ignoring uncertainty across alternative scene interpretations, rely on miscalibrated VLM predictions, produce jointly inconsistent pairwise obstruction relations, and offer no guarantees about the impact of discarded hypotheses on the final decision.

Method: CPOR-Grasp calibrates and fuses VLM, depth, and amodal-mask cues to estimate obstruction probabilities, induces a distribution over valid obstruction graphs, and marginalizes over these graphs to compute the likelihood that the target is accessible or that a given blocker should be removed. For tractable inference, it retains only the highest-probability graphs and derives a total-variation bound on the discarded probability mass, enabling certified decisions, adaptive stopping, and principled deferral.

Result: On synthetic and real UNOBench scenes, CPOR-Grasp outperforms state-of-the-art baselines. Calibration error decreases from 0.1416 to 0.0185 on the Gemini Robotics backbone, while graph truncation matches exact inference on 99.74% of decisions using 56 times fewer graphs. In real-world experiments, it achieves a 77.8% average success rate, surpassing SOTA baselines.

Conclusion: CPOR-Grasp provides a calibrated, uncertainty-aware obstruction-reasoning framework with certified decisions via a total-variation bound on discarded probability mass, yielding superior accuracy, calibration, and computational efficiency over existing approaches.

Abstract: Retrieving a target from clutter requires deciding whether to grasp the target, remove a blocker, or defer. Existing methods typically commit to a single obstruction graph or removal strategy, ignoring uncertainty across alternative scene interpretations. They also rely on miscalibrated vision-language model (VLM) predictions and can produce pairwise obstruction relations that are jointly inconsistent. Moreover, current approximations provide no guarantees about the impact of discarded hypotheses on the final decision. We propose CPOR-Grasp, a calibrated probabilistic obstruction-reasoning framework that propagates uncertainty from pairwise evidence to action decisions. CPOR-Grasp calibrates and fuses VLM, depth, and amodal-mask cues to estimate obstruction probabilities, induces a distribution over valid obstruction graphs, and marginalizes over these graphs to compute the likelihood that the target is accessible or that a given blocker should be removed. To make inference tractable, it retains only the highest-probability graphs and derives a total-variation bound on the discarded probability mass, enabling certified decisions, adaptive stopping, and principled deferral. On synthetic and real UNOBench scenes, CPOR-Grasp outperforms state-of-the-art baselines. Calibration error decreases from 0.1416 to 0.0185 on the Gemini Robotics backbone, while graph truncation matches exact inference on 99.74\% of decisions using 56 times fewer graphs. In real-world experiments, CPOR-Grasp achieves a 77.8\% average success rate, surpassing SOTA baselines.

</details>


### [154] [PASSAGE: Scaling Scene-Aligned Motion Learning for Perceptive Humanoid Traversal in Cluttered Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18732)
*Yuxuan Ma,Zicheng Zeng,Chunlin Peng,Zhoujian Li,Zetong Zhao,Zhikai Zhang,Yunrui Lian,Han Xue,Sikai Liang,Weiyi Zhu,Mulin Chen,Chenghuai Lin,Jiayu Zeng,Yanwei An,Songan Zhang,Jiayuan Gu,Jilong Wang,Jingbo Wang,He Wang,Li Yi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: PASSAGE is a perception-conditioned planner-tracker framework that enables humanoid robots to select and coordinate traversal behaviors (stepping over, squeezing past, ducking under) from onboard perception, using 100 h of VR/IMU-captured scene-aligned human motion across 1,500 cluttered scenes, achieving 70.3% contact-free success in simulation and fully onboard real-world deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Humanoid robots can perform traversal behaviors like stepping over, squeezing past, and ducking under obstacles, but learning to select and coordinate these behaviors from onboard perception is challenging. Existing approaches rely on task-specific RL objectives or curated motion libraries, making broad behavioral coverage costly.

Method: Collect 100 h of scene-aligned human motion across 1,500 cluttered scenes via VR and inertial motion capture. A conditional flow-matching planner generates short-horizon references from motion history, a local destination, and a robot-centric multi-layer elevation map. A perceptive whole-body tracker executes them at 50 Hz with geometric feedback. Real-time chunking promotes inter-chunk consistency, and planner-side RL post-training under the frozen tracker improves closed-loop performance.

Result: Without skill annotations or obstacle-specific policies, one planner-tracker pair selects and composes traversal behaviors across unseen geometries. Component ablations quantify each stage's contribution. Scaling data from 6 to 100 h increases mean contact-free success from 48.1% to 68.9% on held-out scenes; the final model with validated scene augmentation reaches 70.3%. The fully onboard system integrates egocentric 3D LiDAR, online occupancy mapping, 6.25 Hz planning, and 50 Hz control on a Jetson AGX Orin, traversing 50 unseen physical layouts without prebuilt maps or offboard computation.

Conclusion: A single perception-conditioned planner-tracker pair, trained on large-scale scene-aligned human motion without skill annotations, achieves broad behavioral coverage and generalizes to unseen geometries, demonstrating effective and fully onboard humanoid traversal.

Abstract: Humanoid robots can step over, squeeze past, and duck under obstacles, but learning to select and coordinate these behaviors from onboard perception remains challenging. Many existing approaches rely on task-specific reinforcement-learning objectives or curated motion libraries, making broad behavioral coverage costly. We present PASSAGE, a perception-conditioned planner--tracker framework for humanoid traversal. Using virtual reality and inertial motion capture, we collect 100 h of scene-aligned human motion across 1,500 cluttered scenes. A conditional flow-matching planner generates short-horizon references from motion history, a local destination, and a robot-centric multi-layer elevation map, while a perceptive whole-body tracker executes them at 50 Hz with geometric feedback. Real-time chunking promotes inter-chunk consistency, and planner-side RL post-training under the frozen tracker further improves closed-loop performance. Without skill annotations or obstacle-specific policies, one planner--tracker pair selects and composes traversal behaviors across unseen geometries. In simulation, component ablations quantify the contribution of each stage. Across three independent training seeds, scaling captured data from 6 to 100 h increases mean contact-free success from 48.1% to 68.9% on held-out scenes, while the final model with validated scene augmentation reaches 70.3%. The fully onboard system integrates egocentric 3D LiDAR perception, online occupancy mapping, 6.25 Hz planning, and 50 Hz control on a Jetson AGX Orin; tests across 50 unseen physical layouts demonstrate traversal without prebuilt maps or offboard computation.

</details>


### [155] [Active perception for robotic harvesting: 3D reconstruction and localisation of tomatoes hidden within clusters in a Mediterranean greenhouse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18738)
*Fernando Cañadas-Aránega,Rowan Border,José C. Moreno,José L. Blanco-Claraco*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A five-stage pipeline reconstructs and localizes tomatoes in clusters, including occluded ones, achieving >90% precision, 82.8% recall, 80.7% mIoU, and 4.2 mm centroid RMSE.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robotic harvesting in Mediterranean greenhouses is hindered by plant geometric complexity and occluded fruits. Existing methods target isolated crops (apples, sweet peppers, peaches), but cluster-growing vegetables defeat fixed sensors, which cannot detect fruits hidden behind the visible surface.

Method: Five sequential stages: i) point cloud acquisition with the AgriSEE Next Best View (NBV) active planner; ii) stochastic noise filtering via Statistical Outlier Removal (SOR); iii) surface classification and segmentation with Region Growing (RG); iv) isolation and recovery of occluded fruits via DBSCAN; v) 3D pose estimation (position and orientation).

Result: Evaluated in multiple occlusion-level scenarios within a simulation framework validated against real-world conditions: precision >90%, average recall 82.8%, mIoU 80.7%, and centroid estimation RMSE of 4.2 mm with high repeatability.

Conclusion: The approach extracts complete cluster geometry and reliably identifies partially hidden tomatoes, confirming technical feasibility and high accuracy for autonomous harvesting operations.

Abstract: Automating robotic harvesting in intensive agriculture within Mediterranean greenhouses requires overcoming significant challenges related to the geometric complexity of plants and occluded fruits. Although existing literature offers solutions targeting crops that grow in isolation (e.g., apples, sweet peppers, or peaches), the fundamental challenge lies in cluster-growing vegetables, where fixed sensors mounted on robotic systems fail to detect fruits hidden behind the visible surface. To address this limitation, this study presents a comprehensive pipeline for the 3D reconstruction and precise localization of each fruit within a cluster, including heavily occluded instances. The proposed methodology is structured into five sequential stages: i) point cloud acquisition using the AgriSEE Next Best View (NBV) active planner; ii) stochastic noise filtering via Statistical Outlier Removal (SOR); iii) surface classification and segmentation using Region Growing (RG); iv) isolation and recovery of occluded fruits through Density-Based Spatial Clustering of Applications with Noise (DBSCAN); and v) 3D pose estimation (position and orientation). This approach extracts the complete cluster geometry, ensuring the reliable identification of partially hidden tomatoes. Evaluated across multiple scenarios with varying occlusion levels within a simulation framework rigorously validated against real-world conditions, the system achieves a precision exceeding 90\%, an average recall of 82.8\%, and a mean Intersection over Union (mIoU) of 80.7\%. Furthermore, it demonstrates high repeatability in centroid estimation with a Root Mean Square Error (RMSE) of merely 4.2~mm, verifying its technical feasibility and high accuracy for autonomous harvesting operations.

</details>


### [156] [QMSR: Query-Conditioned Mask-wise Expert Routing for Robust Open-Vocabulary Underwater Object Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18752)
*Fuming Zhang,Dongyue Huang,Junjie Wen,Lihua Xie*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: QMSR introduces query-conditioned, mask-wise expert routing to adaptively fuse Raw and UIE representations per query-candidate pair, improving underwater open-vocabulary retrieval by 17.6% NDCG@10 over a shared router.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In underwater open-vocabulary retrieval, fixed UIE preprocessing can hurt retrieval and even underperform Raw representations, showing that enhancement should not be applied uniformly. There is a need for adaptive, query-specific enhancement strategies.

Method: QMSR selects one pretrained UIE expert per query-candidate pair and predicts a continuous Raw-Expert fusion strength, preserving Raw semantics while applying adaptive enhancement. Training uses a privileged ranking oracle for expert-selection and fusion-strength supervision, plus an annealed soft-routing relaxation to optimize the hard Top-1 routing policy.

Result: QMSR improves NDCG@10 by 17.6% over an image-query shared router, consistently outperforming fixed UIE strategies and remaining effective on held-out query categories.

Conclusion: Query-conditioned and candidate-specific enhancement routing is effective for underwater open-vocabulary retrieval.

Abstract: Open-vocabulary object retrieval remains challenging in complex underwater environments. Although underwater image enhancement (UIE) can improve visual quality, fixed UIE strategies may even underperform the Raw representation in retrieval, indicating that enhancement should not be applied as a uniform preprocessing step. To address this problem, we propose \textbf{QMSR}, a query-conditioned mask-wise expert routing framework for underwater open-vocabulary retrieval. Specifically, QMSR selects one pretrained UIE expert for each query--candidate pair and predicts a continuous Raw--Expert fusion strength, enabling adaptive enhancement while preserving useful Raw semantics. During training, a privileged ranking oracle provides expert-selection and fusion-strength supervision, while an annealed soft-routing relaxation facilitates optimization of the hard Top-1 routing policy. Experiments show that QMSR improves NDCG@10 by 17.6\% over an image--query shared router, while consistently outperforming fixed UIE strategies and remaining effective on held-out query categories. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of query-conditioned and candidate-specific enhancement routing for underwater open-vocabulary retrieval.

</details>


### [157] [Gated Residual Body-Hand Coordination for Whole-Body Humanoid Teleoperation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18763)
*Ruiming Wu,Shuang Li,Liding Zhang,Alois Knoll,Zhaopeng Chen*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A gated residual coordination framework corrects frozen body-tracking and hand-retargeting outputs to preserve body-hand geometry in humanoid teleoperation, reducing wrist/fingertip errors by 39.2-56.3% while maintaining whole-body tracking.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Whole-body humanoid teleoperation typically composes a motion-tracking policy with a separate dexterous-hand retargeter, but these independently generated commands do not explicitly preserve body-hand geometric relations. This causes mismatches in relative wrist poses and fingertip positions during bimanual interaction.

Method: The approach keeps both modules frozen and applies bounded residual corrections to their outputs. A motion-conditioned action gate allocates correction authority across joint groups, and reference-geometry-dependent reward gates emphasize relevant interaction objectives during training. To establish the nominal body controller on Agile One, multi-pose morphology calibration jointly estimates triaxial scales and effector-local offsets, alongside staged motion dataset curation for a SONIC-based tracker. The residual policy uses human motion references, initial commands, and robot proprioception, without explicit object or contact observations.

Result: In simulation, the framework reduces wrist and fingertip geometry errors by 39.2-56.3% over direct composition on held-out GRAB motions, while preserving whole-body tracking on AMASS. Success rates are 89.03% without residual coordination and 89.29% with it. Ablations characterize the contributions of reward gating, adaptive correction authority, and separate body and hand correction heads.

Conclusion: Gated residual coordination offers an effective, module-preserving method to enforce body-hand geometric consistency in whole-body humanoid teleoperation, improving interaction fidelity without degrading whole-body tracking.

Abstract: Whole-body humanoid teleoperation commonly combines a motion-tracking policy with a separate dexterous-hand retargeter. However, independently generated commands do not explicitly preserve body-hand geometric relations, leading to mismatches in relative wrist poses and fingertip positions during bimanual interaction. We present a gated residual coordination framework that keeps both modules frozen and applies bounded corrections to their outputs. A motion-conditioned action gate allocates correction authority across joint groups, while reference-geometry-dependent reward gates emphasize relevant interaction objectives during training. To establish the nominal body controller on Agile One, we introduce multi-pose morphology calibration that jointly estimates triaxial scales and effector-local offsets, together with staged motion dataset curation for training a SONIC-based tracker. The residual policy uses human motion references, initial commands, and robot proprioception without explicit object or contact observations. In simulation, it reduces wrist and fingertip geometry errors by 39.2-56.3% over direct composition on held-out GRAB motions, while preserving whole-body tracking on AMASS, with success rates of 89.03% without residual coordination and 89.29% with it. Ablations characterize the contributions of reward gating, adaptive correction authority, and separate body and hand correction heads.

</details>


### [158] [TRACER: Adaptive Multi-Robot Social Navigation via Joint Human-Response Prediction and Interaction-Aware Replanning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18776)
*Lan Hu,Minghui Liwang,Wenbo Zhu,Xinlei Yi,Wei Gong,Yiguang Hong,Seyyedali Hosseinalipour*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: TRACER is a bi-directional receding-horizon framework that closes the loop between prediction and adaptation for multi-robot navigation in human-shared spaces, modeling joint robot-entity interactions via a per-entity probabilistic response model and updating persistent identity-bound beliefs to guide replanning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods handle action-conditioned prediction, multi-robot planning, or online adaptation separately, lacking a unified mechanism to model joint robot-entity interactions and adapt future decisions from executed interaction outcomes.

Method: TRACER evaluates candidate robot-team trajectories using a per-entity probabilistic response model that separates individual-robot effects from non-additive pairwise interactions; after executing the selected trajectory prefix, it updates persistent identity-bound beliefs over latent response modes using synchronized observed responses, which then guide subsequent candidate evaluation under probabilistic safety and response-aware cost criteria.

Result: TRACER more accurately captures non-additive multi-robot interaction effects than a capacity-matched additive predictor; persistent identity-consistent evidence improves response prediction and downstream replanning; the full framework improves collision-free completion over an independent-robot baseline on the SocialGym2 multi-robot social-navigation benchmark.

Conclusion: Closing the loop between prediction and adaptation through non-additive interaction modeling and persistent identity-bound belief updating yields more accurate interaction modeling and better collision-free multi-robot navigation performance.

Abstract: Multi-robot navigation in human-shared spaces is inherently interactive: coordinated robot motions influence how nearby entities respond, while those responses provide valuable information for subsequent robot decisions. However, existing methods typically address action-conditioned prediction, multi-robot planning, or online adaptation separately, and therefore lack a unified mechanism for modeling joint robot-entity interactions and adapting future decisions from executed interaction outcomes. To address this gap, we propose TRACER, a bi-directional receding-horizon framework that closes the loop between prediction and adaptation. TRACER evaluates candidate (i.e., alternative feasible future motion plans for the robot team) trajectories using a per-entity probabilistic response model that separates individual-robot effects from non-additive pairwise interactions; after executing the selected trajectory prefix, it updates persistent identity-bound beliefs over latent response modes using the synchronized observed responses. These updated beliefs then guide subsequent candidate evaluation under probabilistic safety and response-aware cost criteria. Experiments show that (i) TRACER more accurately captures non-additive multi-robot interaction effects than a capacity-matched additive predictor, (ii) persistent identity-consistent evidence improves response prediction and downstream replanning, and (iii) the complete TRACER framework improves collision-free completion over an independent-robot baseline on the SocialGym2 multi-robot social-navigation benchmark.

</details>


### [159] [AdaGeoVLN: Selective Geometry Across Representation Depth and Navigation Time for Vision-Language Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18789)
*Quan-Dung Pham,Anh Dao,Danh Vinh Le,Nguyen Viet Tri Pham,The-Anh Nguyen,Zhirui Dai,Yiyu Chen,Tuyen P. Le,Truong Nguyen,Quan Nguyen*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper introduces a streaming Vision-Language Navigation (VLN) framework that addresses two questions: how navigation policies should consume Geometry Foundation Model (GFM) features across representation depth, and how historical geometric evidence should be retained over navigation time, via hierarchical GFM-VLM fusion and navigation-aware GFM-KV memory.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLN requires aligning language with visual observations while maintaining spatial understanding over time. Although GFMs expose intermediate representations throughout their hierarchy, it remains unresolved how navigation policies should exploit these features and retain historical geometric evidence, motivating a joint study of representation depth and navigation time.

Method: The authors propose a streaming VLN framework with two components: (1) Hierarchical GFM-VLM fusion that couples earlier, intermediate, and later GFM representations to successive policy stages rather than repeatedly injecting a terminal feature; and (2) a navigation-aware GFM memory that retains historical VGGT global-attention KV states based on instruction relevance, geometric confidence, and transition novelty under a bounded per-layer budget, providing geometric context for subsequent observations before fusion with the policy.

Result: On R2R-CE and RxR-CE, the method achieves strong performance using a single RGB stream without additional navigation-specific external data. Controlled ablations show multi-depth coupling substantially outperforms repeated terminal-feature injection at matched fusion locations, and bounded navigation-aware retention preserves navigation performance while considerably reducing GFM-KV memory versus larger-memory temporal retention.

Conclusion: These findings support jointly examining the geometric representations exposed to the policy and the historical evidence retained for future inference. Code will be released upon acceptance.

Abstract: Vision-language navigation requires aligning language with visual observations while maintaining spatial understanding over time. Geometry foundation models (GFMs) expose intermediate representations throughout their hierarchy, but how navigation policies should use these features and retain historical geometric evidence remains unresolved. We introduce \method{}, a streaming VLN framework that addresses these questions across \textbf{representation depth} and \textbf{navigation time}. Hierarchical GFM--VLM fusion couples earlier, intermediate, and later GFM representations to successive policy stages instead of repeatedly injecting a terminal feature. Navigation-aware GFM memory retains historical VGGT global-attention KV states according to instruction relevance, geometric confidence, and transition novelty under a bounded per-layer budget. Retained states provide geometric context for subsequent observations before fusion with the policy. Across R2R-CE and RxR-CE, \method{} achieves strong performance using a single RGB stream without additional navigation-specific external data. Controlled ablations show that multi-depth coupling substantially outperforms repeated terminal-feature injection at matched fusion locations. Bounded navigation-aware retention preserves navigation performance while considerably reducing GFM-KV memory relative to larger-memory temporal retention. These findings support jointly examining the geometric representations exposed to the policy and the historical evidence retained for future inference. Code will be released upon acceptance at https://humanoid-research.github.io/adageovln/.

</details>


### [160] [Asymptotically Optimal Multi-Robot Task and Motion Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18813)
*Thi Thuy Ngan Duong,Cheuk Tung Shadow Yiu,Rahul Shome,Yoonchang Sung*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper establishes sufficient conditions for global asymptotic optimality in multi-robot task and motion planning (MR-TAMP) and proposes an efficient algorithm combining evolving per-robot roadmaps with implicit tensor-product search and heuristics like conditional transition sampling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Extending asymptotic optimality from single-robot TAMP to the multi-robot setting is challenging because different task transitions involve different robot subsets, imposing constraints of varying dimensions on the composite configuration space. A globally optimal planner must both optimize motion within each task mode and adequately explore the heterogeneous transitions between them—an aspect not captured by existing asymptotically optimal TAMP algorithms.

Method: The authors first characterize the transition structure of MR-TAMP and derive sufficient conditions for global asymptotic optimality: persistent coverage of relevant transitions plus asymptotically improving motion planning within connected feasible regions. Building on these conditions, they design an algorithm that couples evolving individual-robot roadmaps with implicit tensor-product search, thereby avoiding explicit construction of the composite roadmap. Finite-time efficiency is enhanced via conditional transition sampling, lazy collision checking, and mode- and solution-level guidance, while preserving persistent exploration.

Result: The proposed framework yields an asymptotically optimal MR-TAMP planner that provides global asymptotic optimality guarantees for multi-robot manipulation while efficiently exploiting the structure of individual-robot motion planning.

Conclusion: The paper shows that global asymptotic optimality in MR-TAMP can be achieved by ensuring persistent transition coverage and intra-mode motion improvement, and that these conditions can be realized practically through implicit composite-space search over individual-robot roadmaps augmented with targeted sampling and guidance heuristics.

Abstract: Multi-robot task and motion planning (MR-TAMP) requires jointly reasoning about discrete task decisions and continuous collision-free motions of multiple interacting robots. Although asymptotically optimal algorithms have been developed for task and motion planning, extending these guarantees to the multi-robot setting introduces an important challenge: different task transitions may involve different subsets of robots and therefore impose constraints of different dimensions on the composite configuration space. Consequently, an asymptotically optimal planner must not only optimize motion within each task mode, but also ensure sufficient exploration of the different types of transitions connecting them. We characterize this transition structure and establish sufficient conditions for global asymptotic optimality in MR-TAMP, requiring persistent coverage of relevant transitions and asymptotically improving motion planning within connected feasible regions. Based on these conditions, we develop an efficient asymptotically optimal MR-TAMP algorithm that combines evolving individual-robot roadmaps with implicit tensor-product search, avoiding explicit construction of the composite roadmap. The planner further employs conditional transition sampling, lazy collision checking, and mode- and solution-level guidance to improve finite-time planning efficiency while retaining persistent exploration. The resulting framework provides asymptotic optimality guarantees for multi-robot manipulation while efficiently exploiting the structure of individual-robot motion planning.

</details>


### [161] [SEAM: Submap-Anchored Evidence for Lifelong LiDAR Mapping under Trajectory Deformation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18819)
*Kyuwon Kim*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: SEAM is a LiDAR-based lifelong mapping framework that uses submap-level anchors and direction-aware, voxel-wise evidence to enable efficient dynamic object removal and change detection, achieving higher accuracy and faster processing than existing methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing lifelong mapping methods typically rely on a single anchor spanning the entire session, which makes evidence generation fragile: if the trajectory is later modified by a new session, the entire process must be recomputed. Additionally, unreliable inter-session loop closures can cause alignment errors, and naive evidence models struggle to distinguish dynamic objects from genuine environmental changes.

Method: SEAM optimizes a trajectory using submap-level anchors to generate evidence, and performs dynamic object removal and change detection. Submap-level reprojection keeps evidence reusable even when the trajectory is subsequently modified by a new session, avoiding full recomputation. Unreliable inter-session loop edges are suppressed via a DOP-based confidence measure to prevent alignment errors. A directional voxel-wise evidence model captures occupancy patterns that vary with ray direction, enabling direction-aware separation of dynamic objects from environmental changes.

Result: Experiments on a real construction-site dataset and a long-term multi-session dataset show that SEAM achieves higher accuracy and faster processing than existing methods.

Conclusion: SEAM demonstrates that submap-level anchoring plus direction-aware evidence provides an effective and efficient solution for LiDAR lifelong mapping, improving both accuracy and computational speed over prior approaches.

Abstract: We propose SEAM, a LiDAR-based lifelong mapping framework. Instead of relying on a single anchor spanning the entire session, SEAM generates evidence based on a trajectory optimized with submap-level anchors, and performs dynamic object removal and change detection. Through submap-level reprojection, the generated evidence remains usable even if the trajectory is subsequently modified by a new session, eliminating the need to recompute the entire process from scratch. SEAM suppresses geometrically unreliable inter-session loop edges using a DOP-based confidence measure. Suppressing unreliable loop edges prevents alignment errors. SEAM also uses a directional voxel-wise evidence model. The model accounts for occupancy patterns that vary with ray direction. Direction-aware evidence separates dynamic objects from environmental changes more precisely. Experiments on a real construction-site dataset and a long-term multi-session dataset show that SEAM achieves higher accuracy and faster processing than existing methods.

</details>


### [162] [rMuscle: Robotic Muscle Memory for Efficient Vision-Language-Action Model Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19104)
*Kaijun Zhou,Zhiyang Li,Le Chen,Jinyu Gu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: rMuscle is a real-time VLA inference framework inspired by human muscle memory that exploits cross-execution task similarity via a dual-phase (Context + Action) cache to accelerate embodied robot policies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Factory work is a promising early scenario for embodied AI, and VLA models dominate as the policy paradigm. The inference latency of these models directly affects robot responsiveness and motion smoothness, yet existing VLA inference frameworks do not fully exploit embodied workload characteristics or address the distinct bottlenecks across different VLA inference stages.

Method: The authors first characterize embodied workloads, identifying substantial task similarity across repeated robot executions that extends beyond observations and action trajectories to internal model states. Based on this, they propose rMuscle, a dual-phase muscle-memory cache: the Context Cache reuses visual-token outputs to reduce computation, and the Action Cache reuses neuron activation patterns to reduce weight accesses. Memory footprint and access overhead are kept low via online cache recomputation, sliding-window cache retrieval, and mask sharing across consecutive denoising steps.

Result: rMuscle achieves 1.29-1.42X speedup on RTX 4090 and Jetson Thor across LIBERO, RoboTwin, and physical manipulation tasks, while maintaining the original success rates on real-world robots.

Conclusion: Exploiting cross-execution similarity in embodied workloads through a dual-phase cache enables real-time VLA inference acceleration without sacrificing task success rates.

Abstract: Factory work is a promising early scenario for embodied AI: assigning repetitive manual jobs to robots has clear economic payoff, and a structured station keeps the jobs tractable for current policies. Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models now dominate as the policy paradigm for these robots. The inference latency of VLA models directly affects robot responsiveness and motion smoothness. However, existing VLA inference frameworks do not fully exploit the characteristics of embodied workloads or account for the distinct bottlenecks across different stages of VLA inference.
  In this paper, we first characterize embodied workloads and identify substantial task similarity across repeated robot executions. We further find that such similarity extends beyond observations and action trajectories to internal model states. Drawing on these observations, we present rMuscle, a real-time VLA inference framework inspired by human muscle memory. It exploits cross-execution similarity through a dual-phase muscle-memory cache. The Context Cache reuses visual-token outputs to reduce computation, while the Action Cache reuses neuron activation patterns to reduce weight accesses. We keep both the cache memory footprint and access overhead low through online cache recomputation, sliding-window cache retrieval, and mask sharing across consecutive denoising steps. rMuscle achieves 1.29-1.42X speedup on RTX 4090 and Jetson Thor across LIBERO, RoboTwin, and physical manipulation tasks, while maintaining the original success rates on real-world robots.

</details>


### [163] [KINO: A Keyframe Interface for VLM Planning and Whole-Body Control in Humanoid Loco-Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18869)
*Sitong Chen,Fatemeh Zargarbashi,Jin Cheng,Tianxu An,Stelian Coros*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A hierarchical framework bridges VLM planning and RL whole-body control using motion keyframes—specifying target robot and object poses—as an intermediate representation, achieving task generalisation on a Unitree G1 humanoid.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Humanoid loco-manipulation demands that robots interpret task instructions and scene semantics while producing coordinated whole-body motions, which is challenging to achieve directly.

Method: A VLM selects successive task-relevant keyframes from a predefined library given language instructions, scene observations, and execution feedback. Selected keyframes are retargeted to the current scene to account for object poses and dimensions. A keyframe-conditioned whole-body RL policy then generates joint-level actions to reach these goals. A saliency-based keyframe sampling strategy is introduced for low-level policy training to handle sparse VLM keyframes.

Result: The saliency-based sampling improved end-to-end task success rate from 44% to 92% under sparse VLM keyframes. Evaluated on object pickup, transport, and placement tasks in simulation and on a Unitree G1 humanoid, the system performed one- and two-handed manipulation and generalised to placement locations beyond the training reference data.
