Conclusion: Motion keyframes serve as an effective intermediate representation coupling high-level VLM planning with low-level RL control, enabling generalisable whole-body loco-manipulation on a real humanoid robot.

Abstract: Humanoid loco-manipulation requires robots to interpret task instructions and scene semantics while executing coordinated whole-body motions. We propose a hierarchical framework that uses motion keyframes as an intermediate representation between Vision-Language Model (VLM) planning and Reinforcement Learning (RL) control. Each keyframe specifies a target whole-body robot pose and, when applicable, an object pose. Given a language instruction, scene observations, and execution feedback, the VLM selects successive task-relevant keyframes from a predefined library. The selected keyframes are retargeted to the current scene to account for object poses and dimensions. A keyframe-conditioned whole-body policy then generates joint-level actions to reach these goals. We introduce a saliency-based keyframe sampling strategy for low-level policy training that improves end-to-end task success rate from 44% to 92% when using sparse VLM keyframes. We evaluate our framework on object pickup, transport, and placement tasks in simulation and on a Unitree G1 humanoid. The system successfully performs both one- and two-handed manipulation and generalises to placement locations beyond the training reference data.

</details>


### [164] [Dreaming the Sound of Contact: Leveraging Video and Audio Generation for Zero-Shot Force-Aware Manipulation and Data Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19137)
*Guanhua Ji,Tianyu Li,Dayoon Suh,Yuqian Zhang,Boyan Zhang,Nadia Figueroa*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper augments generated videos with generated contact sounds to produce force-aware manipulation trajectories, using audio loudness to shape a time-varying desired-force profile tracked by a closed-loop force regulator on a Franka Panda robot.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Video-generation-based robot learning produces purely kinematic trajectories without force information, causing failures in contact-rich tasks where appropriate contact forces are essential.

Method: A pipeline jointly leverages generated video and audio to derive motion trajectories and corresponding desired-force profiles from a structured natural-language task prompt. Audio loudness of generated contact sounds shapes a bounded, time-varying desired-force profile, which is tracked during contact by a closed-loop force regulator on a Franka Panda robot. The pipeline is also used as a data generation engine to train closed-loop policies.

Result: On multiple tasks requiring contact, the pipeline achieves successful manipulation where a kinematic-only baseline fails, and policies trained on the generated data accomplish the tasks in a closed-loop manner.

Conclusion: Generated audio provides an effective modality for injecting force information into video-derived manipulation trajectories, enabling contact-rich tasks beyond the reach of kinematic-only approaches.

Abstract: Recent advances in video generation allow robots to learn manipulation trajectories from generated videos. However, these approaches produce purely kinematic trajectories that lack force information, causing failures in contact-rich tasks where appropriate contact forces are essential for success. In this work, we explore augmenting generated video with audio to shape a bounded, time-varying desired-force profile using the loudness of generated contact sounds. We present a pipeline that jointly leverages generated video and audio to derive motion trajectories and corresponding desired-force profiles from a structured natural-language task prompt. We execute these force-aware trajectories on a Franka Panda robot using a closed-loop force regulator that tracks the audio-shaped force profile during contact. We evaluate our pipeline on multiple tasks that require making contact and demonstrate successful manipulation where a kinematic-only baseline fails. We also use the pipeline as a data generation engine to train policies that achieve the tasks in a closed-loop manner. Project website, videos, and dataset: https://dreamingcontactsound.github.io/

</details>


### [165] [Body-Motion Control of a Simulated Aerial Swarm from a First-Person View](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18881)
*Yang Chen,Darius Giannoli,Dario Floreano*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: An upper-body motion interface (torso inclination, hand position, head rotation) mapped to five continuous command dimensions improved FPV swarm teleoperation efficiency versus a conventional transmitter, at the cost of higher physical demand.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: FPV teleoperation of aerial swarms demands that a single operator simultaneously coordinate collective translation, viewing direction, and formation spacing, which conventional transmitters handle poorly.

Method: An upper-body interface maps torso inclination, hand position, and head rotation to five continuous command dimensions, with per-participant calibration of neutral postures and motion ranges. Fourteen participants in a within-subject study navigated a simulated 15-agent swarm through 3D obstacle courses using both this interface and a conventional transmitter.

Result: Body-motion control reduced completion time by 19.4% and centroid path length by 7.0%, and increased path directness. Delivered-command variation fell 88.8% and concurrent command changes were more frequent. No differences appeared in gate-centering error, collection yield, crash or disconnection counts, workload, or usability. All participants reported higher physical demand with body-motion control.

Conclusion: The implemented interface improves FPV swarm navigation efficiency at the cost of greater physical demand.

Abstract: First-person-view (FPV) teleoperation of aerial swarms requires an operator to coordinate collective translation, viewing direction, and formation spacing. We present an upper-body interface that maps torso inclination, hand position, and head rotation to five continuous command dimensions. Neutral postures and motion ranges are calibrated for each participant. In a within-subject study, 14 participants navigated a simulated 15-agent swarm through three-dimensional obstacle courses using this interface and a conventional transmitter. Body-motion control reduced completion time by 19.4% and centroid path length by 7.0%, and increased path directness. Delivered-command variation was 88.8% lower, and concurrent command changes were more frequent. These command measures characterize the complete interfaces, which differed in calibration and filtering. No differences were detected in gate-centering error, collection yield, crash or disconnection counts, overall workload, or usability. All participants reported higher physical demand with body-motion control. The implemented interface therefore improved FPV navigation efficiency at the cost of greater physical demand.

</details>


### [166] [SOL-SLAM: Inverse Compositional Gauss-Newton Direct Registration for Fast Sonar-Only Local SLAM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18893)
*Kalvik Jakkala,Jason O'Kane*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes a dense direct registration approach for FLS-only local SLAM, aligning full acoustic intensity scans to a recursively updated local map via Inverse Compositional Gauss-Newton optimization, achieving sub-meter tracking and odometry performance comparable to multi-sensor fusion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous underwater navigation typically depends on complex, expensive multi-modal sensor suites for global SLAM accuracy, but local reactive behaviors (coarse navigation, obstacle avoidance) require only local consistency, which should be achievable with a single Forward-Looking Sonar (FLS). This FLS-only local SLAM capability remains largely unaddressed. Furthermore, existing acoustic SLAM frameworks rely on sparse feature extraction that discards much of the already information-sparse acoustic returns.

Method: The work introduces a dense direct registration approach that aligns full acoustic intensity scans to a recursively updated local map. Real-time execution is achieved through an Inverse Compositional Gauss-Newton optimization strategy that minimizes computational overhead.

Result: Experimental evaluations show the dense method significantly improves translation error compared to sparse keypoint baselines, maintaining stable sub-meter tracking precision over wide displacement gaps. It delivers odometry performance comparable to multi-sensor fusion pipelines (FLS, DVL, IMU), bypassing expensive payload dependencies in feature-rich environments. Real-world applicability is validated through AUV field trials running the full local SLAM approach onboard an embedded, resource-constrained computer.

Conclusion: Dense direct registration on full acoustic intensity scans enables accurate, real-time, FLS-only local SLAM, eliminating the need for sparse feature extraction and expensive multi-sensor payloads while remaining viable on resource-constrained embedded hardware.

Abstract: Autonomous underwater navigation typically relies on complex and expensive multi-modal sensor suites designed to prioritize global Simultaneous Localization and Mapping (SLAM) accuracy. However, local reactive behaviors such as coarse navigation and obstacle avoidance require only local consistency---a capability that should be feasible using only a Forward-Looking Sonar (FLS), yet remains largely unaddressed, leaving a critical gap in FLS-only local SLAM.
  Moreover, existing acoustic SLAM frameworks predominantly rely on sparse feature extraction methods that discard substantial portions of the already information-sparse acoustic returns. To overcome these limitations, this work introduces a dense direct registration approach that aligns full acoustic intensity scans to a recursively updated local map. Real-time execution is achieved via an Inverse Compositional Gauss-Newton optimization strategy that minimizes computational overhead.
  Experimental evaluations show that this dense method yields significant improvements on translation error compared to sparse keypoint baselines, maintaining stable sub-meter tracking precision over wide displacement gaps. Moreover, this approach delivers odometry performance comparable to multi-sensor fusion pipelines (FLS, DVL, and IMU), bypassing expensive payload dependencies in feature-rich environments. We validate real-world applicability through AUV field trials, running the full local SLAM approach onboard an embedded, resource-constrained computer.

</details>


### [167] [Examining the Difference in Human Behavior Between Virtual and Real-World Human-Robot Teaming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18900)
*Sean Dallas,Absalat Getachew,Motaz AbuHijleh,Andrea Macklem-Zabel,Douglas Zytko,Mark Brudnak,Wing-Yue Geoffrey Louie*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper compares human behavior in a human-robot teaming scenario between virtual and real environments, finding significant differences in workload, strategy, mental models of robots, and trust types.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world prototyping and evaluation of human-robot teaming (HRT) scenarios are costly, so virtual simulation is used as an alternative. However, the validity of virtual experimentation depends on whether human behavior is consistent between real and virtual worlds, which this study investigates.

Method: A user study comparing an HRT scenario conducted in a virtual versus a real environment, using mixed methods to examine team performance and human factors.

Result: Quantitative results showed a significant difference in workload between the two modalities. Qualitative analysis revealed differences in participants' strategy, their mental model of robots, and the type of trust they had for robots between modalities.

Conclusion: Human behavior differs between virtual and real HRT scenarios, which has implications for the validity of using virtual simulation as a substitute for real-world user studies.

Abstract: Prototyping and evaluating human-robot teaming (HRT) scenarios in the real-world is costly. Virtual simulation of HRT scenarios has been adopted as an alternative to conducting user studies in the real-world to investigate user perceptions, behaviors, and performance during human-robot interactions. The consistency of human behavior between the real and virtual-worlds is integral to the validity of utilizing such virtual experimentation. This paper presents a user study examining the difference in human behavior between an HRT scenario conducted in a virtual vs real environment. We employed mixed-methods to examine team performance and human factors during the HRT. Our quantitative results showed a significant difference in workload between the two modalities. Our qualitative analysis expanded on the quantitative results and found differences in participants' strategy, their mental model of robots, and the type of trust they had for robots between modalities.

</details>


### [168] [CaSCo: Cascade-Aware Soft-Collision Motion Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18910)
*Shivaram Kumar,Gaoyuan Liu,Yoonchang Sung*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: CaSCo is a motion planning framework that treats collisions as risk-weighted rather than binary, using an LM/VLM to assign semantic risk and a physics simulator to predict cascaded object collisions, then searches for a path minimizing total incurred risk.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional planning treats collision as binary, but contact consequences vary drastically (brushing cardboard vs. glass/laptop). Direct collisions can displace objects and trigger secondary object-object collisions, so risk depends on the physical evolution of the scene, not just the robot's geometric path.

Method: A vision-language or language model assigns semantic risk to objects; a physics simulator predicts consequences of candidate motions. The framework searches for paths minimizing total semantic risk of objects displaced directly or via cascades. Roadmap states are augmented with predicted object arrangement and the set of risk-incurred objects. It uses an optimal graph-search algorithm with an admissible and consistent cascade-relaxed heuristic plus caching and pruning.

Result: Experiments in cluttered manipulation environments evaluate semantic risk, cascade reasoning, planning efficiency, and real-robot operation.

Conclusion: CaSCo enables cascade-aware soft-collision planning that accounts for semantic object risk and secondary collisions, validated across simulation metrics and real-robot deployment.

Abstract: Conventional motion planning treats collision as a binary constraint, although contact with different objects can have drastically different consequences. A robot may safely brush against a cardboard box while even minor contact with a glass, laptop, or unstable object may be undesirable. Moreover, a direct robot--object collision can move the contacted object and trigger secondary object--object collisions, making the risk of a motion depend on the physical evolution of the scene rather than only on the robot's geometric path. We present CaSCo, a cascade-aware soft-collision motion planning framework in which a vision-language or language model assigns semantic risk to objects and a physics simulator predicts the consequences of candidate robot motions. CaSCo searches for a path that minimizes the total semantic risk of the unique objects displaced either directly by the robot or indirectly through cascaded collisions. Because collisions change the environment, we augment roadmap states with the predicted object arrangement and the set of objects whose risk has already been incurred. We develop an optimal graph-search algorithm with an admissible and consistent cascade-relaxed heuristic and caching and pruning mechanisms for efficient search. Experiments in cluttered manipulation environments evaluate semantic risk, cascade reasoning, planning efficiency, and real-robot operation.

</details>


### [169] [Learning Holistic Whole-Body Loco-Manipulation with a Bipedal Mobile Manipulator](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18930)
*Zhongyu Chen,Yuxuan Nai,Qian Chen,Yidong Zhu,Chen Jing,Qihan Wang,Xudong Li,Zhizhan Li,Leixin Chang,Liangjing Yang,Hua Chen*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A unified reinforcement-learned whole-body controller for bipedal loco-manipulation maps 6-DoF end-effector targets directly to coordinated base and arm actions, autonomously handling reaching, postural adaptation, and stepping without explicit base-velocity or footstep commands.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bipedal loco-manipulation extends robot manipulation beyond the nominal arm workspace by coordinating locomotion and manipulation. This requires a low-level whole-body controller that converts task-level manipulation goals into coordinated arm and leg motions while keeping balance, yet prior approaches typically depend on explicit base-velocity or footstep commands, complicating the interface and coordination.

Method: The authors train a single whole-body controller via reinforcement learning that maps 6-DoF end-effector targets directly to coordinated bipedal base and arm actions. A reward-gating strategy balances the trade-offs among end-effector tracking, locomotion, and balance during training. A temporal context estimator extracts dynamics-relevant information from observation history by combining windowed Transformer encoding, recurrent GRU memory, and auxiliary dynamics prediction.

Result: Real-robot experiments show that the same controller performs reaching, postural adaptation, and stepping under commands from VR teleoperation, a learned diffusion policy, and scripted trajectories, serving as a common end-effector interface for diverse manipulation tasks.

Conclusion: The proposed controller provides a unified end-effector-level interface for bipedal loco-manipulation, enabling autonomous coordination of reaching, posture, and stepping and supporting multiple command sources on real hardware.

Abstract: Bipedal loco-manipulation enables robots to interact with objects beyond the nominal workspace of their arms by coordinating locomotion and manipulation. Realizing this capability requires a low-level whole-body controller that translates task-level manipulation goals into coordinated arm and leg motions while maintaining balance. We present a unified whole-body controller trained with reinforcement learning that directly maps 6-DoF end-effector targets to coordinated actions for the bipedal base and robotic arm. Given only an end-effector target, the learned controller autonomously coordinates reaching, postural adaptation, and stepping without explicit base-velocity or footstep commands. A reward-gating strategy regulates the trade-offs among end-effector tracking, locomotion, and balance during training, while a temporal context estimator combines windowed Transformer encoding, recurrent GRU memory, and auxiliary dynamics prediction to extract dynamics-relevant information from observation history. Real-robot experiments demonstrate that the same controller supports reaching, postural adaptation, and stepping under commands from VR teleoperation, a learned diffusion policy, and scripted trajectories, providing a common end-effector interface for diverse manipulation tasks.

</details>


### [170] ["What's going to happen after I'm gone?": Parent Perspectives on Technology in Supporting Independent Living for Adults with Intellectual Disabilities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18970)
*Alexander Tyshka,Andrea Macklem-Zabel,Absalat Getachew,Foong Ling Chen,Wing-Yue Geoffrey Louie*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Interviews with 16 parents of adults with intellectual and developmental disabilities transitioning to semi-independent living reveal four caregiving themes and yield four design principles for growth-oriented assistive technology.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As adults with IDD move from family homes to semi-independent living, parents relinquish the primary caregiver role and face challenges arranging consistent, quality support. Parents hold extensive explicit and tacit knowledge of their adult child's needs, and understanding their caregiving routines is foundational to determining how assistive technologies can support this transition.

Method: Thematic analysis of interviews with 16 parents of adults with IDD beginning the transition, addressing: how they currently provide daily living support and what makes it effective; their experiences and perceptions of technology; and how they envision assistive technology integrating with the new home or other supports to promote independence.

Result: Four themes emerged: common modes of support; inside the routine to support growth; the fragility of continuity of care across transitions; and participants' perceptions and experiences of assistive technology. From these, four design principles for growth-oriented assistive technology were derived.

Conclusion: The four design principles are: pre-transition onboarding to capture caregiver tacit knowledge; structured scaffolding towards long-term growth; adaptive sensing that responds to day-to-day variability; and customization for the individual balanced with consistency for the care network.

Abstract: Adults with intellectual and developmental disabilities (IDD) are increasingly transitioning from family homes towards semi-inde\-pendent living. As parents hand off the role of primary caregiver, they face numerous challenges in arranging consistent and quality support. Our research centers on understanding these caregiving routines. By focusing on the unique lived experience of parents, who possess extensive explicit and tacit knowledge of their adult child's requirements, we aim to map the management of care they provide. This foundational understanding is essential to identifying how assistive technologies can effectively serve a key role in supporting adults with IDD in this transition. In this work, we interviewed 16 parents of adults with IDD beginning this transition to understand: 1) how they currently provide support for daily living and what makes their support effective; 2) what their experiences and perceptions are regarding the use of technology; and 3) how they envision assistive technology successfully integrating with their adult child's new home or with other supports to promote independence. Our thematic analysis produced four themes: common modes of support, inside the routine to support growth, the fragility of continuity of care across transitions, and participants' perceptions and experiences of assistive technology. Building on these findings, we derive four design principles for growth-oriented assistive technology. These include pre-transition onboarding to capture caregiver tacit knowledge; structured scaffolding towards long-term growth; adaptive sensing that responds to day-to-day variability; and customization for the individual balanced with consistency for the care network.

</details>


### [171] [Information-Based Trajectory Planning for Spacecraft-to-Spacecraft Tracking and Navigation in Cislunar Space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19012)
*Trevor N. Wolf,Brandon A. Jones,Jay W. McMahon*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A trajectory planning method balances information collection and control effort to improve cislunar spacecraft-to-spacecraft absolute tracking, achieving nearly an order-of-magnitude improvement in expected navigation and tracking errors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Expanding use of cislunar space demands alternative navigation and tracking procedures that reduce reliance on ground-based support. Spacecraft-to-spacecraft tracking exploits nonlinear dynamical tracers encoded in relative measurements to infer the absolute states of both observer and target, and the geometry between spacecraft significantly influences tracking performance—motivating geometry-aware trajectory design.

Method: Designs observer trajectories that jointly balance control effort and an information-theoretic quantification of expected spacecraft-to-spacecraft tracking performance. The approach is formulated as an optimal control problem leveraging information gain, supporting multiple low-thrust observation platforms with various sensing modalities and multiple space object targets in planning.

Result: Reports almost an order-of-magnitude improvement in expected navigation and tracking errors for an optical observer operating in a Distant Retrograde Orbit (DRO).

Conclusion: Demonstrates the feasibility and value of information-optimal low-thrust spacecraft trajectory design for current and upcoming cislunar missions.

Abstract: We present a trajectory planning method that balances information collection and control effort to improve cislunar spacecraft-to-spacecraft absolute tracking. Expanding use of cislunar space requires alternative navigation and tracking procedures that minimize reliance on ground-based support. Among efforts to address this need, spacecraft-to-spacecraft tracking exploits nonlinear dynamical tracers encoded in a series of relative measurements to infer absolute states of both an observer and a target. The geometry between spacecraft operating under this mode can significantly influence tracking performance. This work considers this geometrical impact by designing observer trajectories that jointly balance control effort and an information-theoretic quantification of the expected spacecraft-to-spacecraft tracking performance. Our methods are designed for multiple low-thrust observation platforms of various sensing modalities and can incorporate multiple space object targets in planning. By leveraging information gain in the optimal control problem, we report almost an order of magnitude improvement in the expected navigation and tracking errors for an optical observer operating in a Distant Retrograde Orbit (DRO). This work demonstrates the feasibility and value of information-optimal low-thrust spacecraft trajectory design for current and upcoming cislunar missions.

</details>


### [172] [Learning to Stack: Cube-Stacking Imitation Learning from Virtual Reality Demonstrations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19040)
*Gryffin Reizian,Jordan Dowdy,Jean Chagas Vaz*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A VR-based data-collection pipeline in Isaac Sim/Lab enables efficient cube-stacking demonstrations with a custom 5-DoF arm, decoupling collection from dataset construction for reuse across observation/action spaces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Imitation learning is attractive for robot manipulation, but collecting demonstrations is a bottleneck, especially for multi-stage tasks that require repeated scene resets.

Method: An operator uses an HTC Vive Pro 2, Manus Quantum gloves, and OpenXR to issue SE(3) end-effector commands for a custom 5-DoF arm in NVIDIA Isaac Sim/Isaac Lab. The framework separates demonstration collection from dataset construction by replaying recorded trajectories, converting task-space commands into joint-space actions, and re-rendering demonstrations with updated sensor or state configurations, enabling reuse for new observation and action spaces without re-teleoperation.

Result: For a randomized three-cube stacking task (red on blue, green on red), 200 virtual demonstrations were collected in 30 minutes, versus 45 real-world demonstrations; Isaac Mimic generated 100 additional samples. A behavior-cloning policy was trained from the virtual demonstrations using LeRobot-style dual-camera observations and evaluated in simulation.

Conclusion: The VR pipeline addresses the demonstration bottleneck for multi-stage manipulation by enabling rapid, reusable virtual data collection, with higher throughput than real-world collection.

Abstract: Imitation learning is attractive for robot manipulation, but collecting demonstrations remains a bottleneck for multi-stage tasks requiring repeated scene resets. This work presents a virtual-reality data-collection pipeline for cube-stacking with a custom 5-DoF arm in NVIDIA Isaac Sim and Isaac Lab. Using an HTC Vive Pro 2, Manus Quantum gloves, and OpenXR, an operator provides SE(3) end-effector commands to generate task demonstrations. The proposed framework separates demonstration collection from dataset construction by replaying recorded trajectories, converting task-space commands into joint-space actions, and re-rendering demonstrations with updated sensor or state configurations. This allows previously collected demonstrations to be reused for new observation and action spaces without repeating human teleoperation. The task requires stacking the red cube on the blue cube and the green cube on the red cube, with randomized cube placement. In 30 minutes, 200 virtual demonstrations were collected, compared with 45 real-world demonstrations, and Isaac Mimic generated 100 additional samples. A behavior-cloning policy was trained from the virtual demonstrations using LeRobot-style dual-camera observations and evaluated in simulation.

</details>


### [173] [Loco-Loco-RL: Low-Cost Terrain Mapping for Humanoid Locomotion with Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19041)
*Jordan Dowdy,Gryffin Reizian,Jean Chagas Vaz*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A low-cost time-of-flight sensor paired with a token-compressed temporal transformer policy enables robust humanoid terrain locomotion by efficiently using sparse 3D terrain representations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Informative terrain perception is critical for robust RL locomotion policies, but common sensors like depth cameras and LiDAR incur high cost, power, and processing overhead while producing redundant high-resolution data. A cheaper, more compact alternative is needed to feed terrain information efficiently.

Method: The approach uses a low-cost time-of-flight sensor to produce a compact 3D local terrain representation. Proprioceptive and terrain observations are tokenized and processed by a self-attention multi-head transformer to capture within-timestep relationships. Attended tokens are compressed via an MLP-based latent-space token compression module before storage in a rolling 15-timestep history. A second cross-attention multi-head transformer extracts temporal locomotion features from this compact history for policy learning, compressing tokens before temporal aggregation to limit dimensional growth.

Result: Validation through sim-to-real transfer on physical hardware using a terrain-based locomotion benchmark demonstrated robust humanoid terrain walking with low-cost local terrain sensing.

Conclusion: The token-compressed temporal transformer effectively preserves important terrain-observation structure while controlling attention dimensionality over observation histories, enabling robust humanoid terrain locomotion using only low-cost time-of-flight sensing.

Abstract: Informative terrain perception is important for robust reinforcement learning policies in humanoid locomotion. Still, common sensors such as depth cameras and LiDARs incur high cost, power, and processing overhead while often producing redundant, high-resolution data. This work uses a low-cost time-of-flight sensor to provide a compact 3D local terrain representation for humanoid locomotion. To efficiently use this sparse exteroceptive input, we introduce a token-compressed temporal transformer policy. Proprioceptive and terrain observations are tokenized and processed by a self-attention multi-head transformer to capture within-timestep relationships between observation terms. The attended tokens are then compressed through an MLP-based latent-space token compression module before being stored in a rolling 15-timestep history. A second cross-attention multi-head transformer extracts temporal locomotion features from this compact history for policy learning. By compressing tokens before temporal aggregation, the architecture preserves important terrain-observation structure while limiting the dimensional growth of attention over observation histories. We validate our method through sim-to-real transfer on physical hardware using a terrain-based locomotion benchmark, demonstrating robust humanoid terrain walking with low-cost local terrain sensing.

</details>


### [174] [ElastiQP: An Always-Feasible QP Solver for Constrained Robot Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19080)
*Daniel Morton,Jon Arrizabalaga,Zachary Manchester,Marco Pavone*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ElastiQP is a modified dual active-set QP solver that relaxes every inequality constraint with an exact per-constraint l1 penalty while keeping dynamics equality constraints hard, achieved by analytically folding slack variables into the solver to preserve a constant-size condensed linear system; it runs at microsecond speeds, matches or beats leading solvers on feasible problems, and returns usable solutions up to 40x faster on infeasible ones.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As robot capabilities grow, QP-based controllers must handle an increasing number of constraints for safe, reliable operation. Each added constraint increases the chance of momentary conflict, in which case a solver returning "infeasible" leaves the controller with nothing to execute. A solver is needed that degrades gracefully under infeasibility rather than failing outright.

Method: ElastiQP is a modified dual active-set QP solver. It relaxes every inequality constraint using an exact, per-constraint l1 penalty while keeping equality constraints (dynamics) hard. It folds the slack variables into the solver analytically, thereby maintaining a constant size of the condensed linear system.

Result: On a suite of robot control benchmarks, ElastiQP achieves microsecond-level performance, matching or outperforming leading modern solvers on feasible problems. On infeasible problems, it handles them gracefully—confining violations strictly to the conflicting inequality terms—and returns a usable solution up to 40x faster than the best alternative solvers.

Conclusion: ElastiQP provides fast, robust QP solving for robot control, remaining usable under infeasibility by localizing violations to conflicting inequality constraints. It is released as an open-source C++ header-only library with Python and JAX interfaces at https://github.com/StanfordASL/elastiqp.

Abstract: As robot capabilities increase, quadratic programming (QP)-based controllers must account for a similarly increasing number of constraints to ensure safe, reliable operation. Yet, with each added constraint, this introduces more chances of momentary conflict: in which case, a QP solver that returns an "infeasible" status leaves the controller with nothing to execute. To address this, we introduce ElastiQP, a modified dual active-set QP solver that relaxes every inequality constraint with an exact, per-constraint l1 penalty while keeping equality constraints (dynamics) hard. Notably, ElastiQP does so by folding the slack variables into the solver analytically, maintaining a constant size of the condensed linear system. On a suite of robot control benchmarks, ElastiQP achieves microsecond-level performance, matching or outperforming leading modern solvers on feasible problems. On infeasible problems, ElastiQP handles these gracefully, confining violations to strictly the conflicting inequality terms, returning a usable solution up to 40x faster than the best alternative solvers. ElastiQP is available as an open-source C++ header-only library, with Python and JAX interfaces, at https://github.com/StanfordASL/elastiqp.

</details>
